Preface
As this book project entered its final phases, the right to protest seemed to be
increasingly under threat. In July 2024, Amnesty International published a
critical report on “the state of the right to protest in 21 European countries”
and concluded that it was both “under protected” and “over restricted.” Once
considered a litmus test for democracy, protest is being aggressively policed
and protesters subjected to arrest and incarceration. Against the background
of such threats, my book offers many reminders of the historical importance
of protest, of efforts to challenge in the streets the policies of governments
and corporations. Above all, this book shows how people later make sense
of such attempts to change the world and explores the ways in which telling
stories about past protest helps in renewing the fight for social and environ-
mental justice. Understanding better how stories of protest are made and
transmitted will hopefully help in the future defense of civil liberties and in
putting active citizenship at the heart of collective narratives at a time when
grievance seems to prevail.
Occasionally it seemed as if I’d chosen the worst of all times to write
about hope. As the world was darkening in face of war, genocide, immi-
nent climate collapse, and threats to democracy through disinformation
and demagogues, it even felt naive. As my research progressed, however,
it brought me into virtual contact with the memory of so many instances
of committed risk-taking that I became all the more convinced of the value
of my topic. It became my goal to understand how hope becomes transfer-
able through the stories told about it. I have done my best to use historical
evidence and the tools of cultural analysis to do justice to the many efforts
I describe. I hope that my book helps in turning their memory into a
renewable resource for negotiating the future.
This book was conceived in close collaboration with the Remembering
Activism research group at Utrecht University: Tashina Blom, Duygu Erbil,
Emilia Salvanou, Thomas Smits, Clara Vlessing, and Sophie van den Elzen
along with our close associate Daniele Salerno. Our Wednesday afternoon
meetings were a huge source of inspiration, an ongoing delight, and a huge

xiv PREFACE
privilege. Closely collaborating with such a talented team helped me develop
my arguments and expand my horizons in ways that I could never have
anticipated. Conversations with visitors like Lucie Drechselová, David Beor-
legui Zarranz, Aidan McGarry, Lorenzo Zamponi, and many others added
additional perspectives and helped guide me as a scholar of narrative and
cultural memory through the field of social movement studies. Working
closely with Samuel Merrill on the editorial of our special issue of Memory
Studies (2024) titled Remembering Activism was especially rewarding.
In multiple other ways, I have incurred debts. In particular I would like
to mention Red Chidgey, Jenny Wüstenberg, Ton Robben, and Kiene Bril-
lenburg for their feedback on my initial proposal for research into the
memory-activism nexus; and my friend and colleague Rolf Loeber, who
offered invaluable feedback at an early stage but unfortunately did not live
to see the outcome. I am also grateful for the invaluable support of Ste-
fan Berger, Donatella della Porta, W.J.T. Mitchell, José van Dijck, Martin
Everaert, Alison Ribeiro de Menezes, and Barbara To¨rnquist-Plewa, and of

in Amsterdam, whose collections were a treasure trove for the history of
protest.
As I was busy discovering that my topic was both immensely exciting
and unfeasibly vast, I gratefully accepted leads from Bob Becking, Maria
Grever, Zoé Carle, Marie Kra¨mer, Nina Grønlykke Mollerup, Laurens Ham,
Wulf Kansteiner, Joep Leerssen, Andreas Bandak, Susanne Knittel, Jeroen

and Lilí Ní Dhomhnaill. A special thanks to Geert Buelens, who generously
shared his expert knowledge of the history of environmentalism.
In the course of many discussions, sightlines opened on areas beyond

me with stills from his documentary Hambi. In so many ways, this book

Invitations to present my work in progress allowed me to hone my ideas

siasm of the series editors Astrid Erll and Jeffrey Olick has been priceless,
as has been the support of James Cooke at Oxford University Press. I am

guidance in preparing the publication, and to Madelon Nanninga for the
index.
This is in the first instance an academic work of cultural analysis. I have
nevertheless tried to write it in an accessible way in the hope that it will speak
across disciplines and feed back into the more general understanding of how
protest events are culturally mediated and why some memories stick where
others don’t. With readability in mind, I have restricted in-text citations to
writers directly referenced and invite the reader to the endnotes for sources
that have provided background. Unless otherwise indicated, all translations
are mine.
Some elements of this book have already appeared in print elsewhere.
The Introduction is derived from “Remembering Hope: Transnational
Activism beyond the Traumatic,” Memory Studies 11.3 (2018), 368–380.
Chapter 3 integrates and reworks “Differential Memorability and Transna-
tional Activism: Bloody Sunday, 1887–2016,” Australian Humanities Review
59 (2016), 77–95, and “Mediations of Outrage: How the Killing of Protesters
Is Remembered,” Social Research: An International Quarterly 87.3 (2020)
707–733. Sections of Chapter 4 appear in A. Erll and W. Hirst, eds., Com-
panion to Interdisciplinary Memory Research (forthcoming), and in A.
Mattoni, ed., Handbook of Progressive Politics (Cheltenham, UK: Edward
Elgar, 2025), 34–51. Chapter 6 appeared in a shorter form in a special
issue of Memory Studies 17.5 (2024), 1195–1212. Chapter 5 integrates and
reworks “Decomissioning Monuments, Mobilizing Materialities,” in Y. Gut-
man and J. Wüstenberg, eds., The Routledge Handbook of Memory Activism

(London: Routledge, 2023), 21–27, and “Toxic Monuments and Mnemonic

Introduction
Setting the Scene
- On November 2, 2021, during COP26 in Glasgow, Extinction Rebel-
lion activists could be seen dressed up in the signature colors and
Edwardian costumes of the Suffragette movement. Holding banners
recycling Suffragette slogans with “deeds not words” and “vote for the
planet,” they stood outside the offices of JP Morgan to protest against
the bank’s investment in fossil fuel.
- On August 23, 2019, an estimated 200,000 people campaigning for
democracy in Hong Kong held hands in a 50-kilometer chain. The
timing and the form of their protest was chosen to mark the thirtieth
anniversary of the Baltic Way protest against Soviet dominance in Esto-
nia, Latvia, and Lithuania in 1989. Those standing in line in Hong Kong
were also fighting to maintain their independence, while paying tribute
to and drawing inspiration from earlier waves of protest at the other end
of the world.
- On January 15, 1919, the German socialist Karl Liebknecht wrote an
article in which he condemned the violent crushing of the Spartacist
Uprising a few days earlier, while defiantly insisting that a future
revolution was still destined to happen. In calling his article “Trotz alle-
dem” (despite everything), Liebknecht too was paying tribute to earlier
activists and using their work as a model. “Trotz alledem” had been one
of the main slogans of the 1848 Revolution in Germany, derived from a
poem by Ferdinand Freiligrath, which was in turn derived from a 1795
revolutionary poem by the Scottish Robert Burns.1
Three very different occasions, with nevertheless something in common.
Each in its own way illustrates how campaigns to change the world are
bound up with recollections of earlier protest, including ones in the distant
past or in distant countries. Marking dissent in the present and paying trib-
ute to predecessors go together—in overt references, the recycling of slogans
and costumes, the timing of protests to coincide with anniversaries, or in the
Remembering Hope. Ann Rigney, Oxford University Press. © Oxford University Press (2025).
DOI: 10.1093/oso/9780197789711.003.0001

2 REMEMBERING HOPE
very form chosen to express claims. The three examples set the scene for the
two central arguments of this book: that activists work to change the future
while also looking back; and that in looking back, they draw on the mem-
ory of predecessors as this has been sedimented in words, images, and other
media.
Remembering Hope explores the entanglement between memory and
activism through a series of case studies from the recent and less recent past.
Specifically, it examines the role of culture (forms, artifacts, languages) in
connecting different waves of mobilization across generations and geogra-
phies. My overall aim is to provide a new framework for thinking about
collective memory that brings out its futural dimensions and links it to social
transformation. Putting dissenting citizens central stage means shifting to a
relatively understudied arena and to forms of memory work that are directed
toward change rather than preservation.
Memory Studies Beyond “Never Again”
The interdisciplinary field of memory studies has been going from strength
to strength in the last decades. But it remains open to the criticism of being
backward looking. Too great a preoccupation with collective memory, it
is argued, risks diminishing our ability to respond creatively to the seri-
ous challenges of our time. Does collective memory—as a social fact and
as an object of study—help us in negotiating our way toward a more just
and sustainable society? Or does it instead merely offer a distraction from
the challenges of the present by encouraging us to dwell in the past? About
twenty years ago, Andreas Huyssen (2000) linked the intense preoccupation
with memorialization in contemporary society and scholarship to a failure
to imagine the future. Echoing Friedrich Nietzsche’s complaint in his “Use
and Abuse of History for Life” (1997 [1874]) that excessive attention to the
past impedes our “plastic power” to make the world anew, Huyssen ended
his article with an exhortation: “Perhaps it is time to remember the future,
rather than only worry about the future of memory” (38). A decade later,
the apparent incompatibility of future-thinking and memory was still being
noted: “For those who study memory, there is a nagging concern that mem-
ory studies is inherently backward-looking, and that memory itself—and the
ways in which it is deployed, invoked and utilized—can potentially hinder
efforts to move forward” (Gutman et al. 2010: 1).

INTRODUCTION 3
One way of dealing with this “nagging concern” is to rethink temporality.
Traditionally memory has been defined in terms of a two-way relationship
between past and present; as “the presence of the past” (Terdiman 1993); or
as “the interplay of present and past in socio-cultural contexts” (Erll 2008:
2). More recent definitions of memory have tentatively included a futural
dimension.2 This is partly a response to the nagging concerns mentioned
earlier, but above all a response to the changing understandings of tempo-
rality which have challenged linear notions of time and the modernist belief
that the future will always improve on the past. Thus Aleida Assmann (2020
[2013]) has described the extended present as the site where recollections (of
the past) and expectations (for the future) are produced in a mutually illumi-
nating way.3 In these revised understandings of time and memory, the future
and past are no longer mutually exclusive orientations. Indeed, a recent essay
goes so far as to posit “collective future thought” as “the driving force behind
the (re)construction of a collective’s past” (Szpunar & Szpunar 2016: 378).4
Redefining memory to include a futural dimension is not enough, how-
ever, to mitigate the “nagging concerns” mentioned earlier. There is more
at stake: the very understanding of what constitutes collective memory and
what should be the focus of memory studies. As is well known, the field
emerged in response to the atrocities that marked human history in the
twentieth century and the memorial cultures they gave rise to. This led the
editors of The Collective Memory Reader (Olick et al. 2011), a landmark
handbook for the field, to dedicate a section of their Introduction to the ques-
tion “Did the Holocaust cause memory studies?” (29–36). Their answer was
a qualified “yes.” The Holocaust was not the only factor but was nevertheless
of vital importance in bringing memory to the forefront of societal concerns
and in consolidating a historical shift away from celebratory modes of pub-
lic commemoration to ones focused on victimhood, trauma, and calls for
accountability. Whatever its precise origins, there is no doubt that, since it
emerged in the late 1980s, the field of memory studies has gravitated toward
the personal and collective legacies of violence.
Although the geographical scope of research has broadened since then,
the field has by and large been dominated by a traumatic paradigm that both
responds to and feeds into the prevalence of mourning and memorializa-
tion in contemporary cultures of memory. The idea put forward by Ernest
Renan that “suffering connects people more than joy” (1947–1961 [1882]),
has become a guiding principle, if not indeed a dogma. Aleida Assmann, a
leading figure in the field, proposed memory as an alternative to modernist

4 REMEMBERING HOPE
understandings of time. In doing so, she explicitly referred to memory the-
ory as a model for engaging “with the dark, shameful, and traumatic chapters
of one’s own history” rather than for “reclaiming positive traditions” (2020
[2013]: 173). These “dark, shameful, and traumatic chapters” are relevant
for the future, she implied, in the form of accountability for past wrongs and
as a model of things to be avoided in a just society.
This association between memory theory and the “dark, shameful, and
traumatic chapters” of recent history is widespread and has given the field
its moral raison d’être. Accordingly, Alejandro Baer and Natan Sznaider have
linked the study of memory to an ethics of remembering based on the prin-
ciple of “Never Again.” They describe this as a “reverse Utopia” (2016: 4)
in that it is “not geared toward the construction of a new man and a new
society but toward fending off repetitions of horrific pasts” (6). Here too the
future is imagined as the absence of something bad rather than the presence
of something good or as the opening up of new possibilities. Nunca más, nie
wieder, never again.
In line with this tradition, Memory and the Future (Gutman et al. 2010)
envisaged the future in the negative mirror of things to be avoided. More
recently, the traumatic paradigm has persisted in publications linking mem-
ory studies to the current climate crisis, which has been one of the prime
feeders of the recent preoccupation with futurity. Thus, Stef Craps (2017)
has brought the concept of “proleptic mourning” to the analysis of climate
change fiction while Susanne Knittel (2023) has invoked the model of the
Holocaust to describe what she calls “the memory-ecocide nexus.” Although
the empirical focus has shifted to a quite different arena, the latter is still
mapped in terms of suffering and violence. Memory studies continues to be
about the bad stuff.
There are, of course, very good reasons for drilling down on the memory
of violence and war. The field is young, and scholars are still figuring out
which mnemonic practices can best help in dealing with the legacy of his-
torical injustice, including ongoing genocides that are set to be the subject
of future remembrance. But the traumatic paradigm has a cost; that of mak-
ing memory studies implicated in perpetuating the commonly held belief
that violence is the primary subject of collective memory and that victim-
hood is the core of personal and social identity.5 Going beyond grievance
has become all the more urgent at a time when a growing “paranoid nation-
alism” has emerged in the post-1989 era in response to the “shrinking” of a
society’s hopes for a better future (Hage 2003).

INTRODUCTION 5
Memory studies may not be inherently backward-looking, but risks
becoming so if it does not actively seek to extend its purview beyond the
traumatic horizons of dominant practices and dominant discourses. Sticking
to the “bad stuff ” forecloses the emergence of other perspectives on memory
work that might turn it into a resource for imagining the future in other than
negative terms. With the need for an alternative in mind, I propose to move
in this book from the “never again” to the “not yet.”
I start by recognizing that we have sophisticated theories for capturing
the interplay between recollection and amnesia in cases of mass violence
and the suffering occasioned by military conflict. But that we still have a
limited repertoire of analytic tools for capturing the transmission of positive
attachments across space and across generations. Indeed, the very idea of
positivity at this juncture may appear misguided. It conjures up images of
nineteenth-century or socialist-realist happy-end histories; the triumphalist
mode of remembrance that has given bloated statues and larger-than-life
heroes a bad name by presuming that history is finished and the future will
offer more of the same.
Positivity in the present context does not reflect a naive trust that every-
thing will be right with the world or, alternatively, that the best things are
behind us. Instead, it is a way of opening up a perspective on people’s attach-
ments to objects of value and ideals for a good life: the things we care for and
that are worth defending and claiming—for example, by going out onto the
streets to show dissent and demand alternatives.
Taking protest as my focus here paves the way to a critical memory stud-
ies more attentive to memory as an active force in transferring positive
attachments through stories about attempts to challenge the “politics of
inevitability” (Snyder 2017: 88) and hence change the course of history.
Can we capture memory in the mode of “for” rather than “against”? The
following chapters turn away from the theater of war to explore the mem-
ory of oppositional groups and their attempts to create a better future, be
that through public demonstrations or some other form of collective action.
Such struggles often ended in failure and in suffering caused by police vio-
lence. As we will see, however, this does not mean that they are necessarily
remembered in the mode of grievance.
In putting the memory of protest at the center of its concerns, this
book thematizes a key aspect of democratic life. It makes visible a hith-
erto neglected body of evidence about the role of memory in progressive
movements in the last two centuries. In doing so, it also reveals some of the

6 REMEMBERING HOPE
mechanisms whereby “looking back” becomes a way of “looking forward.”
It shows how recollections of the past can become a laboratory for future
scenarios without falling back into a grand narrative of progress or rev-
olutionary transformation. But crucially also, without falling back into a
“monumental” form of history (Nietzsche 1997 [1874]) that presumes the
best has already happened and that the future can only try to repeat the past.
In sum, it shows how memory has been used as a “resource of hope,” to echo
the title of a book by Raymond Williams (1989). As we will see in the next
section, the very principle of hope is antimonumental because it assumes
that the world is still unfinished.
Hope as a Practice of Possibilities
“Hope” is a tricky term since it is a category of practice in everyday use (as
in the famous Hope poster for the Obama presidential campaign) as well as
a category of analysis based on a long tradition of scholarly reflection. While
the following chapters regularly pick up on everyday uses of the term, “hope”
is above all used here as a category of analysis that draws on a wealth of recent
publications. A slew of titles indicate that hope is in the air: Hope in a Demo-
cratic Age (Mittleman 2009), The Paradox of Hope (Mattingly 2010), Hope
Without Optimism (Eagleton 2015), Hope: A Literary History (Potkay 2022),
Hope in the Dark (Solnit 2016 [2004]), “Radical Hope as a Practice of Possi-
bilities” (Henig 2022), The Principle of Political Hope (Goldman 2022), The
Virtue of Hope (Snow 2024), Hoffnung (Grethlein 2024).6 This preoccupa-
tion with hope is part of a broader attempt to kickstart futural thinking after
the loss, around 1989, of the grand narrative of revolutionary transformation
underpinning Marxism and, after the financial crash of 2008 and in face of
the climate crisis, of the grand narrative of automatic progress underpinning
modernist-liberal discourses.7
Across many differences in approach and philosophical framework, the
recent literature on hope yields the following takeaways:
1. Hope is not the same thing as optimism. As Terry Eagleton (2015)
in particular argues, optimism bespeaks passivity. It is the affective
and cognitive disposition of people incapable of envisaging anything
other than positive outcomes; who presume that a rosy future will flow

INTRODUCTION 7
inevitably from present conditions without any action being required.
Hope too has an anticipatory logic, but it is a disposition that is based
on mere possibility, not certainty. It entails attachment to some object
of value despite its present absence and even past denial. It is “not the
conviction that something will turn out well, but the certainty that
something makes sense, regardless of how it turns out” (Václav Havel,
in Potkay 2022: 26).
2. Hope is a disposition to act, and not just a passive waiting. It is “a prac-
tice of imagining possibilities and acting on them” (Henig 2022: 219)
so that a different future can emerge from the present. “Hope requires
that we must act in the present, rather than simply wait for a future that
is always before us” (Ahmed 2014: 184).8 It is a “civic virtue” (Mittle-
man 2009: 12) in being a disposition to take life-affirming action in the
belief that “the world is not yet final” (Goldman 2022: 10). While hope
has thus a futural orientation, it is firmly located as a praxis in the here
and now. Only through the repeated exercise of hope in defense of key
values can the outcome desired ever be realized.
3. Hope is transferable, even contagious. This is more a minor thread
in the literature than a fully developed theme, but is crucial for the
present book. Although hope as a disposition to act in the world
is rooted in individual bodies, it can also become intersubjective
under certain conditions. To begin with: phenomenological condi-
tions, including the affective experience of seeing hope expressed in
other people. Sara Ahmed has described how the “gathering together”
of protesters and the affective bonds stemming from their physical co-
presence creates the sense of the world opening up and of space being
claimed (2014: 184). Manuel Castells describes such an experience in
the words of a participant in the Occupy movement of 2011: “We’ve
got a long way to go, but every so often I get to feel the chill running
up and down my spine telling me that this is what hope looks like”
(2012: 169). As this book will show, hope is also transferred through
the mediated recall of earlier practices of possibility. Images, texts, and
other cultural carriers of memory have the power to reactivate hope
in those who “came after.” This power can be enhanced by a sensu-
ally appealing aesthetic form that, in creating a rupture in everyday
perception, has the power to generate a new sense of future possibility
(Goldman 2022).9 In what follows, I take up this point while being less

8 REMEMBERING HOPE
concerned with the arts per se in creating unprecedented possibilities
than with the role of aesthetic form in reactivating the possibilities that
were already at play in past protest.
4. The memory of hope is inseparable from the memory of defeat.
In common parlance, hope is always against hope, since so many
attempts to change the world have led to disappointment. Defeat is
accordingly a recurrent theme in the memory practices of progressive
movements.10 In his reflections on post-1989 “left-wing melancholia,”
Enzo Traverso (2016) acknowledges this history of defeat while also
refusing to consign struggles for a better world to the past. Traverso’s
invocation of the Freudian opposition between mourning and melan-
cholia might seem, once again, to gravitate toward the idea that
history-is-where-it-hurts. Yet, the vocabulary used (defeat, the van-
quished) helps to reframe loss, not as victimhood, but as a struggle for
a cause whose recollection reopens the paths not taken in history. The
sight of the defeated is always a critical reminder that things could have
gone another way (Traverso 2016: 25–26). This provides a foretaste
of how defeat and hope can be remembered together. As the follow-
ing chapters show, the long-term transmission of hope brings memory
away from the recollection of what actually happened into the ambit
of the possible.
The Memory-Activism Nexus
Remembering Hope was conceived in dialogue with a growing number
of studies with combinations of memory, activism, social movements,
and protest in their titles.11 Emanating both from memory studies and
social movement studies, their lead terms vary depending on whether the
focus is social mobilization broadly defined (“activism”), specific social-
historical formations (“movements”), or specific public performances of
dissent (“protest”).12 What broadly connects them is a desire to work out
how the past-orientation that is the primary constituent of memory relates
to the futural orientation constitutive of attempts to change the course of his-
tory. For those in social movement studies, memory represented a new arena
of research within a larger turn to the cultural and symbolic dimensions
of protest and other forms of contention.13 For those in memory studies

INTRODUCTION 9
(myself included), activism has emerged as a promising arena for taking
the field beyond the traumatic and making it more relevant to the pressing
issues of today’s world. A milestone in this turn to activism was marked by
Anna Reading and Tamar Katriel’s Cultural Memories of Nonviolent Strug-
gles (2015). Although not the first to study the memory of protest, it was
the first to challenge programmatically the preponderance of violence in the
study of memory.14 My book is indebted to Reading and Katriel’s break-
ing of the ice, even as it goes beyond their initial starting point by offering
a more extensive and integrated account of the interplay between memory
and activism (and indeed between violence and nonviolent struggles; see
Chapter 3 below).
In an earlier article (Rigney 2018a), which in retrospect proved to be the
kernel of this book, I proposed the “memory-activism nexus” as a heuristic
framework for studying the entanglements between memory and activism,
which I assume to be a complex and evolving set of relations between dif-
ferent types of memory work.15 Note that I use the term “memory work”
as a reminder that collective memory—in the sense of memory that is
shared—only comes into being thanks to the efforts of multiple actors and
their use of cultural resources. Memory work is the “activity” that occurs
when “human beings are actively involved in the … symbolic transformation
and elaboration of the meanings of the past” (Jelin 2003 [2002]: 5).16 The
memory-activism nexus is the meeting ground between different vectors of
memory work:
1. memory activism. Since my original presentation of the “memory-
activism nexus,” the term “memory activism” has come into wide
circulation with specific reference to campaigns challenging dom-
inant narratives, typically seeking recognition and redress relating
to the legacy of violence and hence remaining within the traumatic
paradigm.17 The immediate goal typically takes the form of public
memorials, apologies, and legal proceedings. In this specific meaning,
memory activism thus relates to “movements about memory” (Daphi
and Zamponi 2019) rather than movements for social transforma-
tion as such.18 Chapter 5 revisits the concept of memory activism and
explores the possible tensions between mnemonic and social change.
2. memory in activism. At stake here are the different ways in which
earlier movements figure in later ones and how this impacts on

10 REMEMBERING HOPE
agenda-setting and strategic choices. Memory can operate implicitly
in the use of the same protest repertoire but also more overtly as
in the marking of anniversaries. The examples given at the begin-
ning of this chapter all showed how the memory of earlier protest
informed later ones, from Extinction Rebellion activists dressed up as
Suffragettes to the Hong Kong marking of the thirtieth anniversary
of the Baltic Way.19 In such cases, activists call upon preexisting and
widely circulating memories.
3. the memory of activism. At stake here are the ways in which activism is
translated into a shareable recollection (which can then be evoked in
later movements). In considering the “memory of activism” our focus
shifts to the media and cultural forms used to capture earlier events
and convey them in a meaningful form to others.20 One can think
here of memoirs, documentaries, and photographs, including iconic
images such as that of the woman facing a phalanx of bayonets with a
chrysanthemum in her hands during an anti-Vietnam war protest in
1967.21
These distinctions are above all heuristic, and ultimately my aim is to show
the entanglement between these vectors of memory work as they play out
over time. After all, memory has to be first constituted before it can be
recalled in later movements. As I was delving into the archive, moreover, it
became apparent that the memory of activism is kept alive by those who have
an affinity with the causes described; by those who have a stake in its being
remembered rather than downplayed or consigned to oblivion. This means
that the remembering of activism is often itself an extension of activism.
Hence, my regular use of the terms “activist memory” (as shorthand for
the memory-activism nexus) and of “activist memory work” (to designate
the work of activists and their supporters in amplifying and prolonging the
impact of protest by ensuring that it is recollected).22
The different vectors of the memory-activism nexus roughly structure the
organization of the chapters that follow here. Memory in activism is cen-
tral in Chapters 1 and 2, the memory of activism in Chapters 3 and 4, and
memory activism in Chapter 5. The final two chapters, Chapters 6 and 7,
bring these strands of analysis together, focusing on activist memory work
and demonstrating some of the ways in which memory work itself becomes
an extension of embodied protest.

INTRODUCTION 11
Cultural Memory Work
My point of access to this dynamic is culture: the stories produced by
participants, observers, and other actors that make activist momentum
transmissible in the form of mediated memory. With some notable excep-
tions, social movement scholars have focused on the role of memory in
activism. In line with disciplinary traditions, they have used interviews with
activists as their primary method supplemented by references to “the media”
in the sense of journalism. Priska Daphi and Jens Zimmermann’s study of
recollections of 1968 in the German Blockupy movement (2021) offers a
case in point, as does Lorenzo Zamponi’s study of the role of memory in Ital-
ian and Spanish student movements (2018). Rooted in a different discipline,
this study takes the cultural carriers of memory rather than human ones as its
main observation point. In doing so it extends the notion of “media” beyond
that of journalism to include a much wider range of meaning-making tools
and representational forms. It is less interested in contemporary forms of
reporting as such than in “secondary” forms of cultural production that rec-
ollect the past from a position later in time, sometimes reworking earlier
reports in the process.
Human actors and their agendas are of course important as drivers behind
cultural production. However, this book is not in the first instance about
people but about publicly accessible versions of the past in the form of
images and texts. In line with the principles of actor-network theory (Latour
2005) it assumes that agency is distributed across humans (activists, journal-
ists, artists, chroniclers, and so on) and nonhuman actants (cultural artifacts
and the languages underlying their production). While it acknowledges the
role of stakeholders in producing, reproducing, and struggling to define
memory, its focus is firmly on the observable outcome their efforts in the
form of cultural artifacts.23 The overall argument is that cultural mediation
explains the contagiousness of hope by helping to produce “the certainty
that something makes sense, regardless of how it turns out,” to recall Havel’s
words.
The term “cultural memory,” introduced by literary scholars in the 1990s
and 2000s, refers in its broadest definition to memory that is mediated.24
That is, to recollections of the past that are shared across space and time
with the help of cultural carriers rather than through face-to-face com-
munication: texts, images, monuments, performances, music, and so on. A

12 REMEMBERING HOPE
central assumption in cultural memory studies is that mediation is needed
for memory to become collective in the strong sense of understandings of the
past that are held in common (Olick 1999). Cultural memory work entails
complex operations, whose salient features can be summed up as follows:
1. Mediation creates connectivity. The concept of a “culture of con-
nectivity” (Van Dijck 2013) was developed to describe digital media
ecologies and how they appeal to individual users while also building
networks. Print and broadcasting operate differently and are less indi-
vidualized in their content, but the same basic principle obtains: that
media help in creating networks of the like-minded and hence provide
a basic condition for the sharing of memory. Specifically, they have the
potential to carry out “connective action” (Bennett & Segerberg 2013)
by bringing into the mnemonic community people who never directly
experienced the events recollected.
2. Mediation is inseparable from remediation. Shared memory is not
produced by any single act of mediation, but by the repetition of nar-
rative content across different media and platforms. The concept of
remediation has been adapted from media theory to highlight the
dynamic, processual, and distributed character of memory produc-
tion.25 Shared frameworks of understanding come into being when
narrative content travels across different media as cultural carriers are
recalled, recycled, and reworked in new material forms that, by cir-
culating along different pathways, reach different publics. In being
remediated, events acquire a cultural afterlife that can far outlast the
moment of their occurrence. Uptake in new contexts is an indicator of
memorability.26
3. Cultural carriers have agency in the sense that they can hold the
attention, mobilize emotions, and articulate agendas. One of the key
assumptions in theories of cultural memory is that mediated recol-
lections themselves offer an experience in the present—of language,
color and sound, kinetic performance, rhythm, tangible shapes, sus-
pense. In this way, “the images, ideas and feelings of past liberation
struggles become freshly available and transmissible in times not of
their making” (Chidgey 2018: 1). Aesthetically constructed artifacts
may end up having a greater appeal and impact than “raw” testimony
on the part of participants or journalists. Extended accounts—for
example audiovisual ones—or visually striking images make the past

INTRODUCTION 13
memorable by turning it into a publicly available source of meaning
and affect that operates in the here and now of watching, listening,
reading.
4. Mediation entails complex and iterative processes of meaning-making.
Cultural memory studies has assumed, following Frederic Bartlett’s
approach to memory (1932), that people make sense of experience by
drawing on a repertoire of schemata, templates, or models.27 At stake
in meaning-making, whatever the term used, is the human capacity to
subsume specifics into more abstract categories.28 This is enabled by
an ever-evolving narrative repertoire, including genres (e.g., memoir
versus historiography), plot models (e.g., tragedy, romance, or com-
edy), and cultural codes (e.g., the choice of red to signify socialism
or passion) as well as storytelling strategies. Chapter 1 and Chapter 3
provide detailed illustrations of the ways in which narrative schemata,
some of which are ancient indeed, act as a resource for making sense
of past protest and ongoing activism.
5. Mediation is informed by an implicit understanding of the overall
nature of history and its directionality (or lack thereof ). This is impor-
tant to note since recollections of protest events, being public in nature,
are also informed by a general understanding of the direction of his-
torical travel, of history with a capital H. Hayden White’s influential
concept of “metahistory” (1973) is useful for describing the narra-
tive schematization of History that underpin stories about particular
events. It has already been noted that the metahistorical “grand nar-
ratives” of irreversible progress toward a goal, predominant since
the nineteenth century, have lost their credibility. As we will see in
Chapters 6 and 7, these metanarratives are being replaced by alterna-
tive models of transformative action linked to what has been called
the politics of prefiguration. This stipulates that the transformation
desired should already be implemented at local level since “the future
is now” as one book title puts it (Monticelli 2022).
In the following chapters, I will be applying these basic insights from
cultural memory studies in my analysis of the memory-activism nexus.
However, this is not business as usual. Linking memory to activism
brings into visibility hitherto unexplored forms of cultural memory work
which challenge some of the assumptions that have governed the field
hitherto.

14 REMEMBERING HOPE
. . . With Activism-Specific Variations
Studies of the dynamics of collective memory work have generally assumed
a teleology that moves in the direction of public recognition, often in the
form of a monument erected with official approval. Indeed, as a recent
handbook shows (Gutman & Wüstenberg 2023), memory activism is often
directed toward such recognition, be this in the form of apologies or monu-
ments indicating a state’s willingness to acknowledge the darker sides of its
own history. Although some successful revolutionaries do end up on stat-
ues (Garibaldi and Lenin are cases in point), they are exceptions. Moreover,
my concern here is with the memory of activists qua activists, the majority
of whom did not accede to positions of power. This means dealing with a
shifting constellation of oppositional groups that are remembered—in sur-
prisingly resilient ways—without the support of state institutions or of the
educational system.
This memory work rarely takes the form of a monument and is often anti-
monumentalist. It gravitates toward portable media such as books, images,
slogans, and videos, and toward performative modes of commemoration in
the form of festivals, lectures, and gatherings in small groups, sometimes
outside the public arena as such. These meetings include singing together,
making archives together, or recalling memorable “speeches from the dock”
such as those of executed Irish nationalist Robert Emmet (1778–1803) and
Turkish revolutionary Deniz Gezmi¸s (1947–1972).29 The following chapters
bring to light distinct traditions and mnemonic practices within activist
communities (the popularity of the commemorative calendar as a textual
genre is a case in point that I discuss in more detail in Chapter 2). These rep-
resent a distinct mode of remembrance that I call antimonumentalist since
it assumes that the past is unfinished business and that the world is still to
be made.
Studying these practices meant tracking down ephemeral publications or
images that were produced outside of regular commercial circuits. Since it
often ended in an error message, the search itself highlighted the vulnerabil-
ity of the traces left by activism and the importance of archiving. Above all it
showed that attempts to control the narrative are an intrinsic part of activism
and key to a movement’s impact both as it is unfolding and as it is later
recollected. Mainstream media mediate between activists and the public at
large and offer crucial visibility to protesters even if this comes at a cost.30
Setting up alternative media and communication circuits—be this in the

INTRODUCTION 15
form of periodicals (Chapter 1) or online resources (Chapter 6) has become
a key part of the struggle for contemporary visibility and future remem-
brance.31 In the words of Indymedia Linksunten, “movements get to leave
traces of their passion for future generations because forgotten struggles are
lost struggles” (in Merrill & Lindgren 2020: 264).
The final variation is theoretically the most important and pertains to nar-
rative form. As we will see throughout this book, the memory of activism
does not fit easily into traditional narrative logic, which stipulates that the
outcome determines the meaning of everything that came before. Since
many transformative struggles have only been partially successful and many
ended in failure, activist memory privileges beginnings rather than endings.
This means recollecting key moments of positive achievement when new
possibilities opened up rather than the “complete” story of what happened
in the long term. As Chapters 3 and 4 in particular show, this sort of retro-
spective sense-making is an act of resistance to the course of history. In the
vicarious form of images and stories, past possibilities are made to re-enter
the present and are given a second chance. This means that activist memory
also challenges the principle underlying traditional theories of narrative that
the meaning of events is determined by their outcome.
All of this suggests that activist memory is a vector of hope: it’s about mak-
ing sense, “regardless of how it turns out,” to invoke Havel once again. This
offers a model of memory work that differs fundamentally from the under-
standing of memory as the return of an intrusive traumatic past. Activist
memory is a form of fighting back. As Judith Butler wrote: “A failed uprising
can become a historically transmitted memory, an unfulfilled promise taken
up by future generations who vow to realize those aims” (2016: 29).
Note here the use of “promise,” indicating both potentiality (“show-
ing promise”) and commitment (“an undertaking to do something”). The
notion of “unfulfilled promise” reframes the relationship between past,
present, and future generations in ethical terms. And indeed, terms like
“obligation,” “debts,” and “commitments” are recurring themes in the mate-
rials we will be dealing with. The idea that people not only remember the
past but also their promises has long been studied in psychology as “prospec-
tive memory” (McDaniel & Einstein 2007). This term has been recently
extended to describe the “collective remembrance of what still needs to be
done, based on past commitments and promises” (Tenenboim-Weinblatt
2013: 4). This book too highlights the importance of prospective mem-
ory, while linking it specifically to the recollection of past moments of

16 REMEMBERING HOPE
promise and the commitments arising from them. I call this retro-prospective
memory: it takes the form of looking back (for example, in celebrating an
anniversary) in order to “remind” people of what was done, what might have
been, and what should be taken as a starting point for thinking about the
future. As the previous paragraph indicated, retro-prospective memory can
also involve recollection in the mode of resistance to the paths history actu-
ally took, thereby “releasing the past from its ‘pastness’ and letting it assume
the vitality of what has always been there” (Azoulay 2019: 350).
In what follows, I give a foretaste of these findings with reference to the
memory of the Paris Commune of 1871, which will be a recurring point of
reference across the different chapters. Since the late nineteenth century, the
promise of the Commune has over and again been recycled by activists and
their supporters in changing contexts. As we will see, however, it is never
remembered in exactly the same way.
The Many Returns of the Paris Commune
The Paris Commune began on March 18, 1871, and collapsed on May
28, of the same year. It was violently repressed by government forces in a
final “Bloody Week” that left thousands dead, thousands deported to New
Caledonia, and thousands more in exile. As its name suggests, the Paris
Commune itself looked back to the revolutionary Commune of 1789–1795
as well as to the communes of medieval cities. It would itself be remembered
in the names of revolutionary events in Cartagena (1873), Mexico (1912),
Shanghai (1927), Hungary (1956), Paris (1968), Chile (1973), and New York
(2011).32 Chapters 1 and 4 will lay out in more detail the shifting frameworks
within which the Commune of 1871 has been remembered. Here I simply
call attention to one recurring theme: the idea that the Commune had been a
“party.” Writing in 1965, for example, the historian Henri Lefebvre described
the events of 1871 as having been in essence a festival: a “spring festival,” a
“magnificent festival . . . to which the people of Paris had treated itself and
the world,” a moment of “joy” (1965: 21).33 This framing of the occasion as
a party did not come out of the blue, but resonated with a tenacious, albeit
marginal, tradition of commemorating the Commune in a festive way.
Figure I.1 shows a poster from 1880 announcing the imminent anniver-
sary of the Commune. The event is to be marked in New York in the form
of a “grand festival” with music and dancing, with the proceeds going to

INTRODUCTION 17
Figure I.1 Commemoration of the Paris Commune, organized by the Société
des Refugiés de la Commune with other groups. Poster. New York: Co-
operative Printers’ Association, 1880. Public domain. Courtesy the
International Institute for Social History, Amsterdam.

18 REMEMBERING HOPE
those deported to New Caledonia and to the Widows and Orphans of Com-
munards. The organizers included the New York branch of the “Society for
Commune refugees.” Less than ten years after Bloody Week, people were
being exhorted to remember the Commune, and offer material support
to former Communards, through the medium of a party. Surprising as it
seems: in the mode of entertainment, pleasure, and conviviality rather than
of grievance or mourning.34 Despite the Commune’s having involved such
terrible bloodshed, it was recalled as something positive. How could an event
which had ended in slaughter nevertheless leave a hopeful memory rather
than a traumatic one?
Note the timing. Those invested in the Commune’s memory chose to
mark its anniversary on March 18. This was the date traditionally associ-
ated with the beginning of the insurgency when the national guard took
control of the city’s defenses. Defining the Commune by its beginnings
rather than its outcome reflected an enduring commitment to its poten-
tial on the part of the organizers. In addition, the emphatically festive mode
chosen for remembering that potential was a way of renewing its pleasur-
able conviviality, thereby letting the past “assume the vitality of what has
always been there,” to recall Azoulay. In taking this approach, the New
York celebration fitted into a pattern that would last well into the twentieth
century.
In the decades after 1871, as several studies have shown, a vibrant com-
memorative culture was initiated by supporters of the Commune.35 Taking
place on or around March 18, these took the form of on-site meetings, with
speeches but also music, eating, communal singing, games, and dancing. In
the immediate aftermath of 1871, when a virtual damnatio memoriae was
still in force in the French public sphere, small-scale local commemora-
tions nevertheless took place in Paris in the form of communal meals that
were themselves performative recollections of similar meals organized dur-
ing the Commune itself (Rebérioux 1997: 536). In the United States, which
was home to many exiled activists from Europe, March 18 saw an “annual
cycle of lavish festivals, complete with oratory, tableaux vivants, music and
dancing” (Coghlan 2016: 82). One such celebration in Chicago in 1884 took
the form of a mass picnic at the lake, shooting competitions, lots of beer,
and music.36 In London too, as we will see in more detail in Chapter 1, the
Commune was celebrated on March 18 on an annual basis until at least
1917, when it became sidelined in socialist circles by the first “successful”
revolution in Russia.

INTRODUCTION 19
On the eve of one such celebration in 1880, the anarchist Peter Kropotkin
anticipated the sense of connectedness it would generate:
Tomorrow night hundreds of thousands of workers’ hearts will beat in
unison, fraternizing across frontiers and oceans, in Europe, in the United
States, in South America, in memory of the rebellion of the Paris proletariat.
(Kropotkin 1971 [1885]: n.p.)
The “beating in unison” of hearts was no mere turn of phrase. Evidence
indicates that physical togetherness and conviviality—even touch—were key
features of these commemorative events.37 Michelle Coghlan (2016) has
coined the term “sensational internationalism” to describe the importance
of sensory experience (as distinct from textual inscription) as a key trans-
mitter of the memory of the Commune and of the hopes that drove it.
It was through embodied performance—often literally through eating and
singing—that the insurgency was not only recalled but also re-enacted in the
mode of pleasure.38
The Commune celebrations re-enacted a hopeful period in 1871 that
had prefigured a better life. In doing so, they in turn prefigured a future
in which that communal pleasure would be extended. As Eleanor Marx
recalled the first anniversary of the Commune, writing, “despite the tragic
horrors of that terrible year [. . .] we were a merry party. In the midst of
so much suffering, immediately after this overwhelming defeat, these men
and women were gay with the gaiety of perfect faith.”39 The annual rit-
ual on March 18 thus provided a framework for re-enacting hope through
conviviality in a way that enabled it to survive and to be transferred to
others.
Crucially for the overall argument of this book, the focus on March 18
represents a counterhistorical mode of recollection. It challenges one of the
central principles of narrative sense-making, namely that the meaning of
events—as success or as failure—is determined by “the sense of an ending”
(Kermode 1967). The result is remembrance in the mode of resistance. This
entails a refusal to link significance to outcomes and thereby allow history to
be written by the victors. A similar idea was already articulated in the four-
teen theses Sur la Commune, signed on March 18, 1962, by the Situationist
International (Debord et al. 1969). While the Situationists conceded that the
Commune had been a massive failure in terms of its practical outcome, they
argued that its significance lay elsewhere:

20 REMEMBERING HOPE
Theoreticians who examine the history of this movement from a divinely
omniscient viewpoint (like that found in classical novels) can easily
demonstrate that the Commune was objectively doomed to failure and
could not have been successfully consummated. They forget that for those
who really lived it, the consummation [le dépassement] was already there
[emphasis in original].40
Isolating particular moments from the flow of history, and hence going
against classical forms of narrative, implies that time can be viewed
as reversible rather than as an irreversible sequence of forward-moving
events.41 Some moments have a value in themselves, irrespective of what
follows, and in being recollected they revive a promise yet to be fulfilled.42
This recalls Walter Benjamin’s belief that when certain historical moments
are considered in isolation from the continuum of events, they acquire
the potential to mark the beginning of an alternative history.43 Following
this logic, memory can be said to reverse time by returning to moments
of possibility when a different future was still possible. In being brought
into the present as memory—and, in the case of re-enactments as embod-
ied experience—they open up that possibility again as an active part of the
present.
In focusing on memory work carried out from an oppositional posi-
tion, this book provides multiple examples of counterhistorical, resistant
remembrance. In covering a variety of cases relating to the period since
1871, it will also demonstrate the growing importance of small stories
taken out of the passage of time as alternatives to the grand narrative of
History.
Scope and Outline
What follows is a series of case studies relating to the period since 1871,
beginning with socialists and anarchists in the 1880s and ending with cli-
mate activists in the 2020s. This historical time-span allows me to show the
cumulative workings of culturally carried memory and the way older forms
are recycled in new contexts and in evolving media ecologies. It also makes
visible changing metahistorical assumptions, much of it anarchist-inflected,
about the directionality of time, the possibility of progress, and the relation-
ship between past, present, and future. A wide range of cultural forms and

INTRODUCTION 21
practices are covered: periodicals, calendars, photographic images, video,
graffiti, performance, and archives, to mention the most salient. Websites
and social media make their appearance in later chapters and undoubtedly
deserve further elaboration in the future. This study will hopefully ensure
that further developments within digital media ecologies, including AI, can
be situated in a longer history.44
Not that I claim to give an exhaustive account of contentious politics since
they became a feature of democratic life at the end of the eighteenth cen-
tury.45 But I have sought to give more historical depth to studies of the
memory-activism nexus in the belief that the past offers a critical resource
for challenging the apparent inevitability of our present condition. The
geographical focus is primarily European with an emphasis on France,
the British Isles, Germany, Spain, and The Netherlands that reflects my
linguistic expertise and geographical location. Where comparison seemed
especially relevant and I could draw on existing literature, I open sightlines
onto extra-European contexts—to the Americas, the Middle East, and Hong
Kong. I am aware that my case studies are no more than a proverbial drop
in the ocean but proffer them in the hope that they can provide models for
analyzing other cases and a conceptual starting point for a more nuanced
discussion of differences.
Chapter 1 provides an account of the role of memory in activism empha-
sizing how visions of the past and of the future emerge in tandem. Using the
concept of “memoryscape,” it shows how socialists and anarchists in Britain
around 1890 both drew on the past and reshaped that past in formulating
their agendas for the future. A close analysis of the Commonweal journal
over the nine years of its existence illustrates this retro-prospective dynamic
by tracking how the shift from a socialist to an anarchist control of the jour-
nal also played out in its recollections of the Commune and the Chicago
Martyrs.
In Chapter 2, I approach the dialectic between memory and futurity by
zooming in on anniversary celebrations in periods of abeyance between
protest waves. Where the first chapter took a periodical as its key cultural
form, this chapter turns to commemorative calendars, whose importance
in left-wing movements goes back at least to the French Revolution. I use
multiple historical and contemporary cases to explain why activist com-
memoration, in being about unfinished business, has a contentious edge
rather than ritual character and hence periodically spills over into new
mobilizations.

22 REMEMBERING HOPE
The following two chapters turn to the cultural mediation of protest
events and look at the schemata used to turn them into memorable stories.
Chapter 3 starts from the observation that violence against demonstrators
has proved highly memorable, especially events that can be schematized as
a “Bloody Sunday” or as “martyrdom.” I use a range of historical examples,
including the civil rights movements of the 1960s, to show how deeply-
rooted schemata make it possible to turn state violence against itself and
expose its injustice. The mediated recollection of state violence, especially
when condensed into dramatic images, reasserts the patent value of the
lives lost in defiance of their erasure. Chapter 4 turns to the long history
ofCommune remediations, especially in film, in orderto showhowastory’s
aesthetic qualities—sound, shape, movement, suspense—help in overwrit-
ing the reality of the uprising’s violent defeat and in reviving earlier hopes.
Connecting both chapters is the idea of resistant remembrance: in the form
of images, video, texts, or performances, stories offer a pushback to events
and a reminder of the alternative pathways that history might have taken.
Chapter 5 examines the interplay between memory activism and
antiracism in the iconoclastic wave that followed the killing of George Floyd
in May 2020, with specific reference to the toppling of the statue of slave-
trader Edward Colston in Bristol, UK. This case is used to reflect critically
on monumentalism, monuments, and the significance of removing them as
part of larger campaigns. It explains why the toppling of a monument can
be a catalyst for social change but also that monuments themselves, in the
name of petrified “heritage,” can then be instrumentalized to block reform
of the status quo.
Chapter 6 shows how activists shape their future memory by setting up
“autonomous” archives sedimenting the movement into records and hence
giving it a potential afterlife. In particular, I focus on the extensive archiv-
ing activities related to the occupation of squares in Egypt, Spain, New York,
Turkey, and France in the period since 2011. Collectively built and based
on the principle of the Commons, these activist-led archives provide an
instance of radical democracy in practice. They thus translate the basic prin-
ciple of prefigurative politics into the very making of memory; namely, the
idea that “the future is now” and that present action should be in keeping
with the ideals for which one is striving.
Finally, Chapter 7 turns to the role of memory in the climate movement,
where the future at times seems such an overwhelming presence that the past
appears irrelevant. This chapter shows that climate activists nevertheless

INTRODUCTION 23
draw intensively on historical references, both to the distant and the more
recent past, in deciding tactics and formulating agendas. Moreover, images
and documentaries sedimenting protest actions serve as forms of resistant
remembrance in face of ongoing challenges. As I show with specific reference
to the occupation of forest areas often ending in violent clearance, documen-
taries capture moments of hope and the rationale behind protest in sensual
imagery that slows down time. Such moments too prefigure alternative ways
of living. Where Chapter 1 began with grand narratives of revolutionary
socialism, Chapter 7 ends with small stories of small wins, repeated over
and again. What they share is the fact of cultural memory work serving as a
practice of possibilities and hence as a “resource for hope.”

Chapter 1
Memory in Activism
The Commonweal, 1885–1894
Marx on the Spirits of the Past
As he looked back on the revolution of 1848, Karl Marx noted scornfully that
its protagonists were too backward-looking to change the world. They set
out to “create something that did not exist before” but had ended up merely
imitating their predecessors: “anxiously conjur[ing] up the spirits of the past
to their service, borrowing from them names, battle slogans, and costumes
in order to present this new scene in world history in time-honored disguise
and borrowed language” (1999 [1852]).1 In Marx’s reading, memory disables
imagination by bringing the weight of history to bear on the present “like a
nightmare on the brains of the living.” This chapter shows the opposite: that
memory is not a pregiven legacy which determines the pathways for later
action. While historical events are irreversible, memory is both a malleable
and a renewable resource for thinking about the present and imagining the
future. Indeed, images of the past and of the future emerge in tandem and
in a mutually illuminating way.
Writing a half century ago, the historian Reinhart Koselleck already
observed a continuous feedback loop between imagined futures and
remembered pasts or, as he put it, between our “horizon of expectation”
and our “space of experience” (1985 [1979]). Since change is a feature of
modern life, experience and expectation are structurally out of step and
hence subject to mutual revision. The “space of experience” thus provides an
imperfect template for the future. Recollections of the past are nevertheless
an indispensable resource for proximate imaginings of what is possible and
desirable. Although Koselleck’s vocabulary was not that of memory stud-
ies, his idea of a dialectic between memory and futural-orientation offers
a promising alternative to Marx’s view of the past as a disabling weight on
present action. As Koselleck put it: “Hope and memory, or expressed more
generally, expectation and experience… simultaneously constitute history
Remembering Hope. Ann Rigney, Oxford University Press. © Oxford University Press (2025).
DOI: 10.1093/oso/9780197789711.003.0002

MEMORY IN ACTIVISM 25
and its cognition” (1985 [1979]: 257–258). The present chapter takes this
dialectic between past and future as its point of departure for the study of
memory in activism.
As mentioned in the Introduction, a considerable literature has appeared
in the last years on the role of memory in social movements.2 At stake is
the question how memories and legacies inform, inspire, and sometimes
impede collective action.3 The distinction between “implicit” and “explicit”
forms of memory in activism (Zamponi 2013) provides a useful starting
point for summarizing the main threads of this discussion. Explicit mem-
ory entails overt references to figures and events in the past while implicit
memory relates to inherited ways of thinking and doing. The historical use
of barricades illustrates how the implicit and explicit can work together. Bar-
ricades once had a tactical function in blocking access to narrow streets,
which was reflected in their being repeatedly used as part of the “reper-
toire of contention” (Tilly 1995b) and hence expressing the workings of
implicit memory. However, they continued to be erected—for example, in
May 1968—even after modern cityscapes and police equipment had made
them ineffective. Although misplaced from a practical point of view, they
had acquired “a cultural meaning” by the mid-nineteenth century mak-
ing them “all but synonymous with the European revolutionary tradition”
(Traugott 2010: xi), which meant that setting one up became an explicit
tribute to predecessors.
Although the repertoire as a carrier of memory does feature in this
chapter, its focus is above all on explicit memory: intentional and often self-
reflexive tributes to predecessors. As various studies have shown, activists
create a common identity both by reference to a shared cause and by ref-
erence to earlier movements that are objects of admiration or, conversely,
models to be avoided. It turns out that activists, instead of being weighed
down by predecessors as Marx assumed, regularly pick and choose their
own ancestors. As Priska Daphi (2017) shows with reference to the Global
Justice movement and Lorenzo Zamponi (2018) to student movements in
Italy and Spain since the 1970s, activists select ancestors in order to position
themselves within the political field.4 In this way, according to Zamponi,
memory is “one of the cultural factors that structures the environment in
which collective action happens” (2018: 297).
I explore this insight further while also using a more interactive model
which supposes, not only that memory structures the environment but also
that the activist environment structures memory. This is a two-way dynamic.

26 REMEMBERING HOPE
Where others have assumed that activists pick and choose from memories
that are pregiven, this chapter argues instead that the past changes in relation
to one’s position in the present and, conversely, that it offers a continuously
renewable resource for articulating the direction of travel. The key ques-
tion then is not “how a pre-existing shared memory may be deployed” by
activists, but “how a memory comes to be” (Chidgey 2018: 50).5
The concept of “memoryscape” can help in describing such a shifting con-
stellation or relief map of historical references and the practices that produce
it.6 In using the term in this way, I distance myself from recent applications of
“memoryscape” that limit its meaning to the distribution of monuments and
other material sites of memory across a cityscape or landscape. This is too
limited a definition when discussing oppositional groups whose memory is
often not inscribed in public space. For this reason, the term “memoryscape”
is defined here in a non-geographical sense: to designate a heterogeneous
constellation of virtual orientation points as these emerge through differ-
ent forms of cultural memory work. Those reference points are virtual “sites
of memory” (Nora 1997 [1984–1992]) rather than literal places, and their
geographical reach may extend well beyond the territory where they are
invoked. Their number and relative salience shifts as new events occur that
change their meaning retrospectively or render other events meaningful for
the first time. How then do memoryscapes shapeshift? And how does this
reflect position-taking and agenda-setting in the present?
In what follows, I address these questions by examining the memoryscape
of British socialists in late nineteenth-century Britain. An activist-run peri-
odical rather than a movement as such is taken as my point of observation.
This is in line with the assumption that activist environments are mediated
through and through; in line too with this book’s overall concern with cul-
tural mediation. The Commonweal (1885–1994) provides a fascinating point
of departure for examining in vitro the shape-shifting of an activist memo-
ryscape as socialists and anarchists struggled over its nine-year life span for
control of the journal and for political influence in radical circles.
A Radical Hub: Between Socialism and Anarchism
In the late nineteenth century and well into the twentieth, journals were
the foremost exemplars of alternative media.7 As sites of radical thinking
and organizing, they were an integral part of the political process itself. By

MEMORY IN ACTIVISM 27
appealing to individual readers while also creating networks between them,
they were key agents of “connective action” (Bennett & Segerberg 2013).
They were also historically important as laboratories for new ideas and new
vocabularies in which to express them. As we will see here, they were also
important calibrators of the relationship between past, present, and future.
The Commonweal operated with a by-now outdated activist repertoire and
used modes of expression that are quaintly characteristic of a highly liter-
ate and now defunct print-based world. However, it also displays surprising
continuities with contemporary forms of digitally based networked activism
and thus serves as a reminder that all studies of memory in activism need to
be sensitive to historically specific media ecologies.
The Commonweal: Journal of the Socialist League began appearing
in February 1885 with a sale price of a single penny. William Morris
(1834–1896), the founding editor, remained in position until May 1890,
when he resigned in protest at the direction the Socialist League and its
journal were taking.8 David Nicoll (1859–1919), Morris’s successor, was
imprisoned for eighteen months of his tenure as editor because of his sup-
port for the Walsall anarchists and, although others took over his tasks, his
absence meant a temporary disruption between September 1892 and April
1893. With the exception of these months, the journal persisted until forced
to close in 1894 by lack of funds and, linked to this, falling numbers of
subscribers. Its flourishing or decline thus mirrored the support it could
mobilize. It began as a monthly, became a weekly as of May 1985, and ended
up as a fortnightly in its final year. This study is based on a reading of all
issues of The Commonweal, collated from various repositories.9
The journal operated in a media environment already crowded with peri-
odicals linked to political organizations; some of them were long-lasting,
others ephemeral, most of them operating in precarious conditions. The
Commonweal stands out, not so much for the size of its support base (at most
around 500) but because it managed to provide a meeting ground for multi-
ple strands of political thought and for the groups who, in advocating them,
also vied with each other for control of the journal’s direction and for public
support. Partly due to the involvement of high-profile figures such as Morris,
Eleanor Marx Aveling, and Peter Kropotkin, the journal became a significant
node within transnational radical networks with links to a range of politi-
cal actors including anti-parliamentarian socialists, anti-government anar-
chists, anti-imperialists, Irish nationalists, pro-parliamentarian socialists,
Fabians, and so on.10 The journal ultimately succumbed to these differences

28 REMEMBERING HOPE
and, by taking a definitively anarchist turn, lost its support base among
self-identifying socialists. This very ephemerality and mutability make
it especially promising for a study of the dynamics of activist memory
work on a day-to-day basis in the quiet time of long-term organizing,
campaigning, and consciousness-raising rather than in the crisis time of
revolution.
The journal had a practical value as a virtual meeting place and channel
of communication in a print-based world. Subscribing to a journal was not
merely a financial transaction but also a way of demonstrating one’s “sub-
scription” to the values it upheld (there was a continuous struggle to attract
new subscribers and regular pleas for old subscribers to pay up).11 Sub-
scribers to the Commonweal, who received their issues by post, were often
also members of one of the local branches of the Socialist League. Single
issues were distributed at public meetings and demonstrations, as part of
what was called “open air propaganda.” This recurring phrase meant out-
door meetings (“weather permitting,” as befits events in London) designed
to “propagate” the cause by attracting new supporters and, feeding back into
the system, new subscribers.12 Using the analog resources of the time, the
journal was an apparatus for networking, connecting people and causes so as
to create both “imagined” and “embodied” communities of the like-minded
who met virtually through the pages of the journal as well as physically in
the open air and in meetings rooms.13 Fundraising appeals on behalf of indi-
vidual comrades in London who had fallen on hard times figured alongside
reports from local branches of the Socialist League across Great Britain and
Ireland, and from kindred spirits across Europe, the United States, and the
colonies.14 A list of “foreign journals received,” a fixed rubric up to 1890,
indicates that the Commonweal was part an international exchange system
of related publications.15
In short, the Commonweal was not just a conduit for information but
a dynamic assemblage where people and texts interacted. As such, it
demanded continuous adaptation and improvisation to survive and increase
its influence. Each issue marked a moment suspended in “periodic time”
(Turner 2002) between the “last” and the “next” one, an interval filled with
writing and correspondence, but also with meetings and other activities that
fed back into the journal. And all of this demanded a labor of love, a huge
intellectual and physical effort that was itself an energizing and connective
force for those involved. To see one’s periodical flourish was in effect to see
the movement with which one identified grow in strength and enthusiasm.16

MEMORY IN ACTIVISM 29
As long as it kept going, the Commonweal provided, in conjunction with
on-site assemblies, a periodically repeated display of the worthiness, unity
of purpose, numbers and commitment (WUNC) famously considered by
Charles Tilly (1995a) to be markers of a successful movement.
Not surprisingly, reports of branch meetings and public meetings, espe-
cially in the first years, tended to emphasize the enthusiasm of those present,
presumably to attract new adherents. This intense labor of organizing and
writing was an integral part of “capacity building” (Tufekci 2017) as the
movement worked toward the long-term goals of changing society. The
effort put into working together strengthened the bonds between those mak-
ing common cause around evolving ideas of, as the successive subtitles of the
journal put it, “socialism,” “revolutionary socialism,” “communism,” “anar-
chist communism,” and “anarchism.” The sheer variety of competing terms
shows that improvisation was at the very heart of the movement as members
responded to unfolding events and formed new alliances, organizations, and
journals with different lead terms.
In the course of this short yet eventful life, the journal went through sev-
eral identity shifts (Figures 1.1–1.3). If it began as the “Journal of the Socialist
League” (under Morris), it became the “Revolutionary Journal of Anarchist
Communism” (under Nicoll), a subtitle it then lost when Nicoll was in
prison and regained when he took over again. After the demise of the Com-
monweal as such, Nicoll would go on to edit a short-lived journal called the
Anarchist (1894–1897). This marked the definitive overwriting of a socialist
frame by an anarchist one, the latter defined as “a philosophy and political
practice that rejects centralized, hierarchical authority…and works to create
egalitarian relations in which individuals cultivate their freedom while orga-
nizing themselves into voluntary, self- governing communities” (Ferguson
2023: 2).
Figure 1.1 Masthead of the Commonweal, June 1886. Public domain.

30 REMEMBERING HOPE
Figure 1.2 Masthead of the Commonweal, March 1891. Public domain.
Figure 1.3 Masthead of the Commonweal, May 1891. Public domain.
The ideological differences expressed in its masthead also reflected the
journal’s position within a shifting constellation of political organizations.
If the Commonweal functioned for the first half of its life as the flagship
publication of the Socialist League (itself a break-off group from the Social
Democratic Foundation), it gradually lost its connection to organized social-
ism under Nicoll and became more closely aligned with anarchism. This
complex story provides a glimpse into the fissuring, regroupings, and power
struggles going on in radical circles at this time. Above all it reflects the
importance of the journal in offering a platform on which such differences
were played out. Even as the title remained the same, the socialist princi-
ples on which it was founded were slowly undermined until, like a snake
shedding its skin, it re-emerged as an anarchist journal with a different
constituency.

MEMORY IN ACTIVISM 31
The transformation of the journal is reflected in its lexicon. Changing the
language in which the world is described and agendas are expressed is a key
element in contention, as recent studies have shown.17 Lexical change is evi-
dent in the Commonweal in the relative frequency of the terms “socialism”
(and its variants) and “anarchism” (and its variants) during the life span of
the journal. Where the socialist “protest lexicon” (Van den Elzen 2024) dom-
inated in the initial years, this gave way to an anarchist one. Figure 1.4 maps
the relative frequency of the terms “anarchism” and “socialism,” in their vari-
ous adjectival and nominal forms, per issue of the Commonweal, 1885–1893
(due account has been taken of the reduced word count in the later issues).18
It shows an unmistakable turning point in 1890.
This lexical shift is the most obvious marker of a transformation in the
frame governing the journal and providing its coherence. “Frame” is under-
stood here as a coherent cluster of interpretive schemata brought into play in
making sense of events. In the cases we will encounter, these sense-making
schemata range from words or lexical clusters, used to identify events, to
narrative models, used to articulate the relations between them. The con-
cept of frame has been developed by social movement theorists to explain
Figure 1.4 The number of mentions of the terms “anarchism” and “socialism”
in their various adjectival and nominal forms per issue of the Commonweal,
1885–1894. Graph: Max Boiten using Quanteda.io.

32 REMEMBERING HOPE
how campaigns, in making sense across multiple actions and discourses,
succeed in mobilizing people (Snow et al. 1986; Snow et al. 2019). My con-
cern is with meaning-making as such rather than with mobilization, but here
too, frame theory is useful in explaining how the “resonance” (Armstrong &
Crage 2006) between different sites of meaning-making enhances the coher-
ence of a political program and its “capacity to mean effectively” (Polletta &
Chen 2012: 488).
As will become clear later on, the shift in dominant word clusters in the
Commonweal was part of a larger “frame transformation” (Snow et al. 2019:
404) affecting not only the journal’s political agenda but also its memo-
ryscape. Indeed, the new political agenda resonated with the memoryscape
in a mutually reinforcing way that enhanced the movement’s coherence at a
crucial point in its evolution.
Memory Work Through Print
The Commonweal operated primarily through the medium of text: report-
ing, announcing, educating, mobilizing, and networking. Like other jour-
nals, it was “heteroglossic” in Bakhtin’s sense of providing a platform for
different voices and genres.19 These resonated with the journal’s underly-
ing ambition to replace capitalism by “liberty, equality, and fraternity” (as
expressed in the “Statement of the Principles of International Revolutionary
Socialism” that closed many issues from the end of 1889 onward).20 Within
that loose framework there was room for differing opinions, particularly as
regards the means by which “revolutionary” socialism could be achieved;
and over time, these beliefs and, ultimately, the frame itself evolved.
The journal included a remarkable variety of genres and textual formats
each with its own affordances for structuring information and appealing to
the reader.21 There were discursive essays (e.g., on the teachings of Marx);
forensic reports on working conditions at particular locations; serialized
