fiction (most notably Morris’s utopian novel News from Nowhere [1890]);
poems (lots of them); announcements and reports relating to the activi-
ties of the League, including family events; advertisements for publications;
occasional small ads. The diversity of the strategies and modes of address
anticipated Gandhi’s journal Indian Opinion (1904–1915) which, as Isobel
Hofmeyr has shown (2013), used a multiplicity of genres, including poetry,
in order to create new political subjects by not only informing readers but

MEMORY IN ACTIVISM 33
also training their sensibilities through a combination of information, expla-
nation, persuasion, exhortation, and aesthetic pleasure. (This makes all the
more notable the loss of generic diversity in the final year of the Common-
weal’s existence when the newspaper became reduced in size and arguably
more doctrinaire.)
Memory was a key component of this assemblage. Advertisements for his-
torically themed books and pamphlets, historical narratives, metahistorical
reflection, poetry and song, and the distinctive “Revolutionary Calendar”
that appeared in 1888 and 1889—all these interacted to produce the move-
ment’s memoryscape. Crucially, historically oriented articles or poems alter-
nated with ones reporting on current affairs so that past, present, and future
were in continuous dialogue. Moreover, the journal’s textual memory work
operated in tandem with embodied commemorations at different locations.
These were announced in advance and subsequently reported so as to pro-
long their effect while also affording a form of virtual participation to those
who could not attend in person.
In what follows, I briefly outline this textual memory work before zooming
in on reports of related commemorations performed on-site.
Historical Narratives and Metahistorical Frames
Throughout its existence, the Commonweal published serial historical sur-
veys that ran over weeks and even months, often as “tie-ins” to public
lectures. The history of socialism, the medieval communes, the Luddites,
early communism, the Glorious Revolution, the revolt of Ghent, and the
French Revolution (in a series timed to coincide with the 1889 centenary)
were among the topics to receive extensive attention.22 These general surveys
alternated with shorter narratives on more granular topics (e.g., the Mitchel-
stown massacre of demonstrators in 1887, and the execution of the Russian
revolutionary martyr Sophia Perovskaya in 1881).23 Essays like these pop-
ulated the memoryscape with multiple memory sites relating to the history
of labor and political life.
Narratives on particular periods or topics, resonating with each other,
helped situate the cause of revolutionary socialism in a longer tradition of
repeated struggle and inspirational examples. They alternated, moreover,
with large-scale metahistories scaffolding them with a broader understand-
ing of capital-letter History. In the Commonweal, essays with titles like
“Revolutionary Rumblings,” “The Coming Change,” “Signs of Change,” “Is

34 REMEMBERING HOPE
the Revolution Coming?,” and “After the Revolution” attempted to pre-
dict the future transformation of society based on a progressive belief in
the inevitability and desirability of a socialist revolution.24 Reflecting the
major ideological fault lines of the period, there were heated debates about
whether the system would collapse from its inner contradictions or through
revolutionary action, and whether radicals could justify participating in par-
liamentary processes. Irreconcilable differences led first to the defection of
those (like Eleanor Marx) who believed in the value of parliamentary action,
and later to the defection of those (like Morris) who believed that “propa-
ganda of the word” was the best way to achieve extra-parliamentary change
and found themselves increasingly at odds with their anarchist colleagues
who supported “propaganda of the deed” instead.
Whatever the differences in these metahistories, they were all responses
to the fundamental challenge facing all revolutionary thinking: namely, how
to imagine a successful revolution while drawing on the experience of the
past, given the ubiquity of defeat (at least in the short term)? After all the
need for concerted action presumes the failure of predecessors to achieve
definitive success. Unlike the “left-wing melancholia” (Traverso 2016) of
post-1989 socialists, writers in the Commonweal looked confidently to the
future. While recognizing themselves as heirs to past failures, they also saw
themselves (especially in the earlier years of the journal’s existence) as being
much better equipped, thanks in part to Marx’s guidance, to do the right
thing next time round. Contradictions within the present, along with their
own efforts at consciousness-raising, made them confident that the next rev-
olution would be the “real” one. Confidence in the future also marked the
anarchism-inflected later years of the journal. However, where the social-
ists saw the revolution-to-come as the culmination of a historical series, the
anarchists were impatient to “break with” the past while ironically making
this point through memory work.
Poetry and Affective Recycling
Poems were also a major carrier of memory in the Commonweal. The ubiq-
uity of poetry reflects its historical importance as a cultural form in the
nineteenth century, which has no contemporary equivalent (except, per-
haps, in protest music). It worked less to inform readers than to mobilize
sentiment. In promoting poetry and song (the border between them is fluid),
the journal followed in the footsteps of the British Chartists, for whom

MEMORY IN ACTIVISM 35
poetry had represented “a distinctive form of agency and unique form of
historical knowledge” (Sanders 2009: 3). Throughout the nine years of its
existence the Commonweal thus featured original poems on the contempo-
rary world, both by seasoned authors and by workers (the editors received
hundreds of submissions from aspiring poets).25 The quality ranged from
the more literary to the doggerel, with many poems designed to be sung.
The issue of June 30, 1888, for example, featured a come-all-you poem called
“When the Revolution Comes!” to be sung to the tune of “Come, Land-
lord, Fill the Flowing Bowl.” Its rousing chorus ran: “When the Revolution
comes, / When the Revolution comes, / It’s coming fast—our turn, at last!”
As long as Morris was editor, every issue figured at least one poem as well
as many recycled quotations from historical writers, used as fillers between
columns.26
In addition to recent compositions, the journal also called on the cultural
archive by recycling writings and poems associated with earlier moments.
Many were translations, a fact that underscores the Commonweal’s inter-
nationalism and the transnational character of the mediated memory in
circulation. The rationale for publishing often lay in the political creden-
tials of the authors and their association with Chartism (Ernest Jones), the
generation of 1848 (Heinrich Heine, Ferdinand Freiligrath, Sánder Peto¨fi),
and the generation of the Commune (Louise Michel, Eugène Pottier).27 The
issue of May 15, 1886, for example, featured an elegy by Freiligrath on the
life and martyrdom of Robert Blum, a German shot by Austrian police dur-
ing demonstrations in 1848. Written close to the events, it is both lament and
rallying call:
They gave him rest and peace at last; he lies in peaceful raiment dressed:
Then sing in requiem around his grave, an anthem of eternal rest;
Yea, rest for him who has bequeathed unrest to us for evermore;
For in the dim cathedral aisles, where moving masses thronged the door,
Methought through all the noise I heard a sound as of a whisper strange:
“The passing moment is not all; the organs shall to trumpets change!”28
Across multiple issues, readers would have encountered legacy poems
devoted to key episodes in the struggle for workers’ rights (e.g., Georg
Werth’s poem “The Silesian Weavers”), socialist adaptations or rewritings of
well-known songs (e.g., “On the Starving Poor of Old England” to be sung to
the tune of “Union Jack”), and the lyrics of well-known revolutionary songs
(The Marseillaise, the Carmagnole) for singing at political gatherings. In its

36 REMEMBERING HOPE
recycling practices, the printed journal was a feeder for embodied encoun-
ters since there were frequent reports of collective singing in meeting halls
and, on at least one occasion, at a railway station when bidding farewell to a
departing comrade.29
A closer analysis of this huge corpus could show how poems in the Com-
monweal combine the pugnacious with the sentimental. But this general
sketch already indicates how the recycling of poetry added a distinct ele-
ment to the memoryscape. Although some poems like the one about Robert
Blum quoted above did have a narrative dimension, legacy poetry served
primarily to revive the enthusiasm and commitment of earlier generations—
be this in the pleasurable and affect-inducing form of reading on-page or in
the re-enactment of that enthusiasm through collective singing on-site. Lyric
poetry thus enabled what Joep Leerssen (2023), writing on national move-
ments, has called “heart to heart” communication between contemporaries
and across generations. The reprinting of poems by Chartists, the genera-
tion of 1848, and the Communards resonated in turn with the presence of
these movements in the historical essays and book advertisements and with
reports of ongoing campaigns and commentaries on current events.
Many of these poems, songs, and historical essays related to the Com-
mune, a focus that resonated with the parallel prominence of that event in
on-site commemorative memory work. Since the latter was also reported in
the journal, a feedback loop between textual and performative memory work
ensued.
Memory Work Through Commemoration
Anniversaries were marked in the journal in the form of commemora-
tive articles, announcements of on-site celebrations, and reports of those
celebrations in subsequent issues. Chapter 2 discusses the significance of
commemorative calendars in more detail, including the Commonweal’s
weekly “Revolutionary Calendar.” Here I focus on the resonance between
the textual forms of memory work outlined above and the embodied prac-
tices of commemoration taking place at regular intervals in keeping with a
tradition already well established in Chartism.30
Commemorative celebrations were announced in the journal along with
convocations to regular branch meetings of the Socialist League and ad
hoc demonstrations advocating for particular causes. With much cheering,

MEMORY IN ACTIVISM 37
speechifying, and singing, the anniversaries gave a rhythm to memory while
crucially also providing a reason for people to assemble. Even when violence
was being recalled, commemorations celebrated heroic achievements (the
poem about Robert Blum is a case in point). In this regard, socialist mem-
ory culture was of a piece with dominant trends in the pre-trauma age before
World War I.
During the lifetime of the Commonweal, the Paris Commune of 1871 and
the trial and execution of the Chicago Martyrs in 1887 were high points
in the commemorative calendar, in March and November, respectively.
Both events were in the transition zone between “communicative memory”
(based on lived experience) and “cultural memory” (reliant on mediated
accounts) in that veterans attended the meetings along with people whose
memory of events was second-hand.31 While celebrating the Commune
had become something of an international tradition by the late 1880s, the
memory of the Chicago Martyrs was still relatively “raw.” But it too rapidly
evolved into a memory to be celebrated in a way that complemented, and
also competed with, the memory of 1871. The evolving commemoration of
these two events illustrates the fact that activist memoryscapes are not fixed
once and for all, but “vitalities within a field of decisions, sensations and
orchestrations” (Chidgey 2018: 42).
Commemorations, usually taking place in meeting halls rather than out-
doors, relied on the cultural codes of the late nineteenth century and the then
current protocols for conducting public meetings. These included the pass-
ing of “resolutions” which were first “moved” and then “seconded” by the
main speakers, before submission to the assembled company who, in “pass-
ing” the resolution collectively expressed their understanding of the event
and why it should be remembered in that particular way. Resolutions are his-
torically specific instances of the performative use of language—in Austin’s
sense of utterances that “do” things in the world (1962) and do not just
describe it. Resolutions were printed in the Commonweal, often in advance,
and were used to praise past achievements, denounce past and present injus-
tice, and flag the way forward. They usually passed by acclamation. Those
present were thus invited to recall events in the mode of celebration, but
also to take an activist stance in the present, with recollection and activism
overtly combined and sometimes yoked together. Communication on these
occasions served a “phatic” function (Jakobson 1960), meaning that their
primary task was to confirm the connection between those present, while
also linking them to predecessors. At the same time, the meetings were used

38 REMEMBERING HOPE
to steer the movement in a new direction—literally, by “moving” a different
memory.
The Commune and the Chicago Martyrs operated as central memory
sites. As defined by Pierre Nora (1997 [1984–1992]), a memory site is an
event or figure that by virtue of being recalled over and again in differ-
ent media, becomes a common point of reference for particular groups
and a normative benchmark for future action. While Nora’s original model
emphasized how memory sites create convergence and hence provide the
basis for publicly shared memory, subsequent studies have shown that the
meaning of sites is by no means unitary or stable.32 On the contrary, by
virtue of being shared points of reference, they offer a ready-made platform
for negotiating different interpretations of the past and hence for initiating
divergence. As the following cases show, memory sites are crucial in allowing
a memoryscape to be transformed from within.
Narrativizing the Commune
In explaining the dynamics of this transformation, it helps to return to
the principle, briefly discussed in the Introduction, that people make sense
of experience by drawing on a cultural repertoire of schemata, templates,
or models. Since the 1970s there is a consensus that events do not them-
selves take the form of a story but become imbued with narrativity—that is,
“narrativized”—when they are represented in terms of a beginning, middle,
and end.33 What exactly this entails has been the topic of intense debate.
According to Hayden White, one of the pioneers of this discussion, the
essence of narrativity is in the unfolding of a conflict between “desire” and
the “law”: between what a subject wants and what is possible in the cir-
cumstances (1987 [1980]). At stake in storytelling is how humans attempt
to achieve goals in circumstances not of their own making.
The “actantial model” of narrative developed by Algirdas Greimas (1966)
can help in turning this very minimalist definition into a tool of analysis.
In its most simplified form, the actantial model sees storytelling in terms
of the distribution of a fixed number of roles among “actants” (both human
actors and societal or natural forces). It posits that every story is built around
six actantial roles—a subject wanting to realize a goal encounters opponents
as well as allies; the operation of power and its beneficiary. In the case of a
successful quest or struggle the subject and beneficiary become the same,

MEMORY IN ACTIVISM 39
otherwise not. The advantage of this narratological model over others is its
simplicity: it makes it possible to compare multiple configurations of the
same historical events in terms of their allocation of a limited number of
underlying roles.34 Moreover, it can also be applied to the way contemporary
actors narrativize their own actions and intentions, by positioning them-
selves as subjects in relation to particular goals, allies/ opponents, and power
structures.35
With this model in mind, how did the narrativization of the Commune
evolve over the nine years of the journal’s history? And how did this reflect
the changing metahistorical assumptions of those doing the commemorat-
ing? A comparison between the annual resolutions in commemorations of
the Commune provides a point of observation on this process as it played
out during the lifetime of the journal. In applying the actantial model to res-
olutions formulated by nineteenth-century actors, I am not suggesting that
they themselves thought directly in such terms, but that their depictions of
the Commune make sense when analyzed through this heuristic lens. In
choosing their words to recollect the Commune, as we will see, celebrants
made sense of that event by redistributing the role of subject, goal, allies,
opponents, power, and the beneficiary of that power across different actors
and forces. The result was a changing narrative about 1871, but also a dif-
ferent positioning of the celebrants in relation to their own goals and future
actions.
The Commune was commemorated in London on or around March
18, at various meetings co-organized by the Socialist League. In the inter-
vals between these festive events, as mentioned earlier, the Commonweal
regularly reminded its readers of the Commune through examples of Com-
munard poetry and through obituaries for former Communards which, as
the generation died off, gave a long tail to 1871. Every spring, the journal
announced the upcoming celebrations and later reported on the celebra-
tions themselves, while also including other Commune-themed elements
such as essays on “Why We Celebrate the Commune” or advertisements for
relevant publications and copies of W. S. Crane’s iconic poster, presumably
for display in homes and meeting halls.36 In this way, attention was annually
directed to the Commune along multiple channels and over a period of
several weeks.
As was common at the time, those celebrating the Commune across the
country and abroad exchanged telegrams so as to mutually reinforce a sense
of connectedness and of a common memory.37 The telegrams were in turn

40 REMEMBERING HOPE
remediated in the Commonweal. As reflected in subsequent reports, the
celebrations had a strong transnational dimension in terms both of the par-
ticipation of many political exiles and the range of international contacts,
leading one commentator to note that the 1888 celebrations perpetuated the
Commune’s internationalism and that “these international greetings show
that the spirit that removed the Vendôme Column animates the Socialists of
to-day more than ever”(CW 3/24/88).
As a salient memory site, the Commune provided a common reference
point for the many radical groups jostling for support and advantage in Lon-
don in the 1870s and 1880s.38 In the period covered by the Commonweal,
the March celebrations brought together, sometimes on the same platform,
groups such as the Socialist Democratic League, the Socialist League, and
the Fabians, allowing them to temporarily bridge their differences around a
shared affiliation with the Communards. Between the lines of the reporting,
however, there was an ongoing discussion about who should commemorate
the event, in whose company, and in what terms.
As indicated in the Introduction, the anniversary coincided with the
proclamation of the Commune on March 18 and the “collective effer-
vescence” (Durkheim 2001 [1912]) of its beginnings. Along with singing,
speech-making and the passing of resolutions were key features of the Lon-
don events. A closer look at these resolutions shows that the celebrations,
while promoting kinship among the like-minded, also provided occasions
for attempts to redefine their common goal, their opponents and their allies,
and the nature of the power they were up against. Since the Commune failed
to stay in power it was clearly not the ultimate beneficiary of the uprising, but
those celebrating its memory nevertheless saw themselves in various ways
as the beneficiaries of its efforts and empowered by its example. Table 1.1
below offers a schematization of the annual recollections of the Commune
indicating the shifting distribution of roles. Like a word ladder, it shows how
a socialist framing of the events of 1871 (emphasizing labor rights and sol-
idarity) gradually gives way to a memory site that is better aligned with an
anarchist frame (emphasizing freedom from government).
In 1885, the first year of the journal’s existence, the following resolution
was moved:
That this meeting of Revolutionary Socialists assembled to commemo-
rate the Paris Commune of 1871 desires to pay a tribute of respect and
admiration to the heroic men and women who, under terribly adverse

MEMORY IN ACTIVISM 41
circumstances, then showed the world that the workers can organize soci-
ety on a purely communistic basis; that it protests against the infamous
representation of the Commune and its members and supporters by the
reactionary press from 1871 to the present time and that it looks for-
ward eagerly and with all its power to realize to the full these Socialistic
conditions which shall once and forever abolish all class rule. (CW 4/85)
Besides illustrating the verbosity of the age, this complex resolution exem-
plifies the performative power of language. In a retro-prospective move,
it pays tribute to the achievements of the Communards, protests against
their ongoing misrepresentation in the viciously anti-Communard press,
and anticipates a future where the promise of the Commune will be real-
ized. While making these points, the resolution narrativizes the Commune
by distributing roles in the following way: the “workers” are the narrative
subjects; their goal was the emancipation of labor; helped by their heroism
(allies) and despite “terribly adverse circumstances” (opponents) they suc-
ceeded (beneficiary of power) in getting what they wanted. In short, this is
the story of an achievement that allows an implied “us” to look forward with
confidence to a future in which the Commune’s short-lived success becomes
a permanent state.
The resolution a year later in 1886 echoed this narrative with some
variation while also linking it to a fierce denunciation of the mainstream
press:
That this meeting of International Revolutionary Socialists, assembled in
London on the 15th anniversary of the Commune of Paris, has met to com-
memorate the heroic devotion of the Parisian working-classes in the spring
of 1871 to the cause of the people, as embodied in that forerunner of the
socialized administration of the future—the Paris Commune, and to record
its gratitude to those who fell in defense of freedom and the emancipation
of labour. That [this meeting] declares its determination to strive without
ceasing for the overthrow in all countries of the system of class-domination
founded on force and fraud, and maintained by the folly of the workers,
and to establish instead thereof a condition of society based on principles
of social justice and international brotherhood. That it fully recognizes that
the lesson to be learnt from the events of 1871, is that this can only be
achieved by simultaneous and organized forcible action, and, therefore, it
calls upon the wage-workers of the world to unite. Furthermore, it desires

42 REMEMBERING HOPE
to record its abhorrence of the malicious lying of the capitalist press with
reference to this struggle of the people for their own emancipation. (CW
4/86; italics added)
Here too the goal of the Commune was defined in terms of labor rights with
the role of narrative subject assigned to the “working classes” (later “wage-
workers”) who sought “freedom and the emancipation of labour.” In contrast
to the previous year, however, the narrativization puts less emphasis on their
achievements than on the fact they “fell in defense” of their principles. Their
“heroic devotion” has generated a debt of gratitude which later generations
are now enjoined to repay by continuing their struggle and uniting to do so.
The Commune is thus again construed as a forerunner of a socialist future
with its current allies now in the position of narrative subject. Here too the
Commune is unfinished business. In 1886, however, the later generation is
explicitly given the task of redeeming a debt by living up to their example
and by acting together. The final comment on the malicious press seems
more like an add-in; as such it shows how the commemorative occasion is
being used not just for projections of the future, but for direct attacks on
present targets.
The resolutions in 1887, 1888, 1889 use slightly different fomulations but
follow more or less the same model in which the Communards and those
celebrating their memory are identified as workers and their common goal
defined as the emancipation of labor.39 While achievements are always rec-
ognized (the Commune remains a positive example), their relative weight
varies. Notably, the resolution from 1888 echoed the one from 1886 in high-
lighting the loss of life and the sacrifices made for the cause. Following the
logic of martyrdom “the hopes for which so many workers have sacrificed
their lives” (CW 3/10/88) will be redeemed in the future, but only if their
successors continue to pursue their goals (I discuss martyrdom in more
detail in Chapter 3).
In 1890, labor rights remain the ultimate goal, but by now workers are no
longer central stage. The narrative subject has become “soldiers of liberty”
who were struggling “to be the masters of their own lives” and in doing so
had to pay the price of death:
That this meeting once more recalls the glorious memory of the soldiers of
liberty who suffered and died in attempting to reestablish the claim of the
people to be the masters of their own lives, and emphatically declares that

MEMORY IN ACTIVISM 43
we will never cease from the struggle which they so nobly sustained until
Labour has been freed from class domination. (CW 3/29/90)
In 1891, the resolution returns to the suffering that the “martyrs of
the people” endured for the cause of Labor and for “international
solidarity”:
That this meeting of workers of all nationalities places upon record its
undying memory of the brave men, women and children who died for the
cause of Labour and international solidarity in 1871, and its detestation of
the capitalist robbers and murderers who slew and imprisoned the martyrs
of the people. (CW 4/91)
In 1892, the practice of moving a formal resolution on behalf of the assembly
was replaced by a series of speeches (CW 3/26/92). Their narrativizations
shared a shift away from the struggle for workers’ rights to the issue of gov-
ernance. One speaker referred to the Communards as “our glorious dead”
who were “murdered by the power of the state,” intimating that their murder
would be redeemed when, in the next revolutionary moment, “the might of
the people” shall overthrow “wrongs and tyranny.” Another speaker referred
to “the next revolution” in which people will “sweep away all government,”
a claim that resonated with banners to “Freedom” and “Anarchist Commu-
nism.” Finally, veteran Communard Louise Michel surmised that it may have
actually been a good thing that the Commune had failed the first time round,
for it “might only have resulted in the establishment of a fresh tyranny, the
tyranny of the aristocracy of labour.” Despite the lack of a centralizing reso-
lution then, the speakers ended up configuring the Commune in ways that
echoed and reinforced each other. The narrative subject had become “the
people”; their goal was “freedom from tyranny,” their opponent was “the
State,” and it was state power that had prevailed in 1871, leaving the next gen-
eration with the task of doing things differently. Indeed, compared to earlier
commemorations, these anarchist-inflected speeches were more forward-
looking, offering in effect a call for action to “overthrow” the forces of wrong
and tyranny. The anniversary of the Commune might have provided the
occasion, but there was less emphasis on paying tribute than on being alert to
the next revolutionary moment. As another speaker reportedly proclaimed,

44 REMEMBERING HOPE
“the time had passed for philosophical discussions; the hour had arrived for
action” (CW 3/26/92).
The Commonweal was not in production in the spring of 1893, but the cel-
ebrations of 1894 echoed many of these anarchist-inflected themes. Indeed,
the lineup of speakers was almost the same. The events opened with “an
eloquent account of the Commune in its brief and glorious history,” which
nevertheless concluded by emphasizing its failure. In contrast to earlier
accounts which attributed that failure to state power, here the Commune
itself had failed “by trying to legalize itself by forming a Revolutionary Gov-
ernment; which Government, like all others, did nothing but paralyze the
initiative of the people.” In contrast, the next speaker proposed, three things
are necessary for a successful Revolution: “(1) a new idea; (2) a revolution-
ary situation; and (3) courage on the part of the people to take advantage of
an opportunity to revolt” (CW 3/31/94). The idea of “revolt” echoed through
several other speeches, including that of Louise Michel: “Liberty is so attrac-
tive that, once seen, men will fight and die for it. Animals revolt; it is the
natural right of all; it is the hope of the world.”40 Taken together, these inter-
ventions mark yet another shift. The ultimate goal for action had become
the idea of revolt itself linked to permanent freedom from government,
rather than the achievement of international solidarity or the emancipation
of labor. This anarchist version of the Commune differs from the socialist
versions of previous years in the features it highlights as significant (issues
of governance, including the institution of property as these played out in
1871), but also in what it sets out as the ultimate goal (freedom). More-
over, where the socialists clearly positioned themselves as taking over the
baton of the workers’ struggle, the anarchists are only heirs to the Commu-
nards to the extent that they hoped to have learned from past mistakes and
would know how to do things differently. Ancestor worship shaded over
here into “limited apostasy” (Zamponi 2018: 8), the selective rejection of
predecessors.
Across all these resolutions, the “same” memory was not instrumental-
ized in a new way. Rather, the memory itself changed over and again so that
alternative aspects of the Commune could serve as a resource for imagin-
ing the future and framing action in the present. As we shall see in the next
section, these shifting configurations of the Commune operated alongside
the commemoration of another more recent event: the execution of the
so-called Chicago Martyrs.

MEMORY IN ACTIVISM 47
Celebrating the Chicago Martyrs
In 1887, another high point emerged in the commemorative calendar: the
execution on November 11 of the “Chicago Martyrs,” a group of anarchists
accused of being involved in a dynamite explosion at Haymarket Square,
Chicago, which had ripped through a workers’ demonstration for an eight-
hour day, killing seven policemen and at least four civilians. This sequence
of events—from the demonstration to the execution—lent itself to a social-
ist frame (given the link to labor rights) or to an anarchist one (given the
reputation and writings of the men arrested). In the long term, the socialist
framing has arguably prevailed since the memory of the “Haymarket Affair”
later fed into the establishment of May 1 as international labor day.41 In the
first instance, however, it was the “legal murder” of the accused men that
took center stage in the Commonweal. There were regular updates on the
trial of the men as it unfolded over a twelve-month period; after their execu-
tion, attention turned to the importance of keeping their memory alive and
seeking redress for what was perceived as a miscarriage of justice. In line
with their status as “martyrs,” anniversary celebrations centered on the date
of their death in November rather than on the Haymarket demonstrations
in May.
As of 1888, announcements for the November commemorations
appeared as early as September, their significance enhanced by frequent
advertisements for writings by and about Albert Parsons, who quickly
became the figurehead for the entire group, and for portraits of the con-
demned men presumably for display in homes and meeting rooms. As with
the Commune, the journal printed ancillary articles around the time of the
commemoration and subsequently also reported on the celebrations in the
weeks following. In November 1888, the presence of Lucy Parsons, Albert’s
widow and an increasingly prominent activist in her own right, gave an
added edge to the first anniversary in Britain. A living witness to her hus-
band’s legacy, she was also vocal in her support for revolutionary action, if
need be violent action, to destroy the current regime42—a stance that made
the moderate Morris very uneasy.43
Like the Commune, the execution of the Chicago anarchists was a com-
plex site of memory that left considerable freedom for narrativization. The
case brought together an important episode in the labor struggle; an episode
in the history of contention and free speech; an episode in the history
of dynamite attacks and police brutality; and finally, an episode in the

48 REMEMBERING HOPE
history of anarchist thought as carried in particular by Albert Parsons, whose
speeches and autobiography were later published by his widow and regu-
larly advertised in the Commonweal.44 The annual commemoration of the
execution of the four men produced a shifting constellation of these differ-
ent elements in the years covered in the journal. E.P. Thompson (2011: 506)
saw the trial and execution of the Chicago Martyrs as a catalyst in turning
the Socialist League in an anarchist direction. As we will see here, this was
reflected in a gravitation of the memoryscape away from a framing in terms
of workers’ rights to one of freedom from government.
In 1888, when resolutions were still an integral part of the repertoire,
the Commonweal announced on November 10 that the following resolution
would be “moved” at the first anniversary meeting:
That this meeting commemorates the legal murder of five men which
took place on November 11th, 1887 (Parsons, Spies, Fischer, Engels, and
Lingg),45 and the cruel imprisonment of three others (Fielden, Schwab, and
Neebe) by the Government of the State of Illinois for the crime of support-
ing workmen in a labour struggle against their masters, and the further
crime of maintaining the rights of free speech, and emphatically denounces
the interference with these rights in all capitalist countries; an interference
which is the natural result of a so-called Society founded on the robbery of
Labour. (CW 10/27/1888)
The lyrics to a song called “Remember Chicago” (for singing to the Irish tune
“The Meeting of the Waters”) appeared in the same issue and underscored
the idea, articulated in the resolution, that the executed martyrs died “for the
cause of the poor” and for “the honour of labour.” This emphasis on labor
rights reappeared almost verbatim in 1889 and 1890.46
Between 1889 and 1891, things changed. Although speechifying did
remain at the center of proceedings along with the reading out of telegrams,
a distaste for the traditional protocols set in and meetings became less for-
malized. The journal reported in 1891 that the celebration had taken place
“in accordance with our usual custom”; in fact, this was the “new” custom
of doing without a chairman and no longer having keynote speakers mov-
ing resolutions to be approved by the house (CW 11/21/1891). Moreover,
the meeting was advertised as a “commemoration of the legal murder and
imprisonment of the Chicago Anarchists.” In keeping with this shift from
“martyrs” to “anarchists,” the focus also shifted from the labor struggle to

MEMORY IN ACTIVISM 49
the iniquity of the legal system which had condemned the men, and that is
now taken as an indictment of government as such:
[W]e do not meet here only to commemorate an act of capitalistic barbary
however much our indignation may be aroused by that unspeakable crime
of 1887. […] We ask you therefore who are not Anarchists to watch every
day of your lives the evil workings of Government. […] To note how those,
who are “wrapped in a little brief authority” not only make the angels weep,
but make poor humanity bleed. Then you will see that freedom, a life with-
out government and law must be our aim, because man lives best where he
is freest. (CW 11/21/1891)
(CW 11/21/1891)
The names of Parsons, Spies, Fischer, Lingg and Engel will be remembered
when [the] classes and the masses stand face to face in the last great struggle.
[…] In that world all men shall live in happiness and peace for they shall live
without rulers or masters. To realise this new society our comrades lived
and died, for this will be true freedom: this will be Anarchy!
(CW 14/11/1891)
The journal later also reproduced telegrams exchanged on these occa-
sions presenting these as “Some Anarchist greetings.” Several of these also
ended with phrases like “Hurrah for Anarchy!” and “Vive l’Anarchie!’ (CW
11/28/1891). While William Morris had been at pains to dissociate the
Chicago Martyrs at the first anniversary from the explosion of which they
were accused (CW 11/19/1888), one of the speakers in 1891 intimated that
dynamite was justified in some cases and that the damage it caused was noth-
ing like the structural injustice being meted out to workers every day (CW
11/21/1891). But by then Morris had left the journal.
There are no reports from November 1892, since this coincided with the
gap in production due to the imprisonment of the editor already mentioned.
In 1893 the Commonweal again reported on various commemorative meet-
ings across London in an article pseudonymously signed by “Ça ira” (the
title of a French revolutionary song) and prefaced by the triumphant claim
that “after 6 years we have been able to hold real British-Anarchist’ meetings,
with real revolutionary speeches and enthusiasm” (CW 11/25/1893). The
success of these meetings was also measured by their ability to provoke the
police: “[T]he proof of the success of a movement or an idea is measured by

50 REMEMBERING HOPE
the amount of persecution bestowed on it by those who were thought to be
indifferent” (CW 11/25/1893). By now, commemoration had itself become
an act of provocation designed to elicit a repressive response. In short, even
as the anarchists claimed to be heirs to the Chicago Anarchists (their name-
sakes to whose aims they keyed their own goals), they were also reinventing
the very idea of commemoration itself. All of this running parallel to, and
resonating with, the anarchist inflections of the Commune.
Shifting Memoryscapes
The Commonweal shows the continuous feedback between memory and
activism. Using the media technologies of their time, these highly literate
nineteenth-century socialists and anarchists oriented themselves toward the
future by reference to the past. Agreement about the centrality of certain
memory sites created common ground between competing positions; in
turn, the fact that these sites were composite figures of memory allowed
for disagreement to be staked out by highlighting different aspects of the
remembered events. Recollection, plans for the future, and power plays in
the present worked in tandem across different cultural forms, with mem-
ory offering a renewable resource for position-taking as well as an anchor
that linked present groups to earlier generations. In a two-way exchange,
changing ideological frames provided a lens through which the past was
narrativized as a prequel to the present. In turn, commemorative perfor-
mances beyond the pages of the journal became a medium through which
groups jostled for control of the shared narrative in meeting halls across the
city.
None of these mnemonic genres or performances worked alone. Change
occurred slowly through minor innovations in the way the past was recalled
but also in the lexicon used to frame current events. Resonance—the echo-
ing of themes, terms, and perspectives—from one context to another is key
to this complex dynamic. As small differences were reiterated across texts
and practices, they resonated and hence mutually amplified each other so as
to produce new narratives. The overall change from a socialist to an anar-
chist framing, which manifested itself in the dominant lexicon of the journal
(see Figure 1.4), was played out through mnemonic practices as much as
through commentary on current affairs. The goals and achievements, but
also failures, of their predecessors offered a resource for present-day actors to

MEMORY IN ACTIVISM 51
articulate their own goals as narrative subjects, identify their opponents and
allies, and otherwise position themselves in relation to an imagined future.
As the differences between the more formalized modes of commemoration
leading to a central resolution and the less formalized anarchist commem-
orations indicate, ideological differences played into the very practice of
commemoration itself. But none of this happened overnight; the overall
change in framing took place slowly and incrementally through multiple
actions both in text and on-site.
The activist memoryscape would of course go on changing after the Com-
monweal’s demise, when its role as a political-mnemonic hub dissipated and
activity relocated to other journals. That is beyond the scope of this study.
As Chapter 4 will show in more detail, the Commune continued to evolve as
a site of memory as it was taken up in new contexts and viewed through dif-
ferent metahistorical frames. Where it was once remembered as a prequel
to the great October revolution of 1917, it has more recently been redis-
covered as a prefigurative experiment in self-government, as Chapter 7 will
show. This Commune lay entirely outside the purview of actors in the 1880s,
when, across the socialist-anarchist divide, all parties saw the past, present,
and future as being part of the same unilinear and progressive development.
The memory of the Chicago Martyrs too has continued to evolve, with the
anarchist lexicon giving way to a framing in terms of labor rights and social
justice, along with a tendency to highlight “Haymarket” as the key term
rather than the names of the martyrs themselves.47
The Commonweal case carries all the hallmarks of the late nineteenth
century. That activists of the time should have imagined their future by ref-
erence to the past reflected the importance of historicism throughout the
nineteenth century.48 Many of the ideas and practices reviewed here were
also historically specific: the passing of resolutions, the ubiquity of poetry,
the assumption that memory was about celebration, the centrality of print
as a site of mediation, the metahistorical belief that a definitive revolution is
possible. The pursuit of long-term social transformation through long-haul
political organizations with card-carrying members is also more charac-
teristic of historical movements than of the “new” social movements that
emerged in the 1960s. As the rest of this book will show, however, memory
and activism continue their entanglements in evolving media landscapes
and political contexts. Memoryscapes serve both as points of orientation
and active ingredients in activism, providing resources for the articulation of
agendas in the struggle for predominance among competing radical groups.

52 REMEMBERING HOPE
I began with Marx’s comment on the past weighing “like a nightmare
on the brains of the living.” My analysis of the Commonweal has shown,
and other chapters will confirm, the existence of a specifically activist mode
of remembrance that entails a refusal to let the past merely weigh, inert,
on the present. Where official public commemoration usually assumes the
pastness of the past to which tribute is being paid, the anniversary commem-
orations on display in the Commonweal are inseparable from new acts of
contention and the pursuit of unfinished business. Hence the importance of
revolutionary calendars, which I explore in the next chapter.

Chapter 2
Marking Time with Radical Calendars
The Power of an Anniversary
“Mahsa Amini: Protesters Mark One Year Since Death of Iranian Student.”1
On September 16, 2023, various news outlets reported on demonstrations
across Iran dedicated to the memory of Mahsa Amini, a young Kurdish
women killed twelve months earlier. Her death in police custody, after
her arrest for defying the Republic of Iran’s draconian laws relating to
the wearing of the hijab, had sparked a surge of protests under the rally-
ing cry of “Women, Life, Freedom,” during which hundreds were killed or
detained. Now, exactly a year after Amini’s killing, supporters across the
country and especially in the Kurdish regions, defied the authorities by tak-
ing to the streets again, honking their horns and chanting the same slogan.
Similar commemorations took place in friendlier circumstances in other
cities across the world. In parallel to the embodied commemorations-cum-
demonstrations taking place in the streets, there was also an intensification
of online recollections of Amini’s death involving the diaspora.2 In a coun-
termovement that started well in advance of the anniversary, heightened
police activity sought to prevent commemorative gatherings. This included
detaining Amini’s father, prohibiting the family from visiting her grave,
preemptively arresting dissidents, and, on the day itself, trying to disrupt
internet services and hence prevent people from coordinating collective
action. A couple of days before the anniversary, Amnesty International used
the occasion to issue a statement calling for renewed attention “one year after
the uprising” to its many victims and to the ongoing repression in Iran.3
In a study of online cultures of memory in Iran and the Iranian diaspora,
Nafiseh Mousavi (2024) has noted the persistence and ubiquity of recollec-
tions of the hundreds of victims of state repression in 2022, of which Mahsa
Amini was the best known, in daily posts on social media by friends and
family members.4 Why should the level of attention to Amini’s death have
peaked on the anniversary and spilled over from the online environment
onto the streets? Or to put this more generally, why should an “anniversary”
Remembering Hope. Ann Rigney, Oxford University Press. © Oxford University Press (2025).
DOI: 10.1093/oso/9780197789711.003.0003

54 REMEMBERING HOPE
have seemed to all parties a natural—hence predictable—moment both for
remembrance and for protest?
The chapter starts with this recent case because it vividly illustrates how
memory is not just about time but also a matter of timing. Indeed, pub-
lic commemorations can be defined as scheduled remembrance. “One year
after the uprising” provided an occasion for synchronizing and hence ren-
dering collective the recollection of Amini’s death. The very predictability of
its arrival made it possible for different parties—family, activists, the state,
the media, Amnesty—to mobilize in advance with the aim of remember-
ing on the one hand, and of crushing remembrance on the other. Having
a shared date on which to remember Mahsa Amini worked together with
her high-media profile as the face of the “Women, Life, Freedom” move-
ment to provide critics with a new occasion on which to express opposition
to the regime and, in doing so, renew their campaign for greater freedom.
In Chapter 3 I will be exploring in more detail why the killing of protesters
should play the important role it does in activist memory. In this chapter,
I explore the “timing” of activist remembrance and, relatedly, the interplay
between synchronized commemoration and contentious action. As we will
see across multiple examples, predictable anniversary days have the poten-
tial to turn into unpredictable protest waves. But as importantly, they serve
to keep activists’ agendas alive by “marking time” in the intervals between
waves of mobilization.
Annual commemorations have already figured prominently in earlier
chapters. Remembering the Commune on an annual basis, in the form of a
commemorative meeting, provided a moment of stock-taking for radicals in
the late nineteenth century. The periodicity of such stock-taking is at stake
here. Most theorizations of collective memory have presumed that mem-
ory is continuously and steadily operative in social life. The importance of
anniversaries—both small ones and big ones like centenaries or decades—
indicates that there is a periodicity or “rhythm” to the memory of activism,
as Samuel Merrill and Simon Lindgren (2020) have also recently argued.
Focusing on the anniversaries of the killing in 1992 of left-wing activist Sil-
vio Meier by neo-Nazis, they study the evolution of Meier’s remembrance
across different social media platforms over a fifteen-year period, noting
how online mnemonic activity notably intensifies in the days around the
anniversary of his killing.5 Days of intense mnemonic activity alternate
with periods when memory work turns to other sites or decreases overall.
While anniversary days are often linked to public gatherings, synchronized

MARKING TIME WITH RADICAL CALENDARS 55
memory work may also be a more virtual matter of people at different loca-
tions feeling connected through their awareness of its being a significant
date. Calendars, as devices for organizing time and planning actions, are
key to understanding these mnemonic rhythms. As we will see, calendars
as cultural forms have also had an especially tenacious life in the history of
contention.
What follows here is a wide-ranging exploration of the history and func-
tion of commemorative calendars within progressive movements since the
French Revolution. The Paris Commune figures in many of these calendars,
alongside more locally significant events, as part of a transnational canon
of activist memory sites. By bringing together examples from different con-
texts, I show that commemorative calendars have evolved into a specifically
activist medium that has mutated across different media ecologies and mate-
rial forms. They have distinct affordances for combining retrospection with
the future-oriented recalling of promises and commitments and hence offer
an experimental space for thinking differently about time.
Scheduling Remembrance
Any discussion of memory ends up having to deal with time and tempo-
ralities. Whichever definition one uses, it is clear that memory work in all
its forms complicates the common-sense understanding of time as an irre-
versible sequence of unique moments in which past, present, and future
neatly line up.6 Memory invites exploration of the multiple ways in which
the past re-enters the present in the form of recollection, traumatic repeti-
tion, habitual behavior, or the repertoire of cultural forms that shape the way
we make sense of the world. A recent special issue called “Memory Worlds:
Reframing Time and the Past” (Hristova et al. 2020) indicates that memory
scholars have also started to reflect more explicitly on the context-specific
understandings of time that underpin the field itself.
The question of temporal orientation is a key theme in these discussions,
and is linked to the existence of changing “regimes of historicity” (Hartog
2015 [2003]). Such a regime is defined by the metahistorical understand-
ing of the directionality of change (or lack thereof ) that is dominant in any
given period. Despite differences in their approach, both François Hartog
and Aleida Assmann (2020 [2013]) have converged on the idea that we are
living in an age when the “modernist” temporal regime, in place since at

56 REMEMBERING HOPE
least the Revolutionary period, is no longer credible—trust in linear pro-
gression to a better world no longer holds. Enzo Traverso (2016) has pointed
to a comparable loss of confidence in traditional Marxist metahistory with
its expectation that a future revolution could bring about radical change.
In Chapter 6 I will discuss emerging alternatives to such grand narratives
when discussing the politics of prefiguration practiced in recent activism.
The important thing here is that all this talk about regimes of historicity
assumes that our collective understanding of time is not a natural given, but
a cultural construct through which change and duration are interpreted.
In exploring the cultural construction of time, we need to go beyond the
issue of temporal orientation. Eviatar Zerubavel’s Hidden Rhythms: Sched-
ules and Calendars in Social Life (1985) and Time Maps: Collective Memory
and the Social Shape of the Past (2003) offer a good starting point for this
broader discussion. Using “sociotemporal order” as his key term, Zerubavel
shows how societies have developed distinct “temporal maps” (1985: 14).
These are conceptual and practical toolkits for organizing experience by
locating events and activities sequentially along a collectively shared time-
line; measuring duration and parsing experience according to different
timescales; segmenting activities and establishing different zones of experi-
ence (private and public, work and leisure, sacred and profane); and (though
Zerubavel does not dwell on this) orienting oneself in relation to the past and
the future according to changing metahistorical understandings of the direc-
tion of change. The term “sociotemporal order” supposes that this toolkit
is shared. With its help, people plan their lives; coordinate their activities;
register change, repetition, and duration; and more generally, experience
the “rhythms” (p. 10) of their personal and social lives. The sociotemporal
order allows people to live “in the same time” and hence experience them-
selves as contemporaries (at the cost, as others have noted, of relegating
colonial subjects to a different era7). Zerubavel points out, moreover, that
the sociotemporal order of modernity is a double-edged sword. It allowed
productivity to be measured in temporal units so that work-time and leisure-
time could be more clearly distinguished. In the long term, however, this
also enabled the emergence of a 24/7 economy that erodes the collective
alternation of work and leisure, and of public and private time. One way or
another, the sociotemporal order provides the conditions of possibility for
synchronized activity and for the sharing of “special moments.”
Calendars are the central actants in this order. In the first instance, they are
technologies for formatting time embedded in cultural artifacts in print or

MARKING TIME WITH RADICAL CALENDARS 57
some other form. In line with other historians of time, Zerubavel shows that
calendars have a rich history as technologies (pp. 70–100). Different cultures
have invented their own timelines for counting the accumulative passage of
the years and for locating the start of the new year; they have also found
different solutions for dividing up the solar year into weekly units and into
monthly ones linked to the lunar cycle.8 That there has nonetheless been
a gradual convergence across the Gregorian calendar with its origins in the
calendars of the Roman Catholic Church is a reminder that the organization
of time is entangled both with economic life and with power politics and
identities.9 Witness the decision taken by the Ukrainian government in July
2023 to shift the date of Christmas to follow the Gregorian calendar rather
than that of the Russian Orthodox Church.10
Calendar design involves, on the one hand, making a timeline of years
from the putative origins of the community and, on the other hand, cre-
ating periodicity by modeling each year into repeating units. As Zerubavel
notes, in face of those who insist on the merely mechanical and homoge-
nizing aspects of timekeeping, calendars have traditionally also integrated
periodically recurring “marked days” into the linear passage of the everyday.
This takes the form of a weekly “holy day” or day of rest (Friday, Saturday, or
Sunday, depending on the religious tradition involved), but can also entail
the marking of some other special day. Most notable in this regard, and most
relevant to my discussion of the revolutionary calendar of 1793 in the next
section, is the one used for centuries within the Roman Catholic Church;
this links every day of the year to the name of a saint or martyr, or to an event
in the life of Christ. In this very full calendar, every day is an anniversary
with a religious significance even if only some special days are also public
feast-days and days of rest.
Within the framework of the modern nation-state, secular commemo-
rative calendars have largely replaced religious ones. Instead of saints and
martyrs, historical figures and events provide for an annual cycle of com-
memorations around a nationally specific canon of memory sites (Martin
Luther King Day, May Day, Bastille Day, and so on), some of which have an
extra symbolic weight in being designated “a national feast day” entitling
large numbers of people to a day off school or work. Since they concen-
trate remembrance into a single albeit recurring day, anniversaries are the
temporal counterpart of “memory sites” as defined by Pierre Nora (1997
[1984–92]): technologies for intensifying but also containing mnemonic
energies. This is also what gives them their social power. By synchronizing

58 REMEMBERING HOPE
recollection, they help create “mnemonic communities” (Zerubavel 1996).
This effect is enhanced when remembrance takes the embodied form of
public gatherings in which membership of a community is performed and
renewed on an annual basis.
For this reason, commemorative calendars regularly become sites of
contention as different groups struggle for control of the sociotemporal
order. Robin Wagner-Pacifici and E. Colin Ruggero have even argued that
a social movement may be “as much about the transformation of tempo-
ral orientations and commitments among participants as it is about the
transformation of structures” (2020: 683).11 Certainly, as the rest of this
chapter will show, left-wing activism has a remarkable track record of pro-
ducing new calendars. Innovations can reach into the very formatting of
time, as in the revolutionary calendar of 1793, but more commonly, they
involve “marking” a different set of days and linking them to alternative
sites of memory. After 1871, these alternative calendars regularly feature the
Commune in the company of a changing constellation of activist memory
sites.
Resetting the Clock in the French Revolution
The French Revolution is well known for having produced a new calen-
dar. Indeed, the Calendrier Républicain (1793) has become something of a
symbol of radical newness. Wagner-Pacifici and Ruggero, for example, end
their reflections on the need to rethink the temporalities at work in social
movements by reference to this innovation: “After all, the French revolu-
tionaries changed the calendar marking their days” (2020: 693). Decades
earlier, Walter Benjamin (1970 [1942]: 261) noted that the French Revolu-
tion, in experiencing the extended “now” of revolution had “exploded” the
“homogenous empty time” of the clock and reorganized time to reflect this
new historical consciousness. Further back in time, the Paris Commune too
paid tribute to the great Revolution when it adopted the calendar of its prede-
cessors in the final eighteen days of its existence.12 This was more a symbolic
gesture of affiliation than a concerted attempt to radically reorganize the
rhythms of collective life at a point when the insurgency needed all its
resources to survive. In a similar gesture of affiliation, the anti-austerity Nuit
Debout movement in 2016 followed both the Revolution and the Commune
in inaugurating a new calendar in which March 31, when the occupation of

MARKING TIME WITH RADICAL CALENDARS 59
the Place de la République in Paris began, became day 1 while the following
days became March 32, March 33, and so on.13
Clearly, the reform of the calendar initiated by the Revolutionary govern-
ment of 1793 has accrued enormous symbolic value. Jules Michelet, the great
historian of the Revolution, saw the calendar as “part of our heritage,” its
innovations “still vibrant creations where the Revolution subsists and pre-
vails” (2019 [1847–1853], I: 635). For the first time in history, he proudly
wrote, “Man had taken the true measure of time” (I: 632).14 Note the idea of
a new departure. Restarting or “blasting” time, to use another of Benjamin’s
phrases (1970 [1942]: 261), clearly has an imaginative appeal as an epitome
of revolution. But beyond its symbolism as a radical new departure, what
did the official republican calendar actually look like? What were the prin-
ciples underlying its design? A closer look shows that revolutionizing the
sociotemporal order was actually more fraught and less clear-cut than the
calendar’s reputation as a radical rupture suggests.
The famous calendar (Figure 2.1) was ratified by the National convention
in October 1793 and stayed in force until Napoleon abolished it in 1806. Its
design was indeed daring. It famously reset the timeline by starting to count
from the declaration of the French Republic in 1792, making that Year I (An
I) of the new era. In one swoop, it thus cut itself off from both the Chris-
tian era and the ancien régime. It also reformatted the year, replacing the
seven-day week by ten-day units and making the months all have thirty days
(this left a cluster of complementary feast-days at the end of each year which
were initially called “Sansculottides”).15 Finally, the nomenclature was dras-
tically changed, with the monthly units called after seasonal changes and the
days after plants in recognition both of the importance of agriculture to the
economic life of the Republic and of the value attached to a “natural” order.
Enzo Traverso once described revolutions as acts of “creative destruction”
(2021: 156). The revolutionary calendar too had this double character. It was
creative in that it changed the sociotemporal order, but destructive in strip-
ping the calendar of its association with the rhythms of religious life centered
on the weekly celebration of Sunday, the daily commemoration of saints and
events from the life of Christ, and the annual recurrence of major feasts such
as Easter and Christmas. It attempted to destroy attachments to religion,
while also trying to create enlightened republican subjectivities attuned to
new temporal rhythms. In line with other innovations brought about by
the Revolution, these rhythms were organized on the rational principles
of the metrical system. In designing such a calendar and inaugurating a

60 REMEMBERING HOPE
Figure 2.1 Excerpt from the Republican calendar introduced 15 vendémiaire
an 11 (October 6, 1793); used until 1806. Courtesy of the Bibliothèque
nationale de France.

MARKING TIME WITH RADICAL CALENDARS 61
new republican era, the Revolution thus marked a new departure. But it
also—paradoxically—marked the end of history in being premised on the
assumption that the struggle was over; that the nation had come into being as
a new political entity; and that it would continue into the future in the form
of an eternally recurring annual cycle. In consolidating its power with a new
sociotemporal order, the revolutionary government in fact tried to “decree
eternity” (Baczko 1997 [1984]).16 The calendar may have been a “monument
to a historical consciousness” as Benjamin claimed (1970 [1942]: 261), but
it was also a utopian attempt to mark monumentally both the beginning and
the end of history as a site of eventfulness.
In line with this idea, Reinhart Koselleck (1988) noted that the calendar’s
paradoxical recourse to the “timeless” language of nature was more like a
retreat into a premodern understanding of revolution than a step into a
new era. The idea of “revolution,” as Koselleck has argued elsewhere (1985
[1979]) would itself undergo a radical transformation in the aftermath of
events in France in 1789–1795. Until well into the eighteenth century, the
concept of “revolution” was modeled on the movement of the planets and
thus associated with the idea of a return to a natural order. Only in the
aftermath of the French Revolution did the modern idea of revolution take
hold, now understood as an unprecedented transformation which intro-
duces something so radically new that it opens up an “unknown future”
(p. 49). The revolutionary calendar of 1793, introduced in the middle of this
transition, in fact recycled the older understanding of revolution as a form of
radical regeneration, a return to a “natural” state based on rational principles
rather than a new departure in an unknown direction.17 That rationalization
of time gave expression to the Enlightenment ideals that were one of the
drivers of the revolution. But it sat uneasily with subsequent understand-
ings of revolutionary time as an opening up of new, hitherto unimagined
possibilities. Seen in this light, the calendar was indeed of huge symbolic
importance as a way of inaugurating a new era. But its particular format was
also a symptom of the revolutionaries’ struggle to articulate the unprece-
dented conditions they had brought into being and, in doing so, to square
two ideas of time: a closed cycle of repetition or a movement toward change.
These tensions were also played out in discussions about the design of the
new calendar. This was by no means self-evident. Research has shown that
the official Republican calendar adopted in 1793 did not arise in a single
grand gesture, but was one of several competing options.18 For a long time,
it seemed as if the choice would fall on an earlier design by Charles Gilbert

62 REMEMBERING HOPE
Romme, who had called the ten subdivisions of the year after republican
ideals and, in two cases, after key events of 1789 (the Tennis Court oath in
June and the Taking of the Bastille in July). The fact that Romme’s design
was ultimately put aside in favor of Fabre d’Églantine’s nature-based model
underscores the importance attached to a calendar that aspired to reflect
eternity rather than historicity.
A closer look at the print culture of the period shows a proliferation
of almanacs offering alternative calendars. A hybrid cultural form with a
long history and increasing popularity in the eighteenth century, almanacs
offered particular professional or social groups a combination of practi-
cal information, poetry, educational and political essays on various topics,
and, crucially, a calendar for planning the year ahead.19 There is a gen-
eral consensus among historians that the impetus for the new Republican
Calendar of 1793 had come from a highly controversial and outspokenly
anticlerical almanac produced in 1788, so on the eve of the revolution,
by Sylvain Maréchal (who ended up in prison for his pains). The calen-
dar that provided the frontispiece to his Almanach des honnêtes gens (1836
[1788]) had rationalized the months by giving them numerical values along-
side their traditional names. In addition, Maréchal’s calendar extended a
growing tradition in the eighteenth century by offering a secular pantheon
of “great men” as an alternative to the annual cycle of religious feast days
honoring saints and martyrs.20 Each day noted the name of a secular hero—
mainly writers and intellectuals, including Socrates, Pascal, Rousseau, and
Voltaire. Maréchal offered these as models for a new society based on
the egalitarian idea that “there are honest men everywhere” and that one
should not assume that “the only great men are the ones named here.”
Maréchal’s preoccupation with secular heroism anticipated the official deci-
sion taken in 1791 to turn the planned Sainte-Geneviève Church into a pub-
lic place for honoring great citizens: the current Panthéon in the center of
Paris.21
Five eventful years later, Maréchal went on to publish a new post-
Revolutionary version called the Almanach des Républicains, pour servir à
l’instruction publique (1793). Rather than simply recollect “timeless” heroes
who were coevals within a pantheon of immortals, his later almanac had
a narrative component. It includes days marking events since 1789. In
August for example, there are commemorations of the Declaration of the
Rights of Man (in 1789) and of the storming of the Tuileries and the
end of the monarchy (in 1792). In parallel, Maréchal used names for the

MARKING TIME WITH RADICAL CALENDARS 63
monthly cycle bespeaking republican values: the law (January), the people
(February), fathers (March), spouses (April), lovers (May), the mothers of
Paris (June), free men (July), republicans (August), equals (September), rea-
son (October), neighborliness (November), and friends (December). Read
in sequence across the year, the combination of values and of key events
in the Revolution yields a happy-ever-after narrative leading to solidarity
among republican equals and, again, to the end of history. Ending in friend-
ship became all the more tragically ironic in view of the Terreur that followed
in 1793–1794.
Given the availability of other models, the official preference for a calen-
dar devoid of historical references is all the more striking. By the time the
Convention nationale made this choice in 1793 and certainly by the time it
was abolished in 1806, the sheer “eventfulness” (Wagner-Pacific 2017) of the
Revolution kept cutting through attempts to say that it had ended. Within
that turbulent context, attempts to introduce a new sociotemporal order
were no less contested than the Revolution itself. After the Convention had
approved its calendar, as Welschinger shows (1884), a plethora of competing
calendars were published in almanacs. Some of these were dated according
to the Republican era, while others stuck to the Gregorian system or pro-
vided translations between the two systems. The overall tendency was to
complement the rationalized and ahistorical system with meaningful dates
and names. This could be in the form of civic values (as in Aristide’s almanac
of the “virtuous republican” from 1794) or historical events and figures
embodying those values (as in Maréchal’s “republican almanac,” 1793).
Moreover, as the Revolution went through multiple spasms, recent events
started to appear; a new Almanach des honnêtes gens (1797), for example,
included an account of the fall of the “tyrant” Robespierre (Welschinger
1884: 95); while an Almanach royaliste (1795), appropriated the revolution-
ary idea of a new calendar for counterrevolutionary aims, by listing day by
day the names of the “martyrs” who had fallen for the cause of the monarchy
(p. 96). Competing for control of the sociotemporal order had become part
of contention itself. And defining the canon of memory sites was a key part
of the struggle.
Many reasons have been given for the abolition of the official Republican
calendar after the coronation of Napoleon, including the not insignificant
fact that it had interfered so profoundly in the rhythms of everyday life
(for example, making workers wait longer for a day of rest) that people
had difficulty in following it. Moreover, the fact that the new calendar was

64 REMEMBERING HOPE
technocratically devoid of historical and religious meaning may also explain
its lack of appeal and failure to take hold. Sonja Perovic points to the chal-
lenges of articulating the story of the Revolution, noting how difficult it was
to “ensure the cogency of before and after” (2012: 88), given the often dra-
matic change in the historical significance of particular events and figures
as events progressed—where Lafayette was a hero in 1789, for example, he
was persona non grata by 1791; where Robespierre had presided over the
introduction of the new calendar in 1793, he would be beheaded as a tyrant
just a year later.
The very proliferation of new calendars in a context of dramatic change
shows the difficulties of sticking either to a closed model of cyclical time
or to an extended revolutionary “now” when all clocks are suspended, to
recall Benjamin (1970 [1942]: 264). It points to the felt need to recalibrate
the memoryscape so that the “space of experience” could provide some ori-
entation points in moving forward toward an “uncertain future,” to recall
Koselleck’s term. In this volatile context it was also proving difficult to do
without memory.
The official republican calendar as such went out of use and, despite
its brief revival during the Paris Commune, left only incidental traces—
for example, in the title of Marx’s Eighteenth Brumaire of Louis Bonaparte
(1852). Nevertheless, the idea that revolutionary movements should design
their own calendars has remained remarkable persistent and the revolution-
ary calendar as a genre has continued to evolve. As we will see in the next
section, however, what has been picked up in later movements is ironically
the idea of inventing a new commemorative calendar rather than of refor-
matting time on the lines of the rationalized and ahistorical official calendar
of 1793. Moreover, later revolutionary calendars do not “decree eternity” but
look both back to the past and forward to future change.
Revolutionary Calendars: The Persistence of a Genre
As technologies for organizing time, calendars are the medium par excel-
lence of “prospective memory” (Tenenboim-Weinblatt 2013). They remind
us of what we need to do and when. They provide a template for the
planning of new activities and the keeping of promises. By marking anniver-
saries they also schedule designated “times” to remember the past—in the
case of activist calendars, specifically the promises and achievements of

MARKING TIME WITH RADICAL CALENDARS 65
earlier movements. This section shows the tenacious afterlife of the calen-
dar reforms of the French Revolution and, in doing so, explores further
how agenda-setting and remembering, the prospective and the retrospective
forms of memory, work together in commemorative calendars. These range
from calendars that feature in longer publications as well as standalone ones
in the form of posters and, in the tradition of the almanac, pocket-size diaries
or “agendas” covering a year.
Since exhaustiveness is neither feasible nor necessary for my argument,
this section first discusses two exemplary cases, one from the nineteenth cen-
tury and the other contemporary, both from a self-identified revolutionary
tradition.22 It concludes with a brief discussion of the calendars produced
within the context of the Spanish 15-M movement of 2011. This shows how
the calendar as a cultural form continues to be used for activist purposes
within the framework of a metahistorical imagination that is not premised
on the prospect of a final revolution.
The first case brings us back to the commemorative calendar that
appeared in the Commonweal in the period 1888–1889 and was already
briefly mentioned in Chapter 1. Inserted as a text box in the title page of the
periodical, it was explicitly named a “Revolutionary Calendar.” While this
moniker was a clear throwback to the French Revolution, its more imme-
diate roots lay in a Chartist calendar from 1842 which, using the Gregorian
calendar, listed on a monthly basis historical events important in the history
of the movement.23 The Revolutionary Calendar in the Commonweal also
follows the Gregorian calendar in its nomenclature, but it marks every sin-
gle day as the anniversary of a particular event or set of events. This was no
timeless pantheon of heroes. The events recollected all occurred on the date
on which they are now being named. Every day marks an anniversary (there
are no empty days), while many days recalled multiple events. Although they
took place in different years and at different locations, the fact of being listed
together creates a resonance between these multiple historical layers.
On Sunday March 18, 1888, as Figure 2.2 shows, readers of the Com-
monweal were enjoined to remember the 1848 revolution in Berlin, the
beginning of the Commune in Paris in 1871, and the death in 1876 of the
liberal German poet Freiligrath. On Monday March 19, they were to again
remember the 1848 uprising in Berlin; on Tuesday, the death of Irish nation-
alist John Mitchel in 1875, and the declaration of the Commune in Marseilles
in 1871; on Wednesday, the death of Goethe in 1832; on Thursday, a strike
of colliers in South Wales in 1873; on Friday, the trial of Francis Burdett for

66 REMEMBERING HOPE
Figure 2.2 Commonweal, week ending March 24, 1888. Public domain.
seditious libel in 1820; on Saturday, the execution of the radical journalist
Hébert in 1794.
Instead of a single narrative of achievement, this revolutionary calendar
works through the granular accumulation of mutually resonating exam-
ples: relating to intellectuals and activists, milestones in democratization
and social reform, but above all to strikes, uprisings, and other acts of resis-
tance leading to trials and executions. Resonating together across the years,
these create the sense of “work in progress,” pointing in the direction of an
ever-renewed desire to bring about a social revolution despite the obstacles;
a desire that we also saw expressed in the resolutions being passed dur-
ing commemorations of the Commune in these years. In flagging radical
transformation as a recurring aim rather than in celebrating it as something
already achieved, the calendar exemplifies what Enzo Traverso has called
“emancipatory tropism” (2021: 150): a directionality that gives every event
meaning within an overarching agenda. This textual form of commemo-
ration was designed to create a mnemonic community among readers by
synchronizing recollection on a daily basis. This complemented the embod-
ied forms of commemoration on “special days” discussed in the previous
chapter.
Many features of the Commonweal’s “Revolutionary Calendar” reappear
in my second example: the Internationalism Calendar published annually
since 2022 by the Progressive International movement (which, according
to its mission statement, aims to “unite, organise, and mobilise progressive
forces behind a shared vision of a world transformed”24). Where the socialist

MARKING TIME WITH RADICAL CALENDARS 67
Figure 2.3 Progressive International, Internationalism Calendar 2022.
Fair use.
calendar was embedded in a periodical, this one is designed for hanging on a
wall (Figure 2.3). It takes the form of a poster-sized flip-over where each page
corresponds to one month and each month is dedicated to an anniversary
falling within that time period with the exact date circled for emphasis. Some
of the anniversaries relate to persons (Patrice Lumumba, Berta Cáceres, Nel-
son Mandela, Thomas Sankara, and so on), others to movements. QR codes
lead the user to more information; like earlier almanacs, this calendar seeks
to educate its community of users. With just a few exceptions (the Paris
Commune, May Day,25 the Carnation revolution in Portugal in 1974, among
others), the events recalled relate to protest actions and revolutions in the
global south or former Soviet bloc with a particular emphasis on post-1945
anticolonial and anti-imperialist struggles. What emerges month by month
is a transnational Marxist-Leninist revolutionary memoryscape in which
memorability is linked to explosive moments when regimes are challenged
