or overturned, and new ones created.
Every month gets a page and its own keynote topic illustrated by a
signature image occupying the top left-hand corner and placed side by
side with a text box listing days and dates. The theme of the month is

68 REMEMBERING HOPE
explored across some eight panels in text illustrated with thumbnail images.
While some months flag ongoing threats (the danger of nuclear prolifer-
ation, US interference in the Middle East, and so on), most months are
used to highlight historical moments of successful struggle or, conversely,
of counterrevolution and the assassination of activists. More explicitly than
in the Commonweal’s calendar, the users here are enjoined, directly or by
quotation from one of the actors involved, to remember. The recurrent
use of the first-person plural (we, our) interpellates a mnemonic commu-
nity of like-minded political subjects: “We remember those [in Guatemala]
who lost their lives in the struggle for land, for sovereignty, for dignity,
and against imperialism” (June 2022); “Thomas Sankara’s legacy lives on
and remains a testament to the immense power of revolutionary change
in our societies” (October 2022), “As the Palestinian struggle continues,
we recall these extraordinary acts of resistance” (December 2022); “On 11
December, we salute the popular struggles of the Indian farmers, commit
ourselves to learn from their achievements and remember the near 700 peo-
ple who lost their lives in the course of over a year of sustained protest”
(December 2023). Here memory and indebtedness go hand in glove. The
relation between generations is construed as an ethical matter as well as
a cognitive and affective one; remembering entails obligation as well as
inspiration.
This brings me to the central takeaway of these two calendars. Despite
their differences, they engage in a distinct form of memory work that is
both retrospective (looking back on events that have already occurred)
and prospective (looking forward to possible revolution). They perform
retro-prospective memory work, as I called it in my Introduction. Where
a private diary reminds individuals of their promises and commitments
the two calendars discussed here do something similar for collectives,
but with an added retrospective dimension. By reminding their users of
what has already happened (uprisings, strikes, campaigns, new organi-
zational forms, imprisonment, assassinations, and so on), the calendars
constitute the past as unfinished business and pass the baton to asso-
ciates in the present. The term “legacy,” used multiple times in the Inter-
nationalism Calendar, captures some of the complexity of the relation-
ship between “inheritance” and “promise.” The “emancipatory tropism”
of the Revolutionary Calendar assumed that a definitive transformation
of society was possible. Emancipatory tropism is still in evidence in the
Internationalism Calendar, but the sheer number of uprisings that have

MARKING TIME WITH RADICAL CALENDARS 69
happened across the world in the 150 years interval, bespeaks less confi-
dence than a moral and political commitment to keeping the possibility
alive.
Finally, I turn to an activist calendar belonging to the same evolving tra-
dition, but without the tropism that underpins the previous examples. The
Calendario Indignado has been published on an annual basis since 2012 in
memory of the Spanish anti-austerity movement of 2011 (known as 15-M for
the date in May on which the countrywide demonstrations started, some-
times also referred to as the Indignados movement). The 15-M calendar
(Figure 2.4) takes the form of a brightly colored flip-over with a piece of orig-
inal artwork marking each month. Dotted across the weeks are anniversaries
relating to the 15-M movement itself, labor strikes going back to the 19th
century, key moments from the Spanish Second Republic, but also events
that have happened since 2011, such as the mass drownings of migrants in
the Mediterranean and ongoing femicide. In this way, the calendar acts like
a “living archive” (Hall 2008) in recording the past while also continuing to
respond to emergent issues. Over the course of more than 10 years now, the
15-M calendars have recollected the 2011 movement, but not in a historiciz-
ing or monumental way. Instead, they have continued its work by reiterating
its commitments as well as practicing its playful visual aesthetics. This “very
indignant” calendar as it calls itself uses humor and lively colors to reen-
gage with the politics of the 2011 occupation and to celebrate its energies
and values in an aesthetically appealing way. The result is recollection on a
limited timescale rather than a grand narrative of world history – or “pow-
erful historical tendency” (Traverso 2021:39). The Calendarios nevertheless
do memory work. They experiment with the affordances of the calendar
as cultural form to combine the prospective with the retrospective and to
bring promises and commitments back into play. The aim is to reawaken
the “protest sensibilities” (Chidgey & Garde-Hansen 2024) that operated in
2011 by using forms, words, and humor rather than to initiate their users
into a grand revolutionary narrative that pays tribute to heroic predeces-
sors. Their very existence bears witness to a refusal to accept the historical
outcome that emptied the streets.
As this last example shows, the “revolutionary calendar”—always a model
in progress—continues to evolve in tandem with metahistorical understand-
ings of time itself and of the nature of political action. Across all these
iterations, the calendar as technology and as cultural form has offered a
renewable resource for articulating agendas in relation to predecessors and

70 REMEMBERING HOPE
Figure 2.4 Calendario indignado 2023. Archivosol 15M. CC BY-NC-SA-3.0.
for producing new political subjects. The next section develops this point
by turning to the ways in which calendars are materialized as cultural
artifacts, specifically in the form of pocket-sized appointment books that
are themselves a modern variant on the almanac. As a portable medium,

MARKING TIME WITH RADICAL CALENDARS 71
pocket-sized agendas help in the “personalization of contentious politics”
(Bennett & Segerberg 2013) by using media to bring the collective memory
of activism into the private realm and everyday life.
Pocket Agendas: Micropolitics in the Interval
A government decree promulgating a new calendar; a poster-sized flip-over
designed for display on a wall and hence for sharing with visitors; a calendar
published week by week in a periodical and shared piecemeal by readers, a
pocket-sized diary. These materialities have their own affordances and oper-
ate differently in the public arena. In order to grasp the workings of such
everyday artifacts, this section reflects on two recent pocket-sized agen-
das, which mark anniversaries day by day, while also offering quotations
from the cultural archive and space for users to add their own appointments
and reflections. At a time when digital planners have become ubiquitous,
the compact pocketbook format may seem quaintly outdated. For that very
reason acquiring such a diary may reflect the desire not to conform to the
technocratic time of digital planners such as Outlook. As media theorists tell
us, one should not assume that the use of a legacy medium is symptomatic of
an unreflecting adherence to tradition. Opting for a print medium, like opt-
ing to handwrite a letter, has a different valence in the age of digital media
than it had when the choice was not there.26
The Verso Radical Diary and Weekly Planner (2018–2024) has been pub-
lished on an annual basis since 2018 as a B-format paperback. It consists
of a weekly planner spread over two pages, accompanied by a commem-
orative calendar linking the days of that week to particular anniversaries
in the history of activism and oppositional politics (Figure 2.5). Inter-
spersed are essays on related topics written by both canonical and lesser-
known writers—the selection changes each year—while an appendix offers
“Verso reading lists” on such topics as “radical history” and “resistance and
activism” which also changes annually to take into account recent publica-
tions. Like older almanacs, the diary offers an intellectual vademecum along
with tools for planning.
The diary calls itself “radical” rather than revolutionary, with an emphasis
on milestones in social democratic reform alongside demonstrations, strikes,
and arrests. Every year the Paris Commune is recalled on March 18 while
presented less as an uprising than as a “participatory workers’ democracy.”

72 REMEMBERING HOPE
Figure 2.5 2021 Verso Radical Diary, week March 23–March 29. Fair use.
More attention overall is paid to struggles for rights (civil, human, worker’s,
gender) than for the toppling of regimes. Thus the week beginning on March
16, 2020, references the Tolpuddle “martyrs,” who were transported to Aus-
tralia on March 16, 1834, for having attempted to set up a trade union;
the establishment of the Commune on March 18, 1871; the first Kennedy
road blockade in Durban, South Africa, on March 19, 2005, which set off

MARKING TIME WITH RADICAL CALENDARS 73
the “shack dwellers” movement; the Sharpeville massacre of anti-apartheid
protesters on March 21, 1960. From year to year there are minor variations
in the anniversaries recalled and in the images used, but by and large the
same events with the same textual commentary are repeated year by year,
and accompanied by the same quotations from protagonists. The declara-
tion of the Commune is glossed both in 2020 and 2021 by the same quotation
from the Central Committee of the National Guard: “Workers, make no
mistake—this is an all-out war between parasites and workers, exploiters
and producers.”
The level of repetition from year to year suggests that the agenda’s com-
memorative function tends toward the ritualistic invocation of an estab-
lished canon rather than toward the breaking of new ground; moreover, that
this canon has been packaged into a sellable product and hence stripped of
its contentious edge. However, we should not assume that because diaries
(or other calendars) are available for purchase they are only symptoms of
commodification rather than of the economics behind activist cultural pro-
duction. Like buying a subscription to the Commonweal, spending money
on a diary can also be seen as a way of expressing affiliation, literally, of
“buying into” a political idea; of putting one’s money where one’s heart lies.
Moreover, the memory sites recalled and the essays supporting them can
have an educational and socializing value for users less familiar with the
history and exposed to the agenda for the first time.27 In such cases, buy-
ing an agenda for “keeping track of the year ahead,” as the Verso site puts
it, may constitute a form of private induction into the collective memory of
the struggle for rights.28 The educational function of the almanac genre and
specifically its use in the Revolutionary era to create “good citizens” among
willing readers returns here in a new guise.
The second example of a book-format agenda is not part of an annual
series though produced by a publisher known for activist publications
(Figure 2.6). The Turkish-language pocket-sized diary #Diren Direni¸s:
Ajanda 2014 (Metis 2013) appeared following the Gezi Park protests of
2013.29 The opening page tells us that the calendar was planned even as
Gezi was happening; by the time it was actually published, it had become an
attempt to continue the resistance by recalling its hope: “Gezi was a period
that added hope to our anger.” Here again memory is retro-prospective.
Recalling the past picks up on unfinished business and passes on the task
to the reader: “We realize that we cannot finish recording this ongoing resis-
tance and we entrust the agenda to our readers: You can continue writing

74 REMEMBERING HOPE
Figure 2.6 Cover of #Diren Direni¸s: Ajanda 2014. Istanbul: Metis
Publications, 2013. Based on a photograph by Martine Heissat. Reproduced
with kind permission.
while carrying it in your pockets throughout the year.” In line with this idea
of “continuing” the resistance, the cover shows an activist inscribing a mes-
sage in chalk on the pavement (only the first letters are visible, insiders will
recognize the name of Abdullah Co¨mert, a young man killed by police in
2013 when protesting in support of the Gezi Park occupation).30
#Diren Direni¸s: Ajanda 2014, like the Verso Radical Diary, is a tradi-
tional analogue agenda, with space for notes and appointments across the
entire year alongside the marking of anniversaries. It too includes essays
and quotations as well as references to the writers, both contemporary

MARKING TIME WITH RADICAL CALENDARS 75
and historical, transnational and local, who inspire current-day campaigns.
Emma Goldman and Ursula LeGuin, for example, appear alongside Turkish
academic Ahmet Ergenc and avant-garde writer Sevim Burak. Their writings
provide daily food for thought and for poetic sensitization to environmental
and political issues and to the history of protest in Turkey. Like the many
poems in the Commonweal, which I discussed in Chapter 1, literary writ-
ing adds an affective and aesthetic dimension to the articulation of political
causes. Here it is also connected to the marking of anniversaries.
Some of the events recalled relate directly to the protests of 2013 in
Istanbul and elsewhere in Turkey: on January 26, the deterioration of work-
ing conditions for academics; on April 7, an attack on demonstrators with
tear gas and water cannons; on April 19, a worker’s march in Izmir; on
April 29, a warning to the protesters from the governor of Istanbul; on
June 3, the teargassing of demonstrators; on June 5, a mass prayer by Anti-
Capitalist Muslims; on June 7, the violent breakup of the parallel occupation
in Ankara; on June 9, an attack on protesters in Ankara’s Güven Park; on
June 12, the clearing of the occupation by police and construction vehicles;
on September 29, a march commemorating those who had been killed at
Gezi, and so on. References to the events in Turkey of 2013 alternate with
references to earlier protest waves in Turkey and elsewhere, which root Gezi
Park in a longer transnational history of struggle for a democratic society
and the protection of the environment. On April 28, for example, the killing
of a demonstrator in Istanbul in 1960 is recalled; on April 30, the start of
the Mothers of the Plaza de Mayo protest in Argentina in 1977; on May
25, the decision to prolong the general strike in France in 1968, and so
on. In addition, annual feast-days devoted to particular causes rather than
specific events are also marked, including World Labor Day (May 1), Inter-
national Day of Peasant Struggles (April 17), International Sex Workers’ Day
(March 3), International Romani Day (April 8), Women’s Day (March 8),
World Environment Day (June 5), International Cinema Day (June 10), and,
more poetically, the “first warmth of spring” (February 18).
The memoryscape produced in this way reflects the particular concerns
of the Gezi movement (the environment, democracy, social justice) at a
time when the uprising and its repression were very much part of a living,
transnationally networked memory. It shows an awareness of developments
in Asia and Africa, most notable in its referencing of Occupy movements in
Kuala Lumpur (April 14) and Armenia (2012) as well as London (2012). But
even where it invokes memory sites from the transnational activist canon,

76 REMEMBERING HOPE
there is little ritualized about it. Symptomatic in this regard is its recollection
of the hypercanonical Paris Commune. In fact, #Diren Direni¸s: Ajanda 2014
marks the events of 1871 not just once, but twice. Its appearance on March
18 is not unexpected, though its glossing as an event when soldiers took
over from generals is unusual. Unexpected is its reappearance on April 17,
when it is linked to one of the most radical of the Commune’s legislative
acts: its allowing of employees to take over abandoned workshops with the
promise of paying back their debt to the former owners within three years.
The fact of commemorating the Commune twice and then zooming in on
one of its specific reforms, rather than just sticking to a generic invoca-
tion of its beginning or its bloody ending, indicates that #Diren Direni¸s:
Ajanda 2014 is not merely a conduit for established memory, as the Verso
diary tends to be. It carries out its own memory work, by producing new
perspectives on familiar sites and, by bringing them into the same commem-
orative calendar, establishing new connections between events across the
world (between the Occupy movement in Kuala Lumpur, for example, and
the Gezi park occupation; or between Gezi and the mothers of the Plaza de
Mayo). Together with the inclusion of literary quotations and images, such
unexpected perspectives give the agenda an aesthetic dimension beyond
its practical value as a planner. Together with its visual recycling of cre-
ative graffiti from the protests, it harnesses the power of the literary to hold
and absorb the attention, becoming an object to be cherished in its own
right. Chapter 4 will return to the role of the aesthetic in activist memory
work.
#Diren Direni¸s: Ajanda 2014, like the Verso Radical Diary and Weekly
Planner, is a carrier of activist memory but one that is located in the private
sphere of reading and writing, and in the micropolitics of personal engage-
ment rather than in the public sphere of street assemblies. But it is not only
a personal matter. In circulating among like-minded users who bought the
diary presumably because of their affiliation with the Gezi Park movement,
#Diren Direni¸s: Ajanda 2014 achieves a “personalization of contentious pol-
itics,” to recall Bennett & Segerberg (2013). In addition, it has the potential
to connect users at different locations, providing them with a common set of
references and a mnemonic schedule of virtually shared commemorations.
In this way, the diary helps keep alive a sense of community among dispersed
activists in the aftermath of the protest. This sense of community operates
in the first instance in the private sphere rather than on the streets; it does
not assume the existence of a collective “we” in the way the Internationalism

MARKING TIME WITH RADICAL CALENDARS 77
Calendar does. It is designed to keep alive the memory of struggle and the
values behind it in the heart and mind of networked users, thus turning
defeat into a measure of hope in the down-time after the streets have been
brutally cleared.
In their different ways, and with differing degrees of urgency, the two
pocket-sized diaries help shape political subjectivities in the intimacy of pri-
vate usage. In the process, they also socialize their users as members of a
mnemonic community, even if that community never meets in person and
in public. The role of cultural artifacts in shaping political subjectivities as
well as shared agendas has been insufficiently recognized in the scholar-
ship on the cultural dimensions of social movements. Cultural actants are
arguably especially important in the intervals between periods of height-
ened mobilization when movements are in “abeyance” (Taylor 1989) or
suffering the aftermath of repression as in the case of Gezi. More than the
well-packaged Verso diary, #Diren Direni¸s: Ajanda 2014 helps to “mark
time” across the year in the double sense of that phrase. That is, it pro-
vides its user with a resource for “marking” time by remembering different
events, writers, and causes on a daily basis, implicitly in sync with other
users of the diary; and they “mark” time in the sense of filling the inter-
val between the mobilization that was and the mobilization that may come
again. Cycling back is not the preferred option of those who want to go for-
ward. But in the absence of momentum or in the presence of oppression,
anniversaries are important in marking time, if only in the minimal sense of
keeping the memory of contention alive among the members of dispersed
communities.
The Contentious Potential of Commemoration
The focus until now has been on “virtual” commemoration in the sense
of synchronized remembrance triggered by calendars in print form. Under
repressive regimes virtual spaces may be the only safe place for commem-
oration. Nafiseh Mousavi has described social media with reference to
contemporary Iran as providing a vital haven for “dangerous remembering
in volatile spaces” (2024).31 While recognizing that the role of social media
in virtual commemoration deserves further inquiry, I return in this final
section to the relationship between commemoration and contention. When
does virtual commemoration in the private sphere turn into demonstrations

78 REMEMBERING HOPE
in public space? When do anniversary commemorations, predictable and
limited to a day, turn into open-ended contention in which the fight for a
cause is renewed?
In their study of the online remembrance of Silvio Meier, mentioned
earlier, Samuel Merrill and Simon Lindgren (2020) show in consider-
able detail how memory work over time becomes increasingly reduced to
“eurythmic” anniversary moments and, in becoming more ritualistic and
predictable, ends up disconnected from activism. Their analysis ends with
a critical note on the role of anniversaries in “the regulation and neu-
tralization” of activist remembrance. The evidence they present, together
with the more formulaic Verso diary discussed earlier, does indeed suggest
that activist memory sites lose their mobilizing potential over time if they
become too predictable. In this they come to resemble national feast-days,
those state-supported commemorative days which have historically been so
important in ritualistically reaffirming national identity.32 For good reason,
public commemoration has tended to be associated with consensus and with
the reaffirmation and rearticulation of hegemonic values rather than with
challenges to dominant narratives. This means that designing an “alterna-
tive civic commemorative calendar,” as Orli Fridman has shown in a recent
study of memory politics in Serbia (2022: 16), can become an effective way
for publicly expressing dissent. Bringing alternative memory sites into pub-
lic purview is accordingly a key strategy in “memory activism” (more on this
in Chapter 5).
Contentious commemoration is not only a matter of looking to differ-
ent sites of memory. It also entails a different sort of memory work: one
that combines recollecting the past with challenging the powers-that-be
in the present. It is antimonumental and about unfinished business. In
Chapter 1 we already saw an example of this in the way the Commune
was recalled at the same time as the need for worker’s rights was advo-
cated. Contentious commemoration is memory of a cause and memory
with a cause. It connects the past with ongoing causes. Not surprisingly,
when such commemorations take an embodied form, the occasion is often
described, both by participants and observers, as a “rally” or “demonstra-
tion” rather than as a “ceremony.” The nomenclature gestures toward a
different sort of collective action. Where a commemoration is linked to the
cyclical time of an ever-returning anniversary, a demonstration is linked
to the possibility of breaking through the cycle of repetition and creat-
ing a different future by going against inevitability. In such a performative

MARKING TIME WITH RADICAL CALENDARS 79
version of retro-prospective memory work, recollection and making claims
go together.
Beyond the banners and proclamations on such occasions, the very fact of
“coming together” can itself have a mobilizing effect. Christina Jerne (2017),
in her study of the way an anti-mafia movement emerged from commemo-
rations of the mafia’s victims, argues that the affective experience of making
common cause with hundreds of other people in a shared physical space
acted as a catalyst for further collective action and what she calls “event-
making.” Other cases indicate that embodied commemoration can occasion
“event-making” in the form of reviving a struggle now in abeyance. This hap-
pens in the first instance through renewed public displays of numbers and
commitment. It is also triggered by the affective dynamics of being in the
same place at the same time which, as Jerne shows, can produce deviations
from the script. There are many examples of commemorative demonstra-
tions turning from scheduled remembrance into an unpredictable “event”
defined in the strong sense of a development that transcends all precedents
and opens an “uncertain future” or, put more positively, a new field of possi-
bilities.33 They exemplify the “unscheduled workings of political memory”
(Ross 2024: 8): political in the sense of participating in the shaping of the
present rather than simply recollecting the past.
A case in point is offered by the commemoration of the Paris Commune
in the Belgian city of Liège in 1886. On that occasion, the commemora-
tion turned into a protest in favor of workers’ rights that set off a series of
strikes across the country.34 The possibility that predictable commemora-
tion might turn into event-like contention is always present, and explains
why authorities are so anxious about anniversaries and activists so eager to
mark them.35 The dynamic between the commemoration, contention, and
its suppression is perhaps best exemplified in the annual commemoration
of the 1989 Tiananmen Square massacre in the democracy movement in
Hong Kong. Whereas the memory of June 4 has been severely repressed in
the People’s Republic of China, activists in Hong Kong managed, until 2019,
to hold annual vigils on or around June 4 with attendance figures serving “as
a yardstick of the territory’s fractious relationship with Beijing” (Lim 2014:
223).36 The organizers of the annual vigils articulated five operational goals
in a combination of remembering and campaigning that recalls the resolu-
tions passed in nineteenth-century commemorations of the Commune. The
goals were: to release the dissidents, rehabilitate the 1989 pro-democracy
movement, demand accountability for the June 4 massacre, end one-party

80 REMEMBERING HOPE
Figure 2.7 Calendar 2022 commemorating Hong Kong protests of 2019. Artist
Illugulu. Image courtesy of Milan Ismangil. Fair use.

MARKING TIME WITH RADICAL CALENDARS 81
dictatorship, build a democratic China.37 These goals make clear that the
June 4 commemorations, already contentious by breaking the silence around
Tiananmen, were also part of a much larger drive to reconnect with the
pro-democracy movement of 1989 and to pursue its unfinished business in
contemporary Hong Kong.38 Since the pro-democracy movement was sup-
pressed in 2019, the calendar format has been used to resist the erasure of its
memory. Coded references to specific dates function alongside poster-sized
calendars recalling moments of possibility while also reproducing the aes-
thetics and slogans of the movement in the manner of 15-M.39 A calendar for
2022 (Figure 2.7) offers a veritable compendium of references to the Hong
Protests of 2014–2019, serving to keep their memory alive and their cause
on the horizon despite the repression. In Milan Ismangil’s description:
Visible in the background is the lion rock, a famous Hong Kong landmark
and site of frequent protests. It carries a banner with the words “Glory
to Hong Kong,” a reference to the movement’s anthem. A reference to the
Hong Kong way (the local version of the Baltic way) is also visible on the
rock. At the bottom of the poster one can see several protesters in typical
gear (yellow helmets, umbrellas) and, on the left, the statue of a protester
dressed in white holding a flag with the well-known slogan “liberate Hong
Kong, revolution of our times.” All the shop signs in Chinese have been
replaced by messages of support for the protesters such as “Persist” or “I
really fucking love Hong Kong.”
In activist memory the past continues into the present rather than being
closed off from it as an object of historicist veneration or as an occasion
for mourning the irreversible. What is at stake is not the past per se but
a resonance between a past event and a still ongoing campaign for rights
or for regime change. This explains the special power of first anniversaries
since they occupy a transitional position between the past and the unfold-
ing present. The first anniversary of the killing of Mahsa Amini, the example
with which this chapter began, offers a case in point. Once memory loses
its connection to ongoing agendas (as Merrill and Lindgren have shown in
the case of Silvio Meier, 2020), it becomes a mere site of memory and loses
its contentious potential and political edge. Or rather, it becomes an inert
site of memory with no stakeholders using it as a resource for intervening
in the present. More research is needed into the reasons why some events

82 REMEMBERING HOPE
acquire and others lose this capacity to “resonate” with present-day frame-
works for understanding the world and anticipating the future. As I argue
in the next chapter, the answer lies in mediation, which makes it possible
for a single event to be subsumed into a larger story. This explains why the
killing of demonstrators, in particular, is so often the subject of contentious
commemorations.

Chapter 3
Mediations of Outrage
Remembering as Nonviolent Resistance
The Salience of Unlawful Killings
On Sunday November 10, 1888, some 4,000 people marched across London
to Hyde Park to the sound of music, waving banners and shouting out to
the world that it should “Remember Chicago!” and “Remember Trafalgar
Square!”1 This was the highly charged first anniversary of two coinciding
events: the execution of the Chicago Martyrs and, closer to home, the killing
of a demonstrator in Trafalgar Square, London, in what had come to be
called “Bloody Sunday.” The double anniversary provided an occasion for
contentious commemoration, denouncing the “lawlessness” of states that
committed violence against their own citizens and making new demands,
in line with long-term agendas, for more civil rights and workers’ rights.
The occasion offers a prime illustration of retro-prospective memory work.
It also showcases the prominence of violence in the memory of activism,
which will be my topic in this chapter, since killing by state forces is the sub-
ject of the commemoration. As it is in many of the anniversaries discussed
in the previous chapters, from the deaths of Robert Blum, Mahsa Amini,
and Abdullah Co¨mert to the massacre of Tiananmen. How to explain this
gravitation in the memory of activism toward victims? And how does this
gravitation fit into my overarching theme of hope?
Mnemonic Jiu-Jitsu
In their essay collection Cultural Memories of Nonviolent Struggles (2015),
Anna Reading and Tamar Katriel urged scholars to turn the spotlight on
the memory of nonviolent resistance, on “struggle and agency in non-
violent contexts” (3). As mentioned earlier, the present book has been
deeply inspired by Reading and Katriel’s call to redefine the agenda of
Remembering Hope. Ann Rigney, Oxford University Press. © Oxford University Press (2025).
DOI: 10.1093/oso/9780197789711.003.0004

84 REMEMBERING HOPE
memory studies by putting active citizens rather than victims at center stage.
Recovering the history of nonviolence as an instrument of social struggle—
what Martin Luther King Jr. famously called a “weapon” that “cuts without
wounding” (1994 [1986]: 526)—provides a welcome reminder that not all of
history has been violent, and that memory is produced in the civic realm as
well as in theaters of war. However, my research has shown that the memory
of nonviolent struggle is difficult to disentangle in practice from the question
of violence, especially state violence. There are several reasons for this.
1. The term “violence” has a negative connotation and tends to be
assigned in a highly politicized way in order to legitimize the use of
force or deny the legitimacy of certain forms of protest.2 While the
impact of physical force on bodies and things has a physical real-
ity beyond interpretation, an entire range of terms exists to describe
that impact. Accordingly, the dynamics of contention continues into
the afterlife of events in struggles over the power of definition—was
an event that resulted in damage to property and injury to persons
an “unlawful assembly,” an act of “terrorism,” or instead a legiti-
mate expression of dissent that was unlawfully broken up by police?
The demonstrators in Trafalgar Square in 1887 were depicted in the
conservative press as “roughs” and “rioters”3 rather than as peace-
ful citizens exercising the right to protest; similarly, a demonstration
against new policing legislation in Bristol, UK, in March 2021 ended in
an assault on peaceful demonstrators, accused of being a “mob intent
on violence” and representing a “terrifying vision of lawless Britain.”4
At stake is the legitimation of protest, on the one hand, and state
control on the other.5
2. Within movements themselves, the boundaries between nonvio-
lence, passive resistance, civil disobedience, direct action, and mil-
itancy are also matters of continuous dispute. The memory of the
civil rights movements of the 1960s has been dominated by Martin
Luther King Jr. as champion of “nonviolence,” but as several his-
torians have argued, this has occluded the direct-action tactics he
and his associates also debated as well as the fierce pushback from
police and state troopers.6 Although the concept of “nonviolence”
has sometimes been reduced to well-behaved requests for change
in the form of orderly and peaceful demonstrations, the Gandhian

MEDIATIONS OF OUTRAGE 85
legacy on which it rests shades over into a gamut of more proac-
tive and creative forms of direct action and civil disobedience. As
Gene Sharp (1973) has theorized it, “nonviolent action” supposes
an active stance that uses different strategies to disrupt the workings
of the system that is being opposed. It is this active stance rather
than the mere absence of violence that will be central to the analysis
here.
3. It is a historical fact that expressions of “people power” (Arendt 1969)
are often met by the use of physical force on the part of the state. As
a result, the possibility of an attack by the police has become part of
the horizon of expectations of demonstrators and part of the risk they
assume in taking to the streets. The extent of the risk varies, of course,
with the democratic health of the regime in question, but the possi-
bility of violence is always there, if only by virtue of the huge police
presence that has become a structural feature of organized protest.
The history of public protest has become inseparable from incidents
involving the killing and wounding of demonstrators or their impris-
onment and even execution. Long before victimhood became central
to mnemonic cultures as it did in the post–World War II era, pro-
gressive movements have been dealing with the memory of violence.
While occupying the moral high ground in their defense of rights,
protesters inevitably operate from a position of vulnerability vis-à-vis
the superior physical force of the state. For this reason, Sharp included
responses to physical assault as a key feature of nonviolent action.
He referred in this regard to “political jiu-jitsu,” after the martial art
technique of turning the opponent’s force against them. In the case
of protest: “by combining nonviolent discipline with solidarity and
persistence in struggle, the nonviolent actionists cause the violence of
the opponent’s repression to be exposed in the worst possible light”
(1973: 657).
I propose to extend Sharp’s idea of violence being turned against itself
to the arena of memory. Cultural memory work can perform jiu-jitsu by
recalling police violence in such a way that it is exposed as illegitimate.
In earlier chapters, we have seen that history does not dictate the stories
told about it and that there is room for agency in choosing to narrate
events in one way rather than another; celebrating the Commune as a
party offered a case in point. This agency extends to the way in which state

86 REMEMBERING HOPE
violence is remembered—as an act of resistance rather than as an act of
mourning.
Key to this mnemonic jiu-jitsu is framing violence as outrage. The word
“outrage” evokes the egregious violation of a norm or law combined with a
moral judgment on the part of witnesses. The violation triggers a rebound
in the form of righteous indignation or “apt anger” (Srinivasan 2018) on the
part of observers. Those who witness outrage are not just victims stunned
into inaction but also rights-claiming subjects who are angered by the viola-
tions committed. The “outrage” frame—as a category of analysis as well as a
category of practice—opens a perspective beyond the traumatic paradigm. It
makes visible how the memory of the violence perpetrated against protesters
can be part of a dynamic of action and reaction; or, to echo Manuel Castells
(2012: 1–2), a dynamic of anger and hope.
At stake in this chapter are the cultural strategies that arise from this
dynamic. How is outrage mediated so that it becomes a new act of resis-
tance that exposes the opponent’s aggression “in the worst possible light”?
Framed in this way, memory work becomes a form of nonviolent direct
action, as defined by Martin Luther King in his “Letter from a Birmingham
Jail” (1963), namely, a strategy that “seeks so to dramatize the issue that it
can no longer be ignored” (1994 [1986]: 291] my emphasis). The memory
of death is dramatized as part of a larger struggle to control the narrative:
“Death is used as a powerful visual trope to signify the ultimate price of com-
mitment to a political cause and as a rallying cry for future mobilization”
(Askanius 2013: 119).
Narrative Schemata, Narrative Metonyms
My introduction described cultural mediation as sense-making with the
help of existing schemata, cultural forms, and communication technologies.
Making sense of “January 6” or “Tiananmen,” for example, means organiz-
ing information so as to bring out the distribution of actantial roles among
the actors and hence the underlying conflict between a subject’s desire for
certain values and their power to achieve them. In analyzing recollections
of the Commune, Chapter 1 laid out the importance of narrativization as
a key meaning-making tool. It showed that events are not in themselves
memorable but are made memorable in being interpreted with the help of
a medium (language, images) and the application of narrative schemata.

MEDIATIONS OF OUTRAGE 87
This is a complex operation that nevertheless depends on something basic:
the capacity to subsume singular events, actors, and locations into broader
categories. To narrativize is to abstract in an iterative way from specifics to
types and from a sequence of events to a moral “takeaway” (“justice pre-
vailed” or “an outrage was committed,” depending on the particular story
told). As we will see especially in Chapter 4 and Chapter 7, narration entails
more than “schematization” but this remains a core feature.
At this point, suffice it to note that narrative is a wondrously flexible
tool for sense-making since not everything needs to be spelled out by the
storyteller. Sometimes, just naming the event type (a “riot” rather than
“demonstration”) may be enough to imply its takeaway and its place in a
larger narrative. The same capacity to move from specifics to types allows
narrativization to occur in shorthand as well as longhand. While the schol-
arly literature on narrative has tended for focus on full-fledged versions, in
fact narrativization often works in a more inchoate form through brief refer-
ences that invoke stories in a condensed way. Narrative is arguably effective
as a source for collaborative meaning-making since it leaves so much open.
Users are left to fill in the gaps, using their specific knowledge of the case
at hand or more general schemata gleaned from other cases. In a study of
storytelling within the US civil rights movement, Francesca Polletta (2006)
refers usefully to narrative “metonyms”: brief references to a signature event
or actor that are taken to imply a much bigger sequence of events. The idea of
narrative metonyms is especially useful in explaining how photographs and
other visualizations of protest relate to more extended narratives in audio-
visual or verbal form. Since the advent of the illustrated press in the late
nineteenth century, images have been a key feature of the cultural medi-
ation of protest. Before exploring images in detail, more needs to be said
about memorability and the limits of mediation.
Differential Memorability
While schemata facilitate the remembrance of particular events and estab-
lish connections between them, they also produce amnesia regarding
the things they fail to capture. Robert Nixon’s Slow Violence and the
Environmentalism of the Poor (2011) has pointed to the difficulty, for
example, of capturing the quotidian trauma of radical inequality and sys-
temic violence—what he terms “slow violence”—using traditional narrative

88 REMEMBERING HOPE
schemata. While this lack of memorability stems from indifference to certain
classes of victims, Nixon argues, it is also enabled by the fact that “slow vio-
lence” is less easily turned into a story than an attention-and affect-grabbing
set of events concentrated into a brief period with identifiable actors and a
clearly defined ending. There is luckily nothing definitive about the narrative
repertoire, however. New schemata do emerge, albeit slowly, often with the
help of creative narrators who make sense of events, roles, and sequences in
hitherto unimagined ways. I will come back specifically to this point when
discussing aesthetics in Chapter 4.
For now, we note that both memorability and amnesia are produced by
hierarchies of relevance and the availability of sense-making tools. It has
long been known that cultural memory tends to crystallize into particular
“sites of memory” that become the focus of intense reproduction and seman-
tic investment; we have seen examples in earlier chapters.7 As a result of
this dynamic, certain events or figures become the focus of disproportion-
ate attention, and are recollected time and again, while others are sidelined.
What Judith Butler has called “the differential distribution of public griev-
ing” (2009: 38), with reference to media representations of contemporary
wars, is also a structural, if hitherto insufficiently recognized, principle in
public remembrance. The principle of “differential memorability” (Rigney
2016) means that events or situations that are easier to capture using the
available cultural repertoire will be more easily picked up and remediated,
where others are overlooked.
This explains why the memory of activism has tended to crystallize into
moments of confrontation: strikes, trials, executions, assaults, and killings
in the street. Spectacular acts of violence provide a potent resource for cap-
turing power asymmetries in an intensely dramatic way, even offering an
Aristotelian unity of action, time, and place. The fact that the names “Peter-
loo,” “Sharpeville,” and “Tiananmen” are internationally recognizable, not
just as actual locations but also, and above all, as sites where demonstrators
have been killed, is testimony to the particular memorability of outbursts
of concentrated violence against citizens.8 It is presumably for this reason
that Rachid Bouchareb’s film Hors-la-loi (Outside the Law, 2010), about the
long Algerian liberation struggle, begins with the massacre in Sétif in 1945
and ends with one in Paris in 1961; the killings mark in condensed form
the asymmetry between the desire for independence and the power of the
French state.

MEDIATIONS OF OUTRAGE 89
While the gravitation of activist memory toward concentrated acts of vio-
lence inevitably sidelines “slow violence” as defined by Nixon, it has had
the advantage of providing a resource for dramatizing and hence exposing
outrage in an economic and affect-charged form. This power to capture out-
rage is especially valuable for those trying to make their mark from a position
of vulnerability. In what follows, I explore this point further with reference to
two event types that have been repeatedly mobilized in schematizing protest.
The first event type is that of “Bloody Sunday” which, beginning in 1887 in
Trafalgar Square, became a historically specific model for depicting violence
against demonstrators. The second is that of “martyrdom,” an event-type
with deep cultural roots that continues to inform contemporary depictions
of violence against demonstrators. Bringing these two event-types together
also allows me to show something of the long history of cultural forms.
The overall argument of this chapter is that these event-types help operate
a mnemonic jiu-jitsu, to recall Sharp’s term—in dramatizing the oppo-
nent’s violence, they also reassert the moral urgency of the activists’ cause.
The overall claim is that the suppression of protest is highly memorable
in the long term, more memorable than large demonstrations that ended
peacefully, because they have a deep resonance in cultural memory and in
structural anxieties about the limits of people power.
The materials used in this analysis have been mainly drawn from journal-
ism, the primary site for the production of public memory at the interface
between activists and society at large.9 Given the growing importance of
visual culture in the period since the late 1880s, but also the importance
attached by Sharp to the “exposing” of violence, images receive particular
attention alongside nomenclature.
Bloody Sundays
On January 30, 1972, on the occasion of a civil rights demonstration calling
for greater equality for the Catholic minority in Northern Ireland, members
of the British army started shooting into the crowd, killing thirteen unarmed
civilians (a fourteenth died later of his wounds).10 This atrocity is known as
“Bloody Sunday” and is generally considered to have been a turning point
of the Troubles in Northern Ireland.11 In the course of the last fifty years,
Bloody Sunday has produced a rich archive of eyewitness accounts12 and
has been annually commemorated in the streets of Derry and remediated

90 REMEMBERING HOPE
across a range of genres and platforms: in literature, theater, cinema, music,
murals, monuments, museums, and photography.13 It was also the subject
of two major judicial inquiries, one of which (the Widgery report of 1972)
exonerated the army of all blame, while the other (the Saville report of 2010)
acknowledged almost four decades later that its actions were “unjustified
and unjustifiable,” an admission of culpability that prefaced an official apol-
ogy on the part of the British prime minister. In short, Bloody Sunday has
become a central site of memory for the nationalist community across the
island of Ireland. The name stands for a singular, site-specific event with an
enormous local impact, but it is also a metonym for the long-term strug-
gle of the nationalist-Catholic minority in Northern Ireland and, within an
even larger framework, for the long-term anticolonial struggle in Ireland as
a whole.
Although it is usually within a national framework that the significance of
Bloody Sunday has been studied, the memory of this locally specific event
also traveled across national borders with the help of the news media and
the arts. By 2024 there were Wikipedia articles on the topic in no fewer
than forty-six languages.14 Thanks to mediated memory work, Bloody Sun-
day has become something of an international yardstick in interpretations
of state violence against protestors. In 2009, for example, Paul Greengrass’s
movie Bloody Sunday (2002) was screened as part of the bicentenary com-
memorations of an uprising in South Tyrol in 1809, presumably because
the Northern Irish story resonated with the memory of their own Bloody
Sunday: the “Blutsonntag” which took place in Bolsano in 1921 when Ital-
ian fascists killed German-speaking demonstrators.15 In a similar linking of
two mnemonic communities, U2 paid tribute to Iranian protesters during
their concert tour of 2009 by playing their song “Sunday, Bloody Sun-
day” while images from the ongoing demonstrations in Iran were projected
behind them. In short, the moniker “Bloody Sunday” does not only refer
to a specific set of events in Derry in 1972; it also operates “multidirec-
tionally” (Rothberg 2009) by providing a model for interpreting events
elsewhere.
So why did the 1972 “Bloody Sunday” become such a powerful and gen-
erative site of memory? Within the context of Northern Ireland, there is no
doubt that the intense mnemonic investment in Bloody Sunday was fueled
by the official denial of what had happened in the form of the hastily pro-
duced Widgery report. Published just three months after the atrocity, it

MEDIATIONS OF OUTRAGE 91
concluded that the use of force was justified because some of the demon-
strators had fired shots. This blatant denial of what eyewitnesses had seen,
and others had witnessed on television, meant that calls for redress were
amplified by the desire to “undo” Widgery and correct its distortions (the
commissioning of a new report was accordingly a key part of the Good
Friday Agreement of 1998). This compounded injustice was certainly a con-
tributing factor in the event’s memorability. However, it cannot explain fully
why Bloody Sunday should have gained national and international salience
compared to the memory of other innocent civilians killed by the British
Army. Take for example, the drive-by killing of eleven civilians in Belfast
over a three-day period in 1971; this later became known as the Ballymur-
phy massacre, but it only belatedly emerged as a site of memory and the
subject of documentaries.16 The key difference in the memorability of the
two atrocities is that Bloody Sunday answered to a type and resonated with
the memory of earlier outrage.
“Sharpeville, Amritsar, and Bloody Sunday 1920—The parallels are
inadequate”—within twenty-four hours of the killings an editorial in the
Dublin-based Irish Times (January 31, 1972) was inscribing the events in
this lengthy line of civic massacres (while also recognizing obscurely that
the parallels were “inadequate”). This sense of déjà vu was echoed a week
later in a poem called “Elegy for Bloody Sunday, Derry 1972” published
in the Ulster Herald, which referred to the “vivid bloodgash memory/of
murder in the streets/Sharpeville, Amritsar, Bloody Sunday, Derry 1972.”17
Although naming a massacre after the place where it occurred was clearly an
option at one point (“Derry 1972”), a consensus emerged quite quickly on
a different moniker. The name “Bloody Sunday” indeed stuck so firmly that
the findings of the official Saville inquiry were titled “Report of the Bloody
Sunday Inquiry” (2010); and when Prime Minister Cameron subsequently
offered his official apology for these events, he explicitly referenced “the
tragic events of 30 January 1972, a day more commonly known as Bloody
Sunday.”18
In fact, Bloody Sunday was both horribly unique and one in a series. As
Eamonn McCann, one of the civil rights leaders present at Bloody Sunday
and subsequently a tireless campaigner for historical accountability, cap-
tured this paradox: “We have had our Bloody Sunday. It has not been the
first Bloody Sunday. It may not be the last” (1972: [2]). It would appear that
for participants and witnesses, the memory of Bloody Sunday did not actu-
ally begin in 1972, but much earlier. Multiple other “Bloody Sundays” and

92 REMEMBERING HOPE
comparable civic massacres “premediated” (Erll 2009) the events of 1972. It
was the most recent instantiation of an event-type that had developed over
the course of at least a century: an outrageous assault by police on peaceful
demonstrators. Contemporaries had already drawn comparisons between
the outrage of 1887 and “Peterloo” some sixty years earlier.19
Beginning with the so-called Massacre of the Champ de Mars in Paris
in 1791, the civic outrage presents as a specifically modern event-type in
which serious violence is inflicted on peaceful protesters (although some of
the cases mentioned here did not lead to fatalities, they all involved physical
injury). This event-type is modern, linked to the conditions of city living
and to the availability of squares and parks in which citizens assemble to
air their hopes and grievances. It is also above all modern in being linked
to a culture of democracy where the workings of the state are expected to
represent the will of the nation and citizens have the right to demonstrate
(Tilly & Tarrow 2015). Because of this fundamental link between contention
and democratic values, a police attack on peaceful demonstrators is always
outrageous.
Table 3.1 situates events called “Bloody Sunday” within a broader con-
stellation of related memory sites.20 While the causes associated with these

that originally in English or in translation. The central column lists outrages that are
primarily known by the location in which they occurred, but are also regularly referred

here follows the English-language Wikipedia.

MEDIATIONS OF OUTRAGE 93
events alternate between workers’ rights, civil rights, and the right to
national self-determination among others, the central opposition between
active citizens and state brutality remains a constitutive feature. Currently,
“Bloody Sunday” has become so powerfully associated with the events in
Derry in 1972 that the latter has become the prototypical “Bloody Sunday,”
retrospectively transforming all precedents into marked variants: Belfast’s
Bloody Sunday in 1920, Vancouver’s Bloody Sunday in 1938, Selma’s Bloody
Sunday in 1965.21
As these names indicate, but also the phrasing in the statement
“Sharpeville, Amritsar, and Bloody Sunday 1920—The parallels are inad-
equate,” quoted above, the memory of one outrage resonates with earlier
ones. The affective and symbolic impact of one event, as known through the
media, is picked up at another time and another place in such a way as to gen-
erate links between actors at various locations and periods in time. Within
this broader framework of “Bloody Sundays,” some resonances are stronger
than others, especially ones that reflect the entanglement in discourse and
activist networks, already in evidence in 1887, between internationalist
socialism, anti-colonialism, and particular nationalisms.
As mentioned at the beginning of this chapter, the first Bloody Sunday
took place at Trafalgar Square on Sunday, November 13, 1887. On that
occasion some 10,000 people—representing a coalition of various workers’
associations, socialist intellectuals like William Morris and George Bernard
Shaw, and activists involved in the movement for Irish independence—had
marched together to protest against a recent coercion bill restricting the cit-
izen’s right to protest. In line with that policy, the marchers’ approach to
Trafalgar Square was brutally halted by the police, leaving some seventy-five
persons severely injured.
The police violence provoked a follow-up demonstration a week later,
which was also subject to police attack, as a result of which three protesters
lost their lives. A huge funeral procession for one of the dead men took place
on December 18, accompanied by banners and speeches denouncing “the
scandalous brutality” of the police.22 The government and their supporters
justified this “scandalous brutality” as necessary to restore law and order in
the face of an illegal assembly.23 For the demonstrators, in contrast, it was
an orderly exercise of civil liberties that had been broken up by an out-of-
line police bent on suppressing those rights. The latter view was reflected
in the cover design for a commemorative publication produced a month
later which showed a prostate worker, attacked by the police but defended

94 REMEMBERING HOPE
Figure 3.1 Cover design by Walter Crane for William Morris’s poem “A Death
Song,” sold for the benefit of the widow of Arthur Linnell, a demonstrator
killed by police in Trafalgar Square on November 20, 1887. Public domain.
by erect and dignified emblems of justice and liberty (Figure 3.1). These
clashing renditions of the event reflect the ongoing struggle to define the
border between unlawful violence and legitimate protest.

MEDIATIONS OF OUTRAGE 95
The name Bloody Sunday first cropped up in a poster denouncing the
violence posted the next day.24 Within a couple of days the Pall Mall Gazette
was writing about the event that “the working men of London are begin-
ning to call ‘Bloody Sunday.’”25 A couple of years later, another commentator
referred to that day “now known in the melodramatic parlance of vision-
ary politicians as ‘Bloody Sunday.’”26 It was under this name that the first
anniversary of the killing of the demonstrators would be commemorated
in November 1888. Although this name emanated from the activists rather
than their critics, it succeeded in becoming a common point of reference
across the board. Clearly the combination of “Bloody” and “Sunday” spoke
to people. So much so that the same name was subsequently applied to other
cases even after the memory of Trafalgar Square had been overlaid by other
outrages.
By the time Derry’s Bloody Sunday took place in 1972, many other Bloody
Sundays had taken place in the interval. To begin with, the one in St.
Petersburg in 1905, when unarmed demonstrators marching to present a
petition to Tsar Nicholas II were fired upon by soldiers of the Imperial
Guard. Estimates of the number of fatalities have varied enormously, but
the consensus among historians would now seem to put the figure at around
1,000 dead. Whatever its exact origin, the name “Bloody Sunday” (in Rus-
sian: Krovavoye Voskresenye) became the standard moniker. Crucially, it
was the term used by Vladimir Lenin when he gave a lecture in Zurich
in January 1917 commemorating the 1905 Revolution as well as anticipat-
ing the Revolution-to-come: “Today is the twelfth anniversary of ‘Bloody
Sunday,’ which is rightly regarded as the beginning of the Russian revolu-
tion.”27 In the English-speaking press the event also become widely known
as St. Petersburg’s Bloody Sunday, a nomenclature that invited retrospec-
tive linking to the much less bloody violence in Trafalgar Square still in
living memory. The Irish journalist E.J. Dillon, a Russian-based reporter for
various British and American newspapers, claimed the coinage.28 Its sub-
sequent appropriation into Russian would surely have been facilitated by
Trafalgar Square’s being known in Russia through the international social-
ist movement.29 In January 1906, reflecting this internationalism, the first
anniversary of the St. Petersburg massacre was commemorated by Toronto
socialists as part of the “international celebration, held world-wide, of the
first anniversary of ‘Bloody Sunday,’ the eventful day when hundreds of
Russian workmen were slaughtered by the Cossacks of capitalism in St.
Petersburg.”30

96 REMEMBERING HOPE
The next significant Bloody Sunday, and the one most immediately at play
in Irish responses in 1972 was the Dublin Bloody Sunday of November 21,
1920. This took place at the height of the Irish war of independence, and the
naming of the violence on that day has had a complicated history because
it actually involved two sets of killings: in the morning, the assassination
of twelve suspected British spies by Irish independence fighters; in the after-
noon, the reprisal killing by the British army of fourteen civilians attending a
Gaelic football match at Croke Park (since 1884 the symbolic center of Irish
national sports). It would appear that the term “Bloody Sunday” was used
initially in the pro-British press, in an ironic appropriation of the Trafalgar
Square prototype, with reference to the assassination of suspected English
spies.31 The Irish Freeman’s Journal reported the next day (November 22,
1922) on “Dublin’s Bloody Sunday” with reference totheday as awhole,but
with particular emphasis on the afternoon. And in the long term it is the
afternoon Bloody Sunday which has dominated in Irish national memory,
in the process overwriting the memory of the assassinated British agents—
witness several references to it in the initial newspaper reports on the events
in Derry.32 The Bloody Sunday of 1920 retrospectively also upstaged and
occluded the memory of the violent suppression of strikers in 1913 which
had initially been seen, in an echo both of Trafalgar Square and, possibly, St.
Petersburg, as Ireland’s “first Bloody Sunday.”33
The accumulated memory of the earlier Bloody Sundays—Trafalgar
Square, St. Petersburg, and the 1913 strike—presumably all played into the
emergence of “Bloody Sunday” as the name par excellence for the 1920
Croke Park massacre.34 This meant self-consciously inscribing the killings
in a tradition that pitted civil liberties against repression, innocent citi-
zens against state violence. This schematization also made it resonate with
the recent memory of the Amritsar massacre of 1919 (the killing of hun-
dreds of Indian demonstrators by British soldiers)—yesterday “Croke Park
was turned into Amritzar” (sic), wrote the Freeman’s Journal. The idea that
one place (“Croke Park”) could be transformed into another place thou-
sands of miles away (“Amritsar”) indicates the thickening of both space and
time which the idea of a “Bloody Sunday” could bring into operation. As
an event-type Bloody Sunday operated multidirectionally by bringing out
analogies between hitherto unconnected histories. Amritsar, Dublin, Trafal-
gar Square, St. Petersburg: these became part of a multisited, but shared
experience of (colonial, class) repression that was all the more outrageous
precisely because it involved state forces attacking unarmed citizens. As they

MEDIATIONS OF OUTRAGE 97
optimistically exercised their rights, the state abused its monopoly on vio-
lence. At first sight, Bloody Sunday 1920 might seem to deviate from type
since it took place during a football match rather than a street protest, but
since the playing of Gaelic sports was linked at the time to assertions of
independence, the question of rights played a role here too.
In the interval between 1920 and 1972, another high-profile “Bloody Sun-
day” occurred during the civil rights movement in the United States. On
a march from Selma to Montgomery on Sunday March 7, 1965, a crowd
of some six hundred peaceful demonstrators, defending their right to vote,
were met at the Edmund Pettus Bridge by a phalanx of state troopers who,
after a brief standoff, proceeded to engage in a brutal assault on the crowd.
This led to multiple serious injuries, including to leader John Lewis, who
ended up with a cracked skull. The fact that the brutality was captured on
video and almost immediately broadcast on television networks was cru-
cial to its national impact.35 Lewis later described the assault in graphic
detail and summed up the proceedings as “a face-off in the most vivid terms
between a dignified, composed, completely nonviolent multitude of silent
protestors and the truly malevolent force of a heavily armed, hateful bat-
talion of troopers” (1999: 344–345). Lewis subsequently went on to open
March (2013–2016), his three-volume graphic memoir of the civil rights
movement, with an account of the confrontation between protesters and
troopers at Selma, thereby consolidating its centrality as a site of memory
and metonym for the struggle at large. The violent face-off quickly became
known as “Bloody Sunday,” and this is still the most common moniker
though, after 1972, often specified as “Bloody Sunday, Selma.” It is not clear
when and why this particular moniker was adopted, but it seems safe to
assume that the memory of earlier Bloody Sundays had percolated into
US cultural memory through media reporting and the labor movement.36
One way or another, Lewis’s extensive eyewitness account (1999) shows
how “Selma” was experienced in terms of the familiar event-type in pre-
senting a face-off between dignified nonviolent protesters and state forces
which then turns into a brutal attack by the latter and the dispersal of the
crowd.
It is well known that the US civil rights movement was a major source of
inspiration for their counterparts in Northern Ireland (who were reported
to have been singing We Shall Overcome in the run up to the shootings
in Derry). There is every reason to assume a shared memory of “Selma.”

98 REMEMBERING HOPE
Nonetheless, it was the memory of the fourteen fatalities in Dublin on
Bloody Sunday, 1920 which offered a more obvious model than Selma for
the violence in Derry, 1972 given the number of fatalities, the long-term
prominence of 1920 in Irish nationalist memory, and the fact that a conflict
between Irish claimants to rights and the British army was at stake in both
cases. Although the model travelled widely, various filtering mechanisms
were at work (local traditions, ideological affiliations, dating), which affected
the multidirectionality of this memory and the subsequent alignment of
events it effected.
Much more could be said about all of these cases. The point here is simply
to flag the persistent return of the event-type “Bloody Sunday” as a schema
for capturing the memory of civic outrage. For those involved, the same
sort of outrage could seem to be happening over and again at different sites
in a way that amplified the injustice. As the next section shows, however,
there is more to the memorability of Bloody Sundays. It was no coincidence
that the journalist quoted earlier linked the name “Bloody Sunday” to “the
melodramatic parlance of visionary politicians.”
Melodramatic Reversals
Chapter 1 referred to Hayden White’s claim that the basis of narrativity
lies in a conflict between “desire” and the “law.” Going on this definition,
attacks on demonstrators are particularly memorable because, in pitting
aspirations against state violence, they exemplify in a very condensed way
the essence of a good story. More specifically, they do so while bringing
into play yet another schema: melodrama. In The Melodramatic Imagination
(1995 [1976]), Peter Brooks made a compelling case for viewing melodrama
as the aesthetic mode par excellence of modernity. He showed how melo-
drama emerged in the aftermath of the French Revolution out of conditions
of censorship in popular theaters where the use of words was limited and
dramatic gesture all the more important. Its cultural predominance thus
coincided with the growth of modern cities and of democratic political cul-
tures. Whether in the literal form of theater plays or in the more derived
form of “the melodramatic,” it permeated into cultural production at large.
According to Brooks and those like Linda Williams who have extended his
research into the twentieth century, melodrama was so successful because it

MEDIATIONS OF OUTRAGE 99
provided a much-needed imaginative resource for negotiating moral legibil-
ity in a rapidly changing and conflicted world. It offered a tool for “revealing
moral good in a world where virtue has become hard to read” (Williams
1998: 54).
Melodrama works through heightened emotionality, spectacular effects,
mood swings, and moral polarization. It makes the conflict between good
and evil, between villainy and innocence, sensationally visible in a secu-
lar form of revelation. At its center is a virtuous hero-victim; although they
do not prevail, their virtue will be affirmed and recognized, giving a moral
upbeat to the overall story. As Linda Williams has argued (2001: 30), this
moral recognition creates an emotively charged dialectic between pathos (in
the original meaning of “suffering”) and action (the affirmation of agency).
This dialectic explains why the aesthetics of melodrama has provided such
a valuable, and hence much recycled, resource for making moral sense both
in the private and in the political arena.
One of the recurrent narrative devices in melodrama, mentioned by
Brooks, is that of the “interrupted feast.” This is typically a moment of inno-
cent celebration which is radically interrupted by the forces of evil such
that, in a shocking reversal, those celebrating end up in danger. The melo-
dramatic force of such reversals can help explain the special importance
of Sundays in the canon of civic atrocities. That historically there should
have been so many demonstrations in European and American cities on a
Sunday is of course simply an offshoot of the fact that this was generally
when people had some free time. But it also made the violence perpetrated
by the forces of the state against law-abiding citizens exercising their rights
to demonstrate on their free day, traditionally also a holy day, all the more
outrageous.
As a form of melodrama, “Bloody Sunday” thus dramatizes and makes
manifest an overdetermined moral configuration where innocence is pit-
ted against culpability, right against might, citizenry against the state, hope
against its destruction. As a model for remembering, it combines both vic-
timhood and agency or, to use Williams’s terms, the dialectic of pathos and
action: on the one hand, people’s suffering at the hands of state forces; on
the other hand, their agency in seeking to achieve change through peaceful
protest. Bloody Sunday thus exposes in a condensed and dramatic way struc-
tural concerns about the actual power of citizens to exercise their rights in
a modern democracy. This melodramatic concentration means that Bloody

100 REMEMBERING HOPE
Figure 3.2 Trafalgar Square. The Graphic, November 19, 1887. Public domain.
Sundays have also lent themselves to capture in images that have been repro-
duced over and again. Because images demand a concentration on particular
moments they gravitate toward dramatic highpoints.
The persistence of Bloody Sunday as an event-type was certainly facil-
itated by the increasing role of images in news reporting since 1887.37
As a growing literature has shown, visibility is a key part of a protest’s
impact and modern contentious politics has evolved in close alliance
with visual technologies.38 Demonstrators need to be seen to be heard.
But the “space of appearance” (Arendt 1958) in which they strive for
visibility is structured by visual schemata that render certain actors
and certain moments more legible than others, as well as by “norms
of recognition” that are “hierarchical and exclusionary” (Mirzoeff 2017:
n.p.). The cultural afterlife of “Bloody Sunday” is accordingly inseparable
from its rich archive of drawings and photographs, most of it originat-
ing in journalism but now widely remediated in texts, video, and social
media.
The resonance between different Bloody Sundays is enhanced by echoes
between these images and their depictions of movement. The underlying
drama of assertive citizens turning into targets of state violence is performed
over and again in images that foreground three moments in particular.

First, there are images (Figures 3.2–3.5) depicting the tension-laden standoff
between the demonstrators and the police as they line up across an empty
space, a constellation that has had a tenacious afterlife in the visual memory
of other protests.39 Second, there are images (Figures 3.6 and 3.7) depicting
the outburst of police violence, expressed in a chaotic mingling of stand-
ing police and protesters thrust to the ground. Finally, there are images
(Figure 3.8) of demonstrators taking to their heels and running away from
the violence.
