Filled with drama, movement, and energy, these visual schemata bring
into play what Aby Warburg once called a Pathosformula: a visual constel-
lation rooted in cultural memory that, in capturing energy, also energizes

MEDIATIONS OF OUTRAGE 105
the viewer.40 What they encapsulate in each case is the conflict between ille-
gitimate force and legitimate demands. When placed in sequence they also
depict a narrative whereby the strength of the demonstrators is expressed,
denied, and reasserted again. The result is a paradoxical combination of
victimhood and agency, of powerlessness and empowerment which, in
exposing repression, also reasserts the righteousness of the cause.
To highlight the melodramatic character of Bloody Sunday as an event-
type is not to diminish the gravity of historical injustice. It is instead to
understand better the nature of cultural representation: specifically, the
enabling role of the cultural and aesthetic repertoire in capturing outrage,
making it memorable, and communicating that memory in an affective and
mobilizing way. Representing outrage also helps in re-capturing the causes
to which the murdered citizens were committed and the momentum of the
movements behind them. The stickiness of the iconic images—the fact that
they remain in circulation long after they are no longer news—bears witness
to that power.
The following section turns to another schema for remembering violence
against demonstrators: martyrdom. Where the roots of “Bloody Sunday”
are firmly located in the period since the French Revolution, martyrdom
has much deeper historical roots. Where Bloody Sunday captures violence
directed at a crowd, martyrdom captures the suffering of individuals. In both
cases “the affective intensity connected to death is transferred to another
object: civic commitment” (Jerne 2017: 12).
Civic Martyrdom in the Present
“Being killed is an event. Martyrdom is a literary form, a genre,” anthropolo-
gist Daniel Boyarin once wrote (1999: 116). By “genre” he meant a particular
way of making sense of unnatural deaths, which has deep historical roots
in Christianity, Judaism, and Islam.41 The commemoration of martyrs was
a hugely important feature in the annual Christian calendar, briefly men-
tioned in the previous chapter, which was then recycled as a model in
the secular commemorations of activists who had suffered at the hands of
authorities.42 As Jolyon Mitchell’s historical survey indicates, martyrdom has
functioned across different media ecologies and cultural contexts as a “site
of resistance” (2012: 30).

106 REMEMBERING HOPE
The Chicago “martyrs,” discussed in Chapter 1, were following in the
footsteps of the Tolpuddle Martyrs (agricultural laborers from Dorset trans-
ported to Australia for their involvement in a labor dispute in 1834), the
Peterloo martyrs, and the Manchester martyrs (three Irish nationalists
hanged for the murder of a policeman in 1867), to list some prominent
examples from the British Isles. Martyrdom—more specifically, civic
martyrdom—has been deeply embedded in oppositional cultures since the
nineteenth century.43 It was often linked, as in the Chicago case, to exe-
cutions following legal procedures that provided a theater for dramatic
speeches from the dock and apodictic last words. Recent examples of civic
martyrdom in judicial contexts include the execution of the Turkish stu-
dent leader Deniz Gezmi¸s in 1972 and, more internationally famous, of the
guerillero Che Guevara in 1967.44 The concern of this chapter will be with
those who became martyrs in the context of a nonviolent protest rather than
in custody. Down to the present day, martyrdom has continued to be an
important model for sense-making and, as such, a “site of resistance” to the
killing of demonstrators.
Like a “Bloody Sunday,” a martyrdom has a high degree of memorabil-
ity since it is easily turned into a compact “figure of memory” (Assmann
1997 [1992]: 37–48), capturing large-scale developments in an individu-
alized human form. In its most general definition, martyrdom entails the
suffering, usually death, of an individual as the penalty for bearing witness
to a set of beliefs. Running through all instances of martyrdom is the idea
of a free agent who, in undergoing suffering, is steadfast in bearing witness
to some principle. The name “martyr” does not itself have to be used for
the schema to be operative, as long as the martyrdom frame structures the
interpretation of a person’s death or extreme suffering (as in deportation or
long-term imprisonment).45
Etymologically, the term “martyr” goes back to the Greek mártys
(μάρτυς), meaning “witness”; the related terms in Arabic (shahīd) and Turk-
ish (¸sehit) also have their roots in the idea of acting as a witness.46 Witnessing
means in the first instance that the martyr’s death conveys commitment
to a principle. But it also assumes that someone else bears witness to that
death and acknowledges the value of the principles at stake. Seen in terms
of this double act of witnessing, martyrdom is both relational (implying a
secondary witness) and transactional (in that the martyr’s death becomes a
moral debt to be redeemed by the secondary witness). The latter makes it a
powerful transmitter of memory, especially of promises and commitments.

MEDIATIONS OF OUTRAGE 107
A mnemonic cult in the form of relics, rituals, feast-days and, above all,
icons recalling the dead person is accordingly a key element of martyrdom.
The publication cult around the Chicago Martyrs, mentioned in Chapter 1,
illustrates the mechanism. As a schema for remembrance, martyrdom thus
converts extreme vulnerability—as expressed in a dead body—into a source
of renewed moral energy and commitment to a cause. As such it provides
another variation on the dialectic between pathos and action mentioned
earlier in relation to Bloody Sunday.
Martyrdom—as practice, and as idea—has been recognized in a range
of contexts, including cases where death or suffering, bearing witness to a
cause, is self-inflicted. Banu Bargu has coined the term “necroresistance”
to describe the “weaponization of life” through self-immolation, hunger-
striking, or other forms of self-inflicted harm that “wrench[es] the power
of life and death away from the apparatuses of the modern state” (2016: 27).
Necroresistance, she writes, is one of the few forms of political expression
left to actors in an “asymmetrical conflict” (23).47 The shocking death of
Czech student Jan Palach, who set himself on fire in the center of Prague
in January 1969, offers a case in point. Recalling his violent demise became
a way of bearing witness to his refusal to give up the struggle against the
Soviet domination of his country and of acknowledging over and over
again the legitimacy of resisting tyranny. The memorability of Palach’s self-
immolation was from the outset enhanced by its resonance with the national
memory of the martyred Jan Hus, who had been burned at the stake for
his heretical religious beliefs in 1415 and by its resonance with the iconic
self-immolation of the Vietnamese monk Thích Quảng Ðức in 1963.48 More
recently, the memory of the self-immolation of the twenty-six-year-old street
vendor Mohamed Bouazizi, widely interpreted as an act of protest, born
of despair, against the willful destruction by the police of his fruit cart
and hence his livelihood, became an important catalyst in the Tunisian
revolution.49
Civic martyrdom, as I will explore it further here, is the result of state-
inflicted than of self-inflicted harm. Since the scale of the phenomenon
exceeds the scope of a single chapter, I will proceed with reference to selected
cases. These serve to illustrate some commonalities in the remembrance of
martyrs and to flag context-dependent variations. Assuming that martyr-
dom as such is always a product of mediation, I examine the discourse and
the images used in recalling victims of state violence to show how death and
suffering are turned into sites of resistance.

108 REMEMBERING HOPE
Arthur Linnell, killed during one of the protests following London’s
Bloody Sunday, was called the first “political martyr since Peterloo” (Cozens
2018: 45). He was brought to his grave in a huge funeral procession with all
the hallmarks of veneration. In one of the many speeches at the graveside,
William Morris noted that the dead man had hitherto led an obscure life as
a worker, but that, in dying, he “had become a person to be remembered.”
Moreover, Morris went on, the public should remember that if “society had
been differently constituted from what it now was, that man’s life might have
been a delightful, a beautiful one, and a happy one to him.”50 In other words,
to remember Linnell was to recall the violence of his death but also the life
that, in a more just society, he might have led. This is memory in the sub-
junctive mode. In highlighting the life that “might have been,” Morris turned
mourning into affirmation, by asserting the value of the life and the poten-
tial that had been lost. He also turned commemoration into contention,
by pointing not just to the moment of Linnell’s death but to the systemic
obstacles that had prevented his flourishing.
The next example also relates to the death of a worker: Louis Tayenne, a
twenty-year-old striking miner killed by police in 1932, at the height of the
Great Depression, in the Belgian town of Roux. The place of Tayenne’s death
was marked by graffiti; his gravestone (erected through public subscription)
noted that he had died “defending the working classes”; the first anniver-
sary of his death was the occasion for a huge demonstration organized by
the Communist Party, which was filmed and turned into a documentary
called Manifestation pour Tayenne (Fonteyne & Van Ommeslaghe 1933).
This black-and-white silent film begins at the spot where Tayenne was killed,
before showing a crowd waving flags and carrying banners (one of which
says, “we venerate Tayenne”) as they walk slowly by the dead man’s house,
through the town and on to the graveyard where they form a guard of honor
(Figure 3.9). It is clear from the many clenched fists raised in salute and the
impassioned speeches (appearing all the more impassioned because they are
soundless) that the occasion was used not just to commemorate the dead
man, and to venerate his memory as a martyr, but also to continue the fight
for workers’ rights. Memory and mobilization fed into each other; the public
commemoration acknowledged the fact of state violence while also turn-
ing that violence back on itself to produce a new display of solidarity. In its
collective and organized character, this cult of Tayenne exemplified a long
tradition of socialist-communist martyrology focusing on workers in action
against the forces of capitalism.51 As we will see in Chapter 4, martyrdom has

also been an important feature of communist and socialist commemorations
of the Commune.
The list of demonstrators more recently killed in action and remembered
in the mode of martyrdom is too long to be treated exhaustively. Like Arthur
Linnell, the first “political martyr since Peterloo,” who was deprived of a
good life, and like Mahsa Amini, whose killing was discussed in Chapter 2,
most of these young people only entered into public visibility at the point of
their death: Luigi Trastulli, a twenty-one-year-old Italian steelworker killed
in March 1949 by police on the occasion of a rally against the signing of
the North Atlantic Treaty by the Italian government;52 Benno Ohnesorg, a
student killed by police during a demonstration in West Berlin in 1967;53
Francesco Lorusso, a twenty-five-year-old student member of a militant
left-wing organization, who was killed by police during a demonstration in
Bologna in 1977;54 Carlo Giuliani, a twenty-three-year-old shot dead during
clashes with the police at the G8 summit in Genoa in 2001;55 Neda Agha-
Soltan, a student shot dead on the occasion of a demonstration in Teheran
in 2009;56 Pouya Bakhtiari, a twenty-seven-year-old electrical engineer who

110 REMEMBERING HOPE
was shot in the head while protesting in Karaj, Iran, in December 2019;57
and the many protesters killed during demonstrations in Cairo in 2013,
some of them wearing a headband self-identifying as potential martyrs, not
seeking death but rather indicating they had the courage to die if a repressive
regime made this necessary.58
The list can be extended to victims who were not themselves activists, but
whose unlawful killing by the state became the rallying point for demon-
strations demanding justice and changes to the underlying system that made
such injustice possible.59 This would include Emmett Till, the African Amer-
ican teenager murdered in 1955, whose brutalized body fed into the civil
rights movement through the many reproductions of his image;60 Khaled
Said, the young man who was beaten to death by police in Alexandria, Egypt,
in 2011, and whose memory is believed to have played an important role
as a “visual injustice symbol” (Olesen 2013) in the run-up to the Egyptian
revolution; and most recently, George Floyd, whose brutal killing by police
in May 2020 became the central reference point in an international wave
of Black Lives Matter protests against systemic racism. In all these cases,
extreme vulnerability became transformed into action on the part of wit-
nesses, whose memory work turned their deaths into vectors of a larger
struggle.
Witnessing: The Face of Opposition
The memorability of civic martyrdom is reflected in the intensity and
frequency with which these lost lives are recalled. Remediations of their
story—in songs and poetry, makeshift memorials, graffiti, but most impor-
tantly, in reproductions of their portraits—turn their memory into a site
of resistance.61 Individual victims give a face—in this case, literally so—
to the memory of protest, making it concrete, condensed, portable, and as
a result, easy to recall at other moments and other locations. The visual
activism relating to Neda Agha-Soltan’s death offers a case in point: the
display of her image played a key role in mobilizing reaction to her death
and hence prolonging criticism of the regime.62 Remediated over and over
again in the form of stencils, graffiti, and posters, the visual and material
presence of portraits turns the memory of the dead into an active force in
the cityscape. The widespread activist practice of remediating photographic
portraits into stenciled drawings, which are more easily reproducible than

collective icons by rendering the images more abstract.63 The faces of the
“martyrs” of the Egyptian Revolution were painted, for example, on a wall
opposite the American University in Cairo, giving them a material presence
in the ongoing demonstrations apparently so powerful that the authorities
felt compelled to efface them. The portraits “carried weight as embodied
evidence of state violence, as tools for holding the state accountable, as mobi-
lizers of continuous protests, and as a source of inspiration” (Mittermaier
2015: 586). Reflecting this significance, images of dead demonstrators are
regularly displayed side by side to form a transnational canon of mutually
resonating cases.
The Greek student Alexis Grigoropoulos, killed in Athens in 2008, figures
on banners, murals, and online side-by-side with Gezi “martyr” Berkin
Elvan (Figure 3.10 provides an example). Elsewhere, Grigoropoulos has fig-
ured side by side with Lorusso and with Ian Tomlinson, killed by a blow
from a police officer at the G20 summit in London in 2009.64 While some
of these images originated in photojournalism, they are now circulated and
remediated in other arenas, both in the street and online.

When carried in the hands of demonstrators (Figure 3.11), the display
of portraits expresses an embodied commitment to remembering the dead
person and to redeeming the debt they have left behind by opposing the
root source of their killing. Moreover, “the spectacle of death” (Askanius
2013) in the case of civic martyrdom is paradoxically connected to the idea
that the dead person is actually still “alive.” When images of the “Gezi Park
martyrs” were displayed as the protests continued in Istanbul in 2013, they
were regularly accompanied by shouts of “They are among us” or the written
phrase “He is among us.”65 Similarly, “Francesco is alive and fights along
with us,” was used in the commemoration of Lorusso; while “Carlo Vive”
(“Carlo is alive”; Figure 3.12) is a slogan associated with displays online and
on location of images of Carlo Giuliani.66 As Amira Mittermaier points out
(2015), the spectacle of death that is martyrdom is paradoxically more about
the meaning of life, and what it is to lose or live a life, than it is about death as
such. This echoes William Morris’s lament for the dead worker’s life “which
might have been a delightful, a beautiful one, and a happy one to him.”
The power of the portraits to evoke life is enhanced by the fact that the
images shown are usually based on photographs taken when their subjects

MEDIATIONS OF OUTRAGE 113
Figure 3.12 Restored Mural for Carlo Giuliani in Casetta Rossa, Rome,
October 16, 2016. Photograph: MatteoxNardone; Imago/Pacific Press Agency.
Image Alamy.
were flourishing. Photographs of fatally wounded bodies do circulate, as in
the case of Emmet Till and the iconic photograph by Jeffrey Miller of the
Kent State University shootings in 1970.67 But what circulates above all are
images based on snapshots of the victims when the latter were very much
alive and often smiling; this gives them the quality of “dialectic images”
(Traverso 2021: 26), which simultaneously evoke life in its fullness and the
imminence of their untimely death. The result is a highly visible and, once
again, melodramatic contrast between right and might such as we have seen
earlier in relation to Bloody Sunday. The brutal conversion of life into death
is often enhanced by the heartrending contrast between the youth of the vic-
tims and the brutal foreclosing of their lives. The youthfulness of their faces
is an ever-renewed reminder of the unnaturalness of their deaths and the
meaning of life itself; and more specifically, of the loss of “what might have
been” in the lives of these young people. This too is memory in the subjunc-
tive mode; of fatal moments when history could—and should—have gone
another way. Barbie Zelizer has aptly referred to the “subjunctive voice” of
images, which couches what is depicted in an interpretive scheme of “what
could be” rather than what was (2010: 163). In this case, the “subjunctive

114 REMEMBERING HOPE
voice” of the images is used in activist memory work where reclaiming the
right to life and reaffirming the values of a good life are deeply entangled
with denouncing unlawful violence.
Displaying images of soon-to-be-dead youth is an act of nonviolent resis-
tance that deflects state violence into a defense of life and hence into renewed
grounds for continuing the fight. “Nonviolence is less a failure of action
than a physical assertion of the claims of life,” as Judith Butler has written;
it uses strategies “to recast the living as worthy of value, as potentially griev-
able, precisely under conditions in which they are either erased from view
or cast into irreversible forms of precarity” (2020: 24). Remembering the
dead thus gains a prospective meaning in the continued need to struggle for
rights.
The Stories That Get Away
This chapter argued that outrage is the best framework within which to
approach the memory of violence against demonstrators. We have seen how
that violence is captured and hence rendered memorable using schemata
such as “Bloody Sunday” and civic martyrdom. In the process, violent
assaults on demonstrators become caught up in a dynamic of action and
reaction whereby memory becomes a mobilizing force and a renewed act
of nonviolent resistance in which the opponent’s force is used against them.
The cases show that, although violence creates victims, its victims are not
necessarily remembered as such. Remembrance can rebound into renewed
action, based not only on outrage but also on a resilient hope for a world
in which (young) life can flourish. In this resistant form of remembrance,
recollection serves to counter police power by reaffirming the value of life.
Indeed, resistant memory work may be typical of oppositional movements
as they leverage their vulnerability vis-à-vis the state.
However, not all events of police violence lend themselves to capture in
the form of a dramatic reversal on the lines of a Bloody Sunday or in the
iconic figure of a martyr. The gravitation toward these schemata means that
“slower” or less condensed forms of violence against demonstrators may fail
to register as collectively memorable—fail to reach the headlines or to be
remediated. This is not because they are not painful and terrifying for every-
one involved, but because there is no language for talking about them (and,
in cases of extreme repression, no platform for doing so). In revisiting the

MEDIATIONS OF OUTRAGE 115
history of Bloody Sundays, I was struck by their huge symbolic resonance
but also by their small scale: one episode, one day, and a limited number
of casualties. When police repression is spread out over a longer period of
contention, and the outbursts of violence against demonstrators is ongoing
rather than concentrated, other models of sense-making are needed but are
not necessarily available.
There have been no more “Bloody Sundays” since Selma and Derry, the
schema reaching both a high point and an end point in the civil rights era.
For police brutality to be “outrageous,” the default must be the right to peace-
ful protest. When that right is eroded, as is currently occurring in Europe
and the United States among other places, or when police brutality becomes
routine as it does in regimes where democracy is aspirational at best, vio-
lence against demonstrators is no longer representable within the traditional
melodramatic framework of a “Bloody Sunday.” In such cases, martyrdom
offers the best hook for capturing state violence by individualizing it—Mahsa
Amini or Mina Daniel, the citizen-journalist killed in Cairo in 2011, offer
cases in point. These individual deaths give a powerful face to the larger
protest in which they were involved, albeit at the cost of upstaging other
victims who, despite being unlawfully killed, never become “a person to be
remembered,” to recall Morris’s words.68
But the logic of martyrdom with its intense focus on individuals and the
investment in their memory is also at odds with relentless oppression at
scale. Between 600 and 3,000 citizens were killed on one day in the Rabaa
massacre in Cairo on August 14, 2013, and between 350 and 1,500 Iranian
protestors over a four-day period in November 2019.69 According to what
schemata can such awful violence be remembered, now and in the future?
Mindful of this, Amira Mittermaier finishes her account of “Death and Mar-
tyrdom in the Arab Uprisings” (2015) by admitting to being haunted by the
idea that all these deaths, if they are not redeemed in future developments,
may prove in the end to have been “utterly meaningless” (604). This somber
conclusion highlights the challenge of making sense of violence. As a “prac-
tice of hope,” to recall David Henig’s term (2022), resistant remembrance
operates against the odds.

Chapter 4
The Agency of the Aesthetic
Keeping the Commune Alive
“Vive la Commune” in Nuit Debout (2016)
In March 2016, protests in France against proposed labor reforms morphed
into more general calls for a renewal of democracy in a countrywide move-
ment that came to be known as Nuit Debout. The epicenter was the Place
de la République in Paris, where general assemblies were held every evening
until well into the month of June.1 In addition, multiple smaller committees
set about specific tasks, including the provision of an alternative news ser-
vice to counteract the influence of mainstream media, the organization of
more daily aspects of community life (food supplies, a communal library,
concerts, and educational events), and the making of a movement archive.2
These activities have been seen as a way of “reinventing democracy” (Guion-
net & Wievorka 2021) by bringing a transformed society into being through
concrete initiatives impacting, however briefly, the experience of everyday
life in the here and now. Like the other Occupy movements that spread
across the world in the 2010s, Nuit Debout practiced in this way a politics
of prefiguration (more on this in Chapter 6).
Nuit Debout was a singular event. It was also déjà vu. As both aca-
demic observers and participants noted, it implicitly and explicitly recalled
the occupation of other squares across the world in the preceding five
years: in New York, Madrid, Cairo, Athens, and Istanbul, to name the most
prominent.3 It was “the Occupy movement in Paris” (Gensburger 2019:
21). An awareness of predecessors was explicitly voiced in Nuit Debout’s
self-representations as well as performed in the repertoire of the nightly
assemblies. The use in particular of hand signaling was both a tactic and
a tribute to the experiments in direct democracy in the other “movements
of the squares” (Gerbaudo 2014).
The temporal horizon of the movement’s memoryscape stretched beyond
recent events, however, and hence beyond living memory into a culturally
Remembering Hope. Ann Rigney, Oxford University Press. © Oxford University Press (2025).
DOI: 10.1093/oso/9780197789711.003.0005

THE AGENCY OF THE AESTHETIC 117
mediated one. In her chronicle of Paris during this period, Sarah Gensburger
noted that “in several different places on the square, different messages have
appeared referring to the Paris Commune” (2019: 139); and, later, that the
“Commune is omnipresent on the ground and on the walls of the square”
(140).4 The Place de la République was symbolically renamed Place de la
Commune, while “Vive la Commune” (Long Live the Commune) and ‘Que
revive la Commune’ (May the Commune Come Back to Life/Be Revived)
appeared in the form of handwritten graffiti on various walls (Figures 4.1
and 4.2).5
The language of these exhortations is “performative” (Austin 1962) in the
sense that it seeks not just to reproduce the present but also to transform
it by reconnecting with the Commune. Apparently, there was a resonance
between 2016 and the memory of 1871. The latter entered into “the fig-
uration of the present,” to use the words of Kristin Ross, as “a laboratory
of political invention, a kind of usable archive of legacy ideas and prac-
tices” (2021: 222) that helped in understanding the present and formulating
possible futures.6
Figure 4.1 Vive la Commune. Graffiti. Paris, April 12, 2016. Photograph:
Sarah Gensburger. Reproduced with kind permission.

118 REMEMBERING HOPE
Figure 4.2 Graffiti at entrance to Metro station. Paris, April 18, 2016.
Photograph: Sarah Gensburger. Reproduced with kind permission.
In another example of retro-prospective memory work, Nuit Debout drew
on the Commune as a source of inspiration while also looking forward to
new things. In the French context, turning to 1871 also meant turning away
from ’68, which had become associated with a toothless student revolt and
with the current Socialist government whose policies were seen as a betrayal
of the Left’s commitment to resolving social inequality.7 The Commune pro-
vided an alternative model for action (how to “occupy” a city and reinvent
social life) as well as a tradition of popular protest into which protesters of
2016 could inscribe themselves and, in doing so, enhance their own wor-
thiness and virtually increase their numbers. As we saw in Chapter 2, the
Commune was part of the transnational left-wing canon and as such it

THE AGENCY OF THE AESTHETIC 119
also figured in Occupy Wall St., Gezi Park, and in uprisings in Syria since
2011.8 But within the Parisian context the events of 1871 had additional
resonance.9
This raises the question: When activists invoked the “Commune” in 2016,
what was being remembered? In any case, something positive. So, how could
something that ended in such disaster still be perceived as something to
be revived? In my Introduction, I gave an initial answer to this question
in showing how the Commune was remembered in the 1880s in the coun-
terhistorical form of a party celebrating its hopeful beginnings rather than
dwelling on its atrociously violent end. Since something similar seems to be
going on in 2016, this chapter sets out to explore how Commune memory
evolved in the interval.
I shall argue that (1) the mobilizing force of invocations of the Commune
in 2016 was linked to their resonance with a long history of narrating the
events of 1871 in different media and cultural practices; (2) these narratives
were designed to redress the mnemonic injustice of initial attempts to erase
the Commune’s memory; and (3) they operated by overwriting the memory
of the Commune’s defeat by the memory of its achievements and its promise.
Finally (4), I conclude that the insistent emphasis on the “aliveness” of the
Commune exemplifies a distinct form of activist memory work: one that
resists historical outcomes in order to reconnect affectively with an earlier
moment of hope in radical transformation.
This develops further the point made in Chapter 3; namely, that cultural
schemata help in offering resistance to history by channeling outrage into
contention and hence refusing to accept the outcome as definitive. This
chapter emphasizes the power of mediation itself—the agency of words,
images, sounds, shapes—in providing pushback to the fact of defeat and in
reasserting “the claims of life,” to recall Butler’s phrase (2020: 24).
How can an event that objectively “failed” nevertheless continue to be
remembered as something to be “revived”? Even more intriguing, how was
it possible for an event that ended in slaughter to be associated so persis-
tently with life and the living? While “Long Live” is the standard phrase to
voice enthusiasm and is used for multiple causes, the variant “May the Com-
mune Come Back to Life” or “May the Commune be Revived” (Que revive
la Commune) adds a new dimension to the standard phrase. As something
that calls for revival, it is implicitly no longer alive without being definitively
dead. Two years after Nuit Debout, an association called “Faisons vivre la
Commune” [Let’s bring the Commune back to life] would be founded with

120 REMEMBERING HOPE
a similar mission to literally revive the Commune.10 This chapter traces the
curiously persistent association of the Commune with “life” and “vitality”
across the shifting frames within which it has been remembered.
Mediation: Shorthand and Longhand
Beginning as it does with slogans, this chapter draws attention to a hith-
erto undertheorized factor within the dynamics of memory production that
I briefly flagged in Chapter 3. Namely, the alternation between “longhand”
and “shorthand” versions of a story as content is recalled in new contexts.
Like monuments and monikers such as “the Commune,” slogans and graf-
fiti are metonymic reminders of extended narratives told elsewhere which
call on a tacit knowledge of events and their overall meaning. They mobilize
recollections that are presumed shared, while also allowing for productive
imprecision regarding what exactly is being recollected. Indeed, shorthand
recollection serves primarily to engage people’s sense of belonging to a
mnemonic community. Like flags or symbols, they have recognition value.
Longhand narratives, in contrast, may be so detailed as to offer a vir-
tual experience of events, hence turning the reader or viewer into a witness.
Whatever meaning the shorthand invocation of the Commune carried for
passers-by in 2016, this was shaped by decades of longhand and short-
hand recollection diffused across all sorts of practices and media: memoirs,
histories, films, documentaries, graphic novels, songs, graffiti, exhibitions,
political speeches, commemorative rituals. Where shorthand invocations
serve as reminders of a memory, longer narrations accumulatively produce
new interpretive frameworks over time. As this chapter shows, they do so in
a sensual form using color, sound, language, movement, and spatial com-
position to speak to readers and viewers. To draw on a distinction made by
Raymond Williams (1970: 38–39), where shorthand evocations are “signals”
that trigger recognition, longhand narratives are “signs” that in a sensuous
form open up new possibilities for meaning and hence for world-making.
The alternation between shorthand and longhand narratives, and the space
for ambivalence the regular use of shorthand affords, helps explain how the
memory of the Commune has not only survived but also, as the following
will show, been gradually adapted across time.
The Commune has had an intense cultural afterlife, including many works
that have been reproduced, translated, and remediated over and again to

THE AGENCY OF THE AESTHETIC 121
meet the needs of new publics. Later works are often based on the creative
recycling of earlier ones, sometimes through brief citation, sometimes in the
narrative design as a whole, or sometimes just in the use of red and black in
cover designs, which has become something of the Commune’s trademark.
Although the Communards were defeated militarily in 1871, they went on
to win the battle for memory if only for the simple reason that their oppo-
nents were ultimately less invested in keeping its legacy alive.11 In contrast,
sympathizers succeeded over time in producing and reproducing hundreds
of memoirs, histories, films, plays, novels, graphic novels, images, and songs,
not to mention Karl Marx’s Civil War in France (1871) and Prosper-Olivier
Lissagaray’s Histoire de la Commune de 1871 (1876), both of which were
very widely circulated and translated.12 Through this accumulated cultural
work Commune memory evolved as a resilient “counter” memory in a dou-
ble sense. First, it resisted hostile attempts to erase its traces, often with the
help of graphic eyewitness testimonies. Second, it was regularly mobilized
in the service of oppositional politics.13
It is impossible to deal here with every narrative of the Commune, not
to mention diffuse shorthand invocations spread across time and space. In
order to capture the complexity of this enormous corpus I take a two-track
approach, beginning with an outline of general trends before moving to spe-
cific practices of longhand narration in order to show how they reassert the
value of life.
Martyrs of the Commune: Seeing Red
In the first instance, the voices of the Communards were silenced in the
public arena, and they only gradually began to be heard after an amnesty
in 1880 which also allowed those in exile to return to France. Until World
War I, the memory of 1871 continued to be passed down by eyewitnesses
in the form of face-to-face contact within families and commemorative ban-
quets organized by veterans’ organizations. The latter included the “Friends
of the Commune,” founded in 1883 and still going strong in 2016. In parallel,
and of increasing importance with the passage of time, cultural carriers of
memory became responsible for creating stakeholders in the memory of the
Commune beyond the scope of those who had been directly involved.
As multiple studies have shown,14 the memory of the Commune was
shaped in the early years by its opponents—hence the notoriety given to

122 REMEMBERING HOPE
the participation of women, depicted as harridan-like pétroleuses, and to the
destruction wrought on the city of Paris.15 Memory work was in effect a con-
tinuation by other means of the military conflict between the insurgency
and the government. This was most evident in the careful reconstruction
of the Vendôme column that the Commune had so dramatically destroyed
(I return to this in Chapter 5). By 1874 the huge monument to Napoleon
was back in place and looking exactly as it had done before the rupture of
the Commune, as if the latter had never happened. By 1880, in a spirit of
reconciliation, Léon Gambetta, then president of the Chamber of Deputies,
claimed that it was time “to close the book” on the previous decade by grant-
ing amnesty to the Communards.16 This was an invitation to move forward
on the basis of a new Republican consensus in what was in effect a form of
“prescribed forgetting” (Connerton 2008) of the enduring fault lines within
the nation. This proved impossible.
Eugène Pottier’s assertion that “La Commune n’est pas morte” (“The
Commune is not dead”) in the eponymous title of the song he wrote in 1886
was a way of keeping that “spirit” alive among friends. The uprising may
have ended in violence, incarceration, and deportation on a vast scale, the
logic went, but that does not mean the end of the Commune. Its “spirit”
lives on in memory. Pottier’s assertion ex negativo shows that, from the out-
set, remembering the Commune was an act of resistance to attempts to deny
its significance and consign it to the past. The “aliveness” of the Commune
can thus be traced back to its immediate aftermath when the fierce fight-
ing mutated into a life/death struggle over its memory. Hence also Louise
Michel’s exhortation “Vive la commune morte!” (1886: 220).
Madeleine Rebérioux has shown in an authoritative study how different
political groups on the French left jostled for the legacy of the Commune
after 1880 and interpreted it within a range of party-political frameworks
(1997 [1984]: 545–546). Public commemorations of the event ended up
crystallizing around the “Communards’ Wall” (Mur des Fédérés) at the Père
Lachaise cemetery and around the anniversary of the Bloody Week in May
when thousands of Communards were executed. In the first half of the
twentieth century, the annual procession became a ritualized part of the
commemorative calendar of the Communist and Socialist Parties, its mean-
ing strongly linked to the story of a working-class martyrdom, which would
be redeemed at some future date by radical change effected via the ballot
box.17 The annual procession in May became the principal “site of mem-
ory” of the Commune and an annual rallying point for left-wing groups,

THE AGENCY OF THE AESTHETIC 123
even if people from different political parties didn’t always march together.
The annual procession, still a living tradition, reached its high point in
1936, when an estimated 600,000 people participated in what was both a
commemoration and a show of antifascist strength.18
In a book with the resonant title La Commune n’est pas morte (The
Commune Is Not Dead), Eric Fournier reports on occasional attempts to
integrate the memory of the Commune into mainstream Republican mem-
ory by presenting it as a continuation of 1789 (2013: 148). But it has figured
above all in oppositional memory work rather than in institutionalized
forms of “political memory” (Assmann 2006) supported by state institutions.
The first postwar procession in May 1945 coincided with the return of pris-
oners from the Nazi camps, and marked an exceptional broadening of the
groups involved in the commemoration. Those deported (be they part of the
Communist resistance or Jewish noncombatants—in 1945 this distinction
was not drawn) were seen as having shared a destiny with the Communards.
As Hélène Parmelin wrote of the occasion in her testimonial novel La montée
au mur ([The Ascent to the Wall] 1950):
Those who had been deported during the fascist war marched towards the
Communards’ Wall; Mauthausen, Buchenwald, Auschwitz became allied
to the martyred Communards, those who starved in Thiers’ camps, those
killed at Père Lachaise.19
Note the emphasis here on martyrdom. As discussed in the previous chapter,
a martyr’s death for a cause places a moral burden on those bearing witness
to it. Conjoining those martyred by fascism in the 1940s with those martyred
by the Thiers government in 1871 added an extra historical layer and hence
extra weight to that transgenerational debt.
Although the annual procession to the Communards’ Wall is predictable
and ritualized, it too enacts a type of resistant remembrance. In Chapter 3
we saw how martyrdom as a narrative schema was embedded in highly
visualized forms that, in their sensual presence, offered pushback to the
fact of the martyr’s death by reasserting the value of life. In the case of the
annual processions, the pushback lies in the size of the crowds and in the
preponderance of the color red, displayed in clothing, banners, and other
accessories. Figure 4.3 shows the procession in 2021 on the occasion of
the 150th anniversary, which also includes a banner proclaiming that the
Commune is alive.

124 REMEMBERING HOPE
Figure 4.3 Annual procession to the Mur des Fédérés, May 29, 2021.
Photograph: Abaca Press. Image Alamy.
The political symbolism of the color red is well known.20 Recent stud-
ies have also noted its affective force and linked it to a radical use of color
and sound to provide an affective and aesthetic dimension to political
mobilization. In the Introduction I referred to “sensational international-
ism” (Coghlan 2016) as a political-aesthetic strategy in the late nineteenth
century. The use of color, sound, and drama among activists marked the
extension of popular culture into the political arena—something we have
also noted in discussing the aesthetics of melodrama in Chapter 3. Com-
munication that extends beyond semantics into sensations and affect is not
limited to nineteenth-century contexts; similar points have been made about
the “political aesthetics” (Webb et al. 2014) of recent protests.
Michelle Coghlan has already noted the overwhelming presence of the
color red at meetings commemorating the Commune in the late nineteenth
century, in the form of banners and flags, but also in the sashes worn by key
figures like Louise Michel and Lucy Parsons, who thereby literally embod-
ied the spirit of the Commune (2016: 10, 100). The ubiquity of red at the
annual procession to the Père Lachaise cemetery perpetuates this practice,
providing an annually renewed show of strength through the use of color,
shapes, sounds, bodies in motion (something similar was in evidence in

THE AGENCY OF THE AESTHETIC 125
the Communist commemoration of the dead worker Tayenne described in
Chapter 3). The size of a crowd is not just significant as a measure of sup-
port, but also as a “sensational” presence in its own right that shapes the
affective charge of the event recollected. In later sections of this chapter, I
discuss the aesthetic dimensions of memory work in greater detail. But first,
I turn to the emergence of an alternative to the martyrdom model in the
1960s.
The Centenary in a Flea Market (1971)
Since the 1960s, the martyrdom schema has been losing its mobiliz-
ing power along with the political parties and the grand narrative of
social transformation with which it was entangled. Although there is some
debate about the exact timing, it is generally agreed that the Commune
was reframed in the early 1960s by a discourse emphasizing its utopian
dimensions.21 Where 1871 once exemplified the martyrdom of the work-
ing class, memorialized in the Communards’ Wall, the emphasis shifted
from its failure to its achievements. The Commune became the hopeful
prefiguration of a society transformed by revolution, direct democracy,
or a new experience of the everyday (to mention some of the causes to
which it has been linked). By now this positively inflected Commune
seems to have percolated through to the annual pilgrimage to the Père
Lachaise cemetery; in 2021 the Association des Amies et Amis de la Com-
mune de Paris 1871 announced it as a “festive rally” (“rassemblement
festif ”).22
Although the frame only changed slowly in the course of the 1960s, writ-
ings by the Situationist International stand out as symptoms of that change
and, given their cultural influence, perhaps also as its catalyst. They pro-
nounced the Commune to have been the “greatest party of the nineteenth
century” (Debord et al., 1969), an emphasis on the festive echoed a couple
of years later by the sociologist Henri Lefebvre, who called it the “last popular
party” (“la dernière fête populaire,” 1978). As already mentioned in my Intro-
duction, the Situationists acknowledged that the uprising was “objectively
doomed to failure,” but insisted nevertheless that it was a success by virtue
of the mere fact that it had come into existence. In rejecting the conventions
of “the classical novelist” for whom the end determines retrospectively the
significance of the beginning, the Situationists were also suggesting that a

126 REMEMBERING HOPE
different narrative model might yield a different memory and hence open
new perspectives on the future.
The Situationists were being provocative, but in fact they were actually
recalling an earlier tradition rather than breaking radically new ground.
They were looping back to a persistent albeit minority tradition in 1871
remembrance, already described in the Introduction, which highlighted the
beginnings of the uprising rather than its ending and celebrated its memory
in the mode of a party rather than that of a funeral. Consciously or not, they
were also recycling a discourse common among contemporary observers,
notably Jules Vallès, who had greeted the inauguration of the Commune in
the Cri du peuple as a festive moment that was both a new breakthrough and
(in yet another temporal twist) a return to the spirit of 1792. He had called
it “a party [fête] that was revolutionary and patriotic, peaceful and joyful”
(Vallès & Denis 1871, March 29). Note that the French term “fête” can mean
either a more or less spontaneous party or an organized public festival (as in
the Revolutionary fêtes of 1789–1799), but that for convenience sake I will
translate it here as “party” or use the adjectival form “festive.”23 Whatever
the term used, at stake is a view of the Commune as a source of pleasure or,
as Kristin Ross more recently put it, as the realization of “communal luxury”
through the reorganization of everyday life and the sharing of food (2015a).
Semiclandestine celebrations of March 18 had taken place in Paris from
the late 1870s on, in the form of communal meals and collective danc-
ing.24 We saw in the Introduction how a similar mode of commemoration
became widespread in radical circles outside France. On one such occa-
sion in London in March 1891, the exiled Louise Michel recalled the
Commune:
In 1871 the people did not understand us and called us bandits because
we demanded freedom and the means to freedom. […] The 18th of March
sounds through the world not for what was done, but for the ideas to which
it has given birth. We were happy then, so happy, because it was a lovely
thing to live our life and struggle. (CW 04/1891)
Note the emphasis on possibility in “the ideas to which it has given birth.”
Note, too, the constellation of positively coded terms, culminating in its
being “a lovely thing to live our life and struggle.” Terms like this have res-
onated since the late 1960s with a discourse associating the Commune with
celebrations of life and happiness.25

THE AGENCY OF THE AESTHETIC 127
This reframing of the Commune was not just a matter of remembering
different aspects of 1871, but also of remembering in a different way: by re-
enacting the past in the present rather than by historicizing it as a remote
event. This is what happened in the centenary commemorations of 1971.
If the Commune was in essence festive, and traditional narrative principles
no longer valid, then perhaps the most appropriate way to commemorate
its centenary would be to have a party? Hence the happening organized in
March 1971 by a radical collective looking for an alternative to the annual
procession to the Communards’ Wall (around this time there were also
reports of the Communards’ Wall having been vandalized in protest at the
fossilizing appropriation of their memory for party-political interests26). I
call this an “anti-commemoration” for lack of a better term. For indeed, the
plan was not to have a commemoration in the usual sense: “No commem-
oration. Demonstrations [manifestations] in the spirit of the Commune,”
announced Tout! (March 29, 1971: 1). The periodical Tout!, from which I
have gleaned these reports, was a Maoist, anarchist, gay-rights-supporting
newsletter that started in September 1970 and managed to produce sev-
enteen issues before disappearing in the summer of 1971.27 Since the life
cycle of the journal coincided to a significant extent with the months around
the centenary, the Commune figured prominently in its memoryscape as
a resource for thinking about the present and acting for the future. In the
weeks before March 1871, the journal called for a huge popular party and, in
parallel, for volunteers to recreate a “living commune” [“commune vivante”]
across the country so as to revive communard practices beyond the day of
the commemoration itself.
Their emphasis on new forms of sociality echoed the emerging associ-
ation between the Commune and the radical reinvention of everyday life
first formulated by Henri Lefebvre (1978). To illustrate what a future “liv-
ing commune” might look like, Tout! (April 23, 1971) recycled a painting
from 1871 called “Doing the Laundry at Place Pigalle” and dated March 1871
(Figure 4.4).
Depicting washerwomen and soldiers gathered peacefully around a foun-
tain, the painting was presented as exemplifying the new social relations that
the Commune had brought into being: “It’s the relations between people that
havechanged” (“C’estlesrapportsentre les gensquiontchangé”).28 Inrecy-
cling this image, Tout! was going back to the past to imagine the future. In
another temporal short circuit, it defined the meaning of the Commune for
the present as a “renewal of life” in terms that resonated with Louise Michel’s

128 REMEMBERING HOPE
Figure 4.4 La lessive, Place Pigalle, mars 1871. Water-based inks. Artist:
Isidore Pils. Public domain. Image courtesy Musée Carnavalet, Paris.
recollection of 1871, quoted earlier in this chapter, as a moment when people
“could live their life.” As Tout! put it:
The Paris Commune stands for people joyfully taking charge of their own
life in all domains; an explosion of the imagination of the people of Paris
thanks to their newly proclaimed freedom. Men, women, children, young
and old, everyone could express their ideas and participate fully in the
renewal of life brought about by the Commune. (Tout! March 29, 1971,
1; my emphasis)29
Although permission to hold “a huge people’s party” re-enacting this
“renewal of life” was refused by the police, the celebration did go ahead at a
much smaller scale. It took place on March 21 at the Marché aux Puces, the
flea market situated at the Porte de Clignancourt on the edge of Paris.30 The
date placed it in the tradition of strategically commemorating the promise
of the Commune by concentrating on its beginnings rather than its end.
Instead of a ritual procession to the cemetery, moreover, it took the form

THE AGENCY OF THE AESTHETIC 129
of an unscripted “happening” at a flea market, which was designed to sur-
prise, shock, amuse, and provoke. Enter three souped-up trucks operating as
floats, carrying activists dressed in hippy gear, playing loud pop music, and
dancing. The initial reaction, at least according to the report in Tout!, was
one of laughter and indeed “an explosion of joy” as some onlookers joined
in the dance. Although the police quickly intervened, leading to scuffles and
arrests, Tout! nevertheless concluded that the event had been a success: “The
party only lasted 15 minutes, but 15 minutes like that leave a mark.”
The party at the flea market was probably never destined to last very long.
But that was also the point. In highlighting the historical value of a party that
lasted a mere fifteen minutes, Tout! was echoing the Situationists’ theses on
the Commune to the effect that the outcome does not determine the mean-
ing of an event and that, for those who experienced it “the realization was
already there” (Debord et al. 1969). Instead of approaching the Commune
as a stepping stone in a longer trajectory, the flea market happening recon-
nected with a past moment of happiness and thereby, if only for a mere 15
minutes, prefigured an alternative future. In Chapter 6 I will come back in
greater detail to the relation between memory and prefiguration.
More relevant to this chapter is the aesthetics of what happened at the
flea market. It was the performative equivalent of the Situationists’ rejection
of the classical novel. In its unpredictability and playfulness, the anti-
commemoration made a mark albeit a small one. Behind its tactics, of
course, were aesthetic ideas that had taken root in the 1960s, based on the
idea of radical transgression and the hijacking of traditional forms, or what
the Situationists called détournement (literally: diversion). At the flea mar-
ket, it was the very idea of a ritualized commemoration that was hijacked
through an unscripted performance. Whereas commemoration is tradition-
ally oriented toward a past it historicizes, the 1971 intervention recalled the
Commune by reviving it in the present in a joyful and creative manner. The
happening was performative both in the sense that it took the form of street
theater and in the stronger sense of an intervention that makes something
happen, in this case, a strengthening of the association between the Com-
mune, freedom, vitality, and positivity. At the small scale of fifteen minutes
on the outskirts of Paris and registered in an ephemeral journal… but picked
up again more than fifty years later in this book.
In the following sections, I move from street performances to cultural
production as a site of Commune memory. In doing so, I show how other
aesthetic strategies intervene sensorially in the micropolitics of reading,

130 REMEMBERING HOPE
viewing, and listening and how, in doing so, they recollect the Commune
as an energizing and “live” event with the power to communicate hope.
Before turning to particular works, some introductory remarks are needed
on aesthetic agency.
The Power of Aesthetics
Chapter 3 demonstrated how narrative schemata make particular events
legible by reference to an event type. The idea of a “martyr” can be
expressed as easily by a film, a text, a photograph, or a performance since
it entails a template that underpins cultural carriers of memory irrespec-
tive of their medium and how it is used. Building on earlier remarks about
the sensorial—indeed, sensational—aspects of remembrance, the spotlight
turns here to the aesthetics of the cultural carriers of memory. The word
“aesthetic” derives from aisthētikos: relating to the senses. I use it here specif-
ically with reference to mediated experience and to the attention-producing
way media are used in particular instances.
How do color, sound, shape, word choice, suspense, movement, and
sequencing capture and hold the attention and appeal to hearts and minds?
This question relies on the basic distinction made in narratology between
the story and the storytelling, between fabula and syuzhet.31 This relates to
the analytic distinction between, on the one hand, the sequence of events
represented (fabula) and, on the other hand, the mediated form through
which readers and viewers apprehend those events (syuzhet). Attention
turns here to the latter; specifically to the words, shapes, colors, perspectives,
and forms through which the memory of events reaches the public.
Modern aesthetic theory assumes that some cultural carriers are more
striking and hence memorable than others and explains this by the uncom-
monness of their form and the particular quality of attention this provokes.
In a famous formulation, Roman Jakobson (1960) defined the “poetic” func-
tion of communication in terms of the capacity of an utterance to draw
attention to itself. He meant poetic, not in the narrow sense of verse, but
in the broader sense of things that are artfully or imaginatively constructed.
In such cases, we become aware, not only of what is conveyed, but also of
the way the medium is being used to do the conveying.
There is a general consensus that a form becomes striking when it presents
as something new against the background of what is already familiar and

THE AGENCY OF THE AESTHETIC 131
legible. In Derek Attridge’s terminology, aesthetic experience is an event
linked to the perception of “singularity” (2004). A singular cultural expres-
sion invites us to keep returning to it because it offers a sensual, cognitive,
and affective experience that cannot be reduced to a single “take-away” or
exhausted in a single viewing, listening, or reading. Things that predictably
follow a script tend to become “consumable” artifacts that are rarely remedi-
ated.32 In contrast, objects that operate aesthetically intervene in the present
by capturing and holding our attention in a rewarding way.
This account of the event-like character of aesthetic experience echoes
the theories of Russian Formalist Viktor Shklovsky. In “Art, as Device”
(1917), he influentially explained the aesthetic in terms of a disruption of
habitual patterns of perception brought about by the use of a “compli-
cated form.” Where everyday communication is based on straightforward,
automatized patterns of recognition and the principle of efficiency, aesthetic
communication deviates from these patterns and, in thus “defamiliarizing”
communication, also invites us to see the world in a new way:
The device of art is the “estrangement” of things and the complication of
the form, which increases the duration and complexity of the perception.
(Shklovksy 2015 [1917]: 162)
While Shklovsky’s insistence on the formal qualities of art works seems
to ignore their content, he did not assume that formal experimentation
was a value in itself. It was of vital importance in de-automatizing our
understanding of the world and in opening up new perspectives on it:
And so this thing we call art exists in order to restore the sensation of life,
in order to make us feel things, in order to make a stone stony. The goal of
art is to create the sensation of seeing, and not merely recognizing, things.
(162; emphasis added)
Seen in this way, aesthetic form is at the heart of the human capacity for
world-making. It enforces a break with old habits of thought and by giv-
ing people a resource for imagining the world differently, it changes the
conditions in which they act. In this sense it has agency.
These aesthetic theories were developed with respect to artistic practices
as such. But they are also useful in understanding the cultural mediation of
memory more generally as well as the agency of particular carriers. Cultural

132 REMEMBERING HOPE
memory work yields artifacts and performances that not only recall the past
but also create something new in the present: an aesthetic experience that is
memorable in its own right while also opening up new perspectives on the
world that restore, in Shklovsky’s words, “the sensation of life.”
In his “Use and Abuse of History for Life,” already mentioned in the
Introduction, Nietzsche pointed to a tension between history (understood
as inheritance) and life (understood as the transformative power to break
free of that inheritance). While a sense of history is important, he argued, it
needs to be offset by a plastic power that gives us the freedom to innovate
while selectively and critically engaging with the past. The rest of this chapter
brings Shklovsky and Nietzsche into dialogue by linking aesthetic agency to
reinventions of the world. It shows through a series of examples how the cre-
ative use of media helps in critically reshaping the meaning of the past. In
an earlier publication, I argued for more recognition of the role of the arts
in shaping and reshaping collective memory, with reference to the ways in
which the arts had contributed to the bringing into visibility of the hitherto
occluded memory of colonial soldiers in the European armies in World War
I (Rigney 2021b). At stake here is something different: the power of the aes-
thetic to overwrite defeat and resist the actual course of history by “reviving”
hopes and possibilities through storytelling.
Storytelling That Resists Outcomes
This exploration of the agency of the aesthetic takes us away from shorthand
invocations of the Commune to longhand narratives, and from macro-
cultural trends to the close-reading of particular carriers. Within the enor-
mous Commune-related corpus, I concentrate on the medium of film
because of its cultural importance since the early twentieth century. My anal-
ysis is based on twelve standalone films produced in the period 1914–2021
(two of them post-2016) offering longhand accounts of the Commune as it
unfolded across time.33 Their very existence assumes the Commune is worth
being brought to the attention of a new generation be that for fifteen minutes
(Guerra 1914) or for almost five hours (Watkins 2000). Some of them can
be classified as documentaries, combining voice-overs from first-person wit-
ness accounts with closeups of legacy documents, drawings, and paintings,
while others are dramatized versions of the Commune using contemporary
actors. They all exploit the affordances of film as a medium to bring the

THE AGENCY OF THE AESTHETIC 133
Commune into the present. Like the demonstrators holding pictures of their
dead comrades, they offer a pushback to history.
Each of the twelve films configures the events of 1871 in its own way,
while referencing the same baseline set of events from the origins of the
Commune in the Franco-Prussian war to its establishment and subsequent
defeat. Where the Soviet-era The New Babylon (Kozintsev & Trauberg 1929)
emphasizes the decisions taken by the comité du salut publique at the Hôtel
de Ville (hence underscoring the role of the revolutionary government), the
more recent La Commune (Paris, 1871) (Watkins 2000) emphasizes instead,
in line with current understandings of 1871, the work done on the ground
in the transformation of the everyday and the organization of social life.
Bringing the Commune into the present entails selecting particular
episodes for highlighting as well as narrative strategies for representing
them. While all films have more or less the same fabula (beginning in March
and ending in May) each film presents as a different syuzhet which artfully
draws on narrative and cinematic techniques, including imaginative recon-
struction, to recall the events of 1871 in a visually impressive form. The basic
story is known, including the outcome, but the syuzhet is a source of unpre-
dictability and hence the focus of attention. There are nevertheless some
recurring strategies.
The most basic point is that film “animates” past events, be this in the
form of dramatization using actors or dramatization using archival images
in sequence. The latter is best exemplified by Raphaël Meyssan’s Les damnés
de la Commune (2021), where the camera zooms in and pans out from legacy
images, accompanied by voice-overs inspired by contemporary eyewitness
accounts; the result is an animated version of the Commune whose striking
form engrosses even if the basic storyline is already known.34 The unpre-
dictability of the unfolding syuzhet makes the mediation into a happening
in its own right which slows down and de-automatizes perception.35
Illustrating animation of a more literal kind, La Commune (Paris, 1871)
(Watkins 2000) uses actors, many nonprofessional, to re-enact the insurrec-
tion in a performance which was simultaneously filmed in the mode of a
black and white documentary. The actors play out the roles assigned them,
inventing the script as they go along. In the process, the unfolding action
gains an extra layer for the viewer-witness: it is both a drama played out
in the present, affecting those involved, and an imaginative reconstruction
of the past.36 Moreover, the blatantly anachronistic use of “anchor men”
and live street interviews suggesting the Commune was breaking news, in

134 REMEMBERING HOPE
combination with intertitles more typical of silent film, produces a defamil-
iarizing double vision in which the past unfolds unpredictably in the present
of the film. In this way, aesthetic form brings out potentialities at each turn
of the story, “releasing the past from its ‘pastness’ and letting it assume the
vitality of what has always been there,” to recall the words of Ariella Azoulay
quoted in the Introduction.
Animation is also a key feature of Armand Guerra’s La Commune (1914),
the earliest known film on the topic. Although showing poor mastery of the
still young medium, the makers were clearly excited by the possibility of pre-
senting people in motion. Its highly theatrical reconstruction of the lead up
to the proclamation of the Commune tellingly leads into archival footage of
actual places and people. Specifically, it shows a reunion of Communards
in 1911, both men and women, as they gather beside a flag (presumably a
red flag), and their names appear one after another on screen. The mov-
ing images are literally used to “revive” the Commune by showing the
resilience of these veterans in the new medium. Guerra’s short film, orig-
inally produced by an anarchist collective called “Cinéma du Peuple,” is
currently available in several versions on YouTube. In one case, uploaded
from archive.org in 2020, the silent images have been given a soundtrack by
some contemporary “friend” of the Commune; as the veterans stand and
move jerkily in front of the camera we now hear the elegiac sound of an
accordion playing the Commune evergreen “Le temps des cérises.”37
Nor is this mobilization of music exceptional. Whether used in an instru-
mental form to modulate mood or in the form of collective singing to
re-enact the experience of solidarity, music plays a key role in these films
as a mediator between the past of 1871 and the present moment of viewing
and listening.38 Watkins’s movie, for example, includes a striking sequence in
which Communards facing the firing squad start to sing a drinking song, one
after the other creating a chorus of free agents defying fear by recalling the
pleasures of life. The film goes on to close with the collective singing of the
“Marseillaise,” followed by “Le temps des cérises,” followed finally by an Ara-
bic song called “El Menfi” sung collectively to the sound of hand drumming.
This musical choice is in line with the importance accorded in Watkins’s
film to North African actors, their presence serving both as a reminder of
the coincidence in time between the Commune and the Mokrani revolt in
Algeria, which also started in March 1871, and the importance of North
African immigrants in today’s Paris. The sound of singing, especially col-
lective singing, is a forceful way of re-enacting solidarity and reasserting life

THE AGENCY OF THE AESTHETIC 135
in a way that transcends the difference between past and present.39 In the
energizing presence of human voices—sound is always in the present—the
films perform the aliveness of the Commune for a new generation of viewers.
This is also behind the recent “La Commune en chantant” initiative orga-
nized by the “Let’s Bring the Commune Back to Life” association, mentioned
earlier.40
The positioning of prolonged singing at the end of a movie is all the
more effective given that it can profit from what is known in narratology
as the “recency effect” (Perry 1979); that is, the enhanced impact of the last
thing encountered in the syuzhet. Managing the recency effect is of particu-
lar importance in remembering the Commune given that its experiment in
popular governance ended in slaughter. In diverse ways, however, the film-
makers ensure that history does not have the last word. With the exception of
Guerra’s truncated account, each narrative inevitably pays extensive atten-
tion to the violence inflicted on the Communards in the last week of May
(though only La semaine sanglante draws attention to this in its title, which
can be explained by its having been made for a series on “great battles”; Gallo
1976).41 The long-term impact of the violent dénouement is mitigated in the
various films by the use of pushback strategies, typically in the final scenes,
which assert the enduring spirit of the Commune. Recalling music or slogans
is part of this. Hence a voice-over telling us that those who died on the last
barricade did so while shouting “Vive la commune” or “Vive la révolution
sociale” (Lalloui 2004); the sound of the “Internationale” (Condon 2021);
a photograph of the last veteran who died in the Soviet Union in 1942 to
the sound of “Le temps des cérises” (Gallo 1976); an image of a flag carry-
ing the words “Vive la Commune!” (Guerra 1914), and shouts of “Vive la
Commune” and of “We will be off to Paris” followed by an image of “Vive la
Commune” scrawled on a wall (Kozintsev & Trauberg 1929; more on this
below). In combination with the other techniques used to animate the Com-
mune these strategically placed assertions of support for the cause serve as a
final pushback reminder of the vitality of the Commune: a powerful memory
if no longer a political force.
The handwritten graffiti of “Vive la Commune” in Kozintsev and
Trauberg’s The New Babylon (1929) reappeared in Gallo’s Paris, 1871: La
semaine sanglante (1976) and Condon’s 1871, La Commune (2021). This
recycling reflects the enduring fascination not just of the slogan but of the
slogan in the materialized form of a graffiti, a form that would be recalled
again in 2016. This recycling is testimony to the Russian movie’s lasting

136 REMEMBERING HOPE
power to convey and perpetuate that fascination. The concluding section
of this chapter will accordingly zoom in on The New Babylon and its use
of graffiti in order to explain the connection between memorability and the
agency of the aesthetic.
The New Babylon (1929)
The New Babylon (Novvij Vavilon) (the title refers to a department store)
was written and directed in Moscow and Paris by Grigori Kozintsev and
Leonid Trauberg using Marx (1871) and Lissagaray (1876) as their principal
sources of information about the Commune. Both men had been active in an
avant-garde theater group called the “Factory of the Eccentric Actor” (FEKS)
before transitioning to the emergent art form of cinema, at that time still very
much dominated by the aesthetics of melodrama. Although the film treated
a highly canonical event in Soviet Russia, where the Commune was seen as a
dress rehearsal for the revolution of 1917, the film was controversial when it
appeared. Indeed, it was something of a critical flop. Praised for being a “lyri-
cal film, of great dramatic intensity” as far as its depiction of the bourgeoisie
was concerned, its “desire to aestheticize everything” meant its depiction of
the Communards was a failure.42 The film’s innovative use of music com-
posed by Dmitri Shostakovich also compromised its initial reception. The
variable projection speeds of the time meant that the intended interplay
between image and sound was hard to achieve. Moreover, last-minute cuts
to the film (possibly under pressure from the censors) meant hurried last-
minute adaptations to the score. As a result, the film went out of circulation
in the USSR and ended up distributed across Europe and the United States
without its original musical accompaniment. It was only after the death of
Shostakovich that the musical score re-emerged in 1975 to become, since
the early 1980s, an inseparable part of the film.43
Looking back several decades later, Kozintsev described a standoff among
critics between, on the one hand, those who called for greater realism in the
depiction of history and, on the other hand, those who saw in formal experi-
mentation the possibility of capturing the essence of revolutionary action in
an appropriately revolutionary form.44 This critical standoff marked a key
moment in the struggle between the Russian avant-garde, which emphasized
formal experimentation, and the state-supported preference for so-called
socialist realism in art. In the first instance, The New Babylon fell afoul of

THE AGENCY OF THE AESTHETIC 137
this struggle and became sidelined for being overly “formalist” in later his-
tories of Soviet cinema. Ironically, it was also condemned at various points in
the UK, the United States, and France for being overly “revolutionary” in its
obvious sympathy for the Communards (President Pompidou prohibited a
screening on national television on the centenary in 1971). A screening with
live musical performance in Los Angeles was planned to coincide with the
1984 Olympics, though ultimately the USSR withdrew from the games. Since
then, The New Babylon has had something of a revival and is now generally
recognized as one of the classics of Soviet cinema and regularly screened,
sometimes with live performances of the music, at other times using the
restored version with recorded music from 1982.45 A screening also figured
as part of a cultural project in New York in 2014 called “The Days of the
Commune” that originated in the Occupy Wall St. movement.46
It was not the first film on the Commune (that was Guerra 1914), but it
is the one that has had the longest cultural afterlife. It is by no means subtle
in its support for the insurrection or in its depiction of the tragic love story
between a Communard girl and a government-supporting soldier. But this
does not mean that its narration is “consumable” in any straightforward way.
More than any of the other films considered here it tells a predictable story in
an unpredictable way. Made at the high point of Russian experimentation in
the new medium, exemplified in the work of Eisenstein, it offers fast-paced
montage, a portrait-camera creating close-up presences that filled the screen
with little depth of field, and dramatic chiaroscuro. Most notable in this
regard is the sequence (59.05-1.00) leading up to the collective building of a
barricade, where the montage cuts repeatedly from the street level to that of
the first floor, where a man is throwing down furniture to help the cause.
In the latter part of the film (e.g., 1.12.33–1.33.27), reduced lighting and
the work of shadows provide a visual translation of the spectral appearance
of Paris after the Commune evoked in Marx’s Civil War in France (1871).
Throughout, Kozintsev and Trauberg used visual counterpoint to juxtapose
images of bourgeois decadence (associated with a department store called
“the New Babylon,” cafés, and a picnic) and images of the laboring classes.
This visual counterpoint prevents the viewer from ever settling into any sin-
gle position. In sum, the movement between images, in conjunction with the
movement of bodies on the screen, becomes a way of conveying the ener-
gies unleashed by the insurrection directly to the viewers themselves. This
kinetic intensity is the filmic version of the mnemonic energy released by
still images, discussed earlier in Chapter 3.

138 REMEMBERING HOPE
Add into all of this the kinetic intensity of the musical score, which was
a key feature of the film’s innovation even if it only came fully into its own
in the restored version of the film. While initially controversial, this com-
bination of exciting visual montage with the purpose-composed score has
been key to the film’s cultural longevity and its recent revival. Where silent
films of this period were usually accompanied by semi-improvised or ran-
domly selected music, Kozintsev and Trauberg were inspired by the recent
example of Eisenstein’s Battleship Potemkin (1925) to commission a young
Shostakovich to write music that would complement and enrich the film.
Shostakovich’s energetically dissonant music works to underscore the visual
imagery, but not in any straightforward way. The composer set out his strat-
egy in a polemical essay, written in 1929, that can also be read as a manifesto
for film music as a new art form.47 Rather than have the music match one on
one the mood or significance of what we see on screen, his score often intro-
duced a dissonance in the image (we hear a cavalry coming while looking
at an empty restaurant, for example). It also created dissonance between the
perspectives of different characters (while we see a happy crowd in a café,
the music evokes the sadness of one of the characters; while we see the Com-
munards dying, we hear the sound of dance music). As Joan Titus (2016)
has shown, moreover, Shostakovich also drew extensively on musical mem-
ory by “coding” existing compositions into the score to create other sorts of
contrasts. Offenbach’s popular version of the cancan (his “galop infernal”)
was juxtaposed with the sound of the “Marseillaise,” for example, while the
latter was also brought into conversation with the more radical “Carmag-
nole,” which figures in the dramatic building of the barricades.48 In this way,
Shostakovich drew on the musical archive to perform ideological differences
and add an extra level of drama to the syuzhet. The music is no less dynamic
than the visual montage.
Which brings us back to graffiti. A key moment in the film relates to
the dramatic visualization of the words “Vive la Commune!” written on
a wall by a dying Communard (1.12.56–1.13.12). This image marks the
close of a sequence that begins with the joyful building of the barricade
(to the accompaniment of a double-time syncopated ostinato played by
violins) and ends with the “return of law and order” epitomized by walk-
ers with umbrellas moving purposefully in chiaroscuro across the screen
(to the accompaniment of the ostinato played in half time by violins).
Enter a countermelody—a dirge in minor key played by clarinets—while
the camera turns to a prostate figure. This person—unclear whether male

THE AGENCY OF THE AESTHETIC 139
Figure 4.5 Still from G. Kozintsev and L. Trauberg, The New Babylon [Novvy
Vavilon] (1929). Public domain.
or female—is in their death throes but nevertheless reaching upward to
complete a graffiti with the words “Vive la Commune” (Figure 4.5). Hav-
ing shown the prostrate figure and the graffiti from a distance, the camera
zooms in on the writing hand before then moving slowly over the indi-
vidual words so that they are spelled out and engraved in the viewer’s
memory.
This is a dialectic image-sequence par excellence, since it depicts a
moment of death while it also leaves a message of enduring commitment
to the living. Its impact is enhanced by its resonance, for those who know
it, with Charles Michel Guilbert d’Anelle’s painting Expiring Soldier of Lib-
erty (1848) which depicts a dying Polish freedom fighter scrawling the words
“Poland is not yet lost” on a wall.49 The image of the graffiti-writer on the
cover of the #Diren Direni¸s: Ajanda 2014 discussed in Chapter 2 offers yet
another version of this visual trope.
Not only does The New Babylon dramatize the graffiti in this way, the same
image-text reappears at the end of the film, where it follows the shouts of
“Vive la Commune!” as one by one the Communards meet the firing squad.
In the very last moments of the film, the camera zooms in once again on the
graffiti, now detached from the living hand that wrote it (Figures 4.6–4.8).
As we watch the words (1.32.38–1.32.46), we hear a triumphant maestoso
leading into a cadence and a final shift to a major key. While the fabula has
ended in defeat and mass execution, the syuzhet ends on a very upbeat note

140 REMEMBERING HOPE
Figure 4.6 Still from G. Kozintsev and L. Trauberg, The New Babylon [Novvy
Vavilon] (1929). Public domain.
Figure 4.7 Still from G. Kozintsev and L. Trauberg, The New Babylon [Novvy
Vavilon] (1929). Public domain.
