as the energies of the Communards transfer to the slogan, which then forms
a message to the future. The same dying figure writing “Vive la Commune”
was recycled in one of the two original film posters, with the black and white

THE AGENCY OF THE AESTHETIC 141
Figure 4.8 Still from G. Kozintsev and L. Trauberg, The New Babylon [Novvy
Vavilon] (1929). Public domain.
Figure 4.9 Poster for G. Kozintsev and L. Trauberg, The New Babylon [Novvy
Vavilon] (1929). Wikimedia Commons.
of the original now transmuted into sensationalist red, yellow, and black
(Figure 4.9).
As mentioned earlier, The New Babylon’s dramatization of the writ-
ing of a graffiti has also been remediated in later films. Condon’s 1871,
La Commune (2021) draws extensively on Kozintsev and Trauberg’s work

142 REMEMBERING HOPE
(although it does not mention them in the credits) while mixing it with
legacy images as well as dramatization. The reclining graffiti-writer and
the movement of the camera over the words actually appears twice, the
second time in the final minutes (32.06; 50.19). While Gallo’s Paris, 1871:
La semaine sanglante (1976) does not include the image of the graffiti-
writer as such, it does repeat the sequence where the camera moves
across the separate words, accompanied by a voice-over saying “Here
ends hope, here a legend is born” (45.24).50 In both cases, the image is
presented as if based on a historical photograph rather than an earlier
dramatization.
These remediations of the graffiti scenes in The New Babylon reflect
the aesthetic power of that film to “stick” in cultural memory and hence
procreate in later narratives. Where Gallo suggested that “legend” was an
alternative to hope, this analysis suggests otherwise. Legend-making—or to
use the more neutral term: cultural mediation—makes sense by reassert-
ing the value of a cause despite its historical defeat. Kozintsev and Trauberg
were still working in the melodramatic-expressionist mode of earlier cin-
ema while the activists at the flea market were using the surprise tactics of
the 1960s. But in both cases aesthetics is not just part of the décor but an
agent in the larger dynamic whereby the Commune re-entered the present
as a living force with the potential to mobilize new publics and predispose
them to act.
Memory Work as a Practice of Possibilities
Back to 2016. This chapter began with the invocation of “Vive la Commune”
and “Que revive la Commune” at the Place de la République in the context of
the Nuit Debout Occupation. Nuit Deboutists may not all have had detailed
knowledge of the events of 1871, or indeed of the films discussed here. As
I have shown, however, the evocation of the Commune in that space and
in that particular form had deep roots in the history of its mediations as
these mutated across time and diffused across the memoryscape. Slogans
like “Vive la Commune” and “Que revive la Commune” had a mobilizing
force thanks to resonating with an accumulated history of remembering
and forgetting the 1871 insurrection in multiple cultural carriers, includ-
ing music. This “usable archive” allowed the Commune to enter into “the
figuration of the present,” to recall Ross’s phrase.

THE AGENCY OF THE AESTHETIC 143
The possibility evoked—in The New Babylon, at the flea market in 1971,
at the Place de la République in 2016—of the Commune’s coming back to life
is the outcome of a distinctive type of counterhistorical memory work. Mak-
ing the hopeful beginning of the Commune the defining feature of its legacy
challenges the assumption, central to traditional theories of narrative, that
the outcome of events determines their meaning. Like the celebrations of
the Commune I discussed in the Introduction, this counterhistorical mem-
ory work dwells on moments of possibility in the past—the paths not yet
taken—instead of trying to establish direct lines of continuity between past,
present, and future. This too is memory in the subjunctive mode. Walter
Benjamin wrote once that the best way to articulate the world historically is
to capture the memory of turning points in the past that might have led to
greater justice (1970 [1942]: 257). By giving those moments of possibility a
second chance in memory they might still become the beginning of a differ-
ent history. Kozintsev and Trauberg’s foregrounding of the words written by
the dying insurgent, in the form of a “still” as it were, captures such a moment
at the crossroads between death and life. So too does the repetition of “Vive
la Commune” in 2016 open up the possibility of kickstarting a movement
that began in 1871 and that, now retrospectively, is seen as having prefigured
contemporary choices.
What this chapter has shown above all is the power of cultural mediation
itself to change the meaning and affective charge of past events. Whether
in the form of performance, film, or some other medium, mediation chan-
nels, interrupts, and changes memory so that it brings historical possibilities
back into play. In doing so mediation is a vector of hope in the sense of a
practice of imagining possibilities and the readiness to act on them. To be
sure: the contagion of hope described here occurs in the first instance only
in the micropolitics of viewing and listening. It is not necessarily something
carried over into the streets or that occurs at scale—the films and the hap-
pening at the flea market touched a limited number of people at best. They
introduced small, but nevertheless significant differences into the memory
of the Commune.
What I have presented here is qualitative research in the basic sense of
being concerned with identifying unique patterns as they develop over time.
Moreover, it is “qualitative” in being attentive to the capacity of particu-
lar carriers to introduce qualitative changes into memory culture, however
small in the first instance, by changing the predispositions of individual
subjects. It is only through an accumulation of such changes at different

144 REMEMBERING HOPE
sites that collective memory evolves over time. The “reinvention of polit-
ical culture” (Goldfarb 2012) happens in the first instance through small
steps rather than dramatic revolutions. This holds true not just for brief
“happenings” like the one in the flea market but also for the accumulative
production of books, films, plays, and songs about the Commune that has
helped not only to diffuse but also to create that memory in a continuously
emergent way.

Chapter 5
Toppling Monuments
End or Means?
Down with the Vendôme Column (1871)
On the afternoon of May 16, 1871, the band played the “Marseillaise” as a
huge crowd nervously waited for the Vendôme column to be taken down on
behalf of the Commune. The 44-meter-high monument, crowned by a statue
of Napoleon, had been erected in 1805 to the glory of the grande armée
and its victories. Prosper-Olivier Lissagaray, chronicler of the Commune
and a presumed eyewitness to this event, described how the column finally
collapsed after a few false starts. It split into several parts—“like zigzags
of lightning”—leaving the head of Bonaparte to roll across the ground. At
which point, “An immense cheer surged up from thousands of chests like
the cry of a people delivered” and the red flag was raised. Figure 5.1 offers
one of the many visual reconstructions of the event.1
As announced in the official report of the revolutionary government, the
demolition of the Vendôme column was a huge and solemn undertaking
in which materiality, symbolism, and politics were deeply entangled. Dis-
mantling such a huge column without injuring anyone was no simple task.
It had required weeks of planning as well as manpower, control over the
city space, and an estimated 15,000 francs.2 The demolition was originally
scheduled to coincide with the anniversary of Napoleon’s death on May 5,
but technical problems meant its postponement by two weeks. By then, the
Commune itself was in a parlous state and its survival ever more uncertain
as state troops moved in on the city. Indeed, just seven days after the demoli-
tion of the column, thousands of Communards would be slaughtered in the
infamous Bloody Week that ended the two-month social experiment. On the
day the column was demolished, the population was facing starvation.
Given the sheer scale of the undertaking and the immediate threats to the
very survival of the Commune from the encroaching army, it is all the more
surprising to read about the time and resources taken out for the demolition.
Remembering Hope. Ann Rigney, Oxford University Press. © Oxford University Press (2025).
DOI: 10.1093/oso/9780197789711.003.0006

146 REMEMBERING HOPE
Figure 5.1 Destruction of the Vendôme Column by the Commune, May 16,
1871. Contemporary drawing: Anon. Image Alamy.
Clearly, the monument mattered. In fact, this one seems to have mattered
so much that, just a couple of years later, the column that the Commune
had so carefully deconstructed would be carefully reconstructed again by
the French government at huge cost to the Third Republic. As Lissagaray
bitterly concluded, “it took a scaffolding of 30,000 corpses to put Caesar back
on his pedestal” (1876: 321).3
The extensive preparations show that the demolition of the Vendôme col-
umn was not mere impulse. It had also been subject to debate, since not
everyone was convinced it should be destroyed. The artist Gustave Courbet
for one had proposed to relocate the column away from the city center,4 to
no avail. On April 12, the governing body of the Commune decided that
its presence in the city could no longer be tolerated and formally decreed its
solemn demolition. The combination of material presence and symbolism—
it recalled French militarism in a physically overwhelming design imitating
the Trajan column in Rome—was found to be irritating, insulting, offen-
sive. In the words of the official text, the column was (note all the verbal
forms): “an affirmation of militarism, a negation of international law, a per-
manent insult to the vanquished on the part of conquerors” and as such, “a

TOPPLING MONUMENTS 147
perpetual attack on one of the three great principles of the French Republic—
fraternity” (my emphases).5 In short, the column was experienced as a toxic
material presence that was actively polluting the city with the force of an
assault.
Hence the dramatic, memorable, and widely broadcast demolition of the
column at a perilous moment when the military conflict had entered a cru-
cial phase. According to the report in the Commune’s official journal the
next day, the demolition was justified by the moral imperative to remove
from the streets of Paris the memory of a despot who had caused the deaths
of thousands. In a forward-looking gesture, however, the journal also framed
the demolition as material proof of the Commune’s support for justice above
violence, claiming that future monuments would celebrate the memory of
“science, labor, and liberty” rather than of despotism and militarism.6 The
spectacular destruction of the column was a sign to posterity that the new
political regime would foster a different form of public commemoration.
Since the demolition was so dramatic, it left a rich visual and textual
legacy. Besides its role in rallying spirits within the besieged city in May 1871,
the memorable demolition materially helped the Commune to “stick” in cul-
tural memory. It is no coincidence that one of the British socialists quoted in
Chapter 1, invoked the Commune, by reference to “the spirit that removed
the Vendôme Column” and that “animates the Socialists of to-day more than
ever” (CW 3/24/88). Or that the image of the demolished monument should
still be a point of reference in decolonial movements across the world.7
I have started with the case of the Vendôme column because it provides
not just evidence that monuments matter but also food for thought on why
they do. Specifically, it calls for reflection on the relation between political
regime change and mnemonic regime change. This topic has become espe-
cially relevant in light of the recent wave of debates about public monuments
and the rationale for leaving or removing outmoded ones. This chapter con-
tributes to those debates by drawing on cultural memory studies to explain
why monuments matter and when they don’t.
Memory Activism
In my Introduction, I described the memory-activism nexus as the meeting
ground between three vectors of memory work. Subsequently, Chapters 1
and 2 focused on memory in activism while Chapters 3 and 4 focused on the

148 REMEMBERING HOPE
memory of activism. This chapter now turns to memory activism in the form
of campaigns to change public memory, before turning in the final chapters
to memory work as an integral part of activism as such.
Since the publication of Yifat Gutman’s Memory Activism: Reimagining
the Past for the Future in Israel-Palestine (2017) and Jenny Wüstenberg’s Civil
Society and Memory in Post-War Germany (2017), the concept of “memory
activism” has come to occupy a prominent place within the field of mem-
ory studies. It has spawned multiple publications, including the landmark
Routledge Handbook of Memory Activism (Gutman & Wüstenberg 2023).
As scholarship has evolved over the years, the definition of memory activism
has shifted in minor ways, but the core remains the same: activism aiming
for change in public forms of commemoration and in the collective narra-
tive that underpins them. The concept has been emphatically introduced as a
“value-neutral” category of analysis that is theoretically applicable to move-
ments from across the political spectrum.8 Its application has nevertheless
come with the implicit assumption that a more open discussion about the
complexity of a society’s past, including recognition of both victimhood and
perpetratorship, will help in fostering a more democratic and open society. I
share this assumption, while also recognizing that the relationship between
memory and politics is in practice always fraught.
The introduction to the recent handbook defines memory activism as “the
strategic commemoration of a contested past to achieve mnemonic or politi-
cal change by working outside state channels” (Gutman & Wüstenberg 2023:
4; my emphasis). Memory activists “target memory as the crucial way of
intervening in the process of social change from below” (5–6). Presented
in this way, memory activism is both activism about memory and a strategy
for obtaining other long-term goals by changing the nature of public com-
memoration. And indeed the term “memory activism” has started to become
something of a catchall term used to capture the entire memory-activism
nexus in one go. This chapter seeks to halt this drift toward equating move-
ments about memory (the monumental landscape of cities, the timing of
public commemorations) and movements targeting other causes (climate
change, racism, housing). While recognizing that changing the collective
memoryscape has political and social implications (see above), I neverthe-
less distinguish between memory as the “end” of activism and memory work
as a strategic means.9
Having established this point of departure, the following chapter teases
out both the congruence and the tensions between mnemonic change and

TOPPLING MONUMENTS 149
political change, between memory activism and activism more broadly.
It does so by looking at monuments. As physical manifestations of the
collective memoryscape, these are potential sites of contestation between
hegemonic and oppositional perspectives on what a society should remem-
ber or forget. As the demolition and later reconstruction of the Vendôme
column showed, mnemonic change has a deeply political dimension even
if, at a surface reading, it is only a matter of replacing one set of stones by
another. Changing the city’s monumental landscape is “only” about statues
and plaques, but also much more. In what follows I explore this oscillation
between memory and politics with specific reference to recent attacks on
colonial-era monuments. I argue that structural ambiguity—“it’s just about
monuments” and “it’s about much more”—is the very heart of the matter.
X Must Fall, but Why?
Monuments relating to the legacy of European colonialism have recently
been the subject of very visible public controversies across the world. Start-
ing with the highly mediatized “Rhodes Must Fall” movement in South
Africa in 2015, multiple “must fall” movements have targeted public rem-
nants of the historical domination of white Europeans over other peoples.
To date more than 160 statues to the Confederacy have been removed
across the United States (the majority since the murder of George Floyd in
2020).10 In Mexico City, as in many other locations across Latin America, the
statue to Christopher Columbus has been removed after protest by indige-
nous activists.11 In Canada, a monument to Queen Victoria was upended
in Winnipeg as one among many attacks on symbols of the oppression of
indigenous peoples. In Belgium, King Leopold II’s bust was removed from a
park in Ghent in June 2020, while his statue in Brussels has become a site of
regular demonstrations for greater racial equality in Belgium (Figure 5.2).
The estallido social protests in Chile in 2019–2022 included the removal
or transformation of almost 400 monuments celebrating the foundation of
the nation-state on the back of indigenous peoples.12 The dramatic image
of slave-trader Edward Colston being toppled into Bristol harbor hit the
international headlines in June 2020. And the list could go on.13 By now
the iconoclastic wave has passed its mediagenic phase, but it has undoubt-
edly led to ongoing moves to decolonize the physical memoryscape of cities
across the world while also generating resistance to such moves.

150 REMEMBERING HOPE
Figure 5.2 Vandalized monument to King Leopold II, Ghent, Belgium, June 2,
2020. Photo: Wannes Nimmegeers. ID/Photoagency.
The cheering that accompanies the toppling of statues shows they
matter—paradoxically, perhaps never more so than when they are being
literally and figuratively taken down from their pedestals. As the confused
public debates also indicate, however, many people struggle to understand
why monuments can evoke such anger, why they can be perceived as racist,
why they have relevance for the present since they reference the past, and
why removing them is more than mere vandalism. This confusion revolves
around the paradox that monuments are both material objects and carriers
of meaning—there’s always more behind them than what meets the eye. In
trying to understand the complex interplay between memory activism and
antiracism, it is helpful to note that there is nothing new about iconoclasm
and that contemporary controversies have many forerunners.
Surprising as it may seem given their reputation for durability, monu-
ments have been routinely subject to relocation, demolition, and reworking.
The Vendôme column, which had already gone through several makeovers
by the time the Commune arrived, offers a case in point. Other historical
examples lead from the damnatio memoriae tradition of the Romans, to the
destruction of the statue of George III in New York in 1776 (Figure 5.3), to
attacks on icons related to the ancien régime during the French Revolution

TOPPLING MONUMENTS 151
Figure 5.3 A mob pulls down the equestrian statue of George III at Bowling
Green, New York City, July 9, 1776. Painting by William Walcutt, 1854.
Wikimedia Commons.
(for which the Abbé Grégoire coined the term “vandalism”), to the wave
of Lenin and Stalin removals that cascaded across post-Communist Europe
after 1989, to the highly orchestrated and mediated toppling of Saddam Hus-
sein in Baghdad in 2003; to the current shifting around of monuments in the
context of Russian aggression in Ukraine.14
Particularly relevant to the current iconoclastic wave, albeit less well-
known, was the series of monument removals that accompanied decolo-
nization.15 These included the destruction of the statue to Jan Pieterszoon
Coen (director general of the Dutch East India Company) in Jakarta in
1943, the relocation of Queen Victoria from post-independence Ireland
to Sydney and, conversely, the “repatriation” of various statues of Joan
of Arc from Algeria to various cities in France. Repatriation, which also
occurred with British statues, was meant to save monuments from being
beheaded, destroyed, or otherwise “desecrated” at the hands of newly inde-
pendent peoples eager to assert their control.16 That Joan of Arc should
have ended up in Algiers and Luís de Camões in Macau shows that colo-
nialism itself had a mnemonic dimension; both people and memories were
“settled” in occupied territories in the form of placenames and monuments

152 REMEMBERING HOPE
from the metropolitan center.17 I’ve referred to this elsewhere as “memorial
colonization.”18 If the toppling of colonial statues to mark independence had
its own dynamic as part of anticolonialism, it too fits into a long-standing
association between monument-building and the exercise of power, and
hence between monument removal and political change affecting both
colonies and the imperial centers.19
Current debates about colonial-era statues thus stand in a longer tradi-
tion. But there is also something new going on. Recent attacks on colonial
statues have not typically been the byproduct of political regime change
on a par with the fall of Communism or Algerian Independence, but of
civil society campaigns in peacetime. Decolonial memory activism has
occurred incidentally since at least the 1960s, when for example activists
in the Netherlands carried out a series of attacks (including a bombing in
1967) on the statue to General Van Heutsz, notorious for his role in colo-
nial massacres in Indonesia.20 In recent years, there has been a veritable
transnational wave of such contestations. Although implicitly construed as
a step toward a more inclusive society, such activism is not in the first
instance about overthrowing a government or celebrating the fact that one
has been overthrown. Instead, it is directed toward engineering a change
in the collective narrative with, on the horizon, a change in social relations
and policies—for example, relating to policing—so that they are no longer
racist.21 Decommissioning outdated colonial-era monuments is both an end
in itself and a means toward a larger goal. This double focus makes monu-
ment contestation structurally ambivalent; it’s only about monuments, it’s
also about systemic change. This ambivalence is productive (in appealing to
larger constituencies by seeming less political) but it is also risky (by leav-
ing the possibility open that the debate is just about symbols and not bigger
issues).
In exploring this structural ambivalence, I assume that iconoclasm is not
merely a matter of destroying the old, but a resource for effecting change. In
ancient Rome (Omissi 2016), obliterating the memory of fallen tyrants was
a creative process in that it allowed new rulers to profile their own role in the
downfall of their predecessors. Here too we will be dealing with a creative
process, but one that operates from a position of disempowerment. How
do the current attacks on monuments contribute to the transition to a new
collective narrative and, as its assumed by-product, to social transformation?
But also: what are the limits of memory activism as a conduit to broader
aims, when “mnemonic change” and “political change” begin to part ways?

TOPPLING MONUMENTS 153
In order to answer these questions, more needs to be said about the nature
of monuments as such and about the dynamics of collective memory pro-
duction. Most studies hitherto have treated the contestation of monuments
as a discrete phenomenon. In line with this, the term “de-commemoration”
has come into use to designate public rituals—both official interventions and
activist ones—that are designed to mark the negation of previous respect.22
While such rituals are important, as the rest of this chapter will show, they
can never be understood in isolation but only as part of a longer-term
process of remembering and forgetting. And this takes more than public
monuments alone.
The Cultural Dynamics of Un-Forgetting
Memory activism is premised on the possibility that collective memory, as
expressed in the monumental landscape, is subject to change. While monu-
ments seem immutable and the past itself can never be changed, memory
is malleable. For this reason, high on the agenda of memory studies has
been the question of how dominant narratives change over time, how that
change can be negotiated, and whether it can be precipitated by activists
or promoted by educationalists and other civil actors.23 Understanding
“mnemonic transitions” (Cardina & Rodrigues 2020) has become all the
more urgent at a time of increasing global entanglements. These put pres-
sure on established national narratives and challenge their validity while also
generating pushback in their defense.
Before turning to the role of cultural memory work in these processes, it
is useful to recall that remembering and forgetting always go hand in glove.
Not only because memory needs to be selective to be meaningful, but also
because the sense of a shared past and shared present can only be created if
people are prepared to paper over historical cracks. Forgetting is not merely
a negative condition: it enables solidarities in the present by occluding
those conflicts whose memory could be divisive. As Ernest Renan famously
wrote:
The essence of a nation is that its members have many things in common,
but also that they have forgotten many things. [. . .] every French citizen
has to have forgotten the Saint-Bartholomew and the thirteenth-century
massacres in the south of France.24

154 REMEMBERING HOPE
However, this “positive” view of forgetting is fraught with practical and
ethical problems. Ordering someone to forget—forget the Saint Barthélemy,
don’t talk about the war—is itself a reminder. More importantly, it risks
imposing a false and unsustainable unity on the past by erasing injustices
which, according to victims and their advocates, need to be collectively
remembered so that perpetrators can be called to account and society can
proceed on a new footing.
Considerations like these have led memory scholars to identify several
types of forgetting as the structural counterpart of remembrance. Notable
here is the distinction made by the philosopher Paul Ricoeur between active
and passive forgetting.25 As the term suggests, “active forgetting” occurs
when repressive regimes erase records or occlude a story that was once
known; or when postconflict societies agree to a “pact of forgetting” as
occurred in England in the seventeenth century and in Spain in the post-
Franco transition.26 Passive forgetting, in contrast, is forgetting by default
and is much more subtle in its operation. These are the things forgotten
because they are considered unimportant or, more insidiously, because they
are simply invisible. They are the unknown unknowns. The term “colonial
aphasia” has been suggested by Ann Laura Stoler (2011) to capture this
condition as it affects the memory of colonialism. As she defines it, apha-
sia is an inability to make connections, especially to connect evidence of
colonial violence to the dominant narratives that have hitherto shaped Euro-
pean identities.27 As a result, huge swathes of history have fallen outside of
interpretive frames, forgotten in the commemorative arts and other pub-
lic mediators of shared narratives. They are “disabled” (Stoler 2011)—not
because their traces were actively occluded (though this too can happen),
but because people remain unaware they ever occurred or that the people
affected ever existed. Since such histories do not fit into the “systems of rel-
evance” (Irwin-Zarecka 1994) defining memorability, they are overlooked
and hence passively forgotten. Another way to disable history, which will
feature later in this chapter, is to disconnect the past from the present; rec-
ognize that certain events occurred, but then historicize them as “over and
done with” instead of connecting them to the present day, where their legacy
continues to shape habits of thought and modes of action.
The “disabling” of colonial histories represents a new variation on the
point made earlier in Chapter 3 that memorability is differential. Here the
issue is less the shortage of suitable schemata than the authority, often
state supported, of existing narratives. Debates about who and what should
be publicly commemorated are deeply entangled with social, political,

TOPPLING MONUMENTS 155
and economic mechanisms of exclusion. Societies periodically revise their
systems of relevance in response to the “coming into appearance” (Mirzoeff
2017) of hitherto marginalized and overlooked groups. Public monuments,
as the physical manifestation of dominant narratives, can then become the
sounding board for social transformation.
This still leaves the question how oppositional and hitherto occluded his-
tories actually come “into appearance” in the public arena. Memory activists,
committed to promoting alternative stories, certainly play a role. But advo-
cacy as such does not explain how interpretive frames actually change.
Cultural memory work—in the form of archive-building and storytelling—
is also needed in order to develop the new narratives that replace the old
ones. As we know from earlier chapters, shared narratives neither emerge
nor disappear through the power of any single act of storytelling. Instead,
they are produced in a distributed way as they are mediated and remediated,
in shorthand and in longhand, across different platforms. Recognizing the
importance of “plurimedial networks” (Erll 2014) is an important correc-
tive to the tendency in recent public debates to treat public commemoration
in isolation from the memory culture at large and to treat monuments as if
they were isolated phenomena whose toppling could singlehandedly effect
radical change in the world—be that for better or, as the defenders of the
monuments imply, for worse. That being said, monuments have features that
give them a distinct role in these larger cultural dynamics that explains why
such hopes and fears are invested in them.
Before turning to these features in the next section, an extra dimension
should be noted in these dynamics. The work of “countermemory” entails
undermining the old while also putting something else in its place, which
means that new narratives take shape in a dialectic with the ones already in
circulation.28 As the contestation of monuments shows, proposing an alter-
native narrative goes hand in glove with “decommissioning” (Rigney 2023)
the power of the old; with “de-monumentalizing” (Badilla & Aguilera 2021)
what seemed to be set in stone.
How Monuments Become Toxic
The literature on public monuments has provided insights into the chang-
ing aesthetics of public sculpture, especially the transition in the post–World
War II era from nineteenth-century monumentalism to more minimalistic,
interactive, and ephemeral forms.29 Given this historical variation, few

156 REMEMBERING HOPE
attempts have been made to generalize about the way statues and other
sculptural works operate as carriers of memory. In what follows I try to
remedy this, starting from the assumption that monuments can be reduced
neither to their referent (the person or event they depict) nor to their aes-
thetic form (their character as works of art). Instead, they are material
actants in shifting constellations of other objects, media, narratives, loca-
tions, and human actors. Three aspects of this interplay call for special
attention here.
1. Monuments are shorthand carriers of narrative. Where other media,
text or film for example, can present an individual’s life in a detailed
way, monuments give condensed versions of a story that has been told
in more detail elsewhere (see further Chapter 4). They are reminders
of other cultural carriers of memory with which they resonate and
from which they derive their meaning. A public monument is rarely in
the vanguard of mnemonic and social change and instead consolidates
the memory work done in other media by translating particular narra-
tives into a durable form and, in the process, giving them an officially
sanctioned status.
2. Monuments deploy a culturally specific language. Just as there is a his-
tory of textual genres, so too is there a history of monumental forms, a
plastic language of shorthand narration that has changed over time.
Two columns erected in 1806 and 1808, respectively, illustrate this
principle; although the column to Napoleon in the Place Vendôme
in Paris and the column to Nelson in Dublin were dedicated to arch
enemies, they both took the form of a military leader standing on a
classical column. Britain and France shared a commemorative lan-
guage in this period, a language different from the one that later shaped
the monuments erected in the wake of the two World Wars. As various
studies have shown, there was a major shift in the language of pub-
lic monuments in the twentieth century, affecting both their form and
their subject matter.30 Exemplified by the Vietnam Veterans Memorial
in Washington (erected 1982), this sea change in monument-building
entailed a shift away from the celebration of exceptional heroes to the
mourning of “ordinary” victims, from upward-looking to downward-
looking designs, and from figuration to modernist minimalism in the
aesthetic form. This paradigm shift coincided with a gradual change
in nomenclature. Increasingly the term “monument” (often synonym

TOPPLING MONUMENTS 157
for “statue”) has been replaced by “memorial,” the latter referring to a
piece of public sculpture aiming to provoke reflection on victimhood
rather than monologic awe at achievement. It is important to note that
the statues currently being contested, and the subject of analysis here,
are not usually referred to as memorials. They belong instead to the
monumentalist “great men” tradition of public sculpture, first criti-
cized so powerfully in Friedrich Nietzsche’s “Use and Abuse of History
for Life” (1997 [1874]), which linked it to the celebration of past tri-
umphs taken as a blueprint for the future. In assuming that the world
is set in stone they work against progressive thinking.31 Many statues
currently being contested date from the decades preceding World War
I, a period which also coincided with the high point of colonialism and
its celebratory commemoration. It is not yet clear how more recent
“memorials” will fare in the long term; certainly they do not make the
same claim to permanence as their predecessors.
3. Monuments are material presences in the cityscape. They not only recall
stories circulating elsewhere but also occupy strategic sites—especially
city squares, crossroads, and parks, and other places where people
congregate. There is a pecking order to these locations, with cultural
figures often relegated to the leisure spaces of parks while political
and military leaders are situated closer to the seat of government in
major squares or intersections. Since space is a limited resource espe-
cially in cities, monuments are more prone to the logic of competition
than memory work in other media where, as Michael Rothberg has
influentially argued (2009), one memory does not have to exclude
the other. Precisely because space is subject to a competitive logic,
memory activism often targets monuments.
4. Monuments reflect the distribution of power. Since access to money and
the city space is a precondition, monuments are traditionally erected
by the victors and not by the defeated, by the empowered rather than
the precariat, even as they claim to speak on behalf of the commu-
nity at large. The erection of a costly monument expresses financial,
institutional, and cultural power in the present, while the durability
of its material forms seeks to extend that power into the future (often
indeed, in the form of time capsules buried in the foundations and
designed to bear future testimony to the lives of those who had put
up the statue).32 Since oppositional movements do not have access to

158 REMEMBERING HOPE
resources and state support (unless success gave them national signif-
icance), the memory of civic opposition only rarely takes the form of
public monuments or street names; the concentration of the Com-
mune memory in the Père Lachaise graveyard is indicative of this
limited access to public space.33 As we will see in the next chapter,
activist memory crystallizes in other less weighty and more portable
forms—from necessity but also from a belief that history is unfinished.
5. Monuments have agency. Their physical presence is itself a way of
interpellating citizens and commanding their respect, sometimes even
forcing traffic to go around them.34 In the case of works in the mon-
umentalist tradition, largeness is key to that presence. Their very size
helps to proclaim certain values and moments in the past, while pas-
sively forgetting alternatives. In some cases, they actively forget those
alternatives by literally taking their place. The gigantic monument
erected to Stalin in Budapest in 1951, for example, used some 40,000
tons of bronze recycled from older Hungarian statues which had been
melted down for the purpose of imposing a Soviet-based mnemonic
regime and displacing the nationalist one; the very materials used as
well as the sheer size of the monument bespoke the power of the Stalin-
ist regime (it was subsequently destroyed in the 1956 uprising).35 Their
material presence at key sites thus gives monuments a performative
force that exceeds their symbolic meaning. To use Jane Bennett’s term
(2010), they constitute “vibrant matter,” not least because of their tra-
ditionally imposing weight. They are designed to edify, generate awe,
display power, inspire enthusiasm or, as in the case of more recent
memorials, quiet reflection. The physical vibrancy of monuments as
material objects explains why they can become such toxic presences
in the landscape; how they can insult, irritate, and “attack,” to recall
the words of the Communards.
6. The agency of monuments is intermittent. One of the most intriguing
things about monuments is that their vibrancy is not constant. Not
all monuments are active presences all of the time. “There is noth-
ing in the world as invisible as monuments,” Robert Musil famously
wrote: “conspicuously inconspicuous,” they repel our attention “like
water droplets off oilcloth” (2006 [1936]: 64). As the generations who
erected them die off, monuments lose their meaning.36 A recent sur-
vey among Parisians (Gensburger 2020) suggested that many people
indeed overlook monuments in their neighborhood and often are not

TOPPLING MONUMENTS 159
even aware of having seen them at all. Once the initial buzz around
their erection has passed, they fade into invisibility and inertia. All the
more reason to wonder why, in certain cases and at critical moments,
monuments start to become active presences. They step out of the
urban wallpaper as it were and become objects of concern. Andrew
Shanken (2022) describes monuments being “turned off ” as interest
in them erodes and “turned on” briefly on anniversaries or centenar-
ies. In what follows, I argue that they are also “turned on” at crucial
junctures when their presence becomes toxic. Contestation heightens
their physical presence (or their absence in the case of monuments that
have not yet been built but which memory activists are demanding).
7. Monuments provide a physical platform for bringing countermemory
into visibility. A toxic monument also has a practical importance
as a resource, both physical and symbolic, for bringing into visi-
bility the events and actors it had previously overlooked. Precisely
because they are already in situ as the visible embodiment of the
old regime, they have an enabling role in mnemonic transitions and
in the un-forgetting of occluded histories. Undermining their power
to command respect can paradoxically help in the emergence of the
new. Despite outcries to the contrary, recent iconoclasm has rarely
led to the actual destruction of physical monuments, but instead has
brought into play an entire range of decommissioning practices that
deprive the monument of its power to offend. These include refram-
ing, when monuments are moved to museums where they become
“display objects” (Kirshenblatt-Gimblett 1998) rather than agentic
presences in the city space; and resignifying, when monuments are
overwritten with new plaques, graffiti, or laser displays, or juxtaposed
with another monument in such a way as to take on a new mean-
ing.37 Different strategies of creative vandalism and mockery help
“jam” the meaning of existing monuments. Gestures of disrespect are
a way of publicly performing mnemonic change.38 Best practices are
still being worked out by cultural institutions as memory activists
continue to expand their repertoire for disrupting the meaning of
monuments.39 Alexander Thygesen (2024a), for example, describes
how the creation of a portable statue of a dog called Matapacos
during the protests of 2019 simultaneously challenged existing monu-
ments and made visible a countermemory of Chile in the cityscape of
Santiago.

160 REMEMBERING HOPE
As key sites in the physical memoryscape of a city, moreover, toxic
monuments provide a high-profile assembly point where people can
collectively withdraw their assent to dominant narratives. In their
locatedness, monuments offer a public arena for what Victor Turner
(1980) has called a “social drama”: a ritual of transition that helps a
society move from one identity to another. Even for those who resist
such change and defend the old regime, the monuments literally pro-
vide a common ground for staking out disagreement. They help create
“a shared political space—even if that space is conflictual,” to quote
Sarah Gensburger (2020).
8. Monuments are structurally anachronistic. If their material durability
means laying claim to the future, it is also what allows them regularly to
outlive their moral sell-by date and ability to command respect. Pre-
cisely because they have been built to last, they regularly outlast the
context in which they were produced and the cultural values that led
them to be erected. As alternative narratives emerge in other more
rapid response domains, monuments end up serving as the visible,
public, and tangible reminder of an outdated mnemonic regime. If
their erection is often belated (they usually come late in the dynam-
ics of cultural memory given their cost and high profile character), so
too is their contestation belated: they are often one of the last relics of
an older regime. They are mnemonic fossils whose contestation can be
seen as the highly public capstone to a slow process of transformation.
Crucial to the latter is the availability of an alternative narrative slowly
prepared in other forms of cultural memory work.
In what follows, I bring these theoretical considerations to bear on the dra-
matic toppling of the statue to Edward Colston in Bristol, UK, in June 2020
(Figure 5.4). I then use this case to ponder further the relationship between
mnemonic regime change and social transformation, between memory
activism and activism aiming for social justice.
Erected and Rejected by the Citizens of Bristol
The story of Edward Colston (1636–1721) is well known though it has
shapeshifted over time. A merchant operating from the city of Bristol, Col-
ston was a member of the Royal African Company (1680–1692) and, as such,

TOPPLING MONUMENTS 161
Figure 5.4 Demonstrators throw the statue of Edward Colston into Bristol
Harbour, June 7, 2020. Photo: PA Images. Image Alamy.
a key player in the Atlantic slave trade. Having accumulated serious wealth,
he become a benefactor to the city, where schools and hospitals long carried
his name. From the eighteenth century onward, his munificence was cele-
brated in an annual “Colston day” that gave special buns to children and,
later, a free day to workers. In 1895, more than 150 years after his death, a
statue was “erected by the citizens of Bristol as a memorial of one of the most
virtuous and wise sons of their city” as the plaque put it. Paid for by public
subscription,40 it exemplifies the dual focus of such a monument—in recall-
ing the eighteenth-century merchant it also, indirectly, pays tribute to “the
citizens of Bristol” who commemorated him in the last years of the nine-
teenth century. A fine example of academic art, the larger-than-life effigy
of Colston depicts its subject in a contemplative pose, looking down from
a considerable height. The statue itself was 2.64 meters high and stood on a
3.2-meter plinth: a big presence in a very central spot in the city (Figure 5.5).
What exactly did “the citizens of Bristol” want to remember (and to for-
get) when they put up this tribute to Colston? The memorial plaque frames
Colston as “a virtuous and wise man” and hence as a philanthropist and city
father. Within the broader memory culture of the time, this celebration of
a merchant-cum-benefactor was a way of asserting the rights of the middle

162 REMEMBERING HOPE
Figure 5.5 Statue of Edward Colston, Bristol, June 24, 2019. Photo:
Simon Cobb. CC0.1.0.

TOPPLING MONUMENTS 163
and mercantile classes to be immortalized in public statuary and hence of
challenging the hegemonic tradition of celebrating only military heroes and
members of the aristocracy.41 A recent study has also established (Ball 2018)
a link between the promotion of Colston by the city elite and labor unrest
in the early 1890s; the statue was an attempt on the part of the local elite to
re-assert their role as benefactors in face of the growing importance of orga-
nized labor and demands for workers’ rights by making their fellow citizens
“look up” to Colston. Both explanations are plausible and mutually compat-
ible. Whatever exact combination of factors led to the monument, there is
every reason to assume that Colston’s role in the slave trade was not a central
part of the discussion and that colonial aphasia allowed people to “disable”
the violent origins of his wealth while focusing on his philanthropy.
Since 1895 there has been a sea change. Witness the current English
Wikipedia article on the statue which introduces Colston as a “Bristol-
born merchant and trans-Atlantic slave trader.”42 Once a “wise and virtuous
man,” Colston is now a symbol of Bristol’s shameful complicity with slavery;
his statue an anachronistic reminder that a slave-trader was once honored
(albeit in the guise of a philanthropist). Over the course of a century, the
same object acquired a different meaning: framed no longer in terms of class
as it was in the 1890s, but of race. How did this happen and what role did
the statue toppling play in this transformation of Colston?
The dramatic image showing the statue of Edward Colston being dumped
into Bristol harbor on June 7, 2020, hit the international headlines. While to
outsiders and the international media it seemed to be a sudden outburst of
iconoclastic anger or revolutionary effervescence, research has shown that
the toppling itself did not come out of the blue. Demands to have Colson
removed predated the South African–led “Rhodes Must Fall” movement,
but they did not yet have enough critical mass or enough contemporary
resonance to break through the colonial aphasia that surrounded Bristol’s
hero.43 A critical assessment of his life by Henry J. Wilkins, pointing out his
complicity in the slave trade, had appeared as early as 1920.44 In 1998 the
words “slave trader” had been scrawled on the monument, while in 2007 it
had been daubed in red paint. In parallel, there were concerted efforts, spear-
headed by a group called “Countering Colston—Campaign to Decolonise
Bristol,” to have the name and image of Colston removed from public build-
ings (this led to a decision, in 2017, to rename the concert hall and, in
2018, to remove Colston’s portrait from the mayoral office).45 There had also
been lengthy negotiations about the wording of a new plaque which had

164 REMEMBERING HOPE
nevertheless failed to reach agreement on how to formulate the nature of
Colston’s involvement, and hence the implication of the city, in the transat-
lantic slave trade.46 The statue gradually became toxic as a result of this
long-term memory activism.
Although Colston was the most visible target of these decolonial cam-
paigns, they were less about downgrading him as an individual than an
attempt to break through the pervasive “disabling” of the city’s implica-
tion in chattel slavery, which explained both its historical wealth and the
significant Black-Caribbean minority in the city today. Decommissioning
and un-forgetting are two sides of the same coin. Hence the fact that the
anti-Colston campaign ran in parallel to a series of milestones linked to
the recovery of Bristol’s hitherto “disabled” history and the production of
a counter-narrative. New historical research revealing the extent of Bris-
tol’s indebtedness to slavery came into circulation through the efforts of
the local museum and civil society organizations, including a Slave Trade
Action Group founded in 1997. This revised narrative of Bristol’s history
entered into the public sphere, slowly but surely, across different media:
Philippa Gregory’s novel A Respectable Trade (1995) and its adaptation by
the BBC to a four-part TV series in 1998; a commemorative plaque to the
victims of the slave trade (1998); the naming of the Pero bridge after one
of the few identifiable victims (2007); a permanent exhibition on slavery
in the city’s M Shed museum (2018). The national Anti-Slavery Day (inau-
gurated in 2010) also regularly provided an occasion for memory activism,
including most notably an installation from 2018 which highlighted Col-
ston’s complicity with the slave trade by adding the outlines of a slave ship
to the pavement around the statue. Dozens of supine figures were lined up
within the boundaries of the virtual hold while a link was established to con-
temporary forms of slavery through labels such as “domestic servants” and
“fruit pickers” (Figure 5.6).47 The statue of Colston, now toxic, ironically pro-
vided a platform for showcasing alternative perspectives in ways that were
more arresting, if less durable, than the addition of a revised plaque might
have been. Continued tributes to a slave-trader, it was implied, were helping
to buttress ongoing forms of exploitation and inequality.
Taken together, these archival and artistic initiatives indicate, first, that
the groundwork for the toppling of the statue in June 2020 had been pre-
pared in other cultural practices and, second, that they were implicitly
and sometimes explicitly entangled with campaigns to make society more
inclusive and to combat social inequality. In all of this, Bristol’s history

TOPPLING MONUMENTS 165
Figure 5.6 Installation, Anti-Slavery Day, Bristol, October 18, 2018. Photo:
Stuart Holdsworth_inspiringcity.com. Reproduced with kind permission.
was of interest, not as mere antiquarianism but because of its relevance
to the present. As long as the citizens of Bristol did not recognize their
own past, it was implied, social justice could never be achieved. Con-
versely, recognizing that past should lead—if not immediately, then sooner

166 REMEMBERING HOPE
rather than later—to social justice. In a two-way process, local history was
uploaded to the larger decolonial narratives being produced by memory
activists in national and transnational arenas; conversely, these larger nar-
ratives were helping to reframe local history. This process came to a head in
June 2020 in the aftermath of the murder of George Floyd and the resur-
gence of the Black Lives Matter movement it triggered. While the BLM
protesters on the streets in Bristol were primarily protesting against systemic
racism in the present, Colston’s statue became the immediate target of their
anger because, as the narrative above suggests, it had come to symbolize
a mnemonic regime that buttressed racism. It provided a public platform
for bringing the Black Lives Matter narrative into visibility in its spectacu-
lar (and unplanned) toppling into the harbor and then, later, in the posters
and graffiti with which the empty plinth was bedecked (Figure 5.7). As
these examples show, memory activism and antiracism were indeed deeply
intertwined.
Within the emerging counternarrative, the statue honoring Colston had
acquired several new layers: it was a reminder that slavery had occurred in
the past; that those who erected the statue in the 1890s could ignore that
fact; and that the present-day authorities who were letting his statue stand
were indirectly perpetuating the normalization of slavery. Keeping Colston
in place, despite repeated attempts by civil society groups to have it removed
or relabeled, had become symbolic of unreconstructed racism. This argu-
ment would later be used in the legal defense of the so-called Colston Four,
who were accused of criminal damage to the statue; namely, that instead
of being guilty of a criminal act, they were actually “preventing a crime” in
toppling Colston since “it was a criminal offense to keep that statue up.”48 As
a guerrilla retooling of the plaque on the empty pedestal on June 11, 2020,
made clear, the statue that was once “erected by the citizens of Bristol” was
now “rejected by the citizens of Bristol” (Figure 5.8). That a jury should have
acquitted the Colston Four of wrongdoing in January 2022 indicates that this
rejection was widely shared—though not by everyone, as the follow-up will
show.
The day after the statue was dumped in the harbor, it was retrieved by the
city authorities (they were legally responsible for the monument, designated
a grade II structure by Historic England and hence legally subject to special
protection). Faced with the quandary of what to do with an unwanted yet
listed monument, the mayor set up an independent body called the “We Are
Bristol History Committee” assigned the task of coming up with guidelines

TOPPLING MONUMENTS 167
Figure 5.7 Pedestal of the Colston monument, Bristol, June 7, 2020. Photo:
Caitlin Hobbes. CC by 3.0.
to make the monumental landscape of the city more inclusive while also his-
torically accurate.49 In February 2022 the committee recommended that the
Colston statue be kept permanently in the local museum: to become a histor-
ical display object rather than an irritant in the public space.50 Accordingly,

168 REMEMBERING HOPE
Figure 5.8 Reworked plaque of the Colston monument, Bristol, June 11, 2020.
Photo: JMF News. Image Alamy.
it went on permanent exhibition in the museum in March 2024; literally
and figuratively taken down from its pedestal, it was placed in a reclin-
ing position inside a protective glass cabinet and located in an exhibition
space devoted to the Black Lives Matter protests (Figure 5.9).51 Still carry-
ing the daubs of paint left by the protest, it is now hidden from view behind
a wall of banners in an exhibition space devoted to the Black Lives Matter
protests. A poll suggested that 80 percent of the citizens of Bristol support
this relocation.52
In these different ways, the statue now carries the memory of Colston,
the memory of his nineteenth-century supporters, and, finally, the memory
of the protests that led to his toppling.53 The result is a more complex and
palimpsestic story; it is “not an attack on history. It is history,” as the historian
David Olusoga put it.54
The committee also recommended in February 2022 that the empty plinth
be used for temporary artworks while including some periods of “inten-
tional emptiness.”55 Besides changing the monumental landscape of cities
across the world, the present iconoclastic wave is making it necessary to
rethink the nature of public monuments and, indeed, the wisdom of putting
them up at all given their inbuilt obsolescence. This has led among other

TOPPLING MONUMENTS 169
Figure 5.9 Statue of Bristol slave trader Edward Colston, M Shed museum,
Bristol, June 4, 2021. Photo: Zefrog. Image Alamy.
things to the making of ephemeral and interactive public markers of mem-
ory that, in depending on the participation of stakeholders, avoid the trap
of monumentalism. Alexander Thygesen offers a case in point in describing
the activist-led commemorative garden established in Santiago in the after-
math of the 2019 uprising as an “organic monument” (2024b). The relative
paucity of public monuments to activists, mentioned earlier, reflects both a
lack of power and an antimonumentalism that is inherent in a commitment
to change.56
The Limits of Memory Activism
So, some things do change. The dominant view of Colston seems irre-
vocably altered, and his return to unquestioned pre-eminence in the city
inconceivable. Witness the Wikipedia article mentioned earlier. Witness
too the actual disbanding in September 2020 of the Colston society, which
had been instrumental in celebrating his legacy over a period of almost
three centuries. Witness finally the acquittal of the Colston Four of crim-
inal wrongdoing in January 2022, which provided a further endorsement

170 REMEMBERING HOPE
of the legitimacy of the campaign against Colston. At first sight, then, the
case provides a good example of how memory and identity are subject to
negotiation in a democracy. Indeed, we could see it as a success story in
memory activism to the extent that initiatives taken in civil society have led
to mnemonic change as expressed in heritage and educational policies being
developed by the city authorities. In this regard, the toppling of Colston in
June 2020 can be seen as the definitive turning point in a transformative
drama.
However, this neat story needs to be qualified along two lines involving,
on the one hand, the counterprotesters and, on the other, the decolonial
activists themselves. Together these sound a warning against assuming that
mnemonic change feeds into political change, as Gutman and Wüstenberg’s
definition of memory activism implied. On the contrary, the fact that the
campaign against Colston was always both about his remembrance and
about systemic racism meant that the statue could also become a proxy for
politics. In the first instance, on the part of activists who saw it as a step-
ping stone; subsequently also on the part of defenders of the status quo. A
carrier of meaning or an inert object? A toxic perpetuation of the legacy of
slavery or an art work deserving of protection as part of national heritage?
After the statue had fallen the ambiguities as well as the fault lines became
more visible.
To begin with, there was pushback in defense of tradition. Like those who
earlier resisted changes to the plaque, counterprotesters saw criticism of Col-
ston as an assault on what they framed, not as history or memory, but as an
unalienable and unchangeable “heritage.” Framed in this way, any challenge
to Colston’s standing was a threat to collective identity, which was all the
more pernicious since it came from “outsiders.”57 The toppling of Colston
thus also led to renewed expressions of support for tradition, a defense of
heritage that was implicitly a proxy for a politics of exclusion.
Linked to this, there was pushback from the Conservative government,
which introduced legislation proposing sentences of up to ten years in prison
for damage to public monuments.58 The “Police, Crime, Sentencing and
Courts Act 2022 section 50: Criminal Damages to Memorials,” which came
into law in April 2022, is ostensibly about the protection of heritage. But
it is part of a larger policy to limit the space for public demonstrations in
general, and to criminalize those directed toward monuments in particular.
Critics of the new law have pointed to its being less about protecting works of
public art than about blocking the expression of any dissent to government

TOPPLING MONUMENTS 171
policies including those linked to campaigns against racial inequality.59
Here again, monuments matter because, in being “inalienable heritage,” they
have become proxies for a politics of exclusion.
From the perspective of activists, proxy politics come into play in a differ-
ent and more subtle way. The case study has shown how memory activism
formed a productive if informal alliance with the Black Lives Matter move-
ment in June 2020. But since then, what has changed besides Colston’s
status? Yes, Colston has been decommissioned and the city’s mnemonic
landscape is on the way to being more inclusive; a corner has been turned.
However, voices are still being raised about inequality in housing and access
to resources; so what has really changed? Perhaps Colston is the “ultimate
distraction,” one journalist wrote a year later (Cork 2021); why are we still
talking about him “when we’ve got immediate issues in this city?” Although
the statue was clearly important as a symbol of ongoing racism, and its
decommissioning an important activist tactic, its decommissioning has not
(yet) yielded the social transformation that was the activists’ ultimate objec-
tive. As long as Colston was still standing, the statue could be a vector in the
struggle for social justice; the fact that he is now lying prostate in a museum
(along with traces of the protest, ironically now a part of heritage) does not
mean the struggle is over. The drama of the toppling suggests mistakenly that
closure has been reached. Similar risks have been observed in relation to the
drama of public apologies for historical injustice. Those offering the apology
see it as a form of closure, while those receiving it see it as a hopeful start
toward a more thoroughgoing shakeup of relations.60 In short, there’s always
a danger that memory activism becomes an end in itself rather than one of
the means toward an end. Too great a focus on changes in the monumental
landscape might make it seem as if racism is now finished business and that
the protest too belongs in the museum; hence David Olusogu’s warning in
June 2020 that “The ‘statue wars’ must not distract us from a reckoning with
racism.”61
I began this chapter by suggesting that the current iconoclastic wave
seems unique in not being a byproduct of military conflict or politi-
cal revolution, but of contentious politics. They are bottom-up attempts
on the part of activists to change the collective narrative as a step
toward changing society. While the Colston case yields many insights
into the long-term dynamics of remembering and (un)forgetting, it also
points to the limits of memory activism as such in bringing about social
transformation.

Chapter 6
Activist Archiving as Prefigurative Practice
Communards Posing for Posterity (1871)
One of the most famous images to come out of the Commune, made by
Auguste Bruno Braquehais on or around May 16, 1871, relates to the demo-
lition of the Vendôme column. It shows a group of Communards standing
together on the ruins of the toppled monument (Figure 6.1): uniformed
members of the national guard, some of them raising their hats in salute,
workers in flat caps, several men in top hats, a matronly looking woman,
two children standing front and central stage. They stand together in their
differences, demonstrating to the camera the fact that they were once there
and acted together. Like the veterans who figured “live” in Guerra’s movie
La Commune (1914), discussed in Chapter 4, these Communards are posing
for posterity.
Braquehais’s photograph is usually considered simply in terms of its doc-
umentary value as a record of that momentous day in 1871. It can be framed
differently: as an attempt on the part of the Communards to create, in collab-
oration with their supporter Braquehais, a durable record of their collective
achievement in taking over Paris and putting an end both to the old political
regime and the mnemonic regime that supported it.1 In posing for the cam-
era, the Communards were collaborating with the photographer in order to
address future viewers.
This collaborative view of photography is inspired by Ariella Azoulay’s
work on the “civil contract of photography” (2008) which argues that those
who allow themselves to be photographed are not merely objects of the pho-
tographer’s gaze but coproducers of their own depiction. Seen from this
perspective, Braquehais’s photograph not only provides a record of how
the Communards changed the mnemonic regime by destroying the col-
umn; it also shows them laying the foundations for an alternative mnemonic
regime in which they themselves—their lives and hopes—take center-stage.
In place of a material monument, they cooperate with the photographer to
inscribe themselves in an image which, in recording their existence, makes
Remembering Hope. Ann Rigney, Oxford University Press. © Oxford University Press (2025).
DOI: 10.1093/oso/9780197789711.003.0007

ACTIVIST ARCHIVING AS PREFIGURATIVE PRACTICE 173
Figure 6.1 Communards posing on the ruins of the Vendôme Column, circa
May 16, 1871. Photograph Auguste Bruno Braquehais. Public domain.
Courtesy Bibliothèque nationale de France.
the future remembrance of their achievement possible. As Nicolas Mirzoeff
once wrote, “Where the police use photography to order and control, peo-
ple can use photographs to send a message to present and future audiences”
(2017: n.p.).
Braquehais’s photograph allows us to explore further the retro-
prospective character of activist memory. We have seen earlier with
reference to the marking of anniversaries and the composition of narratives
that looking back can be a way of moving forward by reconnecting with
unfinished business and possibilities in the past. Here a new variation
presents itself: activist-driven memory work focused on ensuring the future
remembrance of the unfolding present or the very recent past. How will
what is happening now be remembered in times to come? The memory
work carried out by the Commune photograph is set on capturing the
promises and commitments embedded in unfolding events so that they can
be recalled sometime in the future.

174 REMEMBERING HOPE
Anticipating future remembrance is, as I have found, a recurring theme
in social movements. Up to a point this is simply a byproduct of the futu-
ral orientation of collective action and the hopes driving it. But securing a
place in future collective memory may also become a strategic aim. When
a movement starts to wane or faces an existential threat, as the Commune
did in May 1871, taking steps to be remembered in the future offers a better-
than-nothing alternative to maintaining momentum in the present. We may
not have fulfilled the promise of this moment, the reasoning goes, but at
least we will have made our mark and passed on to others a sense of that
promise and of our commitment. Indeed, when a movement is dissipating,
the records produced during the mobilization become by design or default
a last will and testament addressed to later generations. Braquehais’s pho-
tograph becomes all the more powerful as a testimony to the Communards
because, retrospectively, it came to mark their end. Once we take on board
the idea that both social movements and memories are dynamic in charac-
ter, the temporalities at play become more complex. As events unfold, and
the baton is passed from one set of stakeholders to the next, prospection and
retrospection continuously cross over.
This chapter unravels this complexity by delving deeper into the ways
activists “sediment” their own work. I use “sedimentation” following
Donatella della Porta (2020), who sees it as one of the keys to a movement’s
capacity to reproduce itself and gain footing in society. Movements sedi-
ment in cultural forms—photographs, texts, documentary footage, slogans,
graffiti, lexical inventions.2 We have encountered examples of such sedimen-
tation in earlier chapters. The focus here will be on “archives” as a cultural
form and “archiving” as a social-cultural practice. How do activists engage
proactively in making, collecting, and preserving records so as to ensure
their own afterlife in memory? Building on Arjun Appadurai’s observation
that archives are “important vehicles for building the capacity to aspire”
(2003: 25),3 I show with reference to recent movements that archiving has
evolved into a specific form of activist memory work that seeks to extend
protest in time.
In providing a storehouse for future knowledge, archiving is congenial
to the future-oriented character of activist projects as such. Moreover, this
chapter will argue that archiving is especially congenial to the politics of pre-
figuration that has inspired recent movements. The concept of prefiguration
in politics has been in circulation since the 1970s and became widespread
in the 2000s, offering social movements an alternative to the socialist grand

ACTIVIST ARCHIVING AS PREFIGURATIVE PRACTICE 175
narratives that lost credibility after 1989. Prefiguration describes the belief
that the work of social transformation should begin in small-scale actions in
the here and now rather than being relegated to some revolutionary moment
in the future when everything is expected to change all at once.4 The belief
that the “future is now” (Monticelli 2022) entails the principle that means
and ends should be in harmony; that collective action in the present should
be in keeping with the ideals for which people are struggling. Prefiguration
as praxis is thus “the embodiment, within the ongoing political practice of a
movement, of those forms of social relations, decision-making, culture, and
human experience that are the ultimate goal” (Boggs 1977: 4). As a recent
survey puts it, prefigurative politics is the “experimental implementation of
desired future social relations and practices in the here-and-now” (Raek-
stad & Gradin 2020: 10). In line with these principles, prefigurative action
often takes the form of initiatives for new forms of living together. These
do not directly challenge the system in the form of contention, but instead
establish alternatives. They hollow out the system from within in the form
of “concrete utopias.”5 The small-scale and localized character of such initia-
tives leaves prefigurative politics open to the question whether the model can
ever be upscaled to affect larger parts of the world and for longer periods of
time; and whether prefigurative action alone without more offensive forms
of contention is enough to challenge existing systems.6 Whatever the answer
to these questions, prefigurative politics is about demonstrating possibilities
and, in that sense, it exemplifies hope in action.
This chapter shows that the collective making of a movement’s archive
can itself be a prefigurative praxis exemplifying the social ideals that the
movement aspires to bring into the world at large. What makes archiving
distinctive as a form of prefigurative action is that it is designed to enable
latecomers to connect back to the spirit of the movement with the help of
retrievable records. It helps to “upscale” it in a minor but important way:
by sedimenting the unfolding present in the form of a Commons, activist
archives offer to future users an enduring proof of concept of social trans-
formation. As one activist in the Occupy Wall Street movement put it, “If we
believe ‘another world is possible,’ then another archive must also be possi-
ble.”7 The synergy between prefigurative politics and archiving is facilitated
by the affordances of the archive as a cultural medium: it makes sense of
events, not in the closed form of a narrative, but in an open form that offers
multiple possibilities for meaning-making. This allows it to create future
remembrance in a nonmonumentalist way; that is, without dictating the
meaning of the present to the future.

176 REMEMBERING HOPE
Archiving as Activist Memory Work
Scholars in the field of memory studies have tended to consider archives
as falling outside their purview. Reasons range from the idea that “the
archive” is the locus of official or state-organized knowledge whereas mem-
ory is the site of dissenting voices, to the idea that archives, associated with
written knowledge, exclude other modes of embodied and performative
transmission. Witness the influential opposition drawn up by Diana Tay-
lor (2003) between the “archive” (associated with inscription, fixity, and
state power) and the “repertoire” (associated with ever-renewed forms of
knowledge transferred through performance rather than fixed forever in
script). These critical positions are held together by an assumption that goes
back to Romantic historicism and its gravitation toward the silences of his-
tory; namely, that the most valuable things to remember are things that
have “escaped” and now form the “an-archive” made up of information and
affects that were uncollected and indeed uncollectible in a traditional (read:
script-based and state-run) repository. One of the aims of this chapter is to
integrate archiving into the broader study of mnemonic practices and related
cultural forms.8
In doing so, I take my cue from Aleida Assmann, who has argued in impor-
tant interventions that the “archive” is not the polar opposite of “memory.”
They stand in a dialectical relation as part of an ongoing alternation between
storing information and processing it. Although Assmann’s terminology and
focus have slightly changed over time, her approach remains based on the
idea of an ever-shifting boundary between passive and active forms of mem-
ory, with passive “storage memory” associated with the archive and active
“working memory” associated with narratives in circulation (Assmann 1999,
343–407). The archive is “located on the border between forgetting and
remembering,” meaning it preserves materials in “intermediary storage” and
in “a state of latency” operating between the “no longer” and the “not yet”
(Assmann 2008: 103).
Archives always contain more information than is needed for present-
day sense-making. This very excess means that they have the potential to
become a resource for alternative narratives. They keep open the possibility
that collective memory can be reconfigured in the future thanks to “rogue”
elements (De Kosnik 2016) or new constellations of meaning becoming
visible.9 More so than narrative forms of memory work that look back
on what has already happened and give it a coherent meaning from a
later point in time, archives have a future orientation. They are a site

ACTIVIST ARCHIVING AS PREFIGURATIVE PRACTICE 177
of latent rather than actualized meaning. As Jacques Derrida famously
put it:
[T]he question of the archive is not, we repeat, a question of the past. . . . It
is a question of the future, the question of the future itself, the question of a
response, of a promise and of a responsibility for tomorrow. The archive: if
we want to know what that will have meant, we will only know in the times
to come. (1996 [1995]: 36)
This association between archives and futurity goes against the clichéd view
of them as super-organized arrangements of inert textual records encap-
sulating and perpetuating the perspectives of those in power. It aligns
them instead with “the capacity to aspire,” to recall Appadurai’s words, by
facilitating the intergenerational transfer of promises and hopes.
This provides a new framework for examining the role of archives in
shaping the memory of protest, specifically archives initiated by activists
themselves. This topic brings us from the memory activism discussed in
the previous chapter to activist memory work conducted as an integral part
of activism directed toward social change.10 Where memory activism chal-
lenges hegemonic narratives, activist memory work is about remembering
activism itself. It aims to counter the misrepresentations of given social
movements, but above all to prolong their lifespan and amplify their impact
by giving them a second life in memory. As earlier chapters have shown
with reference to the Commune and the deaths of demonstrators, the battle
for memory—to be remembered and not forgotten, and to be remembered
in one’s own terms rather than those of the state or mainstream media—is
itself an intrinsic part of contentious politics. Although a movement may be
defeated on the streets, the argument goes, it should not be the victors who
get to tell the story. And as the cases here show, the battle for the power of
future definition can start even as a movement is unfolding.
Before turning to specific cases, recent perspectives on “archives” and
“archiving” need to be brought into play.
A Changing Praxis
Theorizations of archiving in cultural studies have often assumed a singu-
lar model of “the” archive, seen as institutionalized collections produced by
professionals, funded by the state, and hence linked to governance and the

178 REMEMBERING HOPE
exercise of power. However, as a wealth of publications in the field of archival
studies has demonstrated, there have been major changes in the nature of
archiving in recent decades.11 These have led to new concepts, practices, and
“archival imaginaries” (Chidgey 2020: 226) that go well beyond the basic
model of state-based repositories. Suffice it here to recall some key discus-
sions so as to provide context for the cases that follow and as a reminder that
archiving is a social-cultural-material practice that has evolved over time,
both within and outside state-funded institutions.
1. The last decades have seen the emergence of community-led archives
dependent on the participation of citizen-stakeholders who play a key
role as producers and users of records. This democratization, facili-
tated by digitization, has created new forms of cooperation between
communities and professional archivists. Many of the latter have come
to see their role less as gatekeepers and custodians than as facilita-
tors and sometimes indeed as activists supporting minoritized groups,
with the term “archival activism” entering the vocabulary of the pro-
fession.12
2. As this reference to minoritized groups suggests, archives have
acquired new functions beyond their traditional role in preserving and
managing records. Online and in-site collections, made by amateurs as
well as professionals, are resources for groups to establish themselves
as collective agents, both to themselves (through shared participation
in the archive) and to the outside world (by displaying their capacity
to “have” a distinct archive and hence distinct history).13 Stuart Hall
(2008) already wrote of the “constitutive function” of archiving, some-
thing that can be observed, for example, in the importance of archiving
within the LGBT+ movement since the 1970s.14
3. The border between passive memory and active memory, to recall
Assmann’s distinction, has in turn become more porous. Curation
and public-facing activities are now among an archive’s core tasks:
exhibitions make selections of materials accessible and bring them
together into meaningful, albeit temporary constellations. As a result,
archives can no longer be conceptualized as closed spaces encap-
sulating a past that is completed, filed away, and only meant to
provide a resource for historians. They are open or “living” sys-
tems that build bridges between the past and present-day concerns,
offer a resource for identity-building and, by bringing materials into
unfamiliar combinations, for new interpretations of the world. For this

ACTIVIST ARCHIVING AS PREFIGURATIVE PRACTICE 179
reason, Phil Cohen (2018) has described archives as having an artis-
tic dimension: they bring “unlikely things together” and, in doing so,
become sites where not only the past is produced but also the future.15
