This changed archival landscape provides a new context for understand-
ing archiving as a mnemonic practice and for archival imaginaries as part
of memory culture. Specifically, the idea of a “living archive,” a term that
runs through the material discussed below and stands in opposition to the
monumental, helps us understand recent practices relating to the archiving
of social movements.16
Toward Living Archives
Even after protesters have left the streets, struggles over the power to define
their actions continue both in narrative and in the making of archives. A
recent essay (Merrill 2024) has highlighted the way the police systemati-
cally make recordings of protest with a view to possible prosecutions and
future surveillance. Protesters and their supporters also look for ways to
build archives while operating from a less powerful position. Being able to
capture the attention of journalists is key to a movement’s success—even if
it comes at the cost of having to answer to criteria set by the media them-
selves.17 If no records are kept, a movement has no chance of re-entering
public memory after it has lost its value as news. Extra effort is accordingly
required to safeguard the memory of protest and, more generally, of what
Ann Cvetkovich has called the “fragility of activism’s histories” (2003: 172).
These efforts are made by activists themselves, by their supporters (there
is often an overlap between these groups), and by friendly institutions
devoted to protecting the legacy of oppositional movements. These include
the state-funded International Institute for Social History in Amsterdam,
an institution that has been providing a home for the archives of social
movements since the 1920s; the Archiv für alternatives Schrifttum based in
Duisburg; and the Contemporaine center in Paris. A recent study by Red
Chidgey and Joanne Garde-Hansen, significantly titled Museums, Archives
and Protest Memory (2024), references key British cases, but also provides
a more generally relevant outline of the professional and ethical dilemmas
involved in the transfer of archival materials from the hands of activists to
those of professional curators as well as some of the creative solutions being

180 REMEMBERING HOPE
pioneered by the latter. The shared aim is the fostering of a “protest sen-
sibility” by preserving and curating the traces of earlier protest, ensuring
that “images, ideas and feelings of past liberation struggles become freshly
available and transmissible in times not of their making” (17).
Although a growing number of such institutions offer a home to activist
collections, as Chidgey and Garde-Hansen show, the relationship between
protest movements and institutions remains a delicate one and, for both
parties, difficult to navigate. Institutions may be reluctant to compromise
their professional autonomy and professional standards by entering into
too close an alliance with oppositional groups given different priorities and
skillsets.18 Conversely, activist groups may be wary of cooperation with her-
itage institutions for fear of becoming commodified or sanitized, or of having
to cede control and even ownership of the movement’s legacy to profes-
sional archivists.19 In response to such ethical and professional dilemmas,
new policies and practices are continuously emerging in tandem with digital
technologies, including the idea of the “post-custodial” (Ham 1981) archive,
which allows the creator of a document or image to have more say in its
management.
It is against this background that we can understand recent calls by
activists to develop their own “autonomous” archives independent of cul-
tural institutions.20 This entails collecting and managing records, using
open source software for digital holdings, that both reflect and advance the
political principles of the movements they document. It also entails an anti-
monumentalist view of archives which sees them as “living” assemblages
rather than dead zones; even as they sediment the past, they continue to
be sites of engagement with new mobilizations. Alycia Sellie et al. (2015)
have developed the term “activist archives” to designate such activist-led
archiving. Exemplified by the Interference Archive in New York, these ini-
tiatives deal with the records of earlier activism while also offering a virtual
or physical place for debate, hence contributing to the “ongoing produc-
tion of social movements with which they identify” (454). Likewise, Rose-
marie Grennan (2024) has described The MayDay Rooms in London as
an “archive, resource and safe haven for social movements, experimental
and marginal cultures and their histories.”21 The creation of such spaces has
been important in the development of rights-based movements involved
in consciousness-raising over a longer period.22 As we will see in the later
sections, moreover, they have also been important, albeit in different ways,
to more ephemeral protest movements.

ACTIVIST ARCHIVING AS PREFIGURATIVE PRACTICE 181
Activist archiving as idea and as practice emerged in tandem with mul-
tiple DIY online resources offering advice to movements wishing to set
up their own archive as well as providing a metadiscourse outlining the
rationale for doing so. The Australia-based The Commons: Social Change
Library, for example, listed among the motives for activist self-archiving the
issue of accessibility (making the movement and its aspirations accessible
to supporters), self-determination (allowing it to shape its own memory),
accountability (allowing it to hold perpetrators of violence against protesters
to account sometime in the future); education (disseminating knowledge
about the movement to latecomers), and continuity (providing inspiration
for later movements).23 The transnational ubiquity of this metadiscourse
indicates that skills in record-keeping and adherence to an archival imagi-
nary have come to be part of the activist toolkit, indeed part of the repertoire
of collective action itself.24 In what follows, I explore a selection of the
archives produced by “the movements of the squares” in the period since
2011 in order to show the centrality of archiving, not only to their repertoire
of contention but also to their prefigurative politics.25
Archives from the Squares (2011–2016)
While the period since 2010 has been called “the mass protest decade”
(Bevins 2023), this analysis will focus on a particular subset of recent move-
ments, namely those taking the form of an extended occupation of city
squares. Specifically, the Egyptian Uprising (2011), the 15-M movement
(2011), Occupy Wall Street (2011), the Gezi Park Uprising (2013), and
Nuit Debout (2016).26 Despite major differences in the political contexts
in which these occupations occurred, they shared a commitment to rein-
venting democracy along radically horizontal lines as part of a politics of
prefiguration (mixed in, depending on the context, with more or less overt
acts of contention challenging government policies).27 This translated into
new forms of assembly but also, in the context of a prolonged occupation
of the city squares where the movements played out, new ways of orga-
nizing daily life on a communal basis: feeding those present, defending
the group from the incursions by the police, providing a communications
infrastructure to foster internal cohesion, and, by proffering images to out-
siders, recruiting new supporters. Documenting events in medias res posed
challenges given the vulnerability of the materials (such as banners made

182 REMEMBERING HOPE
from sauce-stained pizza boxes susceptible to mold),28 the horizontal nature
of the organization, and the sheer volume of images and texts produced
on a daily basis. In contrast to activist campaigns taking the form of dis-
crete demonstrations, the temporally extended character of an occupation
proved conducive to making archives since it gave demonstrators the time
to organize.
Helped by the availability of smartphones, participants produced a huge
number of records—especially images—for immediate circulation on social
media and through the movement’s own channels. Alongside images doc-
umenting clashes with the police and their use of excessive force, activist-
produced records, such as the minutes of the daily assemblies, relate to the
peaceful pursuit of new forms of coexistence in the occupied space. Many
of the images circulated on social media highlighted moments of protesters
sharing, participating, and collaborating in different civic activities and oth-
erwise enjoying themselves. Like the painting of the Place Pigalle in 1871
mentioned in Chapter 4, they offer glimpses of the new social relations
the movement was bringing into existence.29 Writing with reference to the
Spanish 15-M movement, Juan Egea (2022) points to the emergence of a
counter-visuality through photos, including selfies, whose on-the-ground
perspective offered an alternative to the images that were circulating in
the newspapers, which often took the helicopter perspective of the crowd
as a whole surrounded by police.30 In bringing this counter-visuality into
circulation, protesters were able “to send a message to present and future
audiences,” to recall Mirzoeff ’s words.
As images were feeding back into the sense of an emerging community,
people also began to collect records, with a view to constituting collec-
tions that would allow these activities to be remembered in the future.
Beginning with the Egyptian Uprising and 15-M, it became common for
working groups to take upon themselves the task of recording events and
collecting the images, texts, and protest-related realia produced by oth-
ers. In a pioneering article on activist archiving (which also covers two of
the five cases discussed here), Red Chidgey (2020) emphasized the huge
labor and immense care invested in collecting materials in a rapid response
environment—sometimes also at risk of arrest.31 She also notes that the
concept of a “living archive” figured prominently in these labors as “a dis-
course, a practice, and an idea” (2020: 226): the idea that the archive could
continue the work of mobilization and advocacy. Chidgey’s main concern is
with the shifting relations between actors, institutions, and records as well

ACTIVIST ARCHIVING AS PREFIGURATIVE PRACTICE 183
as the structural precarity to which these lead. In what follows, I build on her
work while exploring a wider range of cases and with a different aim: to get
a better grasp of activist self-archiving as a mnemonic practice operating as
a cultural connector between different waves of mobilization and hence as
a vector of hope.
Studies have shown that a concerted effort was made from the early days
of the 2011 uprising in Egypt to collect videos, with a media tent set up for
this purpose in Tahrir Square.32 As the current website puts it, the aim was
to “contribute to the digital memory of the event” as well as to “document
police abuses and killings of protesters” with a view to future accountabil-
ity. A key player in this initiative was the Mosireen collective, which took
upon itself the task of making video records and, after the uprising had been
crushed, of collecting the video footage made by others and even arranging
for its screening.33 Their activities were severely curtailed after the military
coup of 2013 and the repression that followed, but they resumed after sev-
eral years and, in 2018, the archive went online, including no fewer than
42,000 files.34 The name of the archive—858.ma—corresponds to the num-
ber of hours of video footage which, as the home page outlines, is intended
to grow as the archive itself becomes more known, other participants in the
protest add to it, and other protests take place. It is subtitled “an archive of
resistance” and described by at least one commentator as a “living archive”
(Antoun 2012):
858 is, of course, just one archive of the revolution. It is not, and can never
be, the archive. It is one collection of memories, one set of tools that we
can all use to fight the narratives of the counter-revolution, to pry loose the
state’s grip on history, to keep building new histories for the future. (Antoun
2012)
Within days of the start of the 15-M occupation of the Puerta del Sol in
Madrid, which also took place in 2011, a stall was set up in the square
in charge of “documentation and archiving” (Egea 2022). It was run by an
archiving commission that worked not only throughout the occupation but
also in the following years in order to bring multiple collections together
into the integrated Archivo 15M, hence safeguarding the collective mem-
ory of the movement and its material legacy.35 The lengthy minutes of the
committee’s own deliberations, headed by the motto “so that this may not
be forgotten” (“que no se olvide”), can be found online along with a catalog

184 REMEMBERING HOPE
of the archive’s holdings. In contrast to other movements whose documen-
tation is exclusively digital, the core of the 15-M archive is a substantial
collection of material artifacts (banners, placards, and documents, including
many handwritten proposals (“propuestas”) outlining people’s hopes for the
movement).36 This collection is currently housed in the Tres Peces cultural
center in Madrid, along with the 15-M library (a collection of canonical left-
wing political writings as well as literary works, originally available on-site
at the Puerta del Sol during the occupation). While the bulk of the open-
access collection pertains to 2011, it is also a “living archive” in that it invites
contributions relating to new movements in a manner that echoes the work
of the 15-M calendar discussed in Chapter 2.3 7
Occupy Wall Street, which looked to 15-M as its model in many
regards, had its own “Archives Working Group” led by activists with some
professional experience in archiving.38 Against those who did not con-
sider archiving an immediate priority, this subgroup argued that it was
vital to maintain a permanent record of the decisions taken at the Gen-
eral Assembly as well as to save ephemera such as banners from overly
eager museum collectors in search of potential exhibition material. Their
stated mission was to collect artifacts, images, and documents that would
stand as:
evidence of how participatory democracy can work, how culture and pol-
itics connect, and how the 99% can come together to generate social and
economic change. Its mission is to keep OWS [Occupy Wall Street] histori-
cally self-conscious, and guarantee that our history will be accessible to the
public. (quoted in Message 2019b: 172)
Collecting intensified as the occupation began to lose momentum in the face
of police incursions and it continued, with debate on the best strategy to fol-
low in converting the collection into an archive, after the disbandment of
the Zuccotti Park occupation in November 2011. Given the sheer amount of
material available, the online collection encountered major problems with
regard to server space and archival descriptors, leading it to be stored on a
hard drive in the Robert F. Wagner Labor Archives as well as on the Internet
Archive. The most tangible outcome of what fissured into multiple, some-
times competing initiatives to create an autonomous “living” archive was the
collection of documents and realia deposited in the Tamiment Collection of
the New York University library.39

ACTIVIST ARCHIVING AS PREFIGURATIVE PRACTICE 185
Two years later during the Gezi Uprising, as Özge Çelikaslan has shown
in detail (2023, 2024), groups of video activists, most notably a group called
Videooccupy, set out to record events as they were unfolding and produced
hundreds of hours of footage. After the break-up of the protest, other groups
too turned their attention to collecting and organizing the huge volume of
visual materials produced by different actors within the now dispersed
movement. Çelikaslan describes the intense labor as well as the dedication
involved in these archiving efforts, which the activists carried over from
their involvement in the protest itself.40 The Bak.ma archive, as it is called,
is the most significant and resilient outcome of these efforts and recently
celebrated its ten-year existence with an exhibition in Berlin titled “Don’t
Look! bak.ma: 10 Years of Archiving Social Resistance.”41 As its subtitle sug-
gests (“digital media archive for social movements”) Bak.ma has gradually
also incorporated material from other movements, both from before and
after 2013, thus becoming a nodal point for the memory of activism both
inside and outside of Turkey since the 1960s. It answers to the idea of a
“living” archive in that it stores information about past events while also,
like the Archivo 15-M and 858.ma, though to a greater extent, linking them
to ongoing developments both at home and abroad. As a “living” archive,
it not only stores information but also produces new connections between
different events and movements.
A final example. Soon after the Nuit Debout occupied the Place de la
République in Paris in March 2016, a concerted effort was made to archive
the huge volume of texts, images, and realia being produced. A group was
tasked with the management of “communal memory” (“mémoire com-
mune”). The choice of “communal” rather than “collective” is indicative of
the importance attached to the idea of creating a Commons that Nuit Debout
shared with the movements that preceded it. The main output of the work-
ing group was a participatory Wiki Nuit Debout, which is still online.42 This
was set up as a dynamic and open-ended encyclopedia which, on the one
hand, would build up a long-term record of daily events and deliberations
and, on the other hand, create shared points of reference for the emerg-
ing Debout community across the country. Designed for the long haul, the
Wiki was meant to make the entire movement—its geographical reach, mul-
tiple activities, and intellectual genealogy—accessible to its supporters in a
way that transcended the street-level perspective of individual participants
and could help generate more support as the movement unfolded. More
than nine hundred images were uploaded to the site along with text-based

186 REMEMBERING HOPE
announcements and the minutes of the nightly Assemblies. The collection
also included legacy images of 15-M as well as more recent ones relating to
the struggle for Kabylian independence in Algeria: here too archiving and
connective memory work went hand in glove.
“We wanted to restore what was happening, to bear witness to it,” one of
the members of the Communal Memory committee said in an interview in
the mainstream Nouvel Observateur (Richard 2016). Nevertheless, the Wiki
Debout was abandoned as the movement itself lost momentum and met with
increasing pushback from the police. Unlike 15-M, whose archive found a
physical home in a center run by volunteers in Madrid, the Bak.ma online
archive that continues to grow, the 858.ma archive that made a fresh start
several years after the repression of the movement, and the Occupy Wall
Street collections some of which found a home in a friendly institution, the
end result of Nuit Debout’s efforts is more a zombie than a living archive.
Since I first began working on this topic (in March 2020) the wiki has gone
offline but luckily not before it was saved (by me among others) to the Way-
back Machine at the Internet Archive. For those who seek it out, the wiki
continues to bear witness to the commitment and hard work of those who
brought it into being and to the hopes vested in the movement in the first
half of 2016. Although collecting foreclosed, and the collection itself remains
vulnerable, the wiki still testifies to Nuit Debout’s capacity to constitute itself
as a multisited, interconnected, and self-regulating body over the course of
just weeks.
Temporal Rollovers: Between Prospective
and Retrospective
The examples presented above show the importance of activist-led archiving
to the movements of the squares, starting in 2011. There is of course consid-
erable variation in the results: from a zombie wiki to an online database of
images, to a materials-based collection supported by an organization com-
mitted to its preservation and accessibility. The cases also vary in the degree
to which they have remained autonomous or ended up seeking collaboration
with cultural institutions or even incorporation into them. Since maintain-
ing an archive demands considerable human, financial, and technological
resources as well as storage or server space, and since continuity in the
involvement of stakeholders can be at odds with the mutability of activist

ACTIVIST ARCHIVING AS PREFIGURATIVE PRACTICE 187
alliances, the sustainability of these archives is continuously threatened. Fur-
ther problems are posed by police crackdowns,43 by the power of big tech
and its monetization of content,44 and by the financial and ecological costs of
preserving so much audiovisual material on independent platforms. There
are signs of some new alliances between “archiving activists” and activist
heritage professionals willing to take over guardianship of these activist-led
collections of images, texts, and ephemera.45 These initiatives may help in
the future to mitigate the structural vulnerability of activist archives, perhaps
best illustrated by the Egyptian case where the political climate continues
to be hostile to the movement and the chance of shut-down remains ever-
present.46 These activist archives are imperfect and precarious. For this very
reason, they cast light on the complex temporalities at the heart of activist
memory work.
Commentators have drawn attention to “immediation” (Kaun 2016) as
a feature of digital media and noted that recording protest events as they
unfold serves “the needs of the present” (Chidgey 2020: 230–231, emphasis
in original) rather than being an investment in the future. Document-
ing events in medias res is certainly a striking feature of the movements
discussed here, and it was made possible by media technologies, starting
with the mobile phone. Nuit Debout, for one, installed an entire suite of
alternative media channels, including the aforementioned wiki, but also
radio, television, and a live stream of events happening at the Place de la
République. At first sight, the ongoing flow of information in this and other
movements supports the case for presentism. However, the work of the var-
ious archiving groups bespeaks a conscious effort to surpass the temporal
horizon of the present by prospectively recording events so that they could
be remembered in an uncertain future. Once the protest ended, collecting
took on a retrospective character: in a temporal fold, documents and images
now belonged to a past in which the protest still had a future. The archival
infrastructure such as it was provided the material connector between the
as-yet-open-ended event and its post hoc “restitution” (to recall the term
used by the Nuit Deboutists) as a moment of possibility.
If creating an archive during the occupation expresses hope in a move-
ment’s future, maintaining an archive after its demise expresses trust in the
importance of its memory. In periods of “abeyance” (Taylor 1989), archival
memory work becomes contention by other means. For all their differ-
ences, the archival projects discussed above shared a common purpose: to
extend protest by resisting its disappearance from memory. After the streets

188 REMEMBERING HOPE
have been cleared, digital and material records consolidate a movement’s
existence by sedimenting its actions and aspirations into retrievable traces.
Recording in medias res creates a feedback loop within the movement
itself, whereas archival records provide successors with a resource for rec-
ollecting past hopes and achievements. The resilience of the archive—for
as long as this lasts, given the structural vulnerability mentioned earlier—
is itself an achievement. The 858.ma archive has thus been described as a
“means of communication with those who have become despondent,” and
a way of “raising morale” by making personal memories of the movement
accessible to others and refusing to let the collective memory of Tahrir
Square be erased (Antoun 2012). In similar terms, Özge Çelikaslan describes
the Bak.ma archive as a reminder, in a hostile political climate, of the hopeful
moments during Gezi when things still could have gone another way.
To be sure, an afterlife in memory is not the same thing as continued
embodied existence, but a form of life it is. Phil Cohen (2018) has likened
archives to a Noah’s ark that safeguards the records of hopeful resistance
to the status quo, so that they can inspire and educate future generations.
Whether or not the Ark will ever touch land, however, depends on the ability
of political subjects in new contexts to engage with it. As one commentator
on the 858.ma archive put it:
The archive is now available, accessible to whomever would like to uti-
lize it. And yet, it may still be too soon. The undertow of crushed hope
has yet to dissipate. In the present moment, nobody may possess the abil-
ity to activate these images with revolutionary potential. (Della Ratta et al.
2020: 30)
The key term here is “potential.” It recalls the point made by Assmann that
archives are sites of latency, operating in the intermediary zone between
remembering and forgetting, between the “no longer” and the “not yet.”
These archives function as “storage memory” in the first instance. But they
have the potential to become active sites of memory work when materials
are brought back into circulation by interested parties, be these academics,
heritage professionals, or political subjects. Think here of exhibitions (there
have been at least ten exhibitions of banners from the Archivo 15M; mention
has already been made of the Bak.ma anniversary one) as well as of pub-
lished compilations of documents and images (there were several of these
relating to Nuit Debout).47 Only time will tell how the activation of these

ACTIVIST ARCHIVING AS PREFIGURATIVE PRACTICE 189
recent archives proceeds and about their resonance with future horizons.
Having flagged their potential, I will close this chapter by returning to the
more basic question: why has activist memory work in the form of archiving
become so important in recent movements?
Prefiguration and the Storing of Dreams
The gravitation toward archiving was in part opportunistic. As mentioned
earlier, occupation as a mode of protest facilitates archiving since it makes
long-term planning easier than in the case of day-long protests. The avail-
ability of digital tools for making and preserving records also helped. But
there is more. As I will argue here, the recent concern with archiving also
reflects a shift in the metahistorical imagination of activists, away from grand
narratives of revolutionary transformation to the principle of prefiguration.
As Vincent Bevins among others has pointed out (2023: 18–19), the dis-
course informing the various occupy movements was that of changing the
world “now” rather than taking action to provoke a revolution that, down the
line, would change the world. As mentioned at the beginning of this chapter,
prefiguration has evolved since the 1970s into a new way of conceiving polit-
ical change which supposes that social transformation begins here and now;
and that the desired future be folded back into present praxis. Hence the 15-
M slogan ¡democracia real ya! (“real democracy now/already”). Or as the
title of a 2015 exhibition figuring materials from the Archivo 15M put it,
“what is to come has already arrived” (“lo que ha de venir ya ha llegado”).
Both as idea and practice, prefiguration encourages activists to realize their
ideals now rather than merely imagine them as possibilities. Instead of wait-
ing for everything to change, the reasoning goes, one ought to start changing
things now so that the desired future is already part of the present, albeit at
a small scale. As a political practice, prefiguration “is a way for activists to
anticipate the changes they seek. And while everyday micropolitical action
may not trigger a revolution or herald political salvation, it may progres-
sively transform our ways of thinking, behaving, and imagining what society
should be like” (Fians 2023, n.p.).
Prefiguration supposes a commitment to action “now/already” without
the guarantee, however, that today’s small-scale achievements are the pref-
ace to a radically transformed world, though that remains the hope. To
recall the words of Václav Havel already quoted in my Introduction, hope

190 REMEMBERING HOPE
is “not the conviction that something will turn out well, but the certainty
that something makes sense, regardless of how it turns out” (in Potkay 2022:
26). The term “hope” is not usually used in relation to prefigurative poli-
tics, possibly because the former has been misread as optimism. But when
hope is defined in this minimalist way as a willingness to act now because
certain values “make sense” it begins to chime with prefiguration as a valid
alternative to grand narratives of progress.
Activist archives have been described as “prefigurative free spaces.” which
create “new networks of solidarity through the archival process itself ” (Sellie
et al. 2015: 454).48 By “archival process” is meant here the collective effort
made on the part of volunteers to create, organize, and maintain collections.
My analysis builds on this insight while offering further reflection on other
ways in which archiving performs prefigurative memory work.
1. The archiving projects discussed here are prefigurative in enacting
core political ideals in their very mode of production. Besides being
repositories of evidence they are thus also a materialized proof of con-
cept that outlasts the movement itself: the phrase “communal mem-
ory” used in the Nuit Debout movement echoed a more widespread
commitment to the idea of the Commons (Varvarousis et al. 2021)
based on the participation of activists themselves and, where pos-
sible, on the use of open-source software and creative commons
licenses.49 Those behind their making believed that the archive should
be democratically constituted so that it could become a vibrant site
for world-making both in the immediate future and in the long term.
Their very mode of production—participatory, multi-perspectival,
open-ended—embodied the political ideals of the movement being
remembered. As one member of the Occupy Wall Street Archive Work-
ing group put it: “What better way to make the archive accountable to
the people than to make the people accountable for the archive” (in
Message 2019b: 175). Or as the Occupy Wall Street activist already
quoted at the beginning of this chapter put it, “The challenge to
remake the world begins with a radically inclusive, directly demo-
cratic, open-ended, and open-sourced archive.”50 This metadiscourse
and its related practices indicate that “activist archives [do] more than
collect; they also enact the politics of their communities” (Sellie et al.
2015: 457). Crucially, moreover, this prefigurative action is not just
a one-off action in the present. When sedimented in the form of

ACTIVIST ARCHIVING AS PREFIGURATIVE PRACTICE 191
retrievable records, the work of prefiguration acquires an afterlife or,
at least, the potential to be continued by new political subjects in the
future.
2. The materials archived, especially images, prefigure the “desired future
social relations and practices in the here-and-now” (Raekstad &
Gradin 2020: 10). They do so in the form of representations carried
by cultural artifacts rather than in the form of action as such. On the
tenth anniversary of the occupation of the Puerta del Sol in Madrid,
the mainstream newspaper El País described the Archivo 15M as an
archive “that stores dreams,” thus capturing the paradox of a collec-
tion which, in documenting the past, also offers images of a possible
future.51 The many images of the activities taking place around the
occupied squares sediment the micropolitical work entailed in the day-
to-day conduct of the extended protest in a retrievable form. As I noted
earlier, the movements produced a counter-visuality showing people
at the nightly assemblies, talking, and listening, sharing food, working
together, producing music, and so on, with no end in sight. The 15-
M archive’s substantial collection of hand-written propuestas, digitized
examples of which figure in its catalog, serve a similar function in high-
lighting the living subjects at the heart of the movement. In this way,
images and handwritten documents offer a “sensorium” (Egea 2022:
186) that continues the work of embodied protest by translating the
aspirations and achievements of those who demanded “real democ-
racy now!” into sensuous objects. Whenever archival materials are
subsequently brought out of storage and presented to various publics,
a new possibility for continuing the micropolitical work prefigured in
the original protest is created.
3. Finally, there is an elective affinity between the way archives produce
meaning and the prefigurative principles of these recent movements.
Archives are “open” semiotic resources rather than “closed” ones
(Eco 1984); they are sites of possible meaning rather than of a sin-
gle message. Instead of offering a structured narrative where events
have already been interpreted as a meaningful whole (see Chapter 1),
archives give users the freedom to produce different constellations
depending on the pathways they have taken through the records.52
That archives, rather than narratives or monuments, should be the
mnemonic genre of preference in the movements discussed here is
not a matter of “left-wing melancholia” (Traverso 2016) or a lack of

192 REMEMBERING HOPE
historical consciousness. It expresses a positive commitment to leav-
ing the future relatively open to the interpretations and actions of those
who come later in an ongoing and emergent participatory process.53
I say “relatively open” because no matter how big the archive is, it is
always based on selection criteria, including, in this case, the desire to
document the movements from the perspective of the participants and,
in doing so, to prefigure a desired future in which democracy has been
reinvented. Although the choices made in archiving limit the number
of possible stories, archival memory work never takes the form of a sin-
gle story with a single message to be dictated to the next generation.
This is reflected in the 858.ma’s mission statement, which presents the
archive as serving “purposes as yet unknown within struggles both
local and international.”54 Commenting on 858.ma’s position, Naira
Antoun (2012) writes:
The question then is not what story to tell, but how to enable the telling
of multiple stories and narratives. What to collect, how to organize the
material are, of course deeply political questions, for they will shape,
limit, enable what stories it will be possible to tell.
Echoing all of the above, the Archivo 15M has made clear that the meaning
of the movement is not fixed in stone or the property of any single party, and
that any selection made from the archive is no more than a temporary con-
stellation that will enter into dialogue with others. Thus, the guide ironically
notes that the exhibition of selected 15-M posters at the prestigious Reina
Sofia museum (December 2021–July 2022) was available to be enjoyed but
also improved upon, while recommending in the spirit of the Commons that
people should only visit the museum when the entrance was free.55
From Impact to Cultural Afterlife
This chapter explored archiving as a future-oriented form of memory work
whose affordances make for meaning that is latent rather than actualized.
My subsequent analysis of five recent cases showed how activists build the
memory of their own movement and create the conditions for its becom-
ing an object of future recollection. Although it is true that some records
were maintained in order to counter misrepresentations in the mainstream

ACTIVIST ARCHIVING AS PREFIGURATIVE PRACTICE 193
media, the overall aim in the five cases was never just to contest a preexisting
memory but to create a new one; to ensure that the movement itself will not
be forgotten and, more positively, that its promises have a chance of being
reactivated. As an activist mnemonic practice, archiving thus feeds back
into political activism itself. In contrast to the memory activism discussed
in Chapter 5, this activist memory work is about generating a memory for
the future rather than about retrieving an occluded narrative. It is a form
of action in the present that enables future remembrance, while taking an
open form that conveys possibilities rather than dictating a message to later
generations. For this reason I call it a prefigurative mnemonic practice. Per-
haps more than any of the other genres discussed in this book, it exemplifies
the principle of hope: a way of acting in the now to defend certain values
without any guarantee of the outcome.
All this adds new complexity to our understanding of the memory-
activism nexus and the futural orientation of memory work. While my
analysis of activist archiving echoes the discussion of collective prospective
mediated memory spearheaded by Karen Tenenboim-Weinblatt (2013), it
also takes that discussion in a new direction by highlighting the transfer of
promises across generations. Remembering the efforts of protesters ten years
ago means in the first instance recalling other people’s past intentions; only
in the second instance does it become a reminder of our own intentions
and commitments as derived from our predecessors. Linking archiving prac-
tices to the politics of prefiguration shows how the future, in multiple ways,
already begins in the now. This is less a matter of a two-way traffic between
a “past” and a “present” than of a feedback loop between an unfolding
present, an imagined future, and a past that can re-enter the present again
as a mediated “sensorium” of images, texts, and sounds.
The cases outlined here suggest that the power of a movement to “make its
mark” on society by becoming a political factor of significance can be corre-
lated with their “mnemonic capacity” (Armstrong & Crage 2006) to repro-
duce themselves. As we have seen, Nuit Debout had a limited mnemonic
capacity since, after the mobilization had ended, it did not morph into a
curated archive supported by stakeholders. In contrast, the 15-M move-
ment has consolidated its status over time and, through spin-off exhibitions,
contributed to bringing its memory into the Spanish mainstream while
also resisting its appropriation and sanitation. How exactly this mnemonic
capacity correlates to broader political impact is a matter for future research.

194 REMEMBERING HOPE
In the meantime, activist archiving calls for a rethinking of the very
notion of “consequence” or “impact.” Unsurprisingly this has been a big
issue in social movement studies, given its interest in knowing the factors
that contribute to the success or failure of protest actions or other forms of
activism. “Outcomes” have been sought in the biographies of those involved,
in political change, and in “cultural consequences” with the latter associated
with “changes in the norms and behaviors in which political actors operate”
(Bosi et al. 2016: 4).56 Scholars have tried to identify cultural consequences
at the systemic level and to quantify change through interviews and ques-
tionnaires. In line with my overall approach, I am more interested here in
the qualitative changes effected in the micropolitical sphere by cultural arti-
facts. This brings me to reframe the issue of “consequences” and to replace
“impact” by “afterlife.”
The question is no longer whether society changed, but rather, what sur-
vived. What ideas, images, and artifacts did a movement produce, and how
have these sedimented, circulated, procreated? When do people use them
as a renewable resource for understanding present-day concerns and for-
mulating new agendas? In short, what has been the cultural afterlife of a
movement? The idea of “life” resonates with the discourse encountered ear-
lier in relation to the “aliveness” of the Commune. It indicates the capacity
of certain images and stories to generate new versions of themselves in the
form of images but also in the form of action. Cultural memory work enables
prefigurative action to attain a degree of scalability across time.
Earlier chapters showed both the long-term resilience of sedimented
memories of the Commune and their capacity to be adapted in new circum-
stances. We are only at the beginning of this long-haul process in relation to
recent movements. But one thing is clear: making an archive is itself a prac-
tice of hope. Safeguarding the archives of oppositional movements is vital
for ensuring that dissent can be remembered, and its history written in the
future.

Chapter 7
Memory Work in Climate Activism
The Commune Meets COP21
In November 2015, a week before the COP21 summit that would lead to
the Paris Agreement on climate change, some 200 activists from Brittany
set out for the capital on tractors and bicycles in order to make their voices
heard. They wanted to highlight the hypocrisy of the French government
which, while professing to lead the way toward a more sustainable planet,
was planning to turn an ecologically diverse area into the concrete wasteland
of a new—and in the eyes of the protesters and many others, unnecessary—
airport. They had come from the so-called Zad (zone-à-défendre; literally:
zone to be defended) at Notre-Dame-des-Landes near Nantes, where the
airport was planned. Besides challenging government policy, they wanted
to provide living evidence to the public, through the media, that resistance
works. As one of them said in an interview, “We wanted to bring a message
of hope . . . One which shows that it is possible to call to a halt government-
imposed projects that are toxic, unwanted, and ecocidal.”1
The Zad had come into being in 2012, when the site designated for the new
airport was occupied by a coalition of local farmers and eco-activists, who
defied multiple attempts by the police to have them evicted. It exemplified
“ecological prefiguration” (Centemeri & Asare 2022: 131), in the sense of
collective action directed at bringing into visibility alternative forms of coex-
istence with the more-than-human, not merely as a future possibility, but as
a de-growth potential within the world as it already is. The Zad at Notre-
Dame-des-Landes would function as an autonomous community (one that
was “lost to the Republic” as the authorities put it and the activists proudly
repeated) until January 2018.2 At any given time, it offered a home to several
hundred activists who created houses from recycled materials, cultivated the
land and, periodically, fought off the police attempting to clear the camp. At
the time of COP21 the plans for the airport were still in place but, thanks to
the efforts of the occupiers, they had been put on hold. Thanks too to these
Remembering Hope. Ann Rigney, Oxford University Press. © Oxford University Press (2025).
DOI: 10.1093/oso/9780197789711.003.0008

196 REMEMBERING HOPE
efforts, the term “Zad” entered the transnational protest lexicon, offering a
model for similar occupations in France and abroad.3
Marching to the capital city was not typical of a movement so closely
associated with the defense of its own zone. But the high-profile nature of
COP21 offered an opportunity for feeding back a local campaign into the
global struggle for sustainability and climate justice. Since no demonstra-
tions were permitted in Paris itself at that time (a state of emergency had
been declared since the terrorist attacks of November 13, 2015) the Zadists
ended their march at Versailles. There they sat down together with local sup-
porters to a communal meal before the gates of the chaˆteau. As they did so,
one of the farmers who had traveled by tractor from Brittany was heard to
say:
In 1871, the Versaillais [state forces] crushed the Paris Commune. All the
Zads active today are new free communes. And we affirm here [in Ver-
sailles] that these communes will never allow themselves to be dispersed.
(In: Mauvaise Troupe 2018: 37)
And so, the Commune reappears in climate activism. The Zadists appear as
Communards who now “take over” Versailles, not only the most famous site
of the ancien régime but above all the place which had been so firmly asso-
ciated with the government that the soldiers who crushed the uprising had
been called Versaillais (Thiers and his cabinet retreated there in the spring
of 1871). For Zadists to “take” Versailles in 2015 in defiance of an ecocidal
government was to repeat history but also to reverse it. The incident reveals
the continuing power of the Commune to enter the figuration of the present
by bringing cultural memory into play in a protest event.
The connection between climate activism and the Commune was more
than a random statement on the part of one activist. Celebrating solidarity
through communal banquets had long been associated with commemora-
tions of the Commune, as we saw in Chapter 4. Moreover, a large banner was
on display before the gates of the chateau, to which the farmer-activist was
perhaps responding, which proclaimed: “Keep Bourget [the site of COP21],
we’ll capture Versailles. Vive la Commune!”
The slogan on this banner (Figure 7.1) would later be remediated as the
title of the final chapter of the pamphlet Défendre la Zad (2018 [2016]: 35),
written by a collective called Mauvaise Troupe. This pamphlet ends with an
account of the celebratory meeting at Versailles and a rallying call to readers

MEMORY WORK IN CLIMATE ACTIVISM 197
Figure 7.1 Banner displayed at Versailles during a COP21
counterdemonstration, November 28, 2015. Photographer unknown. Image
Paris-luttes.info. Fair use.
to extend the continued success of the Zad in Notre-Dame-des Landes to
other comparable “communes.” It urged them to propagate the idea of a Zad
as a “historical possibility” (38), a proof of concept that autonomous zones
can stave off ecological disaster: “the Zad and everything it represents . . .
offers here and now a precious gleam of hope in this disenchanted age.”4
Défendre la Zad appeared in 2016, just two months after COP21 when
reports of new plans to start work on the airport started to circulate. When
the pamphlet was subsequently reprinted in 2018, the plans for the airport
had been definitively abandoned and the occupation largely disbanded. In a
complex rollover between the retrospective and the prospective, the Zad at
Notre-Dame-des-Landes (as it was ongoing but also after it had reached its
goal) was offered as an inspiration for future actions elsewhere. As the final
words of the text put it (both in 2016 and 2018): “We need to defend the Zad
/ at Notre-Dames-des-Landes / and everywhere else.”5
This brief encounter between COP21, the memory of the Commune, and
the Zadist movement is offered here as a first indication of the ubiquity of
memory work in the climate movement. The term “unprecedented” is often
invoked in reports on the ecological collapse we are currently witnessing.

198 REMEMBERING HOPE
But climate activism does have many precedents reaching back at least to
the 1960s. More importantly, the memory of predecessors—both from the
recent and more distant past—plays a surprisingly important role in articu-
lating strategies and mobilizing support within the movement. The present
chapter makes this point with reference to a wide range of expressions pro-
duced both by activists engaged on the ground and by those who support
their efforts through cultural production in different disciplines. In doing
so, it shows the interplay between memory in activism, the memory of
activism, and the activist memory work that is an integral part of political
action.
The chapter begins by first exploring the memoryscape of climate
activism. In line with the findings of Chapter 1, it shows how activists recon-
figure the memory of earlier movements as a resource for articulating their
own agendas and to justify strategy. The invocation of the Commune at
COP21 gives a taste of what this entails. The spotlight then shifts to the ways
in which recent interventions within the climate movement itself have been
culturally recollected, especially in the form of documentaries. Here I pick
up on the themes of Chapters 3 and 4 by showing how cultural forms and the
arts of storytelling are used as tools of resistance and mobilization. Finally,
I return to the role of hope that is so central to this book, asking how stories
of minor successes weigh up against the urgency of the problem as well as
its scale.
Memory Studies in the Face of Ecological Collapse
Futurity and memory have long had a troubled relationship. As I explained
in the Introduction, the preoccupation with memory in culture and
academia has sometimes been attributed to the collective loss of trust in the
future once fed by progressive or revolutionary narratives. Earlier chapters
identified the futural dimensions of memory work, challenging the idea that
memory is only past-oriented, and showing the multiple ways in which ret-
rospection, prospection, and the imagining of alternative futures go hand in
glove. Chapter 6 discussed archiving practices in recent occupy movements
as a new variation on retro-prospective remembrance based on prefigurative
politics rather than a trust in future revolution. One of the red threads in this
book is that mnemonic practices evolve in response to new metahistorical

MEMORY WORK IN CLIMATE ACTIVISM 199
understandings of the nature and direction of historical change. My final
chapter extends this exploration by turning to the arena of climate activism.
The threats posed by climate change have changed the understanding of
time on which memory studies was based in its first decades. Where the
future could be marked by its absence in the post-1989 era, it has since
returned, albeit as a looming and threatening presence. It is continuously
announcing itself—if it is not already there—in the rapid loss of biodiver-
sity, the exhaustion of the earth’s resources, as well as hugely destructive
but also unpredictable weather events that are read by both climate scien-
tists and ecocritical citizens as presages of even worse to come. Chapter 2
showed how the future became “unknown” after the rupture of 1789 (Kosel-
leck 1985 [1979]: 49); by now it is radically uncertain and tending toward the
dystopian. Although memory studies was long premised on the importance
of ensuring “never again,” recent scholarship across multiple disciplines has
been coming to terms with the possibility of the inverse: that “the worst is
still to come.” The latter scenario is distinguished by its futural orientation
but also by its broadening of the framework to the more-than-human: the
planetary system that humans have been recklessly destroying for their own
gain without thought for the well-being of future generations and of other
species. The imminence of climate collapse, or the growing sense that it is
already happening, has thus folded the future into the present. In this con-
text, the term “crisis” is regularly invoked in public discourse to indicate both
a critical decision point in political and social life and, more problematically,
a chronic state of emergency with no horizon on a long-term resolution.6
The climate crisis thus “breaks up time” (Lorenz & Bevernage 2013). Not
only by bringing the future into the present but also by bringing the past into
it in the form of the long-term impact of irreversible ecological damage with
consequences that are both moral and very material. In this context, Indige-
nous voices have been highlighting the geopolitics of time itself. Extractivist
colonization meant that the destruction of ecosystems and ways of living,
now feared in the Global North, already happened long ago for other groups:
“Our world ended in the 16th century,” as one Brazilian Aboriginal-rights
activist put it (Krenak 2019: 52). Breaking up time in a similar way, Nick
Estes’s account of the long-term struggles of the Lakota peoples against envi-
ronmental destruction, most recently in the Standing Rock encampment
against the Dakota Access Pipeline, is called Our History Is the Future (2019).
These accounts of destruction and resilience are reminders of the human
and ecological cost of modern extractivism. At the same time, they make it

200 REMEMBERING HOPE
imperative to articulate a different metahistory. This needs to be more than
a grand narrative of climate collapse leading, in line with classical modes of
storytelling, to an apocalyptic ending located in the future.
Indeed, the search is on for a “post-apocalyptic” (Cassegård & Tho¨rn
2022) metahistory—premised neither on inevitable progress nor on an ulti-
mate catastrophe that puts an end to everything. Within this context, looking
to the past—taking account, for example, of the fact that some worlds have
already ended and yet life has gone on—may be a more intelligent response
to present problems than sticking to a progressivist or linear model of time
that always locates meaning in the future. One way or another, memory as a
cultural practice in the age of climate change operates in different conditions
from those envisaged by Huyssen (2000) at the turn of the century.
In response to these developments, there has been a growing number of
calls to go beyond the human framework in the study of memory. Stef Craps
has led the way in criticizing “the persistent humanism” that prevents the
field “from adequately addressing the vast spatio-temporal magnitudes of
the Anthropocene” (in Craps et al. 2018: 500).7 It is no coincidence that this
critique has been voiced loudest in cultural memory studies, where scholars
are in conversation with ecocriticism—the branch of cultural analysis that
over the last decades has been studying narratives of environmental destruc-
tion and climate change. The climate crisis, ecocriticism has shown, is also a
crisis in story-telling. The unprecedented condition of the planet is a stress
test for existing narrative schemata.
Robert Nixon’s concept of “slow violence” was mentioned already in
Chapter 3 as a counterpart to the dramatic violence of civic outrage. It was
originally developed to describe how traditional narrative schemata fail to
capture the scale and pace of environmental destruction, including the fact
that its impact is greatest in the Global South. The novelist-historian Ami-
tav Ghosh has made a similar point (2016); namely, that storytelling in the
tradition of the realist novel has not only failed to articulate the ecological
entanglements constitutive of the modern world, but has actually occluded
those entanglements by focusing on individual rather than collective expe-
rience and by narrativizing events into clear-cut causal chains instead of
depicting unpredictable long-term developments. Ghosh’s advocacy for new
modes of storytelling chimes with a lot of writing in the field of ecocriti-
cism that has sought to foster more effective ways of giving expression to
an Anthropocenic imaginary.8 Despite differences in approach and focus,
ecocriticism implicitly trusts in the power of creative storytelling to build a

MEMORY WORK IN CLIMATE ACTIVISM 201
greater awareness of human complicity in ecocide and the global inequali-
ties that accompany it. Since evidence of climate change has been available
for decades without leading to effective action,9 the hope is that the arts can
provide a “spur to action” (Craps 2017: 488) and mobilize people in both
their personal and their political lives.
Reflecting the dominance of the traumatic paradigm outlined in my
Introduction, cultural memory scholars have turned to the language of
trauma and related practices of mourning as a blueprint for capturing the
material and ethical entanglements that characterize the more-than-human
world. Many of the concepts with which the memory of genocide has been
studied—violence, trauma, mourning, violence, grief—are now proving use-
ful for analyzing the “genocide-ecocide nexus” (Knittel 2023), “ecological
mourning” (Craps 2023), and “multidirectional eco-memory” (Kennedy
2017).10 In bringing ecocide and climate change within the remit of memory
studies scholars have thus taken the memory of genocide as their model. This
reflects traditions in the research field but also the fact that contemporary
artists themselves have drawn on a trauma-inflected narrative repertoire to
articulate their responses to the climate crisis. Things may be changing. A
recent article by Stef Craps (2024) calls for attention to the role of the arts in
creating positive attachments to biodiversity rather than merely mourning
its loss. This positivity chimes with the overall emphasis of this book and
will also take center stage here.
Ecocritical memory scholars have been above all concerned with creative
stories about environmental change and the more-than-human world. I turn
here to the very human arena of climate activism.11 Although the climate cri-
sis itself is unprecedented, climate activists use memory, like the movements
discussed in earlier chapters, as a resource for thinking beyond the present
and imagining an alternative future. Their memory work in turn is inspiring
cultural productions in which new metahistories are being elaborated.
Climate Activism: A Shifting Assemblage
Until now I have used the term “climate activism” and “climate move-
ment” interchangeably to refer to what is in fact a shifting assemblage
of movements and organizations, including Greenpeace, Earth First!, Sea
Shepherd, Friends of the Earth, Extinction Rebellion [XR], Fridays for
Futures, Soulèvements de la terre, Just Stop Oil, Ende Gela¨nde, Code Rood,

202 REMEMBERING HOPE
Movimento dos Trabalhadores Sem Teto, and the Chipko movement, to
mention just some of them. The landscape continues to evolve as different
issues and targets emerge and people form new alliances for environment-
related causes. What is called here “climate activism” has roots going back
at least to the anti-nuclear movement and environmental movements of the
1970s as well as to the global justice movement of the 2000s, to mention just
a few ancestors. While some groups covered by this label are environment-
oriented in the sense of wanting to preserve biodiversity, others see the
struggle against extractivism and the fossil fuel industries as part of a broader
struggle against capitalism and for global justice; as the movement evolves
there has been a gradual convergence between environmental and social jus-
tice issues.12 While some climate activists demonstrate in more traditional
ways, others go for more direct-action tactics, be that sabotage or litiga-
tion. The groups figuring here nevertheless share a common commitment
to effecting degrowth, halting environmental destruction, and redressing
inequality through a change in government policies and everyday practices,
often beginning at very locally specific pressure points.
Given the shapeshifting nature of the movement as well as its global scale,
the analysis that follows has no claim to be exhaustive. It has the more
modest aim of describing some of the salient features of climate-related
memory work with reference to several European cases, most notably, the
UK-based Extinction Rebellion [XR], the German Ende Gela¨nde, and the
French Zadist movement mentioned earlier. I consider the expressions of
participants but also of supporters operating in the fields of journalism, the
arts, and academia. This assumes that the cultural memory work of a move-
ment is networked and multisited rather than something produced once and
for all by a single actor. It relies on the combined investment of multiple
actors, those close to the activist center as well as the mediators, profes-
sional and amateur, who address the public at large. It also relies on multiple
cultural forms, including activist performances like that at Versailles, col-
lectively produced pamphlets, images, websites, documentaries, recorded
testimonies, artifacts, and academic commentary.
In line with earlier chapters, the cultural carriers of memory will be the
focus of analysis rather than interviews. Since they sediment stories and
ideas in an affectively powerful and reproducible form, they are actants in
the movement. They refract, amplify, and add a reflective layer to material
practices, creating a feedback loop between meaning-making and embodied
action. Like the archives discussed in the previous chapter, narratives about

MEMORY WORK IN CLIMATE ACTIVISM 203
protest have the potential to mobilize new actors at later points in time. I
analyze cultural production from within the movement itself, produced by
campaigners and supporters, in order to show how a sense of continuity
is produced in a radically uncertain landscape with the help of texts and
images. This is key to the sustainability of the movement itself and the ability
of activists to keep going as they try to turn the tide.13
Affirmative Memoryscapes
Fossil fuels, no thanks (Figure 7.2)—multiple variations on this slogan have
been in evidence in recent campaigns. They recycle and update the anti-
nuclear movement’s signature Nuclear power? no thanks, the iconic red sun
on a yellow background which originated in Denmark and ended up adorn-
ing cars, bikes, and satchels across the world in the largely predigital 1970s.
Since the iconic button was clearly very effective in its colorful simplicity,
it is not surprising that it should be reused. But its reappearance, now tar-
geting fossil fuels and climate change, also illustrates something else: a keen
awareness of the continuity between climate activism and its predecessors.
Reusing the iconic button is also a way of flagging an affiliation with the ear-
lier movement, of building on the memory of the support for that movement
and transferring it virtually to the new protest.14
Climate activists also reference predecessors in more explicit ways. Green-
peace, one of the oldest environmentalist movements, with a history of
direct action dating back to the late 1960s, has long included “stories” on
its website, including recent items on the movement’s founding fathers as
one by one they pass away. It also includes multiple stories on the sink-
ing in 1985 of the Rainbow Warrior (their frontline ship, named after a
North American Cree prophecy: “When the world is sick and dying, the
people will rise up like Warriors of the Rainbow”).15 In Greenpeace’s self-
presentation, the signaling of emerging environmental hotspots thus runs
in parallel to recollecting instances of past resistance. These are explicitly
flagged as “victories and successes” and as representing “half a century of
hope in action.”16 In contrast to the emphasis on overcoming defeat in the
oppositional memory work considered in Chapters 1and 4, and the long
history of martyrdom discussed in Chapter 3, this memoryscape is unex-
pectedly affirmative. While regular reference is made to acts of outrage and
to the killing of activists, heroic figures and episodes of successful resistance

204 REMEMBERING HOPE
Figure 7.2 Fossil Fuels, No Thanks, Anglesea Coal Shut It Down Rally, August
2014. Photo John Englart. CC BY-SA 2.0.
predominate. These are presented, in the retro-prospective mode with which
we are now familiar, both as memories and as inspirations for “alternative
futures” (one of the movement’s keywords). They are things to be cele-
brated. In line with this emphasis on positive examples, the website recently
dedicated a new page to “inspiring environmental victories.”17 These victo-
ries do not add up to an overall ending that would give narrative closure
in the form of a definitive victory for the climate movement in its strug-
gle against governments and corporations. But they are offered as victories
nevertheless.
A canon of inspirational examples also figures in the discourses
that accompany Extinction Rebellion (XR), the transnational movement
founded in the UK in 2018 with a manifesto opening with the words:
“We hear history calling to us from the future.”18 On December 17, 2019,
the “history corner” of the website of Extinction Rebellion UK included,
for example, an item on the fall of the Berlin Wall attributing a lot of
influence to the environmentalist publication Umweltbla¨tter and its defiance

MEMORY WORK IN CLIMATE ACTIVISM 205
of GDR censorship.19 Subsequent entries include the protest music of
Woodie Guthrie in the 1960s; the opposition to the Dakota Access Pipeline
in the 2010s; digital activism calling for accountability in the 1997 massacre
of Indigenous protesters at Acteal in Mexico; the activism of Ken Saro-Wiwa
in the Niger delta in the 1990s; the “Green Scare” of the 2000s, and the
Newbury Bypass protest of the 1990s. Reflecting the networked character of
this memory work, the last three items include links to relevant documen-
taries (similar links are also used by Greenpeace).20 In the memoryscape of
XR, local and transnational reference points figure side by side and resonate
with each other. The absence of any reference to Greenpeace is striking: a
surprising omission at first sight given the latter’s track record and pioneer-
ing role. It can perhaps be explained by Greenpeace’s reputation for direct
action tactics as well as by the belatedness of its linking of environmental
issues to social justice. One way or another, XR’s careful selection of prede-
cessors reflects the findings of Priska Daphi (2024), namely, that activists
pick and choose their ancestors to reflect their own priorities, practicing
strategic forgetting as well as strategic remembering. Echoing the dynam-
ics discussed in Chapter 1, here too reconfiguring the memoryscape is a
tool for articulating different agendas and for position-taking vis-à-vis other
groups.
Memory sites pertaining to the prehistory of climate activism and envi-
ronmentalism are also brought into conversation with ones relating more
broadly to the history of nonviolent resistance.21 The term “non-violent civil
disobedience” is inscribed in the mission statement of XR.22 Along with the
names of political theorists Erica Chenoweth and Gene Sharp, known for
their writings on nonviolence and civil disobedience, references to Gandhi,
Martin Luther King, Nelson Mandela, and the British suffragettes regularly
crop up in XR writing.23 The XR activists, already mentioned in the Intro-
duction, who dressed up as suffragettes in November 2021 to protest against
investment in fossil fuels, were performing a similar tribute to their prede-
cessors (Figure 7.3).24 In paying such tributes, XR places itself in a tradition
defined by figures who, once dissidents, have now become mainstream icons
of successful nonviolent protest.
In his contribution to This Is Not a Drill: An Extinction Rebellion Hand-
book, for example, cofounder Roger Hallam presents the “civil resistance
model” as one whose strategic value in bringing about change had already
been proven in the “civil rights movement in America and the independence
movement in India” (2019: 100), adding by way of additional example the

206 REMEMBERING HOPE
Figure 7.3 Extinction Rebellion activists dressed as Suffragettes, London, UK,
August 27, 2021. Photograph: Wiktor Szymanowicz. NurPhoto SRL/Alamy.
civil rights Freedom Riders of the 1960s (105). On November 3, 2020,
the umbrella site of transnational XR also sought to provide guidance
and inspire enthusiasm by offering “examples of successful civil disobe-
dience.” Besides usual suspects like the UK women’s suffrage movement
and Gandhi’s Salt March, it also recalled less well-known figures: Claudette
Colvin and other women who refused to give up their bus seats in segregated
America; the Tree Sitters of Pureora in New Zealand in 1978; the campaign
against toxic mining in Estonia in 1987; the protests against Unethical Patent
Laws since 1998; the French farmers’ revolt against McDonaldification in
1999; the Yellow Vest movement of 2018–2019. The German Ende Gela¨nde
movement has its own set of local references, but it too describes itself as
looking for inspiration in “the tradition of civil disobedience, as practiced by
Thoreau, the militant Suffragettes, Martin Luther King, and others” (Ende
Gela¨nde 2022: 23). In short: what is offered in this activist memoryscape is a
constellation of instances of successful opposition occurring at different sites
and different times. This is a twenty-first-century version of history teach-
ing by honorific examples and a variation on the pantheons discussed in
Chapter 2. Reflecting the fact that culture is an infinitely renewable resource,

MEMORY WORK IN CLIMATE ACTIVISM 207
it recycles traditional models based on the recurring celebration of saints
and heroes rather than on the narrative recollection of unfolding events.25
These “saints” are invoked in the first instance to persuade activists of the
strategic value of nonviolent civil disobedience and, in the second instance,
to give them legitimacy by calling on the weight of history. Since the concept
of nonviolence is an essentially contested one, as we saw in Chapter 3, the
memory of these iconic figures also serves as a resource for articulating the
fine line between nonviolent protest, civil disobedience, and direct action in
contemporary campaigns. As a result, the canon of icons has become subject
to debate, reflecting concerns within the movement itself about the limits of
civil disobedience.
Witness How to Blow Up a Pipeline (2021), the high-profile polemic by
activist-historian Andreas Malm whose first chapter is titled “Learning from
Past Struggles.” While Malm acknowledges the value of historical analo-
gies, he criticizes the climate movement for its overreliance on a limited
and indeed sanitized version of past protest crystallized around canoni-
cal individuals—this was inhibiting rather than advancing the cause. Being
associated with “respectable” nonviolence may lower the bar for participa-
tion, Malm notes, but it is tactically insufficient given the urgency of the
task at hand. It distorts history, to boot. The commitment of Martin Luther
King Jr. and Mandela to nonviolence, he insists, was always strategic and
not absolute; moreover, the threat of direct action on the part of flanking
actors was an equally important factor in social transformation. “Without
Malcolm X there might not have been a Martin Luther King (and vice versa)”
(50).26 Since civil disobedience as currently practiced is becoming ritualized
and hence losing its impact, the climate movement needs a different set of
memories to find their bearings in the present. Accordingly Malm throws
out some suggestions for putting the memory of more proactive and mili-
tant forms of resistance center-stage (35–49). Isn’t confronting the fossil fuel
industry as urgent today as resistance to national socialism was in 1932 (62)?
Greta Thunberg may be today’s Rosa Parks, but where is the Angela Davis of
the climate movement (51)? This invocation of alternative memory sites is
a bit scatter-fire, ranging as it does from the Haitian revolution to Nazi Ger-
many, to the Palestinian strike of 1936, to the suffragettes’ slogan “deeds and
not words” (though not extending to the direct-action campaigns of Green-
peace). His purpose is clearly not to abandon memory; but rather to give
the memoryscape a shake-up, so that people will think less about nonvio-
lence in the monumental mode of pious recollection and, by turning to other

208 REMEMBERING HOPE
historical cases, consider more militant forms of direct action, including
sabotage.
Anaël Chataignier’s Écosabotage (2024), written by another activist-
academic, offers a similar critique of the “strange amnesia” (51) that is affect-
ing the climate movement and urges activists to “refresh” (19) their mem-
ories so as to diversify their tactics in the present. Chataignier accordingly
offers a list of recommended books and films relating to activism in general,
including works like Gillo Pontecorvo’s Battle of Algiers (1966) and Edward
Abbey’s Monkey Wrench Gang (1975), and to climate activism in partic-
ular (including Malm). Legacy cultural products function alongside new
projects as a resource for alternative perspectives on today’s agendas. Simi-
lar lists have been produced by XR Writersrebel, which also offers a platform
for original eco-related creative writing. The prevalence of reading lists and
libraries underscores the importance of cultural socialization—producing
the shared knowledge of a historical and discursive repertoire—as a feature
of the movements discussed here.27
Malm and Chataignier offer criticism while also flagging alternatives.
Thus Chataignier’s aim in Écosabotage is not merely to destroy bad habits
and policies but also “to already bring into practice the world you would
like to see tomorrow” (2024: 57). This emphasis on prefigurative politics is
echoed by Malm, whose critique of polite modes of nonviolent protest runs
parallel to his naming of positive examples of collective action, including
the “climate camps” that have been organized since around 2006 in the UK
and Germany and which he calls “festivals of prefigurative living and learn-
ing” (2019: 14). For both writers, stopping eco-destructive policies remains
extremely urgent and demands even more effective forms of contention. In
parallel, however, they also flag the importance of generating the imagina-
tive and material conditions for the alternative world to take the place of
the one being challenged. It’s not enough to be “against” the status quo; as
important is being “for” something different.
This combination of destruction and creation is also apparent in the writ-
ings of XR. Echoing the emphasis on “life” explored in Chapter 4, one of XR’s
flagship publications was called Handbook for Life (2024), and, in case there
were ever any doubt, its first chapter is titled “We Want to Live!”28 As XR’s
original mission statement put it, “we need a regenerative culture” in face of
“a breakdown of life.”29 This dual orientation—toward combating ecocide,
on the one hand, and putting in place the foundations of a different society,
on the other—is also reflected in the movement’s memory work, as the next

MEMORY WORK IN CLIMATE ACTIVISM 209
sections show. How is the past used as a resource for thinking in positive
terms about alternatives?
Cultural Memory as Renewable Resource
Imagining sustainable futures can take the form of so-called science-fiction
(or “cli-fi”) as it does in Kim Stanley Robinson’s Ministry for the Future
(2020), which is full of technical solutions to current challenges. At stake
here is another sort of world-making where the ideas and stories already
sedimented in cultural memory are recycled creatively to generate new ideas
about the forms of life that could replace the status quo; to articulate a change
of direction by reference to what has already proved possible.
This brings us back by way of example to COP21. If the Commune reap-
peared on that occasion, this was not just because of the happy historical
coincidence that the protest ended in Versailles. The Commune had long
provided a model for the Zad itself. As the collective authors of Défendre la
Zad put it, “There is something hatching at the Zad that resembles a com-
mune. Something of the Commune of 1871” (Mauvaise Troupe 2018:3 1).
They singled out the Commune of 1871 as the super site of left-wing mem-
ory, recognizable by the upper-case C, while also recalling that the idea of
a commune had been materialized on multiple occasions since the Middle
Ages, including the 1960s. The Zad at Notre-Dame-des-Landes draws on the
“imaginaries” of these many communes in its “search for a desirable present
and a possible future” (32).
Chapter 1 showed how the memory of the 1871 Commune changed
in relation to the socialist and later anarchist frames used to recall it. In
Chapter 4 the Commune returned in several guises, most notably as a fes-
tive event in the Situationist-inflected centenary celebrations of 1971 and
in the idea of a “living commune” as a political ideal. In the late nine-
teenth century as well as in the 1970s, shifting values and expectations in
the present produced a different memory of 1871; conversely, that different
memory helped activists articulate their agendas in the present. The sedi-
mented memory of the Commune, mediated over and again across different
platforms, functions in this way as a renewable resource for position-taking.
The Zadists too had their own variation on the Commune of 1871
with many echoes of anarchist versions in the 1890s. In the words of the
same pamphlet, “an irrepressible emotion took hold of the inhabitants of

210 REMEMBERING HOPE
Paris who became, behind the barricades, masters of their everyday lives”
(Mauvaise Troupe 2018: 31; my emphasis). Chapter 4 noted that an asso-
ciation between the Commune and a radically different “everyday life” had
been doing the rounds since at least 1970s, inspired by Henri Lefebvre’s the-
orization of the everyday as the site where political change is produced (2024
[1947–1981]). The most recent variation on this idea has come from the
American academic Kristin Ross, author of an earlier work on the Com-
mune called The Emergence of Social Space (1988) and more recently of
Communal Luxury: The Political Imaginary of the Paris Commune (2015a).
The latter was translated almost immediately into French as L’imaginaire de
la Commune (2015b), a title resonating in Défendre la Zad’s use of the term
“imaginaries.”
As Ross has explained elsewhere (1988: 8), her own intellectual and polit-
ical socialization was shaped by her experience as a student of 1968 and
by her reading of Lefebvre and the Situationists (1988: 8). Her innovative
recycling of the former’s theorization of the everyday found expression in
her account of the Commune as a haven for craftsmanship and for bene-
ficial changes to the fabric of the lived environment. In demonstrating the
Commune’s creativity, she turned the spotlight on lesser-known actors such
as Élisée Reclus (1830–1905): Communard, socialist-anarchist, vegetarian,
internationally renowned geographer, and author of such books as Histoire
d’un ruisseau (History of a Stream; 1869), Histoire d’une montagne (History
of a Mountain; 1875–1876), and L’homme et la terre (Man and the Earth;
1905–1908). In today’s parlance Reclus would be called an ecological thinker
or eco-anarchist, an anachronistic vocabulary whose appositeness in this
case underscores how changing perspectives in the present bring new his-
torical figures and bodies of thought to light which, in being recycled, help
us in turn to understand the present. Given this feedback loop, it comes as
no surprise that there has been something of a Reclus revival in recent years
(just as there has been renewed interest in the ecological aspects of Marx’s
thought30). With the help of Reclus among others, Ross innovatively con-
nected the history of the Commune, which is usually Paris-centered, to the
larger history of the (lived/natural) environment. As an academic memory
worker bound to the rules of evidence, she has helped extend the afterlife of
1871, which she clearly finds an inspiring event, by activating the archive in
such a way as to make it resonate with contemporary values and concerns.
Although Ross’s book was not the only inspiration, it did become
a point of reference for the Zadists who invited her to come to

MEMORY WORK IN CLIMATE ACTIVISM 211
