Notre-Dame-des-Landes to talk about it in 2016 (Ross 2024: 67–74). Ross
also spoke publicly on at least one occasion in support of the Zad31 and
translated an earlier pamphlet by the Mauvaise Troupe (2018 [2016]), hence
giving it more impact. The Zad subsequently became the focus of her latest
book The Commune Form: The Transformation of Everyday Life (2024).32
This book in turn has strongly influenced my own understanding of the Zad
and its place in the history of protest. And so, the chain of transmission,
recycling, and adaptation extends into the present work.
But here Ross and I part ways. She refers to the Commune as a “form”
that continues to be materialized in different circumstances of resistance
and revolt. This terminology suggests that the Commune is a timeless idea
that keeps returning in new guises. It leaves implicit the media pathways,
aesthetic forms, and paper trails through which the memory of earlier
communes and that of 1871 in particular has been transferred to later gen-
erations: the focus of this book. As we have seen, cultural production across
multiple arenas—including academic work—has played a key role in shap-
ing, sustaining, and transforming the idea of the Commune over time. Its
recurrence as a point of reference cannot be disconnected from these histo-
ries of mediation and from the dynamics of memory work that reactivates
earlier accounts.
The discussion so far has looked at the rearview mirror through which
climate activists position themselves in relation to earlier protest events and
negotiate their way forward by looking to the past for inspiration. The spot-
light now shifts to narratives about climate activism itself. In the fullness of
time, these too may become part of the memoryscape of future movements;
in the short term, they feed back into mobilization.
Storytelling in Mobilization
In a landmark study Francesca Polletta (2006) showed the value of story-
telling in advocacy. In bringing minoritized voices into the public arena,
stories told by individuals work accumulatively to persuade outsiders of
the legitimacy of particular claims. In what follows, I build on this basic
connection between storytelling and advocacy, while turning specifically to
mediated stories about the climate movement itself where the voices of indi-
vidual activists are embedded in complex cultural objects rather than heard
in an oral setting.

212 REMEMBERING HOPE
And stories there are. In preparing this chapter, I was astonished by the
sheer number of standalone films, books, pamphlets, and video clips recall-
ing protest actions relating to the climate movement and its predecessors
in the 1960s and 1970s. Many of these capture locally specific experi-
ences. Many also travel under the radar of critics and even of the public at
large, having been typically produced within the framework of “alternative
media” (Atton 2015)—activist publishing houses, production companies,
independent bookstores, and fringe festivals. In line with the prefigurative
principle that ends and means should chime, including the principle of the
Commons, publications are often authored collectively and anonymously:
witness books by Collectif comm’un (2019), Ende Gela¨nde (2022), and the
above-mentioned Mauvaise Troupe (2018).
The collectively authored Ende Gela¨nde: We Shut Shit Down (2022) exem-
plifies memory work through storytelling or what has been called “narrative
activism.”33 It seeks to prolong protest by turning it into a story that can
influence the public once brought into circulation. By its own account, Ende
Gela¨nde: We Shut Shit Down aims to “capture a piece of the movement’s his-
tory” and hence to “share inspirational moments and experiences” (12).34
It includes sections on the history and future direction of the movement, a
glossary of key terms, and, crucially for the discussion here, a section on “the
Ende Gela¨nde experience.” Here the reader finds autobiographical accounts
by activists—by JJ, Manni, Kalinia, Alice, Johanna, and Marc, respectively—
about their experiences in the movement and how they became involved.
This is propagation through drama and affect rather than facts and figures
about the fossil fuel industry. The opening chapter is especially noteworthy
for its literary style, providing a dramatic account of what it felt like to be
involved in one of Ende Gela¨nde’s direct action attempts to halt the work-
ings of a lignite coal mine in western Germany. The narrator pulls out many
rhetorical stops to give a sense of what it was like to join one of the “climate
camps” mentioned by Malm, to prepare for action and wait suspensefully
for the moment to attack, to feel the adrenaline-fueled thrill of breaking en
masse through the police lines before being arrested; the shared joy of doing
and acting together (2022: 26–27). This is classic drama, building to a climax
and focalized through a first-person narrator.35 Although JJ has no illusions
about the material impact of their brief disruption to the mine, they sug-
gest that the action at least yielded enduring stories which will help generate
enthusiasm for the movement when reproduced in cafés, on social media
and, presumably also, in the book we are now reading (17).

MEMORY WORK IN CLIMATE ACTIVISM 213
This textual testimony about an Ende Gela¨nde action itself amplifies the
story told a couple of years earlier in the documentary Ende Gela¨nde: Der
Film (Grewes & Staiger 2019). Now available online with English subtitles
and seen to date by 29,000 viewers (not huge for YouTube but not noth-
ing either), Ende Gela¨nde: Der Film shows an embedded activist-journalist
accompanying a group of young activists as they join in a coordinated tres-
pass of the surface mine at Garzweiler in the west of Germany, an action
that lasts several days. The older journalist mediates between the experi-
ence on the ground and the viewing public by giving a platform to their
ideals, excitement, exhaustion, fears, hopes, and, finally, their satisfaction
at having collectively broken through the police cordon to reach the mine.
Part of this is captured in conversations with and between the activists;
as importantly, it is captured in visually eye-catching ways. Illustrating the
importance of visuality to the impact of protest, the film thus ends with
an aerial view of the vast open mine, the epitome of devastation, across
which the Ende Gela¨ndists are running. Dressed in their signature white
overalls, they seem to pour over the perimeter of the mine, their move-
ment forming a contrast to the harsh fixity of the devastated landscape
bare of all life; their diminutive size a contrast to the huge scale of the
canyon.
Appearing within months of the protest, the documentary brought that
protest event back into circulation as a narrativized audiovisual memory. It
ends on an upbeat. The activists talk about the excitement of the day and
their conviction that they will be back for more; this is then followed by a
sequence of singing and dancing that also recalls the energizing use of move-
ment and music in The New Babylon (Chapter 4). It would be going too far
to say that the attempt to halt the diggers was carried out with the aim of pro-
ducing images. But there is an element of truth in this. Climate activists too
show a keen awareness of the importance of visuals in garnering attention
and attracting support. Where the archives discussed in the previous chapter
were designed to prolong a movement by ensuring it can be remembered
in the future, here audiovisual memory work—produced in collaboration
with the activists—is designed to contribute to the movement’s sustainabil-
ity. Activists who watch the footage may renew their commitment by reliving
the drama; interested outsiders, if they take the trouble to watch, may be
seduced by the images into becoming a supporter. Mediation and embod-
ied collective action alternate and feed back into each other, and continue
to do so as long as a meaningful story is brought into play. These offshoots

214 REMEMBERING HOPE
of the movement are part of “the social organization of the capacity to mean
effectively” (Polletta & Chen 2012: 488).
Documentary Film as Active Witness
Documentary turns out to be a key genre in climate activism. By this I mean
audiovisual compositions in which archival or purpose-made footage is cre-
atively combined to build up stories with a truth claim, often with the aim of
advancing a political cause.36 Documentary has a strong record as an activist
genre, including, for example, such classics as Ellende in de Borinage (Ivens
& Storck, 1934: on striking miners in Belgium) and Barbara Kopple’s Harlan
County U.S.A. (1976: on striking miners in Kentucky). Although it is by no
means certain that the films I discussed will later gain the status of classics,
they certainly have contemporary relevance in “the social organization” of
the movement’s “capacity to mean effectively.”
So far, building archives has not been a notable part of climate activism,
perhaps because archives are slow burners and hence less congenial to a
movement where a sense of urgency is key. In contrast, mediated storytelling
in the form of audiovisual footage is ubiquitous, produced both by partici-
pants in particular protest actions and by “active witnesses.” This is the term
used in the documentary Notre dame des luttes (Castell 2012: 36.36) by one
of the local farmers who had come to express solidarity with the activists in
the Zad when it became clear that they were having to deal with immense
practical problems in a rain-soaked and mud-sodden environment. The idea
of an “active witness” recalls the importance of witnessing to martyrdom
(Chapter 3). In the case of Notre dame des luttes and the other documen-
taries I discuss later, what is being witnessed is activism as such rather than
an unlawful killing. Where a “moral witness” (Margalit 2002:147–182) tes-
tifies to the perpetration of injustice, the “active witness” is someone who,
in bearing witness to acts of resistance, also potentially contributes to their
impact.
“Active witnessing” frames documentary storytelling as a form of active
engagement both on the part of producers (who use their time and
resources) and on the part of receivers (who take time out to watch a movie).
In documentary, experiences and observations are put back into the world
to be “witnessed” and, hopefully, passed on in words and deeds. This is
propaganda in the root sense of propagation, meaning the “breeding” of

MEMORY WORK IN CLIMATE ACTIVISM 215
plants. Publicly available stories in documentary form serve to “propagate”
the memory of activism and, in appealing to readers and viewers, act as mul-
tipliers. The aesthetic dimensions of such narratives—their power to grab
and hold a receiver’s attention—is a key facilitator of this process. Although
documentaries are not usually associated with aesthetics, I will argue in what
follows, and in line with my findings in Chapter 4, that aesthetic form is a
key part of the activist memory work they perform. I do so with reference to
films about one type of protest: tree-sitting.
Living Among the Trees
Tree-sitting and other modes of occupying forest spaces have been a part
of the activist repertoire since at least the “Battle of Waller Creek” in 1969,
when a group of students from the University of Texas at Austin climbed into
a grove of live oak and cypress trees to protect them from being chopped
down to make way for an extended football stadium.37 The Chipko move-
ment in Northern India, inspired by Gandhian notions of civil disobedience,
famously saw women hugging trees in the 1970s in order to protect them
from being felled with potentially disastrous consequences both for the envi-
ronment and for the integrity of their communal life. The tree-sitting in New
Zealand’s Pureora National Park in 1978, an ultimately successful protest
against logging, has already been mentioned as part of the memoryscape of
Extinction Rebellion UK. There is no shortage of examples of this highly
localized form of civil disobedience.
One such high-profile case is Julia “Butterfly” Hill, who lived up in a
61-meter-high tree in northern California for a record-breaking 738 days in
order to save the 1,000-year-old redwood from being logged; she was suc-
cessful in the end, at least when it came to saving that single tree. Her story
was the subject of a memoir (Hill 2000) and a documentary (Wolens 2000;
where one witness likened her to Rosa Parks; 58.16). Hill has also figured
in other documentaries, including Tree-Sit: The Art of Resistance (Ficklin
& Andrews 2001), and is presumed to have been the main inspiration for
the tree-sitting character Olivia Vandergriff in Richard Powers’s Pulitzer-
winning novel The Overstory (2018). Although Hill’s action was linked on
occasion to broader campaigns of the Earth First! movement, her story is
very much that of the heroically defiant individual who communes with
nature while defying gravity and the police; her memoir is tellingly subtitled

216 REMEMBERING HOPE
“The Story of a Tree, a Woman, and the Struggle to Save the Redwoods” (Hill
2000).
Hill’s story fits into a narrative tradition within US environmentalism
lauding heroic individuals in their struggle to protect nature, stretching
from Henry David Thoreau’s Walden, or Life in the Woods (1854) to the
eco-warrior Edward Abbey, author of a novel about environmentalist civil
disobedience called The Monkey Wrench Gang (1975) and himself the sub-
ject of the documentary Wrenched (Lincoln 2014). There is certainly more
to be said about the schemata underlying such narratives and their possi-
ble link to specifically North American wilderness imaginaries. However,
my concern in what follows is with a different sort of tree-sitting, namely,
ones embedded in the collective occupation of forests; ones that are both
acts of resistance to the destruction of trees and prefigurative experiments
in new ways of living together. These are “new,” but also “old” in the sense of
being attempts to return to a frugal guardianship of the natural world more
characteristic of premodern and indigenous cultures than of extractivist cap-
italism. In creating alternative living spaces or “ecotopias” forest occupations
become material affirmations of alternative values. Tree-sitters are not only
acting against ecocide but also, and crucially, in favor of life. It is a matter of
Lebenswert, what makes life worth living, as one of the activists interviewed
in the documentary Trees of Protest insists (Schader 2019: 07.13).
Woods offer an arena for protest very different from cities, if only because
they immediately place humans in an environment that extends the scope of
the political into the well-being of other species. Where in earlier chapters
I discussed the cultural memory of city-based occupations and demonstra-
tions, here the focus turns to a different tradition: that of setting up camp in
an area threatened with destruction from mining, logging, or other develop-
ers. As Kristin Ross points out, eco-occupation is a form of protest that works
primarily by defending what is valuable rather than by seeking to destroy the
enemy (2023b: 69–79). Hence the term “Zone to be defended.” The German
slogans “Hambi Stays!” (Hambi bleibt!) and “Lützerath Stays!” (Lützerath
bleibt!) also capture the idea of defending what has long existed as well as
the new community brought into existence by the protest itself.38
The Zad at Notre-Dame-des-Landes is the prime French example of a
defensive eco-occupation in a rural setting, including the forested area at
Rohanne. As participants regularly pointed out, Notre-Dame-des-Landes
drew on the memory of an earlier eco-occupation at Larzac in southwestern
France (1971–1981), subject of the award-winning documentary Tous au

MEMORY WORK IN CLIMATE ACTIVISM 217
Larzac (Rouad 2011). This drawn-out occupation, based on collaborations
between farmers and eco-activists, ended by forcing plans for a military air-
base in a sheep-farming area to be abandoned. Comparable occupations
have had a similar resonance in other countries. The most salient UK case
is “the Battle of the Newbury Bypass,” which also figures in the XR memo-
ryscape. This was the months-long occupation of woodland in the British
Midlands in 1996 to protect it from a planned motorway; although all the
trees were felled in the end, the protest is nevertheless believed to have
had an inhibiting effect on further developments. In the Netherlands, most
salient was the “Battle for Amelisweerd” near Utrecht in 1982, which also
involved the partially successful occupation of trees in protest against a
planned motorway and is now part of the canon of local history.39 The most
salient German case relates to the repeated occupation of the forest of Ham-
bach in the period 2012–2018, which turned “Hambi” into an inspirational
memory site in later campaigns against lignite mining. In Ende Gela¨nde:
Der Film (2019), mentioned earlier, some demonstrators can be seen car-
rying “Hambi” flags in their march to Garzweiler; more recently, similar
flags appeared during the occupation of Lützerath, known as “the Rhineland
ZAD,” which was violently dispersed in January 2023.40 There is thus a con-
tinuous rollover between action and remembered actions, especially ones
that have had some measure of success, even if that success is neither total
nor definitive.
There seems to be something that speaks to the imagination—something
highly memorable—about people defending trees that are bigger than they
are and set to last longer. Not only are trees themselves eye-catching, they
have a long cultural association with the idea of life itself and, since the
revolutions of the late eighteenth century, with ideas of freedom.41 For-
est occupations presumably also gain an extra appeal from their resonance
with the cultural memory of romantic outlaws living in the woods (as in
Robin Hood against the Sheriff of Nottingham).42 One way or another,
documentaries about tree-sitting are legion.
Mention can be made here, for example, of Tales of Resistance: The Bat-
tle of the Newbury Bypass (Lowe 2018), brought out almost twenty years
after the last protesters had been removed and the last tree felled, and New-
bury: Wars of the British Tree People (Purdue & Stucke 1996), brought out
in the immediate aftermath of the occupation and billed as “a picturesque
and daring tale of British anti-road protest, told from 50m up a majestic
pine tree.”43 Quand les arbres s’agitent (Automedia de la Zad 2012) and

218 REMEMBERING HOPE
Notre-Dame-des-Landes: Opération Astérix (Turpin 2013) similarly portray
the defense of the Rohanne forest as part of the larger Zad at Notre-Dame-
des Landes. Trees of Protest (Schader 2019) and Hambi: Der Kampf um den
Hambacher Wald (Reiter 2019) both depict the occupation of Hambach as
part of the larger struggle against open-pit lignite mining in West Germany.
Like the many films of the Commune discussed in Chapter 4, these docu-
mentaries share a common fabula. Despite the differences in the contexts
depicted, they tell the same basic story about an asymmetrical struggle
between the developers, supported by police and heavy machinery, and de-
growthers supported by their commitment and ingenuity. The story begins
with a threat to the woods, proceeds to a middle when the trees are occupied
and (sort of ) ends when all the tree houses are destroyed and the protesters
arrested by heavily equipped police. In some cases we hear that the destruc-
tion of the trees was later postponed, reduced in scope or, very occasionally,
canceled.
Narrative theory, as the reader will know by now, has traditionally sup-
posed that the meaning of events is determined by their outcome, be that a
“happy ever after” or some other resolution or dénouement. Although sev-
eral films end on a minor upbeat, no film ends with a “happy ever after” or
even definitive ending, since it’s made clear that this local conflict is part of
a much bigger and ongoing struggle against planetary destruction. In these
films, however, it is the middle and not the end that determines the overall
meaning of the story. This has everything to do with the artistry of the narra-
tion and the agency of the aesthetic, to recall the discussion in Chapter 4. The
claim is not that these documentaries are all artistic masterpieces, though
some are very well made. It is simply that they use the resources of cinema—
color, montage, sound, camera movement, spoken word, and so on—to
make certain parts of the protest “stick in memory” more than others. A
closer look at one documentary, which I take to exemplify general trends,
should make this clear.
Hambi (2019)
Hambi: Der Kampf um den Hambacher Wald (Hambi: The Battle for the
Hambach Forest) (2019), directed by Lukas Reiter, has been shown at mul-
tiple film festivals and televised, and is now available through public libraries
in Germany. It was described by the mainstream Der Spiegel as a “monument

MEMORY WORK IN CLIMATE ACTIVISM 219
to the activists who, in a David-and-Goliath manner, had opposed the
destruction of the forest by the energy company,”44 One of several documen-
taries on the topic, Hambi tells the story of the occupation of the Hambach
Forest, which since 2012 has been repeatedly occupied in protest against a
lignite mine that not only supports the fossil fuel industry but also threatens
the trees.45 The documentary focusses on the 2018 occupation and is based
on footage produced as the occupation was ongoing, so in anticipation of its
becoming memory in the future.
Hambi begins with the image and the sound of an enormous machine
moving ominously in darkness across a devastated landscape, before switch-
ing to the green forest. This sets up a counterpoint that continues throughout
the documentary as it alternates between images of the police, active wit-
nesses who have come to show their support for those living in the trees,
and the tree-sitters themselves. In the process, the soft green of the trees
alternates with the black uniforms of the police, bird cries and human voices
with the sound of machines. Chapter 3 discussed melodrama in terms of the
visual opposition between might and right; here we find an environmen-
talist variation of this opposition, now between environmental destruction
and the value of life itself as displayed in the color and shape of the
trees.
As events move slowly but inevitably toward the final stand-off with the
police (interrupted when one of the treesitters accidentally falls to his death)
the narration regularly puts the passage of time on hold by dwelling on life
among the trees and inviting the viewer to enjoy the moment itself. As one
of the activists remarks: “This here, it’s all about images” (09.21). The film
offers a “sensorium,” to recall Juan Egea’s description of the 15-M archive,
of a poetic world very different from the one where lignite is mined. Two
subjects in particular hold the attention of the camera.
First, the oddly constructed tree houses (Figure 7.4), which are displayed
from below as well as used as observation points for looking down. Tree
houses are “crazy” structures high up in the trees that speak to the protesters’
ability to think and live outside of the box. A recent exhibition and subse-
quent publication on “protest architecture” (Elser et al. 2023) describes tree
houses as joyful exercises of the freedom to invent something new beyond
the constraints of what is predictable and normal.46 Improvised with recy-
cled materials, strangely shaped, ingeniously constructed, and often located
high in the foliage, they are photogenically memorable precisely because
they are so odd (Figure 7.5). Their strange shapes express the creativity and

220 REMEMBERING HOPE
Figure 7.4 Still from Hambi: Der Kampf um den Hambacher Wald, dir L.
Reiter (2019). Transparent Film. Reproduced with kind permission.
courage of those who occupy them and give visual expression to the idea
of living differently as the camera follows the everyday rhythms of cooking,
eating, working, and talking among the trees. The result is an aesthetic expe-
rience for the viewers. Here it is useful to recall Viktor Shklovsky’s theory
introduced in Chapter 4, which defined the aesthetic in terms of its power
to break through habits of perception so that we can see things in a fresh
light.
The attention paid to life in the tree houses is paralleled by the camera’s
dwelling lovingly on the trees themselves as they tower above the human
actors (Figure 7.5). The trees’ height, girth, and ability to sway in the breeze,
often seen from below, make for strong visual presences that extend the
experience of the aesthetic into the more-than-human world. Trees, tree-
houses, and humans coexist as part of an ecotopian experiment in living
frugally and in harmony with nature.
At times it is hard to know, as in Figure 7.6, where people end and
trees begin. Images like this are complemented by the voices of protesters
talking—again lovingly—about their experiences of “living on a living being
that moves” (1.08.17). Just before the final treehouses are destroyed and
the protesters arrested we hear one of them saying, “Nobody can take this
memory away from me” (1.09.34). Her phrasing echoes Louise Michel’s rec-
ollection of the Commune as a moment of great happiness: “We were happy

MEMORY WORK IN CLIMATE ACTIVISM 221
Figure 7.5 Still from Hambi: Der Kampf um den Hambacher Wald, dir L.
Reiter (2019). Transparent Film. Reproduced with kind permission.
Figure 7.6 Still from Hambi: Der Kampf um den Hambacher Wald, dir L.
Reiter (2019). Transparent Film. Reproduced with kind permission.
then, so happy, because it was a lovely thing to live our life and struggle”
(CW 04/1891; also quoted in Chapter 4).
In the last minutes of the film, the camera pans out to show the larger for-
est, followed by a black screen telling us that the last tree hut in the Hambach
forest was destroyed in October 2018 (1.19.32). This is then followed by the

222 REMEMBERING HOPE
Figure 7.7 Still from Hambi: Der Kampf um den Hambacher Wald, dir L.
Reiter (2019). Transparent Film. Reproduced with kind permission.
announcement that the energy company had decided to put a moratorium
on further tree cutting in the region, followed by yet another reversal, in
which we hear that the “the protest is still going on today” and the tree huts
are back because the threat of destruction is still there. In this curiously stut-
tering manner, the narrative arc that appeared to have reached some sort of
closure takes off again. (This stop-and-start again ending echoes the Collec-
tif Comm’un’s history of forty years of resistance at Notre-Dame-des-Landes
which ends with, “Nothing is Finished; Everything is Starting” [2019:217]).
The final image in the film shows the forest and the devastated landscape
behind it, but also, towering above them yet another crazy high structure on
which a protester is sitting (Figure 7.7). This composition evokes a double
freedom: on the one hand, a negative freedom from the tyranny of economic
growth, however short-lived and however ongoing the threat; on the other
hand, a positive freedom to reinvent the world.
So what can we conclude from this? To begin with, that the film extends
the protesters’ resistance to ecological destruction by bringing their occupa-
tion of the woods back into memory. In doing so, it deploys one of the central
tactics of nonviolent resistance by using visual counterpoint to expose the
violence of the enemy and oppose right to might.
However, the film does more than denounce destruction. Above all, it
celebrates the things worth fighting for. It is against mining and fossil

MEMORY WORK IN CLIMATE ACTIVISM 223
fuels, to be sure, but it’s also for a different set of values. The alternatives
are not just morally right but also aesthetically pleasing and appealing:
lovely. This double movement of acting against but above all for is cru-
cial to the documentary’s impact and it is a key feature of activist memory
work as I have presented it in this book. With the help of the camera
and narrative montage, the experiment in living differently is made more
memorable than the fact that the occupation was short-lived. In short,
we’ve been dealing here not just with the memory of protest, but with a
different way of telling stories that downplays outcomes and makes the
“might have been” meaningful instead. To recall the words of Havel once
more: “[Hope] is not the conviction that something will turn out well,
but the certainty that something makes sense, regardless of how it turns
out.”
Small Wins, Big Stakes
This chapter began by observing, with Amitav Ghosh and others, that the
climate crisis was also a crisis in storytelling; that new narrative schemata
and new frames were needed to understand the threats we face but also,
as importantly, to generate alternative scenarios for the future. The insistent
references to predecessors, and the discussions about which predecessors are
relevant, shows how climate activists use memory as a resource for impro-
vising their way through radical uncertainty. In a predominantly affirmative
memoryscape, canonical sites of memory are invoked as positive examples.
They are models for strategic action rather than part of a larger narrative
about the direction of change.
The examples of documentary storytelling I subsequently considered do
reflect the struggle to develop new ways of narrativizing the world and, in
their stop-and-start again endings, they also help in developing alternative
models. These are less focused on dramatic turning points leading to defini-
tive endings, or on monumental stories of collective progress spearheaded by
heroic individuals and ending in grand finales or apocalyptic endgames, but
on something different. In the future this difference may get its own name
and be related to the rhythms of resilience and the articulation of positive
attachments. But just as the Zad looked back to the Commune, I too find it
useful here to call on some of the insights of my predecessors as a renewable
resource for thinking about the present.

224 REMEMBERING HOPE
In his influential Metahistory (1973), already mentioned in my Introduc-
tion, philosopher of history Hayden White argued that people make sense
of historical events with the help of plot models that are deeply rooted in
cultural memory. He identified four models as particularly important. These
are romance, where a hero gains definitive victory and final liberation; satire,
where such a victory is shown to be impossible; tragedy, where some people
are forced to undergo a terrible fate; and comedy, where “hope is held out
for the temporary triumph of man” through “the occasional reconciliation of
the forces at play in the social and natural worlds” (White 1973). Rereading
this typology more than fifty years later, one is struck by its anthropocentric
worldview. It is all about what happens to “men” in a universe that was not
necessarily made for our happiness; where what we want as humans is now
running into the power of the planet. Moreover, it is still premised on the
idea that the telos or ending of a narrative determines the meaning of the
events narrated.
Despite these limitations, White’s typology offers a tool to think with. The
idea of comedy is especially promising, since there the ending is at best provi-
sional.47 In White’s definition, which builds on a long critical tradition that
does not consider comedy merely a laughing matter, it is associated with
new beginnings and with regeneration. It helps us to make cognitive and
affective sense of an imperfect world by organizing events so that they end
with a temporary resolution of conflict and a life-affirming renewal of social
bonds. Witness Shakespeare’s comedies, which always close with a party and
with the promise of new life without there being any illusion that all the
problems of the world have been solved. Seen in this way, the environmen-
talist narratives studied in this chapter can perhaps best be described as new
variations on the “comic.” Despite the grimness of the overall context, they
are life-affirming even as they open the possibility of future threats that will
require renewed action. One of the remarkable findings of this chapter is
that environmentalist memory work celebrates minor successes along with
the beauty that still exists, the moments of happiness that still occur, and the
possibilities that might be realized again, despite the many dark clouds on
the horizon. These are small stories about small wins. But the stakes are big.
As such, this activist memory work represents a distinct type of comic
sense-making, It differs from satire (supposing the futility of action) and
from tragedy (supposing the powerlessness of utter defeat). And it also dif-
fers from the happy-ever-after “romance” of social revolution that sustained
left-wing thinking for so long, In this comic mode, there are no clear-cut

MEMORY WORK IN CLIMATE ACTIVISM 225
beginnings, middles, and ends, but rather repeated uprisings and repeated
attempts to bring alternatives into being even if they are ephemeral and even
if, in the first instance, they are limited to a single patch of wood. But the
very fact that these encampments happened and woodland destruction was
delayed is already a proof that things could—and should—be different. In
discussing the carnivalesque celebration of the centenary of the Commune
in Chapter 4, I quoted the phrase, “The party only lasted 15 minutes, but
15 minutes like that leave a mark.” The activist memory work evident in
this chapter also challenges the idea that the outcome of a series of events
determines their meaning or, in keeping with comedy, that a definitive
“happy ever after” is possible. Capturing the memory of resistance in craftily
wrought stories prolongs the protest by mobilizing witnesses, recruiting new
members, and adding to the repertoire of possibilities from which future
action can be drawn. It makes sense to keep going.
Which brings us to Greta Thunberg’s 2016 visit to the World Economic
Forum in Davos, where she famously chided the assembled world leaders:
“Adults keep saying. ‘We owe it to the young people to give them hope.’ But
I don’t want your hope. I don’t want you to be hopeful. I want you to panic.
I want you to feel the fear I feel every day. And then I want you to act.”48 In
rejecting “hope” as an excuse for nonaction, Thunberg associates that con-
cept with an optimistic and facile trust that everything will work out for the
best. In proposing “fear” as a better starting point for action, she certainly
got the attention of decision-makers. Her point of course was that change is
needed immediately because there is no long term in a world on fire.
This chapter has shown that hope—as a practice of possibility, not a pas-
sive expectation that things will get better—does have a positive role to play
among climate activists. This takes the form neither of optimism nor of com-
placency. It answers instead to the more qualified understanding of hope,
which I set out in my Introduction, as a readiness to act because it makes
sense to do so despite the reality of past failures and present threats. While
the activist memory work examined here recognizes such threats, it is also
remarkable for using recollection to affirm the positive values which, in mak-
ing sense, also make the struggle worthwhile. This is small comfort, but it is
also not nothing. As Paolo Freire wrote in his Pedagogy of Hope, “Without a
minimum of hope we cannot so much as start the struggle. But without the
struggle hope dissipates” (1994 [1992]: 9).49 As this book has shown, turning
action into memory is part of that dynamic.

Conclusion
This book began by noting that public cultures of memory in the last cen-
tury have become tied to the idea that collective memory is by default about
suffering and grievance. It set out to challenge this assumption by bringing
alternative mnemonic traditions into visibility; specifically, traditions relat-
ing to the history of protest and the attempts made by activists to reinvent the
world. My seven case studies have provided ample evidence of the entangle-
ments between memory and activism. Together they show that there is more
than one way to remember collectively; and that positive action to achieve
social and environmental justice has been at the heart of a significant body
of memory work, much of it never analyzed before. In exploring concrete
cases, my book has opened the possibility, based on historical precedents,
that future practices of public commemoration might be different. In appeal-
ing to history for possibility, this book too is an act of hope that an academic
study will percolate into public discourse.
At the very least, this book has extended the repertoire of examples on
which future theories of collective memory can be based. My claim is not
that memory work relating to protest is representative of all forms of mem-
ory work in society (that would raise the question of what representativity
in collective memory entails) or even that it is statistically relevant. On the
contrary, the scope has been limited to oppositional memory work. This
very limitation has helped in bringing to light the dynamics of mnemonic
practices where contention is both the object and the purpose of recollec-
tion. Although activist interventions have a limited political presence, my
case studies show both that protest can have a long and productive afterlife
in culturally mediated memory and that this memory feeds back into later
mobilizations. As such it is a vector of hope.
Chapter 1 revealed that collective action and recollection work in tandem
and help constitute each other. The past offered a renewable resource with
which socialists and anarchists in late nineteenth-century Britain articulated
their differences. A comparable interplay between memory and agenda-
setting was also witnessed in the reappearance of the Paris Commune in
Nuit Debout (Chapter 4) and in the Zadist movement (Chapter 7). In order
Remembering Hope. Ann Rigney, Oxford University Press. © Oxford University Press (2025).
DOI: 10.1093/oso/9780197789711.003.0009

CONCLUSION 227
to capture this bifocal movement of looking back while planning forward
I introduced the term “retro-prospective memory work.” This reappears
in multiple variants across my seven case studies, including in the form
of anniversaries related to protest events which, with the help of radical
commemorative calendars, serve as periodic reminders of promises and
commitments that have yet to be fulfilled. Chapters 3 and 4 showed with ref-
erence to atrocities committed against protesters that recollection becomes
a way to reconnect with moments of possibility—with past futures—when
history might have gone another way. To be sure: those past possibilities
only return virtually, but they do so with the performative force of an idea
or scenario that shapes expectations and hence steers action.
That performative force is enabled by mediation. One of my central argu-
ments is that memory work intervenes in the present and that the stories
sedimented in the form of texts and images have agency. Chapter 3 showed
how narration itself can perform an act of resistance. By reasserting the value
of lives lost, cultural recollection turns what seemed to be an over-and-done
history back into unfinished business. It offers pushback to history as an
irreversible sequence of events, exposing to the world that what happened
should never have happened. Chapter 4 gave examples of a similar push-
back with reference to recollections of the Paris Commune. In particular, it
highlighted the aesthetic strategies used to overwrite the defeat of the Com-
munards by making their cause the one to be remembered and hence giving
it a second chance. Chapter 6 brought another variant of resistant remem-
brance into play in the form of activist self-archiving. It showed how recent
activists have sought to proactively to ensure their own afterlife by sediment-
ing their plans and actions into records, hence providing other people with
the means to remember them and the chance of giving their protest a second
life in later mobilizations.
Cultural memory work enables actors to be connected across space and
time with the help of different communication technologies. Above all, as my
cases have shown, it is a praxis with the power to convey possibilities. The
agency of cultural forms, and specifically of aesthetic compositions, has been
one of my central themes. Instead of assuming that media are transparent
conduits for preexisting and definitively shaped memories, my case studies
have shown that mediation is a sense-making and affect-generating praxis.
It gives the past meaning by schematizing information. It also gives it a pub-
licly accessible material presence in the form of words, images, sounds, and
shapes, whose energizing aesthetic properties make past events memorable

228 REMEMBERING HOPE
for new publics and help them look at the world differently. The cultural
sedimentation of memories thus provides a key to understanding how recol-
lections of protest are transferred from one movement, and one generation,
to the next. In my Introduction, I defined hope as a future-oriented form
of action, as a practice of possibilities based on the belief that whatever its
outcome, the action itself makes sense. My seven case studies have shown in
different ways how past hope is culturally recalled but also how that cultural
memory work becomes a vector of new hope and part of its contagion.
If my cases show the importance of cultural memory work, they have
also brought home the fact that “culture” is not given once and for all.
Like politics, it is a work in progress. The repertoire—of historical refer-
ence points, forms and genres, sense-making schemata, keywords, and so
on—is continuously evolving as people recycle old forms in new contexts.
Indeed, the cases examined here brought some extraordinary forms of cul-
tural resilience into play, beginning with the canon of memory sites that
are invoked over and again as reference points in different contexts. The
commemorative calendars discussed in Chapter 2, as well as the environ-
mentalist pantheons outlined in Chapter 7, show how common reference
points serve as connectors within and across movements. New names and
events are regularly added and locally inflected, but the Commune and 1848,
Gandhi and Martin Luther King Jr., to name some of the most obvious, make
up the backbone of a persistent tradition. So too does the cultural reper-
toire used to remember protest. Cultural forms like the calendar, graffiti,
and slogans, and cultural schemata like that of martyrdom create less obvi-
ous but no less persistent traditions. This cultural repertoire continues to
evolve in tandem with new media technologies, the shapeshifting of activism
itself, and the creativity of storytellers. The most important takeaway here
is that culture—both the repertoire and particular expressions—constitutes
an actant in its own right that enables the memory of protest and shapes the
manner of its transmission.
In the process, it also evolves in response to developments in the political
and social arena. The historical span covered in my book has helped to bring
this out, particularly in relation to the slow sea change that is occurring in
relation to the narrativization of historical events. One of the mainstays of
modernist time has been the idea that it is possible to make sense of history
by turning it into a narrative with a beginning, middle, and end—the lat-
ter conceived in progressive terms as the culmination of some meliorative
development. The socialists with whom we began in Chapter 1 supposed

CONCLUSION 229
too that history would culminate in a final revolutionary denouement giv-
ing meaning to everything that preceded it. Subsequent chapters disturb this
picture in two ways. First, many of the cultural forms analyzed—festive com-
memorations, calendars, photography, archiving, documentaries—brought
unexpected temporalities into play; they carried memory, but less in the
form of a coherent sequence of events than in the form of highpoints or
special moments captured outside of the irreversible flow of time. More-
over, the audiovisual narratives I analyzed in Chapter 4 seemed to be less
concerned with setting out an irreversible sequence of events than with
confounding the idea of a definitive ending. The climate movement docu-
mentaries analyzed in Chapter 7 also showed narrative becoming detached
from the traditional logic where the end determines the meaning. While sto-
rytelling remains important, it is no longer reliant on linear notions of time
and development. The growing importance of images reflects this change,
but also may have partly caused it.
Such changes in the nuts and bolts of cultural memory work occur in tan-
dem with changes in the metahistorical understanding of the overall shape
of time and history. Between the 1880s (the subject of Chapter 1) and the
2010s (the subject of Chapters 6 and 7) the idea of a grand narrative ending
with the future transformation of the world gave way to a politics of prefig-
uration, which demanded that change start in the here and now without
presuming that collective action has a pregiven telos. These metahistori-
cal positions provide quite different starting points for sense-making. In
Chapter 7 we saw how stories about tree-sitting highlighted—through color,
sound, composition—the values at stake in recent actions. In this way cul-
tural memory work serves to reaffirm over and again the vision of a desired
“life” despite its not having prevailed and despite its now being under threat.
Such stories play an important, but hitherto unrecognized, role in the pol-
itics of prefiguration by “upscaling” it. The take-up of prefigurative action
in memory extends its impact across time and publics as part of a long-term
alternation between action and mediated recollection. Cultural practice and
prefigurative politics continue to co-evolve.
My focus has been on “left-wing” traditions in memory work, both in their
classical socialist and more recent anarchism-inflected forms. Readers may
wonder whether I shouldn’t have spent more time on exploring the role of
memory in right-wing activism. To be sure, memory is mobilized in ethno-
populist activism, most notably in the defense of monuments as we saw in
Chapter 5, and in invocations of a formerly “great” world that can be made

230 REMEMBERING HOPE
great again. However, my aim has not been to provide an account of memory
and activism across the political spectrum. It has been to analyze memory
work linked to the contagion of hope. This meant seeking out places where
the past is being used not as a focus of nostalgia or of a return to a lost Eden,
but as a resource for building a world different from both the past and the
present. Whereas historicist approaches to memory posit the past as some-
thing that can be disconnected from the now or sectioned off in the name
of immutable heritage, the activist memory examined here assumes that the
world is not definitively shaped and that the past is full of unfinished busi-
ness and unrealized possibilities. Other studies will have to work out how
such activist memory fares within the field of force in which it tries to oper-
ate. As Chapter 5 shows, this includes the counterforce of those defending
heritage and the weight of the status quo.
This book has at least made one thing clear. Looking back is not the
prerogative of conservatives. The different forms of retro-prospective mem-
ory described in the preceding chapters show that memory and progressive
politics can and do work together. Culturally mediated memory gives unful-
filled possibilities a second chance. Hope is a renewable—and culturally
contagious—practice of possibilities.

22. The full range of activist calendars remains to be mapped. Mention can here be made of

so as to express a “cult of Humanity”; and linked other divisions of the year to a detailed list
of the historical figures constitutive of the “positivist Pantheon,” who were to be remembered
on a monthly or daily basis. As an example of a more directly activist use of a calendar, men-
tion can also be made of the American Anti-slavery Almanac (American Anti-Slavery Society,
1836–43) whose calendar highlighted every month, using both image and text, an event in
the recent past exemplifying the horrors of slavery. Finally, the Votes for Women pocket calen-
dar (Women’s Social and Political Union, 1911), although it does not have a commemorative
aspect, exemplifies the radical tradition of selling pocket-size calendars as portable signs of
affinity with a movement.
23. Published in the Evening Star newspaper on November 30, 1842, the “Chartist Calendar”
listed significant events for each month in the yearly cycle, including many events that were
milestones in the evolution of Chartism itself. For an excellent overview of the culture of
commemoration within the Chartist movement, see Roberts 2019.
24. Mission statement of the Progressive International: https://progressive.international/about/
en (accessed January 29, 2024). The calendars appear both in a US and a “rest of the world”
version; the latter has been used here.

sacre” of worker-demonstrators in 1886; but without any mention of the anarchist mar-
tyrs who featured so prominently in commemorations in the early years as shown in
Chapter 1.

and as a resource for new generations of activists.

33. The violence against strikers in 1913 was called “Dublin’s Bloody Sunday” in the Irish Inde-
pendent, July 29, 1914; and “Ireland’s First Bloody Sunday” in the Kerryman, January 22, 1916.
The deaths of twenty-two civilians as a result of sectarian violence in Belfast on July 20, 1921,
were initially known as “Belfast’s Bloody Sunday” (Parkinson 2004). But this name did not
“stick,” presumably because it was upstaged by Dublin 1920, but also because it did not fully
answer to the prototype (the deaths were on a Sunday but involved sectarian violence rather
than state terror against demonstrators). For a similar reading of the shifting nomenclature
of the Belfast violence of 1921, see https://www.theirishstory.com/2010/06/24/four-bloody-
sundays/ (accessed March 4, 2024). The salience of Dublin’s 1920 Bloody Sunday was strength-
ened by its being the subject of an annual commemoration in the following decades; Croke
Park was subsequently one of a select number of locations visited in 2011 by Queen Elizabeth
II on the first state visit of a British monarch to post-independence Ireland.
34. The 1905 Bloody Sunday was well-known in Ireland, as evidenced in the Freeman’s Journal,

does my concept of “civic martyrdom” extend to those who seek martyrdom through seeking
harm to others (for example, as suicide bombers).

NOTES 241
between 1871 and 2016 (Combis 2016). The Commune was apparently sufficiently in evi-

49. I am very grateful to Joep Leerssen for drawing my attention to this parallel and for his help
in analyzing the musical score.

a wall in New Caledonia, where the Communards had been imprisoned (02.00); this suggests
that inscribing the slogan on walls was a performative practice before being remediated in film.

xt=According%20to%20an%20April%202020,the%20power%20to%20decree%20removal

Jakarta in 1943 can be found at: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=j-crU3j-XnA (accessed
July 31, 2024).

ria drawing on a French canon of references. The commemoration of Camões in relation to

ument Indië-Nederland; debate is still ongoing; https://nl.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monument_

28. The term “countermemory” became common currency through the English translation of the

shows how every new history of the French Revolution uses forms of “intertextual antagonism”
to position itself in relation to earlier ones (53-62). The mechanism was recently illustrated in
the substitution of the statue of an Indigenous woman in Mexico City for one of Columbus;
https://www.bbc.com/news/world-latin-america-58893051 (accessed July 31, 2024).
29. The history of public monuments is illustrated in Saunders 2018; Savage 2018 [1997]; Leerssen

1995; Winter 1995; Yarrington 1988; Riegl 1982 [1928]. On the more experimental language
of post–World War II memorials, see also Huyssen 2022; Young 2016; Lupu 2003; Young 1993.
30. While Young (1993, 2016) locates the turning point in the post-Holocaust world, Winter (1995)

nated here in the Puerta del Sol: We were asleep, We woke up”; https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/
Movimiento_15-M (accessed August 1, 2024). In 2000 a memorial was put up to the antinu-
clear Greenham Common Women’s Peace Camp (1981–2000), notably in the form of a garden
and on the site of the protest rather than in an urban center. In a similarly off-center position,
the monument to the Chicago Martyrs, like the one to Commune, was erected in a ceme-
tery; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haymarket_Martyrs%27_Monument (accessed August 1,
2024).
34. On the strategic locatedness of monuments, see especially Shanken 2022; Leerssen & Rigney

39. See, for example, the report “Unstable Pedestals: Controversial Monuments in Public Space”;

505–506.
